Precis & Comprehension

Summarising, main idea, inference and vocabulary.

🎯 Take Mock Test

Learning Objectives

  • Understand the fundamental concepts of Precis & Comprehension
  • Apply key formulas and techniques to solve problems
  • Practice with exam-level questions to build speed and accuracy

Precis & Comprehension: The Art of Condensation and Understanding

In the context of UPSC Mains, the ability to distill complex arguments into their essential core is as vital as retaining them in full. This lesson bridges two critical skills: Summarising (Precis), which requires rigorous adherence to the original author's logic and tone without personal bias, and Comprehension, which demands that you infer meaning, identify the main idea, and navigate vocabulary nuances hidden within a text. Mastery here ensures you can handle the high-level reading passages found in both the compulsory language paper and the optional papers.

Precis: The Mechanics of Reduction

A precis is not merely a short summary; it is a faithful, condensed restatement of the original text that preserves its argumentative structure. The standard rule of thumb is to write a precis that is approximately one-third the length of the source passage, though the primary metric for success is whether the main idea and supporting reasons remain intact.

How to Identify the Main Idea

The Main Idea is the central thesis or argument that the author intends to prove. To find it, you must look past the introductory fluff and the concluding remarks to locate the sentence that connects all supporting evidence. In exam settings, the examiner often asks you to identify the "central thought" or the "over-arching theme," which requires you to distinguish between a specific detail (a fact) and the general principle it illustrates.

Inference: Reading Between the Lines

An inference is a logical conclusion that must be drawn from the information provided, even though it is not explicitly stated as a sentence. It requires you to connect two pieces of information in the text to understand a relationship or a hidden implication. For instance, if a passage states that "sales dropped after the price hike" and "competitors did not raise prices," the inference is that "the competitor's lower price was a factor in the sales drop."

Vocabulary: Contextual Usage

Vocabulary in this module refers to understanding how words function within a specific context rather than just their dictionary definition. The examiner tests your ability to recognize connotations, synonyms that fit the tone, and antonyms that contradict the flow of an argument. You must be able to replace a word with another that carries the same weight and emotional resonance in that specific sentence.

Solved Example 1: Précis Construction

Technique: The Claim-Reason-Conclusion Filter
Passage: The rise of remote work has revolutionized the corporate landscape. Many employees now prefer working from home because it offers a better work-life balance and eliminates daily commutes. However, this shift poses challenges for team cohesion and spontaneous collaboration. Some experts argue that virtual meetings cannot replace the energy of face-to-face interactions. Despite these drawbacks, companies are investing heavily in digital tools to bridge the gap.
Solution: The rise of remote work has transformed corporate life by offering better balance but challenging team cohesion; while experts doubt virtual meetings can replace physical interaction, companies are investing in digital tools to maintain collaboration.

Solved Example 2: Comprehension and Inference

Technique: Distinguishing Fact from Inference
Question: Read the sentence below. Which statement is an inference rather than a fact?
"The ancient city was abandoned after a severe earthquake destroyed its main aqueducts, and the surrounding region became arid."
Solution: The correct inference is: "The lack of water sources likely caused the population to leave."
Reasoning: The text states the city was abandoned (Fact) and that aqueducts were destroyed (Fact). It does not explicitly state *why* they left, but it implies a causal link between the destruction of water supply and abandonment. Therefore, linking the two is an inference.

Common Traps

  • Adding Personal Opinion: Never insert your own agreement or disagreement with the author; a précis must be neutral.
  • Changing the Tone: If the original text is formal, do not rewrite it in slang; if it is critical, do not make it sound optimistic.
  • The "List" Mistake: A précis must be a continuous paragraph of prose, not a bulleted list of points.
  • Ignoring Connectors: Words like 'however', 'therefore', and 'consequently' are crucial for maintaining the logical flow; removing them breaks the argument.

Memory Tricks

  • The "So What?" Test: For every sentence you write in a précis, ask yourself "So what?". If the answer is just a detail that supports the main point, keep it. If it's just an example, drop it.
  • Vocabulary Swap: When unsure of a word's meaning in context, look at the surrounding words. If the sentence talks about "growth" and "expansion," a word like "thrive" fits better than "survive."
  • The One-Sentence Rule: Try to summarize the entire passage in one sentence first. This forces you to identify the absolute core idea before expanding it into a paragraph.
Exam-Day Strategy for Précis & Comprehension
Do not start writing immediately. Spend the first 2-3 minutes underlining the topic sentence of every paragraph in the passage. This visual map will prevent you from getting lost in details. When writing, use a pen that allows easy crossing out; if you realize a sentence is redundant, cross it out and rewrite the paragraph with better connectors to ensure smooth flow. Finally, always check your word count against the original length before submitting.

The Nuance of Synthesis: Beyond Simple Summarization

Moving beyond the mechanical reduction of text, advanced precis writing demands synthetic reconstruction. This is not merely cutting words; it is re-engineering the logical architecture of the argument. In high-level comprehension tasks, you must distinguish between a summation (listing points) and a synthesis (integrating themes). The examiner often presents passages where the author shifts tone mid-argument or introduces a counter-narrative that contradicts the initial premise. A basic student might summarize both sides equally, but an advanced candidate must identify the author's ultimate stance. If the text moves from optimism to caution, your précis must reflect this pivot using transition words like "ultimately," "despite," or "conversely." The goal is to capture the trajectory of thought, not just the static content.

Technique: The Pivot-Point Extraction Method
Question: Read the following passage. Identify the central thesis and draft a précis that captures the shift in argument.

Passage: "The traditional model of education relies heavily on rote memorization, which ensures a baseline of knowledge retention among students. However, recent pedagogical studies suggest that this method stifles critical thinking and creativity. While some educators argue that foundational facts are non-negotiable, others contend that the curriculum must evolve to include project-based learning. Consequently, many modern institutions are integrating digital tools to foster collaboration, even if it means sacrificing some traditional lecture time."

Solution: While rote memorization ensures knowledge retention, its limitation in fostering critical thinking has prompted a shift towards project-based learning; consequently, modern institutions are integrating digital tools to prioritize collaboration over traditional lecturing.


The Lexical Precision: Antonyms and Connotative Shifts

Vocabulary in the UPSC context is rarely about obscure words; it is about connotative precision. The examiner tests your ability to recognize when a word like "significant" implies importance versus magnitude, or when "liberal" denotes political ideology versus generosity. A critical skill is spotting antonymic traps within the text. For instance, if an author praises a policy as "beneficial," but later describes it as "onerous," you must capture this contrast in your précis without using contradictory adjectives that confuse the reader. You must learn to replace words with synonyms that preserve the emotional weight. If the original text uses "dismal" (negative), do not replace it with "bad"; use "gloomy," "bleak," or "disheartening." This level of vocabulary control demonstrates a deep reading comprehension, which is essential for the Comprehension paper.

Technique: The Connotation Check
Question: In the sentence "The government's decision was a bold move," what is the most appropriate antonymic replacement for "bold" that fits a context of risk?

Options:

  1. A. Cautious
  2. B. Rash
  3. C. Prudent
  4. D. Deliberate
Technique: The Connotation Check (Solution)
Solution:
Correct Answer: B. Rash
Reasoning: "Bold" in this context implies taking a risk. While "cautious" is an antonym, it lacks the specific nuance of risk-taking. "Rash" captures the negative connotation of excessive risk often implied when contrasting with "bold." "Prudent" is too positive, and "deliberate" is neutral.
Technique: The Connotation Check (Solution)
Solution:
Correct Answer: B. Rash
Reasoning: "Bold" in this context implies taking a risk. While "cautious" is an antonym, it lacks the specific nuance of risk-taking. "Rash" captures the negative connotation of excessive risk often implied when contrasting with "bold." "Prudent" is too positive, and "deliberate" is neutral.
Exam-Day Strategy: The "Silent Reader" Drill
Before writing your précis, read the passage silently once without writing anything. Ask yourself: "What is the author's emotional arc?" If you feel tension between two paragraphs, that is where your transition words must go. This mental rehearsal prevents you from getting stuck on syntax and ensures your summary flows logically.


Advanced Error Spotting: Multiple Faults in Syntax

In the grammar section of the Comprehension paper, questions often present sentences with multiple errors simultaneously. You must identify which error is the most fatal to the sentence's logic or grammatical structure. Common traps include subject-verb agreement errors hidden by intervening clauses, misplaced modifiers that change the meaning, and dangling participles. For example, in a sentence like "The report, written by the committee, was delayed due to the lack of data," the error might be subtle: the participle "written" modifies "report"; the sentence "The report, written by the committee, has been delayed" is grammatically sound. Advanced aspirants must learn to parse complex sentences mentally before selecting an option.

Technique: The Syntax Deconstruction
Question: Identify the sentence with the most severe grammatical fault.

Options:

  1. A. The committee, having met yesterday, decided to postpone the meeting.
  2. B. Having met yesterday, the decision was made by the committee to postpone the meeting.
  3. C. The decision to postpone the meeting was made by the committee having met yesterday.
  4. D. The committee decided to postpone the meeting after having met yesterday.
Technique: The Syntax Deconstruction (Solution)
Solution:
Correct Answer: B
Reasoning: In option B, the phrase "Having met yesterday" is a dangling modifier. It grammatically modifies "the decision," implying that the "decision" met yesterday, rather than the committee. This is a classic structural error often tested in advanced grammar sections.
Exam-Day Strategy: The "Subject Hunt"
When facing complex sentences, immediately identify the subject and the main verb. Ignore all the modifiers (phrases starting with 'which', 'that', 'having'). If the subject and verb do not agree in number or person when you strip away the modifiers, you have found the error.


Common Traps for Advanced Sections

  • The "Neutral" Trap: Avoid making your précis sound robotic or neutral when the original text is passionate. You must mirror the author's tone.
  • The "Detail Creep": Do not include specific examples (e.g., names of cities, dates) unless they are central to the argument; this wastes precious word count.
  • The "Connector Overload": Using too many transition words (however, therefore) can make the précis sound disjointed. Use them only where the logical shift is critical.
  • The "Passive Voice" Trap: While passive voice is sometimes necessary for objectivity, overusing it weakens the argument. Prefer active voice unless the actor is unknown or irrelevant.

Structural Mapping of Argumentative Texts

Effective précis writing begins with a reliable map of the passage’s logical architecture. Argumentative texts typically follow a pattern: a claim, supporting evidence, counter‑considerations, and a concluding synthesis. The first step is to segment the passage into these functional units. Use marginal symbols — such as “C” for claim, “E” for evidence, “R” for rebuttal, and “S” for synthesis — to tag each paragraph or clause. This visual coding helps avoid the common error of treating illustrative detail as a primary argument. Once the map is complete, the précis can be assembled by stitching together the tagged units in their original order, preserving the logical flow while discarding redundant exemplars. A well‑constructed map also reveals hidden dependencies; for instance, a concession paragraph may appear to weaken the claim but actually strengthens it by pre‑empting objections. Recognising such rhetorical moves ensures the summary reflects the author’s strategic intent rather than a surface‑level reading.

When the passage contains multiple sub‑arguments, create a hierarchical outline: a top‑level thesis, second‑level supporting points, and third‑level data or examples. This hierarchy guides word‑budget allocation — allocate more words to the thesis and major supports, fewer to illustrative data. In practice, a 300‑word passage reduced to roughly 80 words typically retains the thesis (around 15 words), two major supports (around 30 words), and a concluding implication (around 15 words), leaving the remainder for necessary transitions. The map also aids in identifying logical gaps; if a claim lacks evidence, the précis should note the omission rather than invent support. This disciplined approach aligns with the expectation that a précis demonstrates comprehension of structure, not merely lexical compression.

Advanced aspirants should practice mapping on editorials, policy briefs, and judicial opinions, where argumentation is dense and layered. Begin with a single‑paragraph editorial, then progress to multi‑page extracts. Over time, the mapping process becomes more automatic, allowing the candidate to allocate mental bandwidth to vocabulary selection and tone preservation during the actual exam.

Finally, remember that structural mapping is not a mechanical checklist. The goal is to internalise the author’s reasoning pathway so that the précis reads as a coherent mini‑essay, not a bullet‑point list. The transition words you choose — “consequently”, “nevertheless”, “moreover” — must reflect the logical relations uncovered in the map.

Handling Ambiguity and Polysemy in Vocabulary

Vocabulary questions in comprehension papers frequently test the ability to discriminate between senses of a polysemous word within a specific context. A word such as “sanction” can mean “official permission” or “penalty for disobedience”; the surrounding syntactic frame and thematic field determine the intended sense. The first technique is contextual framing: isolate the clause containing the target word, then list all plausible dictionary senses. Eliminate senses that clash with the clause’s polarity (positive/negative), register (formal/colloquial), or collocational partners. For example, in “The council imposed a sanction on the errant contractor,” the verb “imposed” and the noun “contractor” strongly favour the punitive sense.

Second, exploit morphological clues. Prefixes such as “de‑”, “re‑”, “un‑” often signal a reversal or intensification that narrows the semantic range. “De‑escalate” cannot mean “to increase tension”. Suffixes like “‑tion”, “‑ment”, “‑ity” convert verbs into abstract nouns, shifting focus from action to result. Recognising these shifts helps avoid the trap of selecting a synonym that matches the root but not the derived form.

Third, use the “substitution test”. Replace the target word with each candidate synonym in the original sentence and read aloud. The version that preserves both grammaticality and the author’s tonal nuance is the correct choice. This test is especially valuable for near‑synonyms such as “mitigate”, “alleviate”, and “ameliorate”. While all three convey reduction of severity, “mitigate” often collocates with “risk” or “damage”, “alleviate” with “pain” or “burden”, and “ameliorate” with “condition” or “situation”. These subtle collocational preferences are often decisive in vocabulary items.

Fourth, develop a personal “polysemy log” during preparation. Record each ambiguous word encountered, its multiple senses, and the contextual cues that disambiguated it. Review this log weekly; the repeated exposure builds an intuitive sense‑selection mechanism that operates faster than conscious analysis under exam pressure.

Technique: Contextual Sense Selection
In the sentence “The diplomat’s terse reply was interpreted as a rebuff,” which of the following best captures the meaning of “terse” as used here? Options: A. Concise B. Rude C. Detailed D. Polite
Correct Answer: A. Concise. Reasoning: “Terse” primarily means “using few words”. While a terse reply can be perceived as rude, the word itself does not carry an inherent negative judgment; the negative inference comes from the context (“interpreted as a rebuff”). Option B imports an evaluative tone not present in the lexical item. Options C and D are antonyms. The substitution test confirms: “The diplomat’s concise reply was interpreted as a rebuff” retains the original meaning, whereas “rude reply” adds an unwarranted judgment.
Exam‑Day Strategy: The Two‑Pass Vocabulary Scan
During the first reading of a passage, underline every word whose sense feels uncertain. Do not pause to resolve them. On the second, targeted pass, apply the contextual framing and substitution test only to the underlined items. This two‑pass method prevents vocabulary bottlenecks from derailing overall comprehension timing.

Synthesizing Multiple Passages for Comparative Précis

Comparative précis tasks require the candidate to condense two or more texts that address a common theme from different perspectives. The first operational step is thematic alignment: create a two‑column table listing the central claim of each passage alongside its key supporting points. This visual alignment makes convergences and divergences immediately apparent. For instance, Passage A may argue that technological automation enhances productivity, citing macro‑economic data, while Passage B contends that automation erodes skilled labour markets, citing sociological case studies. The comparative précis must present both positions without conflating them, using neutral framing such as “Passage A emphasizes… whereas Passage B highlights…”.

Next, determine the hierarchical relationship between the passages. Are they opposing, complementary, or sequential? If opposing, the précis should allocate roughly equal space to each stance, preserving the balance the examiner expects. If complementary, the synthesis can merge overlapping evidence while attributing distinct insights to each source. Sequential passages — for example, a historical overview followed by a contemporary analysis — demand a chronological transition that signals the temporal shift.

Word‑budget discipline is stricter in comparative précis because the total allowance is shared. A typical 250‑word limit for two 300‑word passages means each source receives roughly 100 words, with about 50 words reserved for comparative connectors and a concluding synthesis. Prioritise the thesis and the most distinctive evidence; discard illustrative anecdotes unless they are the sole support for a unique claim.

Finally, craft a concluding sentence that captures the dialectical outcome: “Together, the passages suggest that automation’s net impact depends on policy frameworks that mediate productivity gains against labour displacement.” This synthesis demonstrates higher‑order comprehension — the ability to evaluate, not merely summarise.

Critical Evaluation of Authorial Stance and Bias

Beyond extracting information, advanced comprehension demands an assessment of the author’s stance, underlying assumptions, and potential biases. Begin by identifying the author’s epistemic position: is the text descriptive, prescriptive, analytical, or advocacy‑oriented? Descriptive texts aim for neutrality; prescriptive texts embed normative judgments. Look for modal verbs (“should”, “must”, “ought”) and evaluative adjectives (“unfortunate”, “remarkable”, “dangerous”) as markers of prescriptiveness.

Next, trace the evidence hierarchy. Does the author rely on primary data, secondary analyses, expert testimony, or anecdotal illustration? A heavy dependence on anecdotal evidence in a policy argument may signal a possible confirmation bias. Check for omitted counter‑evidence: a balanced treatment typically acknowledges at least one significant objection. The absence of such acknowledgement may indicate selective framing.

Consider the publication context. An article in a corporate‑funded think‑tank journal may reflect institutional leanings toward market‑based solutions, while a piece in a labour‑union newsletter may foreground worker protections. While the exam does not require external knowledge of the outlet, the passage itself often contains self‑identifying cues — “our research shows”, “the industry argues” — that reveal affiliation.

When writing a précis that must reflect the author’s tone, mirror the stance without endorsing it. Use attribution phrases: “The author contends that…”, “According to the passage…”. This preserves objectivity and signals to the examiner that you have distinguished the author’s voice from your own.

Time‑Management Strategies for Multi‑Passage Comprehension

Multi‑passage sections typically allocate 45–60 minutes for reading, analysis, and writing. A disciplined time budget prevents the common scenario where the first passage consumes disproportionate time, leaving insufficient minutes for synthesis. Adopt a fixed‑interval schedule: roughly 8 minutes for initial reading of all passages (skimming for thesis and structure), 12 minutes for detailed annotation and mapping, 15 minutes for drafting the comparative précis, 5 minutes for vocabulary and inference questions, and 5 minutes for final review. Adjust intervals to suit the actual paper length.

During the skimming phase, read only the first and last paragraphs of each passage, plus any headings or sub‑headings. Note the thesis statement and the concluding implication. This macro‑view enables rapid thematic alignment without deep reading. In the annotation phase, apply the structural mapping symbols introduced earlier, but limit marginal notes to one‑word tags; verbose notes eat time.

Drafting should follow a “template‑first” approach: write the comparative framework — introductory sentence, thesis‑by‑thesis summary, synthesis — before inserting passage‑specific details. This prevents the writer from getting stuck on phrasing a single paragraph while the clock runs. Use placeholder brackets for exact phrasing (“[insert Passage A’s key statistic]”) and fill them during the review window.

For vocabulary and inference items, answer them in the order they appear, but flag any that require re‑reading. Return to flagged items only after the précis draft is complete. The final review focuses on three checkpoints: word‑count compliance, logical connectors, and tonal fidelity. A quick read‑aloud (sub‑vocal) catches awkward transitions that silent reading misses.

  • Treating illustrative examples as primary arguments in the précis.
  • Ignoring the author’s modal verbs, leading to a neutral summary that misrepresents a prescriptive stance.
  • Allocating unequal space to passages in a comparative task despite explicit instruction for balanced treatment.
  • Selecting a synonym that matches the root sense but violates collocational norms of the target word.
  • Failing to map the logical structure before writing, resulting in a disjointed summary that omits key transitions.
  • Spending excessive time on the first passage and rushing the synthesis, producing an incomplete comparative analysis.
  • Over‑using passive constructions in the précis, which obscures agency and weakens the logical flow.

Rhetorical Structure Analysis for Précis

Effective précis writing begins with a systematic dissection of the source text’s rhetorical architecture. First, identify the macro‑structure: does the passage follow a problem‑solution, cause‑effect, compare‑contrast, or chronological pattern? Recognising the dominant pattern preserves logical flow instead of merely stitching isolated sentences. Then map the micro‑structure by locating the thesis, topic sentences, and concluding synthesis. In argumentative texts, the thesis is often signalled by modal verbs such as “must”, “should”, or “ought”, while expository texts may open with a definitional clause (“X refers to…”).

Discourse markers — conjunctions, adverbials, transitional phrases — scaffold the argument. Mark discourse markers (e.g., “however”, “consequently”, “furthermore”, “in contrast”, “for instance”) in the margin. These markers signal argument direction: concession to rebuttal, evidence escalation, or illustrative digression. When drafting, replace marker clusters with a single precise connector retaining the logical relationship (e.g., “although” for concession, “therefore” for inference). This condensation prevents a list-like précis.

Paragraph‑level analysis should note each block’s function: introduction, evidence, counter‑argument, rebuttal, or conclusion. In many examination passages, the counter‑argument paragraph is short but crucial; omitting it distorts the author’s stance. A useful heuristic is the “claim‑evidence‑warrant” triad derived from the Toulmin model. Identify the claim, the data offered, and the implicit warrant that links them. If the warrant is unstated, the précis must reflect that gap rather than invent a justification.

Finally, evaluate the text’s rhetorical appeals — ethos, pathos, logos. While a précis does not reproduce stylistic flourishes, awareness of the dominant appeal informs tonal fidelity. A passage heavy on statistical logos demands a neutral, data‑driven summary; one leaning on ethical authority may require attribution phrases such as “the author cites expert testimony”. By internalising this analytical routine, candidates convert passive reading into active structural reconstruction, the core skill examiners reward.

Logical Fallacy Detection in Source Texts

Advanced comprehension questions test the ability to spot flawed reasoning in the passage. The most common fallacies in competitive‑exam texts are the straw‑man, false dichotomy, hasty generalisation, appeal to authority, and post‑hoc ergo propter hoc. A straw‑man occurs when the author misrepresents an opposing view to make it easier to refute; the précis must note the misrepresentation without endorsing it. A false dichotomy presents only two alternatives while ignoring a spectrum of options — look for language such as “either…or” without qualifying “or a combination of both”.

Hasty generalisation appears when a broad conclusion is drawn from an insufficient sample. In passages citing a single case study to support a policy recommendation, flag the leap. Appeal to authority is signalled by phrases like “renowned economist X argues” without accompanying data; the précis should attribute the claim (“according to X”) rather than present it as fact. Post‑hoc reasoning assumes causality from temporal succession; words like “after”, “since”, or “following” used as causal links are red flags.

To detect these fallacies systematically, apply a three‑step check: (1) isolate the conclusion, (2) trace the premises offered, (3) test the logical bridge. Ask whether the premises, if true, necessarily support the conclusion. If the bridge relies on an unstated assumption, mark it as a potential fallacy. In comparative précis tasks, contrasting how two authors handle the same issue — one using sound inductive reasoning, the other committing a false cause — demonstrates higher‑order analysis.

Examiners often embed a fallacy in a seemingly innocuous paragraph and then ask an inference question such as “Which of the following best describes the flaw in the author’s argument?” Recognising the fallacy type allows a precise answer: “The argument commits a hasty generalisation by extrapolating national trends from a single regional survey.” Practising with annotated editorials from reputable journals sharpens this skill without requiring external knowledge.

Fallacy Identification Worked Example
Passage excerpt: “Since the new traffic regulation was introduced, road accidents have decreased by 12 %. Therefore, the regulation is the sole cause of the improvement.” Question: Identify the logical flaw and rewrite the sentence to reflect a cautious inference.
The passage commits a post‑hoc ergo propter hoc fallacy, assuming that temporal precedence proves causation. A cautious rewrite: “Road accidents fell by 12 % after the regulation was introduced; the regulation may have contributed to the decline, though other factors cannot be excluded.”
Exam‑Day Strategy for Fallacy Spotting
During the first skim, underline any causal language (“because”, “since”, “led to”) and any absolute quantifiers (“all”, “never”, “sole”). In the detailed read, test each causal claim against the evidence provided. If the passage offers only correlation, note the gap. When answering inference questions, choose the option that names the specific fallacy rather than a vague “weak argument”.

Comparative Tone Calibration Across Passages

Multi‑passage précis tasks require the writer to harmonise divergent authorial voices while preserving each stance. Begin by classifying each passage’s tone on a continuum: objective‑analytical, persuasive‑advocacy, reflective‑speculative, or critical‑skeptical. Objective texts favour passive constructions, hedging (“suggests”, “indicates”), and balanced evidence. Advocacy texts employ active verbs, strong modals (“must”, “should”), and evaluative adjectives (“crucial”, “unacceptable”). Reflective pieces often use first‑person pronouns and modal qualifiers (“perhaps”, “might”). Skeptical tones feature rhetorical questions, concessive clauses (“although”), and explicit doubt markers (“it remains unclear”).

Once tones are labelled, decide on a unifying reporting style. The safest approach is a neutral, third‑person summary that attributes every claim: “Passage A argues that…”, “Passage B contends…”. Avoid blending tones — e.g., do not convert an advocacy passage’s “must” into a neutral “suggests”. If the instruction asks for a “balanced synthesis”, allocate roughly equal word‑count to each passage’s core thesis, then add a synthesis sentence that juxtaposes the positions without favouring one.

Watch for tonal shifts within a single passage. An author may start analytically and end persuasively; the précis must capture the shift by signalling it: “Initially presenting data, the author concludes with a policy recommendation.” Use discourse markers such as “subsequently”, “ultimately”, or “in closing” to flag the transition. In comparative tasks, align parallel structures: if Passage A’s thesis is summarised in a subordinate clause, render Passage B’s thesis in the same syntactic slot.

Examiners penalise tonal distortion — turning a cautious claim into a definitive statement or vice‑versa. Practice by rewriting editorials from different publications (e.g., a government white paper vs. an NGO report) into a single 150‑word comparative précis, then checking each attribution against the original tone.

Evidence Grading and Citation Mapping

High‑scoring answers demonstrate the ability to rank evidential weight of cited sources. Adopt a three‑tier hierarchy: Tier 1 — primary data (original surveys, experiments, official statistics); Tier 2 — secondary analyses (meta‑studies, literature reviews, expert commentaries); Tier 3 — anecdotal or illustrative material (case vignettes, personal narratives, media reports). When the passage cites “a 2022 World Bank report”, classify it as Tier 1 if the report presents raw indicators, Tier 2 if it offers interpretive commentary.

Map each claim to its supporting tier. In the margin, note “T1”, “T2”, or “T3” beside the relevant sentence. This visual map reveals whether the argument rests on robust data or leans on illustrative anecdotes. For précis writing, prioritize Tier 1 and Tier 2 evidence; Tier 3 material can be omitted unless the question explicitly asks for examples. In inference questions, a claim backed only by Tier 3 evidence is a prime candidate for “cannot be inferred” or “overstated”.

Citation mapping also aids detection of selective framing. If a passage cites three Tier 1 studies supporting one view but omits two comparable Tier 1 studies contradicting it, the author may be cherry‑picking. The précis should reflect this imbalance by phrasing: “The author cites three studies indicating X, while not addressing contrary findings.” Such nuance signals critical evaluation rather than passive summarisation.

During timed practice, allocate two minutes to evidence grading after the initial read. Create a quick table: Claim | Tier | Source. This habit converts a dense passage into a structured evidence matrix, enabling rapid retrieval for both précis and objective questions.

  • Confusing rhetorical structure with mere paragraph order, leading to a précis that follows the surface layout but misses the logical hierarchy.
  • Labeling every causal phrase as a fallacy without checking whether the passage supplies a mechanistic explanation.
  • Merging distinct authorial tones into a single neutral voice, thereby erasing the contrast the examiner expects in a comparative précis.
  • Treating anecdotal illustrations as primary evidence and allocating them equal weight in the summary.
  • Omitting the counter‑argument paragraph because it appears short, resulting in a one‑sided précis that misrepresents the author’s stance.
  • Using vague attribution (“the passage says”) instead of precise tier‑based citation (“the passage cites a 2022 World Bank dataset”).
  • Failing to adjust connector density when condensing, producing a précis that reads as a list of disjointed statements rather than a coherent argument.

Discourse Marker Mapping for Cohesive Précis

Discourse markers — conjunctions, adverbials, and lexical bundles such as “however”, “consequently”, “in addition”, “for instance” — signal the logical architecture of a source text. A précis that preserves the argument’s flow must replicate the hierarchical relations these markers encode rather than merely copying sentence order. Begin by segmenting the passage into macro‑units: thesis, supporting evidence, concession, rebuttal, and conclusion. Tag each unit with the dominant marker(s) that introduce it; for example, a paragraph opened by “nevertheless” typically marks a concession‑rebuttal pair. When condensing, replace extended marker clusters with a single, high‑information connector that retains the same relational force: “although” can subsume “even though”, “despite the fact that”, and “while”. Avoid the trap of over‑compression, which deletes the contrastive cue and yields a flat list of statements. In comparative précis tasks, align marker families across passages so that parallel structures (e.g., “first… second… finally”) occupy identical syntactic slots, enabling the examiner to see the structural correspondence at a glance.

Advanced practitioners build a personal marker taxonomy: additive (furthermore, moreover), adversative (however, on the contrary), causal (therefore, hence), sequential (subsequently, ultimately), and illustrative (for example, namely). During the first read, annotate the margin with the taxonomy code (A, AD, C, S, I). This visual map reveals whether the author’s argument is predominantly linear, dialectical, or evidence‑driven. When the passage shifts marker density — e.g., a sudden surge of adversative markers — it often signals a pivotal counter‑argument that must be retained even under severe word limits. Conversely, stretches heavy with additive markers may be summarised by a single “the author enumerates three reasons” construction. Mastery of marker mapping reduces the cognitive load of re‑reading and prevents the common error of re‑ordering ideas in a way that inverts the original logical dependency.

Exceptions arise in highly technical or legal texts where markers are sparse and logical relations are encoded through syntactic embedding (relative clauses, nominalisations). In such cases, supplement marker analysis with clause‑level dependency parsing: identify the main verb of each sentence and trace its arguments. The précis writer then reconstructs the logical skeleton by converting embedded clauses into explicit connectors, ensuring the condensed version remains readable without the original’s dense syntax. Practice by selecting editorials from contrasting publications — one with overt markers, one with implicit cohesion — and producing side‑by‑side marker maps before drafting the précis.

Quantitative Data Extraction and Representation in Summaries

Passages in economics, public policy, and science frequently embed numerical evidence — percentages, growth rates, confidence intervals, and index values. A high‑scoring précis does not merely copy figures; it evaluates their evidential tier, selects the most decisive data points, and presents them in a compact, comparative format. Start by isolating every quantitative claim and tagging its source tier: Tier 1 (primary dataset, official statistics), Tier 2 (secondary analysis, model output), Tier 3 (illustrative anecdote). Discard Tier 3 figures unless the question explicitly requests examples. For Tier 1 and Tier 2 data, note the metric, baseline, direction, and magnitude (e.g., “GDP growth accelerated from 4.2 % to 6.8 % YoY”). When multiple figures support the same claim, retain only the most recent or the most authoritative source, and indicate the range if the passage presents a spread (e.g., “estimates range between 5.1 % and 6.3 %”).

Representation choices affect word economy. A single comparative clause — “growth rose from 4.2 % to 6.8 %” — replaces two separate sentences. When the passage contains a time series, compress it into a trend descriptor plus the terminal values: “a steady upward trajectory culminating in 6.8 %”. Avoid reproducing tables verbatim; instead, encode the salient comparison in a parenthetical: “(up 2.6 pp)”. If the passage juxtaposes two datasets (e.g., urban vs. rural literacy), use a parallel structure: “urban literacy 87 %, rural 62 %”. This parallelism mirrors the source’s comparative intent while halving the word count. Be vigilant for unit inconsistencies — mixing absolute numbers with percentages — and normalise them in the précis to a single metric, noting the conversion if it is non‑trivial.

Exceptions occur when the passage’s argument hinges on methodological nuance: confidence intervals, p‑values, or model specifications. In such cases, the précis must preserve the qualifier (“the effect is significant at p < 0.05”) because omitting it changes the epistemic status of the claim. Similarly, when the author explicitly critiques a dataset (“the 2021 survey under‑samples marginalised groups”), the précis must retain the critique rather than the raw figure. Practice by extracting data from government economic surveys and rewriting the executive summary in 120 words, then checking each retained figure against the tier‑tagging rubric.

Technique: Tier‑Based Data Condensation
A passage states: “According to the 2022 National Health Survey (Tier 1), infant mortality fell from 38 per 1,000 live births in 2015 to 28 in 2021. A 2023 academic review (Tier 2) attributes the decline to expanded immunisation coverage, though it notes the survey under‑reports remote districts. An NGO field report (Tier 3) cites a village where mortality dropped to 15 after a local clinic opened.” Write a 60‑word précis segment that reflects the evidential hierarchy.
The 2022 National Health Survey records a decline in infant mortality from 38 to 28 per 1,000 live births (2015‑2021). A 2023 review links the drop to broader immunisation but flags under‑reporting in remote areas. The NGO anecdote is illustrative and omitted from the core claim.
Exam‑Day Strategy
During the first two minutes, sketch a three‑column table: Claim | Tier | Key Figure. This visual matrix lets you instantly decide which numbers survive the word‑count cut and which qualifiers must travel with them.

Cross‑Cultural Reference Resolution in Multilingual Passages

Compulsory language papers increasingly present excerpts that embed foreign‑language terms, cultural allusions, or code‑switched quotations. The précis writer must decide whether to translate, transliterate, or retain the original token, guided by the target audience’s expected familiarity and the examiner’s rubric for lexical fidelity. A systematic approach: first, catalogue every non‑English token; second, classify each as (a) a proper noun with an established English rendering (e.g., “Bhagavad Gita”), (b) a concept lacking a direct equivalent (e.g., “ubuntu”, “saudade”), or (c) a rhetorical flourish where the original language carries affective weight (e.g., a Hindi couplet). For class (a), use the standard Anglicised form; for (b), provide a concise gloss in parentheses on first occurrence (“ubuntu (communal humanity)”) and thereafter use the gloss; for (c), retain the original script or transliteration and append a brief functional note (“the couplet invokes divine longing”).

When the passage juxtaposes multiple cultural frameworks — say, a comparative essay on Confucian “ren” and Kantian “duty” — the précis must preserve the comparative structure without collapsing the distinct semantic fields. Map each concept to its philosophical lineage and note any translational controversies (e.g., “ren” rendered as “benevolence” vs. “humaneness”). The condensed version should signal the equivalence debate: “the author contrasts Confucian ren (often translated as benevolence) with Kantian duty”. This alerts the examiner that you recognise the nuance rather than imposing a monolithic translation. Avoid the pitfall of over‑glossing: inserting a full definition for every term inflates word count and disrupts flow. Instead, rely on the examiner’s familiarity with high‑frequency cultural references in the syllabus (e.g., “dharma”, “karma”, “tao”).

Exceptions arise in legal or diplomatic texts where the original wording is legally binding (e.g., treaty articles in French). In such cases, the précis must quote the exact phrase in the source language and indicate its official English translation, because any paraphrase could alter the obligation. Practice by selecting multilingual editorials from international journals, building a reference‑resolution table, and producing a 150‑word comparative précis that respects the classification scheme.

Strategic Paraphrasing Under Word‑Count Constraints

Paraphrasing is not synonymous with synonym substitution; it is a structural re‑encoding that preserves propositional content while altering syntactic form. Under strict limits (e.g., 120 words for a 600‑word passage), the writer must prioritize information density: each retained clause should carry a distinct logical function (claim, evidence, concession, implication). Begin by drafting a full‑length “free” summary, then apply a three‑stage compression pipeline. Stage 1 — clause merging: combine co‑referential clauses using relative pronouns or participial phrases (“The policy, which was enacted in 2019, reduced emissions” → “The 2019 policy reduced emissions”). Stage 2 — nominalisation: convert verb‑heavy constructions into noun phrases (“The committee recommended that the budget be increased” → “The committee’s budget‑increase recommendation”). Stage 3 — ellipsis of recoverable context: drop repeated subjects, temporal anchors already established, and generic hedges (“It is important to note that” → omit). After each stage, recount words; stop when the target is met, then verify that no logical relation has been lost.

Quantify the compression ratio for each paragraph: original words ÷ précis words. Paragraphs with ratios above 5:1 are candidates for aggressive nominalisation; those near 2:1 may only need connector tightening. Preserve the author’s modality markers (“may”, “must”, “likely”) because they encode epistemic stance; replacing “must” with “should” changes the force of a recommendation. When the passage contains a numbered list of arguments, retain the enumeration (“first… second… third”) but compress each item to a single noun phrase (“first, the fiscal deficit; second, the current‑account gap”). This preserves the rhetorical architecture while maximising brevity.

Exceptions: literary or philosophical passages where stylistic texture constitutes part of the meaning (e.g., a metaphor‑rich essay). Here, over‑compression erases the aesthetic dimension the examiner may test. In such cases, allocate a higher word budget to the metaphor cluster and compress surrounding exposition instead. Practice by timing the three‑stage pipeline on past paper passages, recording the word count after each stage, and noting where logical fidelity breaks down.

Evaluating Implicit Assumptions and Presuppositions

Every argument rests on unstated premises — assumptions about causality, value hierarchies, or definitional boundaries. A précis that surfaces these hidden layers demonstrates critical reading beyond surface summarisation. Use a two‑column audit: left column lists each explicit claim; right column asks “What must be true for this claim to hold?” For instance, the claim “Increasing the minimum wage reduces poverty” presupposes (i) employers will not offset wage hikes by cutting hours, (ii) the poverty line is income‑based, and (iii) the affected workers are primary earners. Tag each presupposition as factual (empirically testable), normative (value‑laden), or definitional (concept‑bound). In the précis, embed the most consequential presuppositions as qualifying clauses: “assuming employment elasticity remains low, the author argues that raising the minimum wage curtails poverty”. This signals to the examiner that you have mapped the argument’s logical dependencies.

When multiple passages are compared, align their presupposition profiles. A comparative précis can then juxtapose not only the theses but also the divergent background assumptions: “Passage A assumes market‑clearing wages; Passage B assumes institutional rigidities prevent adjustment”. Such alignment often reveals the root of the disagreement, which is precisely what high‑mark inference questions target. Be careful not to import your own assumptions; only those recoverable from textual cues (modal verbs, conditional constructions, scope particles) are legitimate. If a presupposition is ambiguous, note the ambiguity rather than resolving it arbitrarily.

Exceptions occur in highly technical texts where domain‑specific axioms (e.g., “rational agents maximise utility”) are treated as shared knowledge. In those contexts, the précis may omit the axiom if the intended audience (the examiner) is presumed to share the disciplinary background. However, if the passage explicitly challenges the axiom, the challenge must be retained. Practice by selecting policy briefs, performing the presupposition audit, and rewriting the executive summary with embedded assumption tags, then checking against the original for any lost logical nuance.

  • Treating discourse markers as decorative rather than structural, leading to a précis that misrepresents logical hierarchy.
  • Copying raw numerical tables instead of condensing them into comparative clauses, wasting word budget and obscuring trends.
  • Over‑glossing cultural terms or omitting necessary qualifiers, which either inflates the summary or erases semantic nuance.
  • Applying uniform synonym substitution without structural re‑encoding, producing a paraphrase that retains the original’s verbosity.
  • Ignoring implicit presuppositions, resulting in a summary that presents contested claims as uncontested facts.
  • Failing to preserve modality markers during compression, thereby altering the author’s epistemic stance.
  • Assuming domain axioms are universal and omitting them when the passage explicitly interrogates them.

Argument Reconstruction Through Propositional Logic

Precis writing at the advanced level demands the ability to strip a passage down to its logical skeleton. Propositional logic provides a formal vocabulary — conjunction, disjunction, implication, negation — that makes the hierarchical relations between claims explicit. Begin by segmenting the text into atomic propositions: each declarative sentence that can be assigned a truth value becomes a node. Next, identify the connectives that bind them: words such as “therefore”, “since”, “unless”, “either…or” map onto logical operators. Represent the structure in a directed graph where arrows indicate inferential flow from premises to conclusion. This graph reveals hidden dependencies; for instance, a passage may present a chain “If inflation rises, the central bank tightens policy; inflation is rising; therefore the bank will tighten”. The graph shows a modus ponens pattern, allowing the précis to compress the three sentences into a single conditional clause: “Given rising inflation, the central bank will tighten policy”. When multiple independent arguments coexist, the graph separates them into distinct sub‑graphs, preventing the précis from conflating unrelated lines of reasoning. Practice by converting editorials into propositional trees, then rewriting the executive summary using only the logical connectors that survive the compression. This method helps ensure that no inferential step is lost while word count shrinks dramatically.

Metaphor Preservation and Semantic Density Management

Literary and philosophical passages often embed meaning in metaphor clusters that resist nominalisation. A metaphor such as “the state is a ship navigating a storm of dissent” carries both a structural analogy (state ship, dissent storm) and an evaluative stance (navigation implies agency, storm implies danger). Over‑compression that replaces the metaphor with “the state manages dissent” erases the author’s rhetorical texture, which examiners may test through inference questions about tone or implied attitude. The solution is a two‑tier budget: allocate a higher word quota to the metaphor cluster and compress surrounding exposition aggressively. First, flag every figurative expression using a semantic‑density heuristic — note clauses where multiple source‑domain mappings cluster. Dense clusters receive a “preserve” tag. In the précis, render the metaphor verbatim or with minimal lexical substitution that retains the source‑target mapping (“the state, likened to a ship, steers through a storm of dissent”). All non‑metaphorical sentences undergo standard reduction: nominalise verbs, delete hedges, merge coordinate clauses. A final word‑count audit checks that preserved metaphor words form a meaningful share of the précis; a rough benchmark of one preserved metaphor segment per five total words suggests balanced density. This disciplined approach safeguards aesthetic meaning while meeting strict length limits.

Technique: Metaphor‑Aware Précis Construction
Original passage (120 words): “The economy resembles a garden tended by policymakers. Weeds of corruption choke the seedlings of investment. Without diligent weeding, the harvest of growth will wither. Recent reforms act as fertilizer, yet the soil of public trust remains depleted. Only sustained transparency can restore fertility.” Write a précis of no more than 45 words that retains the central metaphor and its evaluative force.
Précis (42 words): “The economy, figured as a garden, suffers corruption’s weeds choking investment seedlings; reforms serve as fertilizer, but trust‑depleted soil demands sustained transparency to restore fertility.” The metaphor (garden, weeds, seedlings, fertilizer, soil, fertility) is preserved verbatim; explanatory clauses are nominalised and merged.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a passage is metaphor‑rich, spend the first thirty seconds marking every figurative phrase. Reserve roughly one‑third of your word budget for those marked segments; compress the rest using standard nominalisation. This prevents the common error of flattening the author’s imagery while still meeting the length constraint.

Statistical Summarization with Uncertainty Qualification

Passages that embed quantitative evidence — survey percentages, growth rates, confidence intervals — require a précis that conveys both magnitude and epistemic status. Raw numbers copied verbatim waste space and obscure trends; instead, translate data into comparative clauses qualified by the original uncertainty markers. For example, “GDP grew 6.2 % (±0.3 %) in Q2” becomes “GDP grew roughly 6 % in Q2, with a narrow margin of error”. Preserve modal adjectives such as “estimated”, “projected”, “preliminary” because they signal the author’s confidence level. When multiple statistics appear, group them by theme (fiscal, monetary, social) and express the group as a single trend statement: “Fiscal indicators — deficit, debt‑to‑GDP, revenue growth — all improved modestly”. If the source presents a table, extract the directional movement (up, down, stable) and the order of magnitude, then discard the exact figures unless the question explicitly asks for them. Always retain the original hedging language (“likely”, “suggests”, “may indicate”) because replacing “may indicate” with “shows” upgrades the epistemic force and risks misrepresenting the author’s stance. Practice by converting statistical abstracts into three‑sentence summaries, then checking that every retained numeral is accompanied by its qualifier.

Multi‑Source Synthesis for Comparative Analysis

Comparative précis tasks present two or more passages that address a common theme from divergent perspectives. The goal is not a side‑by‑side list but a synthesized argument map that aligns claims, evidence, and presuppositions across sources. Start by building a claim‑matrix: rows are thematic sub‑topics (e.g., “cause of inequality”, “policy remedy”), columns are sources. Populate each cell with the source’s core proposition, stripped to a propositional form. Next, identify convergence (identical or compatible propositions) and divergence (contradictory or incommensurable propositions). Convergent cells can be merged into a single synthesized statement attributed to “both passages”. Divergent cells require a contrastive clause: “Passage A attributes inequality to skill‑biased technical change, whereas Passage B emphasizes institutional capture”. Preserve each source’s modality — if Passage A uses “must” and Passage B uses “could”, the synthesis must reflect that difference. Finally, order the synthesized statements to follow a logical narrative: definition, diagnosis, prescription, implication. This structure aligns with typical expectations for high‑mark comparative answers. Regular drills with paired editorials from opposing ideological outlets sharpen the ability to produce a coherent, balanced précis under time pressure.

Adaptive Précis Strategies for Varied Prompt Types

Examination prompts differ: some ask for a “summary”, others for a “critical précis”, “executive summary”, or “answer in 50 words”. Each prompt type imposes distinct constraints on content selection and rhetorical stance. A plain summary demands neutral condensation; a critical précis requires the writer to surface the author’s assumptions, evaluate evidence quality, and flag logical gaps — all within the same word limit. An executive summary prioritises actionable recommendations and omits background context. A strict word‑count prompt (e.g., “in 50 words”) forces aggressive nominalisation and the deletion of all but the thesis and its primary support. The adaptive workflow: first, read the prompt and tag the required output genre. Second, run the three‑stage pipeline (segment → logical map → draft) but adjust the compression ratio at stage three according to the genre tag. For critical précis, allocate extra words to assumption tags and evidence grades; for executive summary, allocate extra words to recommendation clauses. Third, perform a genre‑specific audit: verify that modal markers match the prompt’s epistemic stance, that no personal opinion intrudes in a neutral summary, and that the word count falls within the prescribed band. Mastery of this adaptive loop reduces the risk of mismatch where a candidate produces a perfect neutral summary for a critical précis prompt and loses marks for missing evaluation.

  • Treating all prompt types as identical, leading to a neutral summary when a critical précis is required.
  • Discarding uncertainty qualifiers from statistical data, thereby overstating the author’s confidence.
  • Flattening metaphor clusters into literal paraphrase, erasing the rhetorical texture examiners test.
  • Merging divergent claims from multiple sources without preserving each source’s modality.
  • Applying a uniform compression ratio across passages of differing genre, causing over‑compression of literary texts and under‑compression of data‑heavy texts.
  • Neglecting to map presuppositions in comparative précis, so the root disagreement remains hidden.
  • Failing to adjust word‑budget allocation for metaphor‑dense segments, resulting in a précis that either exceeds the limit or loses the central imagery.

Précis Construction for Scientific Abstracts: Preserving Methodological Nuance

Scientific abstracts follow the IMRaD convention — Introduction, Methods, Results, and Discussion — each carrying distinct epistemic weight. A précis collapsing Methods into a single phrase loses reproducibility information: sample size, randomisation protocol, blinding status, and statistical tests must survive compression. When the source reports a p‑value, retain the exact threshold (e.g., p < 0.01) and confidence interval; replacing “p < 0.01” with “significant” upgrades the claim and misrepresents the author’s caution. Effect sizes (Cohen’s d, odds ratios) and their uncertainty bounds are non‑negotiable — they convey practical significance independent of sample size. Results often contain multiple endpoints; group them by domain (primary, secondary, exploratory) and preserve the authors’ hierarchy. In the Discussion, distinguish conclusions directly supported by data from speculative extensions — mark the latter with original hedging (“suggests”, “may indicate”). A disciplined précis mirrors the abstract’s logic: research question, design in one dense clause, key quantitative findings with qualifiers, calibrated inference. Practise by converting ten structured abstracts into 80‑word précis, verifying every numeral retains its qualifier and no methodological detail essential for replication is omitted.

Condensing Legislative and Regulatory Texts: Retaining Scope, Exceptions, and Definitions

Legislative drafting relies on a hierarchy of definitional, operative, and procedural provisions. A précis omitting the definitions clause (often Section 2) collapses the instrument’s semantic boundary; terms such as “person”, “enterprise”, or “public authority” acquire statutory meanings distinct from ordinary usage. Operative sections — granting powers, imposing duties, creating offences — must retain their modal verbs: “shall” denotes mandatory duty, “may” discretionary power, “must” a condition precedent. Exceptions and savings clauses (e.g., “Nothing in this Act shall affect…”) are frequent examiner traps; they carve out substantive carve‑outs altering the main rule’s scope. Schedules, often appended, contain binding tables, forms, or transitional provisions; a précis must signal their existence even if content is omitted. Commencement provisions determine temporal applicability — immediate, appointed day, or phased — affecting validity of actions taken before the appointed date. Delegated legislation references (rules, regulations, orders) extend the primary text; note the enabling section and degree of parliamentary scrutiny (negative/affirmative resolution). When condensing, preserve the logical sequence: definitions → scope → substantive provisions → exceptions → procedural mechanics → commencement. Use nominalised phrases (“the imposition of a duty on…”) to compress without losing deontic force. A 120‑word précis of a 30‑page Act should still let a reader reconstruct the regulatory architecture: who is regulated, what conduct is prohibited or required, what defences exist, and when the law takes effect.

Worked Example: Legislative Précis
Section 2 defines “manufacturer” as any person engaged in the production of goods for sale. Section 5(1) provides that a manufacturer shall obtain a licence before commencing production. Section 5(2) states the licensing authority may grant a licence subject to conditions. Section 6 creates an offence for production without a licence, punishable with fine up to ₹1 lakh. Section 7 exempts manufacturers employing fewer than ten workers. Section 8 empowers the Central Government to make rules for licence renewal. The Act comes into force on a date notified by the Central Government.
The Act defines “manufacturer” (s. 2) and mandates licensing before production (s. 5(1)); the authority may impose conditions (s. 5(2)). Unlicensed production is an offence punishable by a fine up to ₹1 lakh (s. 6), but manufacturers with fewer than ten workers are exempt (s. 7). Rule‑making power for renewal rests with the Central Government (s. 8). Commencement is by notification.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a legislative passage appears, first underline every definition, modal verb, exception, and commencement phrase. Allocate one sentence of your précis to each of the five structural layers (definition, scope, duty/power, exception, procedure). This checklist guarantees no legally operative element is dropped under word‑count pressure.

Summarizing Historical Narratives: Balancing Chronology, Causality, and Historiographical Debate

Historical passages intertwine chronological sequence, causal argument, and the historian’s interpretive stance. A précis merely listing events forfeits the analytical core examiners reward. Isolate the central thesis — e.g., “the 1857 revolt accelerated colonial administrative centralisation” — then map the evidence hierarchy: primary sources (official reports, letters), secondary syntheses, and the historian’s evaluative language. Causal claims must retain modal qualification: “contributed to”, “precipitated”, “was a necessary condition for” are not interchangeable. When the text juxtaposes competing schools (Marxist, Subaltern, Revisionist), signal the debate without adjudicating: “While Marxist scholars emphasise economic exploitation, Subaltern studies foreground peasant agency.” Dates serve as anchors; keep only turning points (e.g., 1857, 1919, 1947) and discard routine markers. Quantitative data — troop numbers, revenue figures — should be preserved with source attribution (“British parliamentary returns, 1858”). Preserve the historian’s concluding assessment (“the revolt’s legacy remains contested”) as it frames historiographical significance. A 100‑word précis of a 2,000‑word essay thus contains: thesis, two or three causally linked events with modal verbs, one historiographical contrast, and the author’s final judgement.

Handling Implicit Argumentation in Philosophical and Theoretical Texts

Philosophical prose often advances arguments through enthymemes — premises left unstated because assumed shared — and thought experiments functioning as counterfactual conditionals. A précis must surface the hidden premise without inserting the summariser’s assumptions. Identify the conclusion first (often signalled by “therefore”, “hence”, “it follows that”), then reconstruct the minimal premise set validating the inference. Preserve modal operators: “necessarily”, “possibly”, “contingently” indicate the logical strength the author ascribes. When a thought experiment appears (e.g., “imagine a brain in a vat”), treat it as a premise‑generating device; note its role (“the brain‑in‑a‑vat scenario illustrates epistemic scepticism”) rather than narrate it. Technical terminology — “supervenience”, “qualia”, “de re/de dicto” — must be retained because paraphrase alters conceptual content. If the text references a canonical debate (e.g., realism vs. anti‑realism about universals), name the positions and the author’s alignment. Distinguish the author’s positive thesis from anticipated objections, often introduced by “one might object that…”. A faithful précis of a 1,500‑word article in 80 words therefore contains: the central claim, the reconstructed enthymematic premise, the key thought experiment’s logical function, the technical term cluster, and the author’s stance within the named debate.

Précis of Dialogic Transcripts: Preserving Speaker Voice, Turn‑Taking, and Pragmatic Force

Interview, debate, or parliamentary transcripts follow conversation‑analysis principles: adjacency pairs (question‑answer), preference organisation (agreement vs. disagreement), and speech‑act sequencing (assertion, challenge, concession). A précis flattening dialogue into monologue loses interactional evidence examiners test. Segment by speaker turns, then code each for illocutionary force — e.g., “Minister: asserts policy success (assertive)”; “Opposition MP: challenges data source (directive)”. Preserve hedging (“I think”, “it appears”) and boosting (“clearly”, “undoubtedly”) as they index epistemic stance. When a speaker concedes (“I accept that point”), note the concession as a structural pivot. Politeness strategies — positive face (“we all want progress”) and negative face (“if I may suggest”) — reveal power dynamics; retain at least one exemplar of each. Quoted material must stay attributed; indirect reporting (“the Minister claimed…”) is acceptable only when original wording is not essential to pragmatic force. Time‑stamps or turn numbers are unnecessary, but the sequence of moves (challenge → evidence → rebuttal → concession) must be preserved. A 90‑word précis of a 10‑minute exchange thus maps the interactional architecture: opening claim, primary challenge, evidential response, secondary challenge, concession, and closing summary, each tagged with speaker role and speech‑act type.

  • Dropping modal verbs (shall, may, must) in legislative précis, turning obligations into options.
  • Replacing exact statistical qualifiers (p < 0.01, 95 % CI) with vague “significant” language.
  • Listing historical events chronologically without preserving the author’s causal modality.
  • Omitting the hidden premise of an enthymeme, making the philosophical argument appear invalid.
  • Converting dialogic turns into a single narrative voice, erasing speech‑act distinctions.
  • Discarding definitional clauses in regulatory texts, causing scope ambiguity.
  • Neglecting hedging or boosting markers in transcripts, flattening epistemic stance.
These advanced sections equip you to handle the full spectrum of source genres that appear in compulsory language papers.

Précis of Legal Judgments: Preserving Ratio Decidendi and Obiter Dicta

Legal judgments are structured around a binding core — the ratio decidendi — and a persuasive periphery known as obiter dicta. A précis that collapses the two loses the doctrinal hierarchy examiners test. Begin by locating the court’s final order (the dispositive paragraph) and then trace backward to the legal principle that necessarily supports it. That principle, stripped of case‑specific facts, is the ratio. Any statement of law not essential to the outcome, even if articulated by the same judge, is obiter and must be flagged as such (e.g., “the Court observed, obiter, that…”). Preserve the citation format used in the source (e.g., Kesavananda Bharati v. State of Kerala, (1973) 4 SCC 225) because the authority of the ratio depends on the court level and bench strength. When a judgment contains multiple ratios (alternative grounds), list each with its logical scope: “Ratio 1: Parliament cannot amend the basic structure (Art. 368). Ratio 2: Judicial review is part of the basic structure.” Distinguish concurring and dissenting opinions; a concurring ratio may narrow the majority’s principle, while a dissenting obiter may later become persuasive. Retain procedural posture (appeal, review, writ) because it determines the standard of review (e.g., “the High Court exercised its supervisory jurisdiction under Art. 227”). Quantify the bench (e.g., “7‑judge Constitution Bench”) — this signals precedential weight. Finally, compress the factual matrix to the minimum needed to illustrate the legal test (e.g., “the amendment sought to curtail judicial review of constitutional amendments”). A 120‑word précis of a 3,000‑word judgment therefore contains: court and bench, procedural posture, the dispositive order, each ratio with its doctrinal label, any pivotal obiter marked, and the factual illustration.

Summarizing Scientific Data Visualizations: Tables, Graphs, and Statistical Significance

Scientific passages often embed tables, bar charts, scatter plots, or forest plots that carry the evidential weight of the argument. A précis must translate visual density into prose without losing statistical nuance. First, identify the primary outcome variable and the effect measure (risk ratio, odds ratio, mean difference, hazard ratio). Note the confidence interval (CI) and the p‑value or Bayesian credible interval; these indicate precision and statistical significance. When a forest plot shows heterogeneity (I² statistic), report it (e.g., “I² = 68 %, substantial heterogeneity”). For dose‑response graphs, preserve the trend description (linear, plateau, U‑shaped) and the quantitative turning point if given (e.g., “maximum benefit at 150 mg/day”). Tables of baseline characteristics should be condensed to the variables that differ significantly between groups (p < 0.05) and those that are clinically relevant regardless of p‑value. If the source uses intention‑to‑treat versus per‑protocol analyses, note which is primary. Preserve the sample size (n) and power calculation when reported, because they frame the reliability of non‑significant findings. Avoid converting a statistically non‑significant result into “no effect”; instead write “the difference was not statistically significant (p = 0.12, 95 % CI −0.3 to 1.2)”. When multiple figures illustrate the same dataset (e.g., a Kaplan‑Meier curve and a hazard‑ratio table), synthesize them: “median survival 14 months (95 % CI 12–16) versus 9 months (95 % CI 7–11); HR = 0.68 (95 % CI 0.52–0.89)”. A 100‑word précis of a 2,500‑word results section therefore contains: study design, primary outcome, effect size with CI, significance marker, heterogeneity or subgroup note, and the author’s interpretive caveat.

Worked Example: Condensing a Meta‑Analysis Forest Plot
A meta‑analysis of ten randomized trials comparing Drug X with placebo for hypertension reports a pooled mean difference of −5.2 mmHg (95 % CI −7.1 to −3.3), p < 0.001, I² = 45 %. Subgroup analysis shows −8.0 mmHg (95 % CI −10.5 to −5.5) in trials using dose ≥ 20 mg and −2.1 mmHg (95 % CI −4.0 to −0.2) in lower‑dose trials. Write a 60‑word précis of this paragraph.
The meta‑analysis of ten RCTs found Drug X reduced systolic pressure by 5.2 mmHg (95 % CI −7.1 to −3.3, p < 0.001) with moderate heterogeneity (I² = 45 %). High‑dose (≥ 20 mg) trials showed a larger effect (−8.0 mmHg, 95 % CI −10.5 to −5.5) than low‑dose trials (−2.1 mmHg, 95 % CI −4.0 to −0.2).
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a passage contains a figure, skim the caption first; it often states the main finding. Then read the surrounding text for qualifiers (e.g., “despite statistical significance, clinical relevance is uncertain”). In your précis, lead with the quantitative result, follow with its uncertainty, and close with the author’s interpretive stance. This order mirrors the logical flow examiners reward.

Handling Metaphorical and Figurative Language in Literary Passages

Literary texts compress meaning through metaphor, simile, personification, metonymy, and symbolism. A précis that paraphrases figurative language into literal prose either loses the author’s semantic density or introduces distortion. The first step is to classify the figure: a metaphor (“the city is a beating heart”) asserts identity; a simile (“the city beats like a heart”) signals comparison; personification (“the city breathes”) attributes agency. Preserve the vehicle (the image) and the tenor (the concept) together when the figure is central to the passage’s argument. If the metaphor structures the entire essay (an extended metaphor), retain the governing image and note its developmental stages (e.g., “the narrative unfolds as a garden: sowing, weeding, harvest”). For clustered figures, map the semantic field (e.g., nautical imagery: “anchor”, “drift”, “storm”) and summarise the field rather than each instance. When a figure is ironic or paradoxical (“the silence screamed”), keep the tension; do not resolve it into a plain statement. Allusions to canonical works (Biblical, Classical, Shakespearean) must be named because they carry intertextual weight (e.g., “echoes of Hamlet’s ‘to be or not to be’”). Preserve the tone markers (irony, melancholy, exultation) that the figurative language creates, using adverbial qualifiers (“grimly”, “triumphantly”). A 90‑word précis of a 1,200‑word literary essay therefore contains: the governing metaphor or semantic field, two or three key figures with vehicle‑tenor pairs, the allusive references, and the tonal trajectory.

Précis of Policy Briefs: Retaining Recommendations, Evidence Grades, and Implementation Timelines

Policy briefs follow a rigid architecture: context, evidence synthesis, options appraisal, recommendation, and implementation plan. Examiners test whether a précis preserves the decision‑making logic. Start by extracting the policy problem statement (often a single sentence). Next, capture the evidence grade using the framework the brief adopts (e.g., GRADE: high, moderate, low, very low; or WHO “strong/conditional” recommendations). Quote the exact grade (“moderate‑certainty evidence”) because downgrading or upgrading changes the recommendation strength. Summarise each policy option with its projected impact (quantitative if given: “Option A reduces incidence by 12 % (95 % CI 8–16)”) and the evidence grade supporting it. The recommended option must be identified verbatim (“the panel strongly recommends Option A”). Then compress the implementation timeline into milestones: “Phase 1 (0–6 months): stakeholder mapping; Phase 2 (6–18 months): pilot rollout in three districts; Phase 3 (18–36 months): national scale‑up”. Preserve any conditionalities (“provided funding exceeds $5 million”) and monitoring indicators (“quarterly coverage ≥ 80 %”). Discard background statistics that do not feed the option appraisal. A 110‑word précis of a 2,000‑word brief therefore contains: problem statement, evidence grade for each option, chosen recommendation with its grade, three‑phase timeline with conditionalities, and key monitoring metric.

Cross‑Referencing Footnotes and Endnotes in Academic Texts

Academic passages often embed critical qualifications, source attributions, or methodological caveats in footnotes or endnotes. A précis that ignores these layers produces an incomplete argument map. Treat each note as a satellite proposition linked to a specific main‑text claim. First, match the superscript cue to its note; then classify the note’s function: citation (source), elaboration (additional data), qualification (scope limitation), or rebuttal (anticipated objection). When a note supplies a primary source (e.g., “See Arch. Nat. Kew, FO 123/45, f. 12”), retain the archive reference because it signals evidentiary provenance. If a note qualifies a statistical claim (“the 95 % CI widens to ± 4.2 when outliers are included”), incorporate the qualification into the main claim (“the effect size is 3.1 ± 4.2”). For methodological notes (“the sample excludes non‑respondents, n = 27”), embed the limitation (“the analysis rests on 27 respondents”). When multiple notes cluster around a single paragraph, synthesise them into a single caveat sentence (“the estimate relies on a non‑random subsample (n = 27) and widens to ± 4.2 with outliers”). Preserve the note’s authorial voice (“the author notes”, “the editor adds”) to maintain attribution. A 80‑word précis of a 1,500‑word article with dense footnotes therefore contains: the central thesis, two key evidential claims with their note‑derived qualifications, one methodological limitation, and the primary source citation.

  • Collapsing ratio decidendi and obiter dicta into a single legal principle.
  • Reporting a non‑significant p‑value as “no effect” instead of preserving the confidence interval.
  • Translating a governing metaphor into literal description, losing the vehicle‑tenor structure.
  • Omitting the evidence grade (e.g., GRADE) that determines recommendation strength in policy briefs.
  • Ignoring footnote qualifications that alter the scope or certainty of a main‑text claim.
  • Presenting implementation milestones without their conditionalities or monitoring indicators.
  • Failing to distinguish authorial voice in endnotes, leading to misattribution of caveats.

Précis of Technical Manuals: Preserving Procedural Steps and Safety Constraints

Technical manuals — whether for aircraft maintenance (e.g., ATA 100/2200 chapters), medical device operation (IEC 60601‑1), or industrial process control (ISA‑88) — follow a rigid procedural architecture: scope, definitions, safety warnings, step‑by‑step actions, verification points, and troubleshooting branches. A précis that collapses this hierarchy into a narrative paragraph loses the conditional logic that examiners test. The first rule is to retain the sequential numbering of critical steps (e.g., “Step 3.2: Torque bolt to 45 Nm ± 2 Nm”) because any re‑ordering changes the safety case. Second, every warning, caution, or note flagged by the standard (ISO 12100 risk‑assessment symbols) must appear verbatim or with an exact paraphrase that preserves the hazard class (e.g., “WARNING – High voltage; risk of electric shock”). Third, verification criteria (acceptance tolerances, test‑point values, go/no‑go limits) are quantitative and non‑negotiable; they must be reproduced with units and uncertainty bounds.
