Fourth, branching logic (if‑then‑else structures for fault isolation) should be rendered as a compact decision table rather than prose, because the examiner checks whether the candidate recognises alternative paths. Finally, reference identifiers (document number, revision, amendment date) are retained to demonstrate traceability. A 120‑word précis of a 3,000‑word maintenance chapter therefore contains: the document identifier, the top‑level safety warning, the first three critical steps with torque values, the verification tolerance, the primary fault‑branch condition, and the revision tag.

Summarizing Economic Models: Retaining Assumptions, Equilibrium Conditions, and Comparative Statics

Economic model summaries — whether of the Solow growth model, the New Keynesian DSGE framework, or a simple Cournot duopoly — are judged on the fidelity of three pillars: assumptions, equilibrium derivation, and comparative‑static results. The assumptions block must list the functional forms (Cobb‑Douglas production, CRRA utility), the parameter restrictions (α∈(0,1), β∈(0,1)), and the institutional constraints (perfect competition, no‑adjustment costs). Omitting any assumption that drives a result (e.g., the assumption of full depreciation δ=1 in the textbook Solow model) constitutes a fatal omission. The equilibrium section should present the key first‑order conditions (e.g., marginal product of capital equals rental rate plus depreciation) and the closed‑form steady‑state expressions (k* = (sA/(n+g+δ))^(1/(1‑α))). Comparative statics — the response of steady‑state capital to a change in savings rate (∂k*/∂s > 0) or to a productivity shock (∂y*/∂A > 0) — must be reported with the sign and, where standard, the elasticity (e.g., εk*,s=1/(1‑α)). When the source text includes a calibration table (e.g., α=0.33, δ=0.05, n=0.01, g=0.02), the précis reproduces the calibrated steady‑state values (k*≈9.2, y*≈2.1) because examiners often ask for numerical illustration. A 150‑word précis of a 2,500‑word journal article therefore contains: the model label, the full assumption set, the steady‑state equations, two comparative‑static derivatives with signs, and the calibrated numeric steady state.

Worked Précis Construction
A 1,200‑word excerpt from a technical manual for a high‑pressure steam valve (ASME BPVC Section VIII) describes: (1) scope and applicable codes; (2) safety warning – “WARNING – Pressurised steam above 15 MPa can cause severe burns”; (3) step‑by‑step inspection: 1.1 Verify nameplate data; 1.2 Measure wall thickness at three points; 1.3 Perform hydrostatic test at 1.5× design pressure; (4) acceptance criteria – wall thickness ≥ 90 % of nominal, test pressure held for 10 minutes without leak; (5) fault branch – if thickness < 90 %, replace valve; (6) document reference – “Doc V‑2023‑Rev 4, 15 Mar 2023”. Write a 100‑word précis.
Doc V‑2023‑Rev 4 (15 Mar 2023) – ASME BPVC VIII high‑pressure steam valve inspection. WARNING – Pressurised steam > 15 MPa causes severe burns. Steps: 1.1 Confirm nameplate; 1.2 Measure wall thickness at three locations; 1.3 Hydrostatic test at 1.5× design pressure for 10 min. Acceptance: thickness ≥ 90 % nominal; no leak during hold. Fault branch: if thickness < 90 %, replace valve. (99 words)
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a passage contains numbered procedures, copy the numbers and critical numeric values verbatim; do not renumber or round unless the word‑count forces it. Use a slash (/) to separate sequential steps in a single sentence, preserving the logical order. For economic models, write the key equation in plain text (e.g., k* = (sA/(n+g+δ))^(1/(1‑α))) rather than describing it, because the examiner awards marks for symbolic fidelity.

Précis of Diplomatic Communiqués: Maintaining Diplomatic Language, Reservations, and Conditional Commitments

Diplomatic communiqués — joint statements, memoranda of understanding, or United Nations Security Council presidential statements — obey a convention‑driven lexicon: “reaffirms”, “welcomes”, “calls upon”, “decides”, “requests”, “underlines”. A précis must preserve the modal hierarchy because each verb signals a different legal weight (e.g., “decides” creates binding obligations under Chapter VII, whereas “calls upon” is hortatory). The first task is to map every operative paragraph to its modal verb and retain that verb unchanged. Second, reservations and understandings appended by signatories (e.g., “State X reserves the right to interpret Article 4 in accordance with its domestic law”) are not boilerplate; they alter the treaty’s effect and must be reproduced with the reserving state’s name. Third, conditional commitments (“provided that”, “subject to the availability of resources”, “pending ratification”) are logical qualifiers; dropping them converts a conditional pledge into an absolute one, a frequent examiner trap. Fourth, cross‑references to prior resolutions (e.g., “recalling resolution 242 (1967)”) are retained because they embed the communiqué in a normative chain. Finally, temporal markers (“within six months”, “by 31 December 2025”) are kept verbatim. A 130‑word précis of a 2,200‑word joint declaration therefore contains: the title and date, the three highest‑modal operative verbs with their objects, two reservations with state names, one conditional clause, the key prior‑resolution reference, and the principal deadline.

Handling Numerical Uncertainty in Scientific Summaries: Confidence Intervals, Error Propagation, and Significance Thresholds

Scientific abstracts and results sections routinely report measurements with uncertainty: mean ± standard deviation, 95 % confidence intervals (CI), standard errors (SE), or Bayesian credible intervals. A précis that replaces “95 % CI = 12.3–15.7” with “about 14” loses the statistical information examiners test. The protocol is threefold. First, preserve the interval format exactly as presented (e.g., “RR = 1.45 (95 % CI 1.20–1.75)”). If the source gives a standard error, convert only when the original also supplies the conversion formula; otherwise retain SE. Second, report the significance threshold used (p < 0.05, p < 0.01, or Bayes factor > 10) because a result described as “significant” without the threshold is ambiguous. Third, carry forward error propagation when the source combines measurements (e.g., “ΔT = (T₂ − T₁) ± √(σ₁²+σ₂²)”). The précis must show the combined uncertainty expression, not just the final number. When a study presents multiple outcomes, summarise each with its own interval and significance flag. A 110‑word précis of a 1,800‑word clinical trial report therefore contains: the primary endpoint with its 95 % CI and p‑value, the secondary endpoint with its CI, the adjusted analysis note (e.g., “adjusted for age and sex”), and the pre‑specified non‑inferiority margin.

Cross‑Disciplinary Synthesis: Integrating Qualitative and Quantitative Evidence in a Single Précis

Policy‑oriented passages often juxtapose ethnographic interview excerpts, case‑study narratives, and econometric regression tables. The examiner expects a précis that demonstrates evidence grading across paradigms. Begin by tagging each evidence strand: qualitative (thematic saturation, participant quotes), quantitative (effect sizes, confidence intervals), and mixed‑methods integration (joint display, triangulation matrix). Preserve the qualitative claim with its illustrative quote truncated to the essential phrase (e.g., “participants described ‘a sense of abandonment’”). Keep the quantitative estimate with its uncertainty (e.g., “β = ‑0.32, 95 % CI ‑0.48 to ‑0.16”). When the source explicitly states a triangulation outcome (“qualitative themes corroborate the negative coefficient”), reproduce that synthesis sentence verbatim. Do not convert qualitative insight into a numeric proxy. Finally, note the evidence hierarchy the author adopts (e.g., GRADE “moderate certainty” for quantitative, “high confidence” for qualitative) because the précis must reflect the author’s own grading, not the candidate’s. A 140‑word précis of a 3,000‑word mixed‑methods article therefore contains: the research question, one key qualitative theme with a representative phrase, the primary quantitative effect with CI, the triangulation statement, and the GRADE ratings for each strand.

  • Collapsing numbered procedural steps into a single narrative, losing conditional branching.
  • Omitting modal verbs (“decides”, “calls upon”) that encode legal weight in diplomatic texts.
  • Dropping reservations or understandings attached by specific states.
  • Replacing a reported confidence interval with a point estimate or vague qualifier.
  • Ignoring the pre‑specified significance threshold and reporting “significant” without p‑value.
  • Converting qualitative themes into numeric scores, thereby misrepresenting mixed‑methods evidence.
  • Failing to retain the author’s evidence‑grading labels (GRADE, CERQual) which are part of the argument.

Précis of Judicial Dissenting and Concurring Opinions: Preserving Doctrinal Splits

A judgment précis that records only the majority holding discards the doctrinal architecture examiners test. Dissenting and concurring opinions map the fault lines of constitutional interpretation and signal future doctrinal shifts. The précis protocol requires four distinct layers. First, identify the ratio decidendi of the majority in a single propositional statement (e.g., "Section 377 IPC violates Articles 14, 15, and 21 because it criminalises consensual adult conduct based on sexual orientation"). Second, tag each separate opinion by author and classification: concurring in judgment only, concurring in reasoning, dissenting in part, or dissenting in whole. Third, extract the operative doctrinal test each opinion advances. In Navtej Singh Johar v. Union of India (2018), Justice Chandrachud’s concurrence introduced a "constitutional morality" test distinct from the majority’s "transformative constitutionalism" frame; Justice Malhotra’s concurrence grounded the right in privacy and autonomy under Article 21; the dissent (absent in this case) would have preserved the "presumption of constitutionality" standard. Fourth, record the stare decisis implication: does the dissent invite overruling of a specific precedent (e.g., Suresh Kumar Koushal v. Naz Foundation, 2013)? A 150-word précis of a 400-page Constitution Bench decision therefore contains: the majority ratio in one sentence; each separate opinion’s label, author, and core doctrinal test; the precedent explicitly overruled or distinguished; and the remedial direction (reading down, striking down, or issuing guidelines). Omitting the concurrence that narrows the majority’s sweep (e.g., Justice Nariman’s "manifest arbitrariness" test in Joseph Shine v. Union of India, 2018, limiting adultery decriminalisation to Section 497 IPC only) converts a nuanced holding into an overbroad rule — a frequent examiner trap.

Summarizing Systematic Reviews and Meta-Analyses: Forest Plots, Heterogeneity, and Publication Bias

A précis of a Cochrane or Campbell systematic review must compress the evidence synthesis pipeline without losing the statistical guardrails that determine credibility. The source text typically presents five structural modules: (1) PICO framing — Population, Intervention, Comparator, Outcome — which the précis reproduces verbatim because eligibility criteria define the inference boundary; (2) search strategy and selection flow — retain the PRISMA flow numbers (records identified, screened, excluded, included) only if the précis word budget exceeds 120 words; otherwise note "12 RCTs (n = 3,452) met inclusion criteria"; (3) risk-of-bias assessment — summarise the RoB 2 or ROBINS-I judgment pattern (e.g., "7 trials low risk, 3 some concerns, 2 high risk for blinding") rather than listing each domain; (4) meta-analytic output — the forest plot data must appear as: pooled effect measure (RR, OR, MD, SMD), 95 % CI, I² statistic, τ², and the model (fixed-effect vs. random-effects DerSimonian-Laird or REML). Example: "RR = 0.78 (95 % CI 0.65–0.93), I² = 42 %, τ² = 0.03, random-effects". Dropping I² or the model choice erases the heterogeneity judgment. (5) GRADE certainty rating — the précis must carry the final certainty label (High, Moderate, Low, Very Low) with the downgrading reasons (risk of bias, inconsistency, indirectness, imprecision, publication bias). If a funnel plot or Egger’s test indicates small-study effects, note "Egger’s p = 0.04, suggesting publication bias". Subgroup analyses and meta-regression covariates (e.g., "effect larger in trials with duration > 12 weeks, β = −0.15, p = 0.02") are retained only when the source flags them as pre-specified. A 130-word précis of a 6,000-word review therefore contains: PICO, included study count and design, pooled estimate with CI and heterogeneity indices, GRADE rating with downgrade reasons, and one key sensitivity analysis result (e.g., "excluding high-risk trials: RR = 0.82, 95 % CI 0.68–0.99").

Worked Précis: Meta-Analysis Summary
Condense the following 220-word extract into a 55-word précis retaining all statistical guardrails: "We included 14 double-blind RCTs (n = 4,210) comparing oral n-3 fatty acids vs. placebo for secondary prevention of major adverse cardiovascular events (MACE). RoB 2: 9 low risk, 3 some concerns (blinding), 2 high risk (attrition). Random-effects meta-analysis yielded RR = 0.91 (95 % CI 0.83–1.00), I² = 38 %, τ² = 0.008. Pre-specified subgroup: trials using EPA-only formulations (n = 3, n = 1,850) showed RR = 0.79 (95 % CI 0.68–0.92), I² = 0 %; mixed EPA/DHA trials (n = 11) RR = 0.95 (95 % CI 0.86–1.05). Funnel plot asymmetry: Egger’s test p = 0.07. GRADE: Moderate certainty (downgraded one level for imprecision). Sensitivity analysis excluding high-risk trials: RR = 0.93 (95 % CI 0.84–1.03)."
Fourteen RCTs (n = 4,210) found n-3 fatty acids did not significantly reduce MACE vs. placebo: RR = 0.91 (95 % CI 0.83–1.00), I² = 38 %, random-effects; GRADE Moderate (imprecision). EPA-only subgroup (3 trials, n = 1,850): RR = 0.79 (95 % CI 0.68–0.92), I² = 0 %. Egger’s p = 0.07. Sensitivity excluding high-risk trials: RR = 0.93 (95 % CI 0.84–1.03).
Exam-Day Strategy: Meta-Analysis Précis
When word count is tight, prioritise in this order: (1) pooled effect with CI and model, (2) I² and τ², (3) GRADE rating with downgrade reasons, (4) one pre-specified subgroup or sensitivity result. Never drop the heterogeneity indices — examiners treat their omission as statistical illiteracy. If the source reports both fixed and random effects, note which the authors adopted as primary. Write "REML" or "DL" only if the source specifies; otherwise "random-effects" suffices.

Précis of Central Bank Communications: Forward Guidance, Policy Stance, and Conditional Projections

Monetary policy statements (RBI MPC resolutions, FOMC statements, ECB accounts) embed layered commitment structures that a précis must decode. The text hierarchy runs: (1) policy action — the exact repo rate change (e.g., "policy repo rate increased by 50 basis points to 6.50 %") with effective date; (2) stance label — the calibrated phrase ("withdrawal of accommodation", "neutral", "accommodative") which is a term of art, not descriptive prose; (3) forward guidance modality — distinguish time-contingent ("rates to remain elevated through 2024"), state-contingent ("until inflation durably aligns with the 4 % target"), and threshold-based ("unless CPI inflation exceeds 6 % for two consecutive quarters") guidance. The précis must preserve the logical connective ("unless", "provided that", "conditional on"). (4) projection tables — retain the median GDP growth and CPI inflation forecasts for each projection year with the assumption set (e.g., "assuming normal monsoon, crude at $85/bbl, no fiscal slippage"). Dropping assumptions converts a conditional forecast into an unconditional prediction. (5) dissent record — name the dissenting member(s) and their preferred action (e.g., "one member dissented, preferring a 35 bps hike with 'neutral' stance"). (6) minority assessment paragraphs — if a member flags a specific risk ("financial stability risks from NBFC leverage"), include the risk noun phrase. A 110-word précis of a 1,200-word RBI resolution therefore contains: rate action with date, stance label, forward guidance clause with its conditionality, two-year projection medians with one key assumption, dissent count and direction, and one highlighted risk flag. Replacing "withdrawal of accommodation" with "tightening stance" loses the operational signalling examiners test.

Handling Counterfactual and Causal Reasoning in Historical and Social Science Texts

Passages in economic history, political economy, and historical sociology advance causal claims through counterfactual reasoning ("but for British land revenue systems, commercialisation would have followed a different trajectory"). A précis that flattens these into simple assertions ("British land revenue systems caused commercialisation") commits a category error. The protocol requires preserving three logical strata. First, the factual antecedent — the observed historical sequence (e.g., "Permanent Settlement (1793) → zamindari consolidation → peasant indebtedness"). Second, the counterfactual conditional — the explicit "if not X, then not Y" or "had X not occurred, Y would differ" clause, retaining the author’s modal qualifier ("likely", "plausible", "certain"). Example: "The author argues that without the Permanent Settlement’s fixity of demand, peasant differentiation would probably have been less pronounced." Third, the causal mechanism — the intervening variable chain the author specifies (e.g., "revenue fixity → zamindar rent-maximisation → subinfeudation → loss of occupancy rights"). The précis must not substitute the author’s mechanism with a generic "exploitation" label. Fourth, evidence grading — if the text cites cliometric estimates (e.g., "Banerjee & Iyer, 2005: districts with landlord systems show significantly lower agricultural investment today, p < 0.01"), retain the effect direction, significance, and citation. Fifth, rival explanations acknowledged — the author’s concession ("though commercialisation also responded to railway penetration") is part of the argument structure. A 140-word précis of a 3,500-word article therefore contains: the central counterfactual claim with its modal strength, the specified causal mechanism in three links, one quantitative evidence anchor with citation, and one acknowledged alternative pathway. Converting "would probably have been less pronounced" into "was reduced" erases the epistemic humility that constitutes the author’s scholarly position.

Précis of International Trade Agreements: Tariff Schedules, Rules of Origin, and Dispute Settlement Provisions

Free Trade Agreement texts (e.g., India-UAE CEPA, RCEP, USMCA) layer four distinct legal instruments that a précis must separate. First, the tariff elimination schedule — identify the staging category for the sensitive sector (e.g., "dairy: TRQ 10,000 MT year 1, 5 % annual expansion, full elimination year 10"; "autos: immediate elimination for EVs, 15-year phase-down for ICE"). The précis retains the TRQ volume, growth rate, and endpoint year — not "gradual liberalisation". Second, rules of origin (ROO) — preserve the product-specific rule (PSR) type: wholly obtained, change in tariff classification (CTC) at chapter/heading/subheading level, regional value content (RVC) threshold (e.g., "RVC ≥ 40 % under build-down method"), or specific processing (e.g., "textiles: yarn-forward"). Dropping the method (build-down vs. build-up) changes the compliance calculus. Third, dispute settlement architecture — note the forum (standing body vs. ad hoc panel), the most-favoured-nation or non-violation complaint availability, the appeal mechanism (Appellate Body vs. none), and the retaliation authorisation rule (e.g., "cross-retaliation permitted in services for goods violations"). Fourth, exceptions and carve-outs — security exception (Art. XXI GATT analogue), cultural exception, essential security interests, and the general exceptions chapeau test ("not applied in a manner constituting arbitrary discrimination"). A 150-word précis of a 2,000-page FTA therefore contains: the staging category for three sensitive sectors with TRQ or phase-out specifics; the PSR type and threshold for the top export sector; the dispute settlement forum and one distinctive procedural rule (e.g., "no appeal, panel report final"); and two named carve-outs with their triggering conditions. Summarising "tariffs reduced" without the staging category converts a legal instrument into a political press release.

  • Replacing a dissent’s doctrinal test with a generic "disagreed with majority" — the test is the precedent value.
  • Dropping I², τ², or the meta-analytic model (fixed vs. random) in a systematic review précis — heterogeneity indices are not optional decoration.
  • Converting state-contingent forward guidance ("until inflation durably aligns") into time-contingent ("for two years") — the conditionality is the policy commitment.
  • Flattening a counterfactual claim ("would probably have been") into a factual assertion ("was") — modal verbs encode epistemic status.
  • Summarising ROO as "liberal rules of origin" without the PSR type (CTC, RVC, specific processing) and threshold — compliance turns on the exact rule.
  • Omitting the dissenting MPC member’s name and preferred action — the vote split signals policy uncertainty.
  • Dropping the GRADE downgrade reasons (risk of bias, inconsistency, etc.) — the certainty rating is meaningless without its justification.

Précis of Constitutional Amendment Debates: Preserving Doctrinal Conflict and Procedural Safeguards

Constitutional amendment debates combine textual analysis, historical intent, and institutional politics. A précis must first isolate the amendment provision — article number, amendment procedure (simple majority, special majority, ratification by states) — and then map the competing doctrinal positions: the “basic structure” doctrine, the “parliamentary sovereignty” argument, and any “judicial restraint” counter‑claim. Each position should be linked to the landmark judgment that crystallised it (e.g., Kesavananda Bharati 1973 for basic structure; Minerva Mills 1980 for judicial review of amendment power). The précis retains the procedural safeguard — referendum, supermajority, or state ratification — because the threshold determines political feasibility. Quantitative anchors such as the number of states required for ratification (half the states for certain amendments under Article 368) are kept verbatim. The author’s modal language (“likely”, “arguably”, “must”) is preserved to show epistemic stance. Finally, any dissenting opinion introducing a distinct test (e.g., Justice Khanna’s “essential features” test) is recorded with the justice’s name and formulation, as it becomes binding precedent.

When the source text traces a chronological sequence — proposal, committee report, parliamentary debate, presidential assent — the précis compresses the timeline into three nodes: initiation, contested vote, and final authentication. The causal mechanism linking political pressure to textual change (e.g., “state‑level agitation → inclusion of Article 31C → judicial review limitation”) is retained in three links, not collapsed into a generic “political compromise”. If the author evaluates alternative amendment routes (e.g., “ordinary legislation vs. constitutional amendment”), the précis notes the rejected route and the reason given (e.g., “ordinary legislation would lack entrenchment”). This layered preservation ensures the précis remains a reliable surrogate for the full debate record.

Summarizing Judicial Review Standards: Tiered Scrutiny, Proportionality, and Margin of Appreciation

Judicial review standards differ across constitutional systems but share a common architecture: a hierarchy of scrutiny intensities tied to the nature of the right or classification. A précis must identify the tier — strict scrutiny, intermediate scrutiny, rational basis — and the triggering classification (suspect class, fundamental right, economic regulation). For each tier the précis records the exact test: “compelling state interest narrowly tailored” for strict scrutiny; “important governmental objective substantially related” for intermediate; “legitimate state interest rationally related” for rational basis. The source’s citation of the controlling case (e.g., United States v. Carolene Products 1938 footnote 4 for tiered scrutiny; R. v. Oakes 1986 for proportionality in Canada) is kept with the case name and year.

Proportionality analysis adds a four‑step sub‑structure: legitimate aim, suitability, necessity, and balancing. The précis preserves the author’s ordering of these steps and any quantitative threshold mentioned (e.g., “the measure must impair the right no more than 15 % beyond the minimum necessary”). The margin of appreciation — the deference granted to legislative or executive judgment — is recorded with its scope (wide, narrow, or none) and the institutional actor to whom it applies (national parliament, regional authority). If the text contrasts the European Court of Human Rights’ “margin of appreciation” with the Indian Supreme Court’s “proportionality” test, the précis notes the doctrinal divergence and the case illustrating each (e.g., Handyside v. United Kingdom 1976; Modern Dental College v. State of Madhya Pradesh 2016). Any dissent proposing a different tier or modified proportionality balancing is captured with the justice’s name and the precise doctrinal shift.

Technique: Extracting the Core Doctrinal Test from a Judicial Review Passage
A passage states: “The Court applied strict scrutiny because the law classified on the basis of race, requiring the state to demonstrate a compelling interest narrowly tailored; the dissent argued for intermediate scrutiny, citing the law’s remedial purpose.” Write a one‑sentence précis of the doctrinal conflict.
The majority invoked strict scrutiny — compelling interest narrowly tailored — for a race‑based classification, while the dissent advocated intermediate scrutiny — important objective substantially related — on remedial grounds.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a comprehension passage lists multiple review standards, tabulate them in the margin: tier, trigger, test phrase, controlling case. This visual map lets you retrieve the exact wording for any inference question within seconds.

Condensing International Human Rights Treaty Reporting: State Obligations, Concluding Observations, and Follow‑Up Mechanisms

Human rights treaty reporting cycles generate three distinct documents: the state party report, the treaty body’s concluding observations, and the follow‑up procedure. A précis separates these layers. First, the state obligation is identified by treaty article (e.g., ICCPR Art. 2(1) “to respect and ensure rights”) and the specific reporting guideline paragraph (e.g., CCPR/C/GC/34 para. 12 on freedom of expression). The précis retains the state’s self‑assessment language (“the State considers that…”) only when it reflects a contested interpretation. Second, the concluding observations are distilled into three categories: positive measures, principal concerns, and recommendations. Each recommendation is recorded with its paragraph number and the treaty body’s modal verb (“should”, “must”, “urges”). Third, the follow‑up mechanism — whether a “request for information within one year”, a “country visit”, or a “procedure under Article 40” — is noted with the deadline and the responsible focal point. Quantitative data such as the number of communications received, the percentage of recommendations implemented, or the timeline for the next periodic report are kept verbatim. The précis also captures any dissenting view within the treaty body (e.g., a member’s separate opinion on the margin of appreciation) with the member’s name and the precise doctrinal point.

When the source text juxtaposes multiple treaty bodies (e.g., CEDAW Committee vs. Human Rights Committee) on the same state, the précis aligns observations by right cluster (non‑discrimination, due process, etc.) and flags contradictory recommendations. The causal link between a specific state policy (e.g., “anti‑conversion law”) and the treaty body’s concern (“violates Art. 18 ICCPR”) is preserved in a three‑link chain: policy → treaty provision → concluding observation. This structure prevents the précis from collapsing distinct legal analyses into a generic “human rights concerns”.

Précis of Environmental Impact Assessment Reports: Mitigation Hierarchy, Cumulative Effects, and Public Participation Records

Environmental Impact Assessment (EIA) reports follow a regulated structure: screening, scoping, baseline study, impact prediction, mitigation hierarchy, cumulative effects analysis, and public participation log. A précis must retain the mitigation hierarchy in its statutory order — avoid, minimise, restore, offset — and note any deviation the project proponent proposes (e.g., “offset only” without avoidance). Each mitigation measure is recorded with its quantitative target (e.g., “reforestation of 120 ha within 5 years”) and the monitoring indicator (e.g., “survival rate ≥ 80 %”). Cumulative effects are summarised by identifying the spatial and temporal boundaries (e.g., “10 km radius, 20‑year horizon”), the interacting stressors (e.g., “water abstraction + thermal discharge”), and the significance threshold used (e.g., “exceeds 5 % of baseline flow”). The public participation record is condensed to the number of consultation rounds, the categories of stakeholders consulted (indigenous communities, NGOs, local government), and any substantive objections that triggered a design change (e.g., “community objection to pipeline routing led to 3 km realignment”). The précis also notes the regulatory decision — approval with conditions, conditional approval, or rejection — and the specific condition numbers corresponding to the mitigation measures.

If the EIA includes an alternatives analysis, the précis lists the alternatives examined (no‑project, alternative site, alternative technology) and the comparative matrix criteria (environmental, social, economic). The rationale for selecting the preferred alternative is captured in the author’s own wording (“selected because it reduces habitat fragmentation by 40 %”). Any supplementary studies (biodiversity offset plan, climate resilience assessment) are referenced by title and version, because later compliance audits rely on those exact documents.

Handling Statistical Modeling in Policy Briefs: Counterfactual Simulations, Sensitivity Analyses, and Scenario Weighting

Policy briefs increasingly embed quantitative modelling results. A précis must distinguish three modelling components: the counterfactual simulation (what would happen without the policy), the sensitivity analysis (how results change with key parameter variation), and the scenario weighting (probabilities assigned to alternative futures). The counterfactual is recorded with its baseline year, the policy variable (e.g., “carbon tax = US$50/tCO₂”), and the outcome metric (e.g., “GDP reduction 0.3 % by 2030”). The sensitivity analysis is summarised by the parameters tested (discount rate, elasticity of substitution), the range explored (e.g., “discount rate 3‑7 %”), and the resulting outcome band (e.g., “GDP impact 0.1‑0.5 %”). Scenario weighting is captured by the scenario names (e.g., “high‑growth”, “low‑carbon”), the assigned probabilities (e.g., “0.4, 0.35, 0.25”), and the weighted average outcome. The précis retains the model name and version (e.g., “GTAP‑E v.7”) and the data sources for key inputs (e.g., “IEA World Energy Outlook 2023”). Any caveat the author attaches — “results assume perfect policy enforcement” — is kept with its modal qualifier (“assumes”, “likely”). If the brief compares two models, the précis notes the structural difference (computable general equilibrium vs. integrated assessment) and the divergent policy implication (e.g., “CGE shows modest employment loss; IAM shows net job gain”). This granularity prevents the précis from presenting a single headline figure as definitive.

  • Collapsing distinct doctrinal tests into a single “review standard” label.
  • Omitting the procedural threshold (e.g., special majority) that conditions an amendment’s validity.
  • Replacing a treaty body’s modal verb (“should”) with a stronger (“must”) or weaker (“may”) verb.
  • Dropping the mitigation hierarchy order and presenting only the final offset measure.
  • Reporting a single model output without its sensitivity range or scenario weights.
  • Failing to attribute dissenting opinions to the specific judge or treaty‑body member.
  • Each of these omissions converts a legally precise summary into a vague policy note.

Précis of Scientific Grant Proposals: Preserving Hypotheses, Methodology, and Expected Impact

A scientific grant proposal follows a highly codified architecture dictated by funding agencies such as the National Science Foundation (NSF), the National Institutes of Health (NIH), and the European Research Council (ERC). The précis must retain the specific aims — usually three to four concise statements — because they constitute the logical backbone of the project. Each aim is linked to a hypothesis that is testable, falsifiable, and explicitly stated; omitting the hypothesis collapses the proposal into a descriptive narrative. The significance section is condensed to the gap in current knowledge, the potential paradigm shift, and the relevance to the agency’s strategic priorities (e.g., NSF’s “Big Ideas” or NIH’s “High‑Risk, High‑Reward” programmes). The innovation paragraph is reduced to the novel conceptual framework, methodology, or technology that distinguishes the work from existing literature. The approach is summarised by enumerating the experimental design, key methodologies (e.g., CRISPR‑Cas9 genome editing, single‑cell RNA‑seq, cryo‑EM), the statistical power analysis, and the contingency plans for identified risks. The timeline is captured as milestones with quarterly or annual deliverables, and the budget justification is represented by major cost categories (personnel, equipment, consumables, travel) with the total direct costs. Any preliminary data that support feasibility are noted with the figure or table reference (e.g., “Fig. 2 shows 85 % knock‑down efficiency”). The précis must preserve the agency‑specific review criteria language (intellectual merit, broader impacts) because evaluators map the summary directly onto those rubrics.

Condensing Corporate Annual Reports: Financial Highlights, Risk Factors, and Governance Disclosures

Corporate annual reports prepared under IFRS or US GAAP contain a mandatory Management Discussion and Analysis (MD&A), audited financial statements, and a Corporate Governance section. A précis for a regulatory or investment audience must first capture the financial highlights: revenue, EBITDA, net profit, earnings per share, and year‑on‑year growth percentages, together with the reporting currency and any restatements. The segmental breakdown (geographic or product‑line) is retained only where it explains a material variance (> 10 %). The balance‑sheet strength is summarised by the current ratio, debt‑to‑equity, and cash‑conversion cycle, noting any covenant breaches or waivers. The risk factors section is distilled to the top‑five material risks (e.g., commodity price volatility, regulatory change in the EU taxonomy, cyber‑security incidents, supply‑chain concentration, climate‑transition risk) with the company’s mitigation actions and the quantified exposure where disclosed (e.g., “€120 m exposure to carbon‑pricing scenarios”). The governance disclosures are reduced to board composition (independent vs. executive directors), committee charters (audit, remuneration, nomination), related‑party transactions, and any shareholder resolutions passed or rejected at the AGM. The audit opinion (unqualified, qualified, adverse, or disclaimer) and the key audit matters (KAMs) are reproduced verbatim because they signal auditor judgement. Finally, the forward‑looking statements are captured with the safe‑harbour disclaimer and the specific guidance metrics (e.g., “FY2025 revenue growth 5‑7 %”).

Technique: Extracting the Core Hypothesis from a Grant Abstract
Given the following abstract excerpt: “We hypothesize that targeted inhibition of the NLRP3 inflammasome will reduce neuroinflammation and improve cognitive outcomes in a transgenic mouse model of Alzheimer’s disease. Aim 1 will test the efficacy of the small‑molecule inhibitor MCC950 in vitro; Aim 2 will assess behavioural rescue in vivo; Aim 3 will evaluate biomarker correlates.” Write a one‑sentence précis of the central hypothesis.
The central hypothesis is that pharmacological inhibition of the NLRP3 inflammasome by MCC950 attenuates neuroinflammation and restores cognitive function in a transgenic Alzheimer’s mouse model.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a précis prompt asks for “the main scientific claim”, locate the word “hypothesize”, “propose”, or “predict” in the source. Copy the clause that follows, strip methodological detail, and retain the causal link (X → Y). This yields a single, high‑density sentence that satisfies both word‑count and content criteria.

Summarizing Legislative Committee Reports: Findings, Recommendations, and Dissenting Views

Legislative committee reports — whether from a standing committee, a select committee, or a joint parliamentary committee — follow a prescribed template: Terms of Reference, Evidence Summary, Findings, Recommendations, and Dissenting/Minority Opinions. The précis must open with the committee’s name, the bill or inquiry title, and the date of adoption. The Findings are condensed to the factual conclusions that are directly supported by the evidence (e.g., “The committee finds that the current threshold for mandatory disclosure under Section 12(3) of the Companies Act 2013 fails to capture 68 % of related‑party transactions”). Each finding is tagged with the paragraph number for traceability. The Recommendations are listed in the order presented, preserving the legislative language (“The committee recommends that the threshold be lowered to ₹10 crore and that a quarterly reporting obligation be introduced”). Where a recommendation is conditional (“subject to the Finance Ministry’s fiscal impact assessment”), the condition is retained. The Dissenting Views are not omitted; they are summarised by the dissenting member’s name, the specific finding or recommendation contested, and the rationale (e.g., “Dr. A. Sharma dissents from Recommendation 4, arguing that the proposed threshold would impose disproportionate compliance costs on SMEs”). Any Supplementary Notes (e.g., “The committee notes the upcoming OECD BEPS 2.0 guidelines”) are included if they affect the implementation timeline. The précis closes with the committee’s voting record (unanimous, majority, or split) and the date the report was laid before the house.

Précis of International Arbitration Awards: Preserving Jurisdiction, Merits, and Enforcement Conditions

An international commercial arbitration award rendered under the ICC Rules, the UNCITRAL Rules, or ad‑hoc proceedings must be précis‑ed with three pillars intact: Jurisdiction, Merits, and Enforcement Conditions. The Jurisdiction section records the arbitration agreement’s scope (e.g., “all disputes arising out of or in connection with the Share Purchase Agreement dated 12 March 2019”), the seat of arbitration (e.g., Singapore), the governing law (e.g., English law), and any jurisdictional objections raised and dismissed (e.g., “Respondent’s challenge under Article 6(3) of the ICC Rules rejected”). The Merits are summarised by the claims, counter‑claims, and the tribunal’s determinations on each cause of action (breach of warranty, indemnity, fraudulent misrepresentation). Quantitative outcomes are retained exactly: “Claimant awarded US$ 12,450,000 in damages plus interest at 5 % per annum from 1 January 2020; Respondent’s counter‑claim dismissed.” The Costs allocation (legal fees, arbitrator fees, administrative expenses) and the Interest rate (pre‑award, post‑award) are noted. The Enforcement Conditions capture any New York Convention grounds for refusal that the award addresses (e.g., “The tribunal expressly finds no public‑policy violation under Article V(2)(b)”). If the award contains a partial award or interim measures, these are listed with their dates. The précis must also note the language of the award and any confidentiality orders that restrict disclosure, because these affect subsequent enforcement proceedings.

Handling Intertextual References in Literary Criticism: Allusion, Intertextuality, and Critical Dialogue

Literary criticism essays frequently embed allusions (brief, intentional references to a prior text), intertextual echoes (structural or thematic resonances identified by the critic), and critical dialogue (explicit engagement with other scholars’ arguments). A précis must differentiate these layers. An allusion is recorded with the source text, the specific passage (e.g., “the ‘still point of the turning world’ from T. S. Eliot’s *Four Quartets*”), and the function it serves in the argument (e.g., “to illustrate the paradox of temporal stasis in modernist narrative”). Intertextuality is summarised by naming the theoretical framework (Kristeva’s “intertextuality”, Barthes’ “textual plurality”) and the pair of texts placed in dialogue (e.g., “the critic reads *Mrs Dalloway* through the lens of *Ulysses*, highlighting parallel stream‑of‑consciousness techniques”). The critical dialogue is reduced to the scholar’s name, the publication year, the core claim, and the critic’s position (agreement, qualification, or rebuttal). For instance: “Following Said (1993), the author argues that Conrad’s *Heart of Darkness* reinforces imperial discourse; however, the critic contends that the novella’s narrative ambiguity subverts that reading.” The précis preserves the modal verbs used by the critic (“suggests”, “demonstrates”, “contests”) because they encode the epistemic stance. Finally, any meta‑critical commentary (e.g., “this reading exemplifies the postcolonial turn in early‑21st‑century modernist studies”) is retained as a framing device for the essay’s scholarly contribution.

  • Omitting the hypothesis statement in a grant précis and replacing it with a generic research aim.
  • Collapsing segmental financial data into a single revenue figure, losing material variance signals.
  • Dropping dissenting opinions from a legislative committee report, presenting a false consensus.
  • Failing to record the arbitration seat and governing law, which are decisive for enforcement.
  • Treating an allusion as a mere quotation without noting its argumentative function.
  • Replacing the critic’s modal verb (“suggests”) with a stronger verb (“proves”), altering epistemic weight.
  • Mastering these nuances ensures precise and exam‑ready précis.

Précis of Technical Standards and Specifications

Technical standards — such as those issued by ISO, IEC, ASTM, and BSI — combine normative requirements, test methods, and conformity‑assessment clauses. A précis must preserve the hierarchy: scope, normative references, terms and definitions, requirements (often numbered), verification methods, and annexes. The scope statement defines the product, process, or service covered; any exclusion (e.g., “this standard does not apply to medical devices”) is retained verbatim because it determines applicability. Normative references are listed with full citation (standard number, year, title) because downstream compliance hinges on the exact version. Requirements are condensed by grouping clauses that share a common object (e.g., “material composition”, “dimensional tolerances”, “surface finish”) and stating quantitative limits (e.g., “tensile strength ≥ 450 MPa”, “roughness Ra ≤ 0.8 µm”). Test methods are summarised by principle (e.g., “ASTM E8 tensile test at 23 °C ± 2 °C”) and acceptance criteria, not by reproducing the full procedure. Conformity‑assessment clauses (type testing, routine inspection, certification scheme) are captured as a decision tree: “If the product passes type test per clause 7.2, routine inspection per clause 8.1 applies; otherwise, re‑qualification per clause 9.” Normative annexes (e.g., Annex A with mandatory tables) are treated as part of the main text; informative annexes are noted only if they affect interpretation. The précis closes with the standard’s status (current, withdrawn, superseded) and the date of the latest amendment, because examiners may test whether the candidate recognises that a superseded standard cannot be cited for compliance.

Summarizing Clinical Trial Protocols

A clinical trial protocol governed by ICH E6 (GCP) follows a fixed structure: background, objectives, design, population, interventions, outcomes, statistical analysis plan, safety monitoring, ethics, and data management. The précis must retain the trial phase (I–IV), design label (randomised, double‑blind, parallel‑group, crossover), and the primary hypothesis expressed as a null/alternative pair (e.g., “H₀: mean change in HbA1c ≤ 0 % vs H₁: mean change > 0.5 %”). Inclusion and exclusion criteria are condensed into a logical set: “Adults 18‑75 y with T2DM, HbA1c 7‑10 %, eGFR ≥ 60 mL/min/1.73 m²; excluding prior GLP‑1 agonist use, hepatic impairment, pregnancy.” Intervention arm(s) are described by drug name, dose, route, frequency, and duration; comparator arms receive identical detail. Primary and key secondary endpoints are listed with measurement time‑points and analysis populations (ITT, per‑protocol, safety). The statistical analysis plan is reduced to the primary test (e.g., “ANCOVA with baseline HbA1c as covariate, two‑sided α = 0.05”), multiplicity control (e.g., “hierarchical testing of secondary endpoints”), and sample‑size justification (e.g., “90 % power to detect 0.5 % difference, 200 subjects per arm, 15 % dropout”). Safety monitoring captures the Data Safety Monitoring Board charter, stopping rules (e.g., “≥ 2 % SAE rate in any arm triggers review”), and adverse‑event grading (CTCAE v5.0). Ethics and regulatory identifiers (ClinicalTrials.gov NCT number, EudraCT, IRB approval date) are retained for traceability. The précis ends with the protocol version number, date, and amendment history, as examiners may ask whether the candidate can identify the operative version for a given analysis.

Technique: Protocol Précis Construction
A Phase III, multicentre, randomised, double‑blind, placebo‑controlled trial evaluates Drug X 10 mg once daily versus placebo in 400 adults with moderate asthma (FEV₁ 60‑80 % predicted). Primary endpoint: change in pre‑bronchodilator FEV₁ at week 12. Key secondary: ACQ‑5 score change at week 12, exacerbation rate over 52 weeks. Sample size: 90 % power, α = 0.05, expected difference 150 mL, SD = 300 mL, 10 % dropout. Safety: DSMB reviews SAEs quarterly; stopping rule if SAE rate > 3 % in active arm. Write a 120‑word précis preserving all quantitative and design elements.
Phase III, multicentre, randomised, double‑blind, placebo‑controlled trial of Drug X 10 mg daily vs placebo in 400 adults (FEV₁ 60‑80 % predicted). Primary endpoint: pre‑bronchodilator FEV₁ change at week 12. Key secondary: ACQ‑5 change at week 12; exacerbation rate over 52 weeks. Sample size: 90 % power, two‑sided α = 0.05, Δ = 150 mL, SD = 300 mL, 10 % attrition. Safety: DSMB quarterly SAE review; stopping if active‑arm SAE rate > 3 %. NCT XXXXXX, protocol v2.1 dated 15 Mar 2024.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a protocol précis is required, first underline every numeral, unit, and statistical term. Then map each to the ICH‑E6 sections (objectives, endpoints, sample size, safety). Draft the précis by copying those mapped items in order; this guarantees no quantitative detail is omitted while keeping word count low.

Précis of Financial Audit Reports

An audit report prepared under ISA 700 (or its national equivalents) contains the auditor’s opinion, basis for opinion, key audit matters (KAMs), other information, responsibilities, and the auditor’s signature. The précis must state the opinion type verbatim — unmodified, qualified, adverse, or disclaimer — because the classification determines the entity’s credibility. The basis for opinion is condensed to the materiality threshold (e.g., “overall materiality set at 5 % of profit before tax, ₹12 cr”) and the audit approach (risk‑based, substantive procedures on revenue recognition, inventory valuation). Each KAM is reproduced with the matter description, the related financial statement line items, and the auditor’s response (e.g., “KAM 1: Revenue recognition for long‑term contracts — tested contract‑by‑contract against IFRS 15 criteria; no material misstatement identified”). The “Other Information” paragraph is noted only if a material inconsistency is reported. The responsibilities section is reduced to the division: management prepares financial statements; auditor expresses opinion. The report date, auditor’s firm, engagement partner, and the jurisdiction of the audit (e.g., “India, Companies Act 2013”) are retained because they affect legal enforceability. If the audit includes an emphasis‑of‑matter paragraph (e.g., going‑concern uncertainty), the précis records the exact wording of the matter and the auditor’s conclusion that the financial statements are prepared on a going‑concern basis. The final line cites the auditing standards applied (ISA 700, SA 700) and the reporting framework (Ind AS, IFRS).

Condensing Urban Planning Documents

Urban planning documents — master plans, zoning ordinances, development control regulations, and environmental impact assessments — share a regulatory hierarchy: vision, land‑use classification, development standards, infrastructure provision, phasing, and monitoring. A précis captures the planning horizon (e.g., “2025‑2045”), the statutory authority (e.g., “under the Town and Country Planning Act 1971”), and the spatial extent (municipal boundary, planning area in hectares). Land‑use categories are summarised in a table‑style list: “Residential = 45 % (R1‑R4), Commercial = 12 % (C1‑C3), Industrial = 8 % (I1‑I2), Green = 20 %, Transport = 10 %, Public‑utility = 5 %”. Development standards — floor‑area‑ratio (FAR), ground‑coverage, setback, height limits — are recorded per zone (e.g., “R2: FAR 2.5, coverage 40 %, setback 6 m, max height 15 m”). Infrastructure provisions are reduced to capital‑expenditure totals and phasing milestones (e.g., “Water supply ₹1,200 cr by 2030; Metro corridor Phase 1 operational 2028”). Environmental mitigation measures (e.g., “storm‑water retention ponds 5 ha, tree‑plantation 200 ha”) are retained because they condition approvals. The monitoring framework — key performance indicators (population density, jobs‑housing balance, air‑quality index) and review cycle (every five years) — closes the précis. Any statutory public‑consultation outcomes (e.g., “3,200 objections received; 12 % upheld”) are noted if they altered the final plan.

Précis of Cybersecurity Incident Reports

Incident reports aligned with NIST SP 800‑61 Rev. 2 and MITRE ATT&CK follow the lifecycle: preparation, detection & analysis, containment, eradication, recovery, and post‑incident activity. The précis opens with the incident identifier, date‑time (UTC), affected assets (IP ranges, hostnames, cloud accounts), and the initial detection vector (e.g., “SIEM alert on T1059.001 PowerShell execution”). The analysis section records the threat actor attribution (if known), tactics, techniques, and procedures (TTPs) mapped to ATT&CK IDs (e.g., “Initial Access T1190 Exploit Public‑Facing Application; Privilege Escalation T1068 Exploitation for Privilege Escalation”). Indicators of compromise (IOCs) — file hashes, malicious domains, C2 IPs — are listed in a compact block. Containment actions are described with timestamps and scope (e.g., “Network segmentation applied at 2024‑03‑12 03:15 UTC; compromised VLAN isolated”). Eradication steps include malware removal, credential rotation, and patch deployment (e.g., “CVE‑2023‑12345 patched on all Windows servers”). Recovery verification cites restored services, data integrity checks (hash comparison), and monitoring enhancements (new detection rules). The post‑incident activity captures root‑cause analysis, lessons learned, and updates to the incident‑response plan (e.g., “Added automated quarantine for T1059.001”). The report’s classification (e.g., “TLP:AMBER”) and distribution list are retained because they govern information sharing.

  • Omitting the standard’s status (current, withdrawn, superseded) in a technical‑standard précis, leading to citation of an obsolete version.
  • Collapsing distinct clinical‑trial arms into a single description, losing dose‑comparison information.
  • Failing to quote the exact audit opinion wording (unmodified, qualified, adverse, disclaimer) in a financial‑audit précis.
  • Dropping quantitative land‑use percentages or FAR values in an urban‑plan précis, making the summary unusable for compliance checks.
  • Replacing specific ATT&CK technique IDs with generic descriptions in a cybersecurity incident précis, erasing traceability.
  • Neglecting the protocol version number and amendment date in a clinical‑trial précis, creating ambiguity about the operative document.
  • Summarising key audit matters without linking each matter to the specific financial‑statement line item and auditor response.

Précis of Legislative Debates and Hansard Records

The précis identifies the legislative body, session, date, and bill or motion. Speakers are grouped by party only when partisan alignment is essential; otherwise, positions are attributed to the office held (e.g., “Minister of Finance”). The summary follows the procedural sequence: first reading, second reading, committee stage, report stage, and third reading. For each stage, it records principal arguments, amendments moved, voting outcomes, and divisions. Rhetorical flourishes, interruptions, and points of order are omitted unless they alter the legislative trajectory. Quantitative data (speakers, time allocated, division margins) are tabulated when they illuminate political weight. The final paragraph notes legislative status (passed, rejected, referred) and key dates (royal assent, commencement). Throughout, official terminology (e.g., “clause 12(3)”) is preserved for statute-book reference.

Summarizing Patent Claims and Specifications

A patent document comprises a title, abstract, background, detailed description, drawings, and claims defining the legal scope. The précis distinguishes descriptive portions from the enforceable claims. The opening line cites the patent number, filing date, priority date, jurisdiction (e.g., “US 10,123,456 B2” — illustrative), and IPC codes. The background is condensed to one sentence capturing the technical field and prior-art deficiency. The detailed description is reduced to essential embodiments highlighting novel features. Each independent claim is reproduced in full or paraphrased with exact language for limiting elements (e.g., “a processor configured to execute instructions stored in a non‑volatile memory”). Dependent claims are summarised by their additional limitations. Drawings are referenced by figure numbers illustrating claimed features. The précis concludes with legal status (granted, pending, expired) and any known litigation or licensing affecting commercial relevance.

Worked Example: Patent Claim Précis
Given the following independent claim: “A method for secure data transmission comprising: generating a session key using a quantum‑resistant key‑exchange algorithm; encrypting payload data with the session key; and transmitting the encrypted payload over a public network.” Produce a précis entry for this claim.
Patent US XXXXXXXX B2 (filed DD Mon YYYY, priority DD Mon YYYY) – IPC H04L 9/00. Independent claim 1: method for secure data transmission comprising (i) generation of a session key via a quantum‑resistant key‑exchange algorithm, (ii) encryption of payload data with said session key, (iii) transmission of the encrypted payload over a public network. Dependent claims 2‑4 add (2) lattice‑based algorithm, (3) forward‑secrecy key rotation every 24 hours, (4) authentication via hash‑based message authentication code. Status: granted, no known litigation.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a patent précis appears, first locate the claim numbers; copy independent claim language verbatim for the limiting steps. Use a two‑column note: left column for claim elements, right column for corresponding specification paragraphs. This prevents omission of essential limitations under word‑count pressure.

Condensing International Sanctions Regimes

Sanctions regimes — whether from the UN Security Council, EU, US OFAC, or regional bodies — share a common architecture: legal basis, designated persons/entities, prohibited activities, licensing exceptions, and reporting obligations. A précis opens with the authorising instrument (e.g., “UNSC Resolution XXXX (2023)”, “EU Council Regulation (EU) YYYY/ZZZZ”), adoption date, and geographic or thematic scope. The designated list is summarised by category (individuals, entities, vessels, aircraft) with entry counts and latest amendment date where feasible. Prohibited measures are enumerated: asset freezes, travel bans, arms embargoes, sectoral restrictions (e.g., “prohibition on export of dual‑use goods to the energy sector”). Licensing or humanitarian exemptions are captured verbatim when they contain quantitative thresholds (e.g., “transactions up to USD 10,000 per quarter for medical supplies”). Reporting requirements — frequency, competent authority, format — are noted for compliance monitoring. The précis closes with current status (in force, amended, terminated) and any mandated review dates.

Précis of Military Operational Orders

Military operational orders (OPORDs) follow a five‑paragraph format: Situation, Mission, Execution, Sustainment, and Command & Signal. The précis mirrors this structure while stripping boilerplate. Situation is reduced to the operational environment (terrain, weather, enemy disposition, friendly forces) and higher‑headquarters intent. The Mission statement — who, what, when, where, why — is quoted verbatim as the commander’s intent. Execution is summarised by phase (shaping, decisive, sustaining), each with main effort, supporting tasks, and key control measures (phase lines, objectives, fire‑support coordination). Sustainment captures logistics priorities (supply classes, medevac routes, maintenance windows) and constraints (e.g., “fuel resupply limited to 48 hours”). Command & Signal records hierarchy, succession, and primary/alternate communication nets with call signs and encryption standards. The précis retains all operational graphics references (e.g., “Annex A – Overlay 1:2,000”) and any rules of engagement or law‑of‑armed‑conflict caveats limiting force.

Summarizing Public Health Surveillance Reports

Public health surveillance reports — weekly bulletins, outbreak investigations, or vaccine‑effectiveness studies — require a précis preserving epidemiological parameters while discarding narrative redundancy. The opening line states the reporting authority (e.g., “WHO”, “CDC”), reporting period, and disease or health event. Core metrics are presented in a compact table: incidence rate (per 100,000), prevalence, case‑fatality ratio, reproduction number (Rₜ) with confidence interval, and vaccination coverage by target group. Methodological notes — case definition, laboratory confirmation criteria, data sources (hospital notifications, laboratory networks, syndromic surveillance) — are retained for comparability. Outbreak investigations are condensed to index case, transmission chain length, risk factors, and control measures (isolation, contact tracing, vaccination campaigns). Trend analysis states direction (increasing, decreasing, stable) and statistical test used (e.g., “joinpoint regression, p < 0.05”). The précis ends with public health recommendations, travel advisories, and the next scheduled report date.

  • Confusing the legislative stage (second reading vs. committee stage) and attributing arguments to the wrong phase.
  • Omitting the exact claim language of independent patent claims, thereby losing the legal scope.
  • Failing to cite the authorising resolution number and date for sanctions regimes, making the précis unverifiable.
  • Dropping the mission statement from an operational order, which is the single most examinable element.
  • Summarising surveillance metrics without confidence intervals or case definitions, rendering the data non‑comparable.
  • Mixing procedural boilerplate (e.g., “the chair called the meeting to order”) with substantive content in any précis.
  • Neglecting to note the current legal status (in force, amended, expired) of the source instrument.

These advanced précis types demand precise structural mapping and verbatim retention of legally or operationally binding language.

Précis of International Climate Agreements: Preserving Mitigation Commitments, Finance Mechanisms, and Review Cycles

International climate agreements such as the 2015 Paris Agreement, the 1997 Kyoto Protocol, and subsequent Conference of the Parties (COP) decisions share a layered architecture: a long‑term temperature goal, nationally determined contributions (NDCs), a transparency framework, a global stocktake cycle, and financial mechanisms including the Green Climate Fund, the Adaptation Fund, and the Loss and Damage fund established at COP27. A précis opens with the treaty name, adoption and entry‑into‑force dates, then states the collective temperature objective (e.g., “well below 2 °C, pursuing efforts to limit to 1.5 °C”). The NDC synthesis is captured by grouping parties — developed, developing, least‑developed — and noting the aggregate mitigation gap from the latest synthesis report. The transparency framework is summarised by reporting frequency (biennial transparency reports), modalities (common tabular formats, technical expert review), and the compliance committee’s facilitative role. The global stocktake occurs every five years; the précis records the most recent stocktake year, its key findings on mitigation, adaptation, and means of implementation, and the resulting political guidance. Financial commitments are quantified: the $100 billion per year goal by 2020, the new collective quantified goal post‑2025, and allocation ratios for mitigation versus adaptation. The précis closes with the current legal status (in force, operationalized by the Katowice Rulebook, supplemented by the Glasgow Climate Pact) and any mandated review dates for the long‑term goal.

When condensing, retain the exact language of the temperature goal and NDC submission deadlines as legally binding reference points. Omit narrative preambles and recurring diplomatic courtesies. Use a two‑column note: left column for each article or decision paragraph, right column for the précis element it feeds. This prevents omission of conditional language such as “in the context of sustainable development” that qualifies mitigation obligations.

Précis of National Budget Statements: Retaining Fiscal Deficit Targets, Revenue Assumptions, and Expenditure Priorities

National budget statements — whether presented as a Union Budget, a Federal Budget, or a Fiscal Strategy Report — follow a predictable fiscal architecture: macroeconomic assumptions (real GDP growth, nominal GDP growth, inflation, current account balance), fiscal aggregates (revenue receipts, capital receipts, total expenditure, fiscal deficit, primary deficit, revenue deficit), sectoral allocation tables, and a medium‑term fiscal policy statement often anchored in fiscal responsibility legislation. A précis opens with the fiscal year, the presenting authority, and the legislative basis (e.g., “Fiscal Responsibility and Budget Management Act, 2003”). Macroeconomic assumptions are reproduced verbatim when they contain quantitative thresholds that trigger fiscal rules (e.g., “nominal GDP growth ≥ 6 %”). The fiscal deficit target is quoted with its percentage of GDP and any escape clauses. Revenue receipts are broken into tax revenue (direct, indirect) and non‑tax revenue, with buoyancy assumptions noted. Expenditure is split into revenue expenditure (interest payments, subsidies, pensions) and capital expenditure (infrastructure, defence, social capital), highlighting the capital‑to‑revenue ratio. The medium‑term framework is summarised by the projected deficit trajectory over the next three years and the debt‑to‑GDP anchor. The précis closes with the budget status (passed, assented, implemented) and any supplementary demands for grants.

Critical to preservation are conditional deficit ceilings, the exact interest‑payment share of revenue expenditure, and the definitions of “capital expenditure” used for the golden rule. Boilerplate such as “the Finance Minister rose to present” is discarded. A three‑column worksheet — assumption, figure, source paragraph — ensures every number in the précis can be traced to the original statement.

Technique: Extracting Conditional Fiscal Targets
A budget statement reads: "The fiscal deficit shall not exceed 3.5% of GDP in FY2025, provided that nominal GDP growth remains above 6%; otherwise the ceiling reverts to 4.0%." Write a précis sentence that preserves the conditionality and the quantitative thresholds.
The government targets a fiscal deficit ceiling of 3.5% of GDP for FY2025, conditional on nominal GDP growth exceeding 6%; if growth falls short, the ceiling automatically rises to 4.0%.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a source text embeds conditional thresholds, underline the trigger and the alternate value. In the précis, use a single clause with “conditional on” or “provided that” to keep the word count low while retaining both numbers.

Précis of Multilateral Development Bank Project Appraisal Documents: Capturing Economic Rate of Return, Safeguard Categories, and Conditionalities

Project appraisal documents (PADs) from institutions such as the World Bank, the Asian Development Bank, the African Development Bank, and the Inter‑American Development Bank follow a standardized structure: project development objective, components and costs, economic and financial analysis, safeguard screening, institutional arrangements, and legal covenants. A précis begins with the project name, loan/credit number, approval date, and total financing envelope. The development objective is quoted verbatim because it defines the results framework. The economic analysis is condensed to the economic rate of return (ERR) or net present value (NPV) with the discount rate used, key benefit streams (e.g., time savings, increased agricultural yield), and sensitivity thresholds (e.g., “ERR remains above 12 % if construction cost overruns ≤ 15 %”). Safeguard categories — Category A (high risk), B (moderate), C (low) — are listed with the specific environmental and social standards triggered (e.g., “OP 4.01 Environmental Assessment”, “OP 4.12 Involuntary Resettlement”). The résumé of the Resettlement Action Plan or Indigenous Peoples Plan is limited to the number of affected households, compensation framework, and grievance redress mechanism. Conditionalities are captured as prior actions (e.g., “enactment of the Water Tariff Regulation before effectiveness”), legal covenants (financial management, procurement, audit), and results‑based disbursement triggers. The précis closes with the implementation schedule, the closing date, and the supervising unit.

Preserve the exact ERR figure, the discount rate, and the safeguard category because they determine project eligibility and monitoring intensity. Omit repetitive background on country strategy and sector context. Use a matrix mapping each appraisal section to a précis element; this guarantees that no conditional prior action is dropped under word‑count pressure.

Précis of Corporate ESG Disclosure Reports: Integrating Materiality Matrices, Scope 3 Emissions, and Governance Indicators

Corporate Environmental, Social, and Governance (ESG) reports aligned with the Global Reporting Initiative (GRI), the Sustainability Accounting Standards Board (SASB), and the Task Force on Climate‑related Financial Disclosures (TCFD) contain a recurring architecture: a materiality assessment, strategy and risk management, metrics and targets, and governance oversight. A précis opens with the reporting entity, reporting period, and the frameworks applied. The materiality matrix is summarised by listing the top five material topics for the company and its stakeholders, noting the double‑materiality perspective where disclosed. Climate metrics are reduced to Scope 1, Scope 2, and Scope 3 greenhouse‑gas emissions (in tonnes CO₂e), the base year, the reduction target (e.g., “net‑zero by 2050, 45 % reduction by 2030”), and the verification status (third‑party assured, limited assurance). Social metrics include workforce diversity ratios, lost‑time injury frequency rate, and community investment figures. Governance indicators cover board composition (independent directors, gender diversity), executive remuneration linked to ESG KPIs, and the existence of a board‑level sustainability committee. The précis closes with the assurance provider’s opinion and any significant restatements from the prior year.

Retain the exact Scope 3 categories reported (e.g., “Category 1: Purchased Goods and Services, Category 11: Use of Sold Products”) because they define the boundary of the company’s carbon accountability. Discard narrative case studies and promotional language. A two‑pass extraction — first pass for quantitative metrics, second pass for qualitative governance structure — ensures that both numbers and policy commitments survive compression.

Précis of Disaster Risk Reduction Frameworks: Preserving Hazard Mapping, Early Warning Protocols, and Institutional Coordination Mechanisms

National and sub‑national disaster risk reduction (DRR) frameworks aligned with the 2015 Sendai Framework for Disaster Risk Reduction typically comprise a risk assessment, a prevention and mitigation plan, a preparedness and early‑warning system, a response coordination structure, and a recovery and build‑back‑better strategy. A précis starts with the jurisdiction, the legal instrument (e.g., “National Disaster Management Act, 2005”), and the planning horizon. The risk assessment is condensed to the primary hazards (seismic, flood, cyclone, drought), probabilistic hazard maps (return periods, peak ground acceleration, flood depth grids), and exposed population and asset values. Early‑warning protocols are captured by the lead agency, dissemination channels (SMS, sirens, community radio), lead‑time targets (e.g., “≥ 30 minutes for flash floods”), and standard operating procedures for evacuation orders. Institutional coordination is summarised through the incident command system tiers (national, state, district), nodal ministries, memoranda of understanding with armed forces and NGOs, and resource‑mobilisation triggers (e.g., “activation of the National Disaster Response Force when casualties exceed 100”). The recovery section notes the damage‑assessment methodology, reconstruction standards (build‑back‑better criteria), and financing windows (contingency fund, insurance, international aid). The précis ends with the review cycle (typically biennial) and the last audit date.

Critical to retain are the quantitative hazard thresholds, early‑warning lead‑time commitments, and the legal authority for compulsory evacuation. Narrative descriptions of past events are omitted unless they justify a specific mitigation measure. A hazard‑by‑hazard table in the working notes — hazard, probability, impact, mitigation action — enables rapid synthesis without losing the granularity examiners expect.

  • Dropping conditional language in climate finance commitments, turning a “mobilize” pledge into a firm guarantee.
  • Omitting the escape clause in a fiscal deficit target, presenting a single figure as absolute.
  • Summarising a project’s ERR without the discount rate or sensitivity range, making the return non‑comparable.
  • Reporting only Scope 1 and Scope 2 emissions while ignoring disclosed Scope 3 categories, understating the carbon footprint.
  • Failing to cite the specific Sendai Framework priority (e.g., Priority 1: Understanding disaster risk) that a national plan addresses.
  • Conflating the early‑warning lead‑time target with the actual achieved lead time, obscuring performance gaps.
  • Neglecting to note the verification status of ESG data, leaving the reader unable to assess reliability.

Précis of International Humanitarian Law Instruments: Preserving Core Principles, Derogations, and Monitoring Mechanisms

International humanitarian law (IHL) instruments — principally the four Geneva Conventions of 1949 and their Additional Protocols of 1977 and 2005 — share a stable architecture: definitions of protected persons, rules on conduct of hostilities, provisions for the protection of the wounded, sick, shipwrecked, and prisoners of war, and mechanisms for implementation. A précis of any such treaty must open with the formal title, date of adoption, entry‑into‑force date, and the number of state parties as of the reference date. The core principles — distinction, proportionality, precaution, and the prohibition of unnecessary suffering — are stated once, using the treaty’s own terminology (e.g., “civilian objects” rather than “non‑military targets”). Derogation clauses are critical: such provisions typically permit derogation only in respect of certain articles and only when a state formally notifies the depositary; the précis must record the exact scope of permissible derogation and the notification requirement. Monitoring mechanisms include the International Fact‑Finding Commission (established under Article 90 of Additional Protocol I), the Protecting Power system, and the role of the International Committee of the Red Cross (ICRC) as a humanitarian monitor. The précis should note whether the instrument provides for a compulsory dispute‑settlement procedure (e.g., a conciliation procedure) and any reporting obligations (e.g., periodic reports under a specific convention). Quantitative data such as the number of states that have accepted the competence of the Fact‑Finding Commission, or the list of reservations entered at ratification, must be retained because they define the operational reach of the treaty. Narrative preambles, historical recitals, and promotional language about “the spirit of humanity” are omitted. A two‑pass extraction — first pass for normative provisions, second pass for institutional and procedural details — ensures that both substantive rules and compliance architecture survive compression.

Summarizing Algorithmic Decision‑Making Documentation: Model Cards, Bias Audits, and Explainability Statements

Documentation accompanying machine‑learning models — often called model cards, system cards, or transparency reports — follows a de‑facto standard popularised by the 2019 “Model Cards for Model Reporting” paper and subsequently reflected in practices by major technology firms and the EU AI Act’s technical documentation requirements. A précis of such documentation must capture the intended use cases, the training data provenance (datasets, collection dates, licensing, known biases), the model architecture (type, number of parameters, training compute), and the performance metrics (accuracy, F1, AUC) disaggregated by relevant demographic or contextual subgroups. Bias audit results are summarised by stating the protected attributes examined (e.g., race, gender, age), the disparity metric used (demographic parity difference, equalised odds gap), the threshold for concern, and the mitigation steps taken (re‑weighting, adversarial debiasing, post‑processing). Explainability statements are reduced to the explanation method (SHAP values, LIME, counterfactual examples), the scope (global vs. local), and any user‑facing transparency artefacts (feature importance dashboards, decision‑reason codes). Regulatory compliance flags — such as whether the system falls under “high‑risk” classification in Annex III of the EU AI Act, the conformity assessment route (self‑assessment vs. third‑party), and the presence of a post‑market monitoring plan — are retained verbatim where they affect legal obligations. Quantitative uncertainty intervals (e.g., 95 % confidence intervals for performance metrics) and the versioning scheme (semantic versioning, git commit hash) are kept because they enable reproducibility. Marketing language (“state‑of‑the‑art”, “unprecedented accuracy”) and extended literature reviews are discarded. A structured extraction template — use case, data, model, metrics, bias, explainability, compliance, version — allows the précis writer to map each paragraph of the source to a fixed slot, preventing omission of mandatory fields.

Technique: Identifying Derogation Clauses in IHL Treaties
A précis of Additional Protocol I (1977) must reflect the derogation regime. Article 75 of Additional Protocol I outlines a derogation regime, stating that a High Contracting Party may derogate from its provisions (fundamental guarantees) only in time of armed conflict and only to the extent strictly required by the exigencies of the situation, provided that such derogation is not inconsistent with its other obligations under international law and is notified to the depositary. Write a single sentence for the précis that preserves the legal limits, the notification requirement, and the non‑derogable core.
Additional Protocol I permits derogation from Article 75 solely during armed conflict, strictly required by the situation, provided it does not conflict with other international obligations and is formally notified to the depositary.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a précis prompt asks for “the legal framework” of a treaty or technical standard, allocate the first 30 seconds to locate the article numbers that define scope, derogation, and monitoring. Write those article numbers in the margin; they become the skeleton of your summary and guarantee you do not miss a mandatory procedural element.

Précis of Multilateral Environmental Agreements: Retaining Obligations, Compliance Committees, and Financial Mechanisms

Multilateral environmental agreements (MEAs) such as the Convention on Biological Diversity (1992), the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (1992), the Basel Convention (1989), and the Minamata Convention on Mercury (2013) share a modular structure: a preamble, definitions, substantive obligations (often differentiated between developed and developing parties), a compliance mechanism, a financial mechanism, and institutional arrangements (Conference of the Parties, subsidiary bodies, secretariat). A précis must open with the full title, adoption date, entry‑into‑force date, and current party count.
