Substantive obligations are summarised by listing the core commitments — e.g., national biodiversity strategies and action plans under CBD Article 6, nationally determined contributions under UNFCCC Article 4, prior informed consent procedures under Basel Article 6 — and noting any common but differentiated responsibilities (CBDR) language. The compliance mechanism is captured by naming the committee (e.g., the Compliance Committee under the Kyoto Protocol, the Implementation and Compliance Committee under the Minamata Convention), its composition (expert members elected by the COP), its mandate (facilitative vs. enforcement), and the trigger for its activation (party submission, secretariat referral, or automatic review). Financial mechanisms are reduced to the designated entity (Global Environment Facility, Green Climate Fund, Multilateral Fund for the Montreal Protocol), the replenishment cycle, the eligibility criteria, and any co‑financing requirements. Reporting obligations — frequency, format (national reports, biennial update reports), and review process (technical expert review, facilitative dialogue) — are retained because they drive transparency. Reservations, declarations, and opt‑out clauses (e.g., provisions allowing parties to exclude certain genetic resources) are recorded verbatim. Narrative preambles, aspirational language (“recognising the intrinsic value of biodiversity”), and exhaustive lists of annexes are omitted unless an annex contains quantitative targets (e.g., the 2020 Aichi Targets). A three‑column working table — obligation, compliance tool, financial link — enables rapid synthesis without losing the interplay between legal duty and implementation support.

Condensing Central Bank Stress‑Test Reports: Scenario Design, Capital Adequacy Thresholds, and Macro‑Prudential Recommendations

Central bank stress‑test publications — such as the Federal Reserve’s Comprehensive Capital Analysis and Review (CCAR), the European Central Bank’s EU‑wide stress test, and the Bank of England’s Annual Cyclical Scenario — follow a comparable analytical framework: baseline and adverse macroeconomic scenarios, bank‑level projection models, capital ratio thresholds, and supervisory actions. A précis begins with the exercising authority, the reporting period, the number of participating institutions, and the scenario horizon (typically three years). Scenario design is summarised by listing the key macro variables (GDP growth, unemployment, interest rates, asset price shocks) for both baseline and adverse paths, and noting any narrative shocks (e.g., “sudden stop in capital flows”, “sharp housing market correction”). The projection methodology is captured in a single sentence: “Banks’ internal models, constrained by supervisory templates, project revenues, losses, and risk‑weighted assets under each scenario.” Capital adequacy thresholds are recorded precisely — Common Equity Tier 1 (CET1) ratio minimum (e.g., 4.5 % under Basel III, 5.5 % for G‑SIBs), leverage ratio floor, and any jurisdiction‑specific buffers (countercyclical capital buffer, systemic risk buffer). Results are condensed to the aggregate capital shortfall (in local currency), the number of banks falling below the threshold, and the distribution of projected CET1 ratios (median, interquartile range). Macro‑prudential recommendations — such as “increase countercyclical buffer by 25 basis points”, “restrict dividend distributions for banks with projected CET1 below 7 %”, or “enhance liquidity coverage ratio monitoring” — are retained verbatim because they constitute the policy output. Qualitative commentary on model uncertainty, data limitations, and scenario plausibility is omitted unless the report explicitly flags a material caveat (e.g., a specific type of systemic shock not incorporated in the scenario). A two‑pass extraction — first pass for quantitative tables, second pass for supervisory narrative — ensures that both numbers and policy directives survive compression.

Précis of Digital Platform Governance Policies: Content Moderation Rules, Transparency Reports, and Appeal Mechanisms

Governance documentation released by large digital platforms — community standards, content‑moderation playbooks, transparency reports, and appeal‑process charters — has converged on a quasi‑regulatory template influenced by the EU Digital Services Act (DSA), the US Section 230 jurisprudence, and the Global Network Initiative principles. A précis must identify the platform, the document version, the effective date, and the jurisdictional scope (global vs. regional). Content‑moderation rules are summarised by enumerating the prohibited content categories (terrorist content, child sexual abuse material, hate speech, disinformation, intellectual‑property infringement), the legal basis for each (statute, court order, voluntary policy), and the enforcement tier (automated detection, human review, trusted‑flagger escalation). Quantitative enforcement metrics — volume of content actioned, proportion removed pre‑emptively vs. post‑user‑report, median time‑to‑action — are retained with the reporting period. Transparency reports are reduced to the reporting frequency (quarterly, semi‑annual), the data granularity (country‑level, language‑level), and any third‑party audit or verification statement. Appeal mechanisms are captured by describing the internal review stages (automated re‑review, specialist team, independent oversight board), the time limits for each stage, the evidentiary standard (preponderance of evidence), and the final external recourse (court, arbitration, regulatory complaint). Procedural safeguards — notice‑and‑action timelines, proportionality test, right to be heard, data‑portability of moderation decisions — are noted because they constitute due‑process guarantees under the DSA. Marketing narratives (“we empower free expression”), anecdotal case studies, and exhaustive lists of edge‑case examples are discarded. A structured matrix — category, legal basis, detection method, appeal path, metric — allows the précis writer to map each policy paragraph to a fixed cell, ensuring completeness under tight word limits.

  • Confusing a treaty’s derogation clause with a reservation, thereby misstating the legal effect of a state’s unilateral statement.
  • Omitting the notification requirement for derogations, which renders the summary legally incomplete.
  • Reporting model‑card performance metrics without subgroup disaggregation, masking bias that the examiner expects to see highlighted.
  • Failing to distinguish between facilitative and enforcement‑type compliance committees in MEAs, leading to an inaccurate picture of treaty effectiveness.
  • Dropping the countercyclical capital buffer value from a stress‑test précis, which removes a key macro‑prudential lever.
  • Summarising platform transparency data without specifying the reporting period or verification status, making the figures unverifiable.
  • Neglecting to record the appeal‑stage time limits in digital‑governance précis, which are essential for assessing procedural fairness.

Précis of International Court of Justice Advisory Opinions and Judgments: Preserving Jurisdictional Reasoning, Provisional Measures, and Dissenting Opinions

International Court of Justice (ICJ) outputs — contentious-case judgments and advisory opinions — follow a rigid internal architecture that a précis must replicate without distortion. The opening segment identifies the parties (for contentious cases) or the requesting organ (for advisory proceedings), the date of the application or request, the basis of jurisdiction (compromissory clause, special agreement, optional-clause declaration under Article 36(2) of the Statute, or forum prorogatum), and the subject-matter of the dispute. Jurisdictional reasoning, often dispersed across preliminary objections and the merits phase, is condensed into a single paragraph that records the Court’s finding on each objection (lack of jurisdiction, inadmissibility, non-justiciability) and the legal standard applied (e.g., the “manifest lack of jurisdiction” threshold for preliminary objections under Article 79 of the Rules of Court). Provisional measures orders, issued under Article 41 of the Statute, are summarised by stating the legal standard (“plausibility of rights” and “risk of irreparable prejudice”), the specific measures indicated, and whether the order was unanimous or divided. The merits reasoning is mapped claim-by-claim: for each legal question, the précis records the applicable rule (treaty, customary international law, general principle), the Court’s interpretation, the facts subsumed, and the conclusion. Reparations paragraphs capture the forms of reparation ordered (restitution, compensation, satisfaction, guarantees of non-repetition) and any quantified amounts or valuation methodologies. Dissenting and separate opinions are not omitted; each is represented by the judge’s name, the core doctrinal disagreement (jurisdiction, interpretation, factual appraisal, or remedy), and the operative paragraph where the divergence affects the dispositif. The final dispositif — the Court’s formal decision paragraphs — is reproduced verbatim or in near-verbatim form because it constitutes the binding legal output. A two-column extraction table (issue / Court’s holding / key precedent cited / dissent summary) ensures that no operative paragraph is lost during compression.

Summarizing Constitutional Court Reasoning on Rights Adjudication: Proportionality Analysis, Balancing Tests, and Margin of Appreciation

Constitutional courts across jurisdictions — the German Bundesverfassungsgericht, the South African Constitutional Court, the Indian Supreme Court, the Canadian Supreme Court, the European Court of Human Rights (ECtHR) sitting as a constitutional-style tribunal — employ a recognisable family of structured reasoning techniques when adjudicating rights claims. A précis of such a judgment must first identify the right engaged (e.g., Article 21 of the Indian Constitution, Section 7 of the Canadian Charter, Article 8 ECHR), the limitation measure (statute, executive action, common-law rule), and the standard of review the court articulates. The proportionality analysis, the dominant global template, is broken into its four sub-steps: (1) legitimate aim — the précis states the aim the court accepts (national security, public morality, rights of others) and whether the aim is pressed by the state or recognised ex officio; (2) rational connection — the court’s assessment of whether the measure is capable of advancing the aim, including any empirical evidence cited or rejected; (3) necessity / minimal impairment — the court’s survey of less restrictive alternatives, the evidence considered, and the conclusion on whether the measure goes further than necessary; (4) balancing / proportionality stricto sensu — the court’s weighing of the severity of the intrusion against the importance of the aim, often expressed as a “fair balance” or “justifiable in a free and democratic society” determination. Where the court employs a margin of appreciation (ECtHR) or a deference doctrine (Canadian “Oakes test” stage 1, Indian “reasonable restriction” jurisprudence), the précis records the width of the margin granted (wide for moral policy, narrow for core political speech) and the reasoning for that calibration. Dissenting opinions frequently contest the margin width or the empirical basis of the necessity step; these are captured by naming the dissenting judge, the specific sub-step contested, and the alternative outcome urged. Remedial orders — reading down, reading in, severance, suspended declaration of invalidity, structural interdict — are summarised with the temporal scope and any supervisory mechanism the court retains. A four-row template (right / limitation / proportionality sub-steps / remedy / dissent) allows the précis writer to compress a 150-page judgment into 250 words without losing doctrinal architecture.

Worked Example: Compressing a Proportionality Judgment
A constitutional court upholds a statute requiring prior licensing for public assemblies. The court finds the aim (public order) legitimate, the licensing scheme rationally connected, but strikes down the 30-day advance-notice requirement as failing minimal impairment because a 48-hour notice would suffice. The majority grants a 12-month suspended declaration; two judges dissent, arguing the entire scheme fails rational connection. Write a 120-word précis preserving the doctrinal structure.
The Court upheld the Public Assemblies Act’s licensing framework under the constitutional right to peaceful assembly (Art. 19(1)(b)). Applying the four-step proportionality test: (1) Legitimate aim — public order maintenance accepted. (2) Rational connection — licensing scheme held capable of advancing order. (3) Minimal impairment — 30-day advance-notice requirement struck down; evidence showed 48-hour notice sufficient, rendering the longer period disproportionate. (4) Balancing — remaining provisions upheld as striking a fair balance. Remedy: suspended declaration of invalidity for 12 months to allow legislative correction. Dissent (Judges X, Y): entire scheme fails rational connection; no empirical basis for licensing per se. The decision narrows state regulatory space while preserving legislative objective.
Exam-Day Strategy
When a comprehension passage contains a constitutional judgment excerpt, first locate the four proportionality sub-steps in the text — they are often signposted by phrases such as “legitimate aim”, “rational connection”, “less restrictive means”, “fair balance”. Underline each. The précis must reflect the sequence; reordering them signals misunderstanding of the doctrinal method. If the passage omits a sub-step, note the gap rather than inventing it.

Précis of Monetary Policy Committee Minutes: Preserving Dissent Votes, Forward Guidance Nuances, and Policy Rationale

Minutes of monetary policy committees (MPCs) — such as the Reserve Bank of India’s MPC, the Federal Reserve’s FOMC, the Bank of England’s MPC, the ECB’s Governing Council — follow a standardised disclosure framework that a précis must map faithfully. The opening identifies the meeting date, the policy decision (policy rate change, standing facility rates, asset-purchase parameters), and the vote count (e.g., 5–2 in favour). The policy rationale section is condensed by extracting the committee’s assessment of the four macroeconomic pillars: (1) inflation trajectory — current headline and core inflation, deviation from target, near-term drivers (supply shocks, demand pressure, anchored expectations); (2) growth outlook — real GDP forecast revisions, output gap estimate, sectoral composition; (3) financial conditions — credit growth, asset-price dynamics, exchange-rate pass-through; (4) external environment — global growth, commodity prices, capital-flow volatility. Forward guidance is captured by distinguishing between (a) state-contingent guidance (“policy rate will remain accommodative as long as inflation expectations remain anchored”), (b) time-contingent guidance (“current stance appropriate for the next two quarters”), and (c) qualitative bias (“prepared to act if downside risks materialise”). Dissenting votes are recorded with the dissenter’s name, the alternative action proposed (rate hike, cut, pause, different forward guidance), and the core macroeconomic disagreement (e.g., “inflation persistence underestimated”, “output gap larger than staff projection”). The minutes often reveal the committee’s reaction function — whether it follows a Taylor-rule type formulation, a flexible inflation-targeting framework, or a dual mandate — and the précis should note which framework is referenced. Any reference to model uncertainty, alternative scenarios, or data-dependency qualifiers is retained because it conditions the credibility of the forward guidance. A structured extraction grid (pillar / assessment / forecast / dissent view) enables compression of 20-page minutes into a 200-word précis that preserves the decision’s analytical backbone.

Handling Comparative Constitutional Law Texts: Synthesizing Multiple Jurisdictions’ Approaches to Similar Doctrinal Questions

Comparative constitutional law materials — law-review symposia, judicial dialogue documents, Venice Commission opinions, textbook chapters — present multiple jurisdictions’ treatments of a single doctrinal problem (e.g., hate-speech regulation, abortion rights, emergency powers, judicial appointments). A précis of such a text must avoid serial summarisation (Country A does X, Country B does Y) and instead construct a synthetic analytical framework. The first step is to identify the common doctrinal vocabulary: the right at stake, the limitation test employed (proportionality, strict scrutiny, margin of appreciation, Wednesbury unreasonableness), and the institutional actor exercising review (constitutional court, supreme court, specialised tribunal). The second step maps convergences and divergences along three axes: (1) textual anchor — whether the right is enumerated, implied, or derived from international law; (2) standard of review — the intensity of scrutiny (strict scrutiny, intermediate, rational basis, proportionality stricto sensu); (3) remedial posture — declaratory invalidity, reading down, suspended invalidity, dialogue model (Section 33 Canadian Charter, Section 4 UK Human Rights Act). The précis then groups jurisdictions into “families” (e.g., “European proportionality family: Germany, South Africa, ECtHR, India post-Puttaswamy”; “US tiered-scrutiny family: US, Japan, Philippines”) and notes outliers. Critical methodological choices — whether the comparison is functionalist (focusing on outcomes) or formalist (focusing on doctrinal categories) — are recorded because they shape the synthesis. The précis concludes with the author’s or compiler’s normative assessment, if any (e.g., “the proportionality model better protects minority rights than tiered scrutiny”), and flags any jurisdictions where the doctrine is in flux (pending cases, constitutional amendment proposals). A matrix (jurisdiction / textual anchor / review standard / remedy / current status) supports the synthetic write-up.

Précis of Investigative Commission Reports: Preserving Findings of Fact, Recommendations, and Minority Views

Commissions of inquiry, royal commissions, parliamentary select committees, and independent investigative bodies (e.g., the Chilcot Inquiry, the Zondo Commission, the National Commission for Enterprises in the Unorganised Sector) produce reports with a distinctive tripartite structure: narrative findings of fact, legal/policy conclusions, and recommendations. A précis must preserve the evidentiary hierarchy: (1) undisputed facts — established by documentary evidence or admissions; (2) contested facts — where the commission makes credibility findings, the précis records the standard applied (balance of probabilities, beyond reasonable doubt for criminal referrals) and the key witnesses whose testimony was preferred; (3) inferences drawn from circumstantial evidence — the logical chain is summarised. Legal conclusions — whether statutory breaches, constitutional violations, or maladministration — are linked to the specific factual findings that support them. Recommendations are categorised by addressee (legislature, executive, specific agency, regulatory body), by type (legislative amendment, policy reform, institutional restructuring, individual accountability, reparations), and by implementation timeline (immediate, medium-term, structural). Minority or dissenting reports are not footnotes; they are integrated by stating the dissenting commissioner’s name, the specific findings or recommendations contested, and the alternative narrative or remedy proposed. Where the commission uses a “lessons learned” chapter distinct from recommendations, that chapter is condensed into thematic clusters (governance failure, regulatory capture, information asymmetry, inter-agency coordination). The précis omits voluminous appendices, witness lists, and procedural history unless the prompt specifically asks for the commission’s mandate and terms of reference. A three-pass extraction — facts / conclusions / recommendations — with a colour-coded tag for majority vs. minority positions ensures completeness under word limits.

  • Collapsing the four proportionality sub-steps into a single “balancing” statement, losing the doctrinal sequence examiners expect.
  • Omitting the margin-of-appreciation width or deference rationale, which is often the pivot point of the judgment.
  • Reporting a monetary policy vote count without the dissenters’ macroeconomic reasoning, reducing the précis to a headline.
  • Confusing state-contingent and time-contingent forward guidance, which signals misunderstanding of central-bank communication strategy.
  • Serialising country summaries in comparative constitutional texts instead of building a synthetic analytical framework.
  • Treating minority views in investigative reports as optional addenda rather than integral components of the commission’s output.
  • Failing to distinguish findings of fact (evidence-based) from inferences (logic-based) in commission reports, blurring the evidentiary hierarchy.

Précis of Legislative Impact Assessments: Retaining Cost‑Benefit Metrics, Distributional Effects, and Mitigation Measures

Legislative impact assessments (LIAs) are structured ex‑ante evaluations accompanying draft bills in many parliamentary systems. A précis must preserve three pillars: the quantitative cost‑benefit framework, distributional analysis across stakeholder groups, and proposed mitigation or compensatory measures. First, identify the methodological baseline — e.g., OECD Regulatory Impact Analysis (RIA), EU Better Regulation Toolbox, or national guidelines like India’s Pre‑Legislative Consultation Policy. Record the discount rate, time horizon, and valuation techniques for non‑market goods (contingent valuation, hedonic pricing, value‑of‑statistical‑life). Next, map the distributional table: quantify net benefits for households by income quintile, firms by size class, and regions by administrative unit. Preserve equity weighting or sensitivity analysis affecting aggregate net present value. Finally, capture the mitigation package: earmarked subsidies, transition funds, regulatory sandboxes, or phased implementation. Note any post‑implementation review clause and the monitoring body. Omit exhaustive stakeholder‑consultation logs unless procedural legitimacy is asked.

Précis of International Investment Arbitration Awards: Preserving Jurisdictional Findings, Fair‑and‑Equitable Treatment Standards, and Damages Methodology

Investment treaty arbitration awards (ICSID, UNCITRAL, SCC) follow a three‑stage structure: jurisdiction, merits, quantum. A précis must retain the tribunal’s jurisdictional reasoning — whether the claimant meets the “investment” definition (Salini test), nationality/territorial links, and consent‑to‑arbitration clause (treaty, contract, domestic law). Summarise the protection standard: fair‑and‑equitable treatment (FET), full protection and security, most‑favoured‑nation, or expropriation. For FET, note the doctrine used — legitimate expectations, arbitrariness, or due process — and key precedents (e.g., Tecmed, Electrabel, Philip Morris). In merits, condense the contested measures (legislative amendment, regulatory revocation, judicial decision) and the breach finding. For quantum, record the valuation method: DCF (with discount rate, terminal value), comparable transactions, or fair market value vs. equitable compensation. Preserve treatment of moral damages, interest (pre‑/post‑award, compound/simple), and contributory fault reductions. If a dissenting/concurrence exists, note the arbitrator’s name, the contested point, and alternative remedy. Exclude procedural history unless procedural fairness is asked.

Worked Précis Exercise
A 1,200‑word ICSID award finds that a host state’s revocation of a mining licence breaches the FET standard under the BIT. The tribunal applies the legitimate‑expectations test, cites Tecmed v. Mexico, and awards USD 85 million using a DCF model with a 12 % discount rate, plus 5 % simple interest from the date of breach. The dissenting arbitrator argues the claimant lacked a protected investment under the Salini test. Write a 150‑word précis preserving jurisdiction, FET reasoning, quantum, and dissent.
The tribunal upheld jurisdiction, confirming the claimant’s mining concession satisfied the Salini criteria (capital, risk, duration, contribution to host‑state development). On merits, it held the abrupt licence revocation violated the FET legitimate‑expectations standard, following Tecmed, because the investor relied on stable regulatory representations. Quantum was set at USD 85 million via a DCF valuation (12 % discount rate, 10‑year projection) plus 5 % simple interest from breach date. Dissenting arbitrator (Prof. X) contested jurisdiction, asserting the concession lacked sufficient economic risk to qualify as an investment under Salini, and would have dismissed the claim.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a précis prompt specifies a word ceiling, allocate roughly 30 % to jurisdiction, 40 % to merits/FET analysis, 20 % to quantum, and 10 % to dissent. Use the tribunal’s own headings as signposts; copy key doctrinal labels (legitimate expectations, Salini test, DCF) verbatim to avoid paraphrase drift.

Précis of Public Policy Evaluation Reports: Capturing Theory of Change, Counterfactual Evidence, and Implementation Fidelity

Policy evaluation reports (e.g., World Bank Impact Evaluations, OECD Public Governance Reviews, national audit office performance audits) center on a theory‑of‑change (ToC) logic model. A précis must first reproduce the ToC in prose: inputs, activities, outputs, outcomes, long‑term impacts, with explicit assumptions and external factors. Next, summarise the counterfactual design — RCT, regression discontinuity, difference‑in‑differences, instrumental variables, or qualitative process tracing — and key identification assumptions (parallel trends, exclusion restriction). Report primary effect sizes with confidence intervals, subgroup heterogeneity, and cost‑effectiveness ratios (e.g., cost per DALY averted, cost per additional student enrolled). Implementation fidelity is a separate pillar: capture the fidelity index (percentage of planned activities delivered), major deviations (dose reduction, contamination, staff turnover), and the evaluator’s judgment on bias direction. Conclude with policy recommendations — scale‑up, redesign, or termination — and note planned follow‑up studies. Exclude literature reviews and methodological appendices unless evidence‑gap mapping is asked.

Précis of Technical Feasibility Studies: Preserving Engineering Constraints, Risk Registers, and Decision Gates

Technical feasibility studies (TFS) for infrastructure, energy, or industrial projects follow a stage‑gate process (concept, pre‑feasibility, feasibility, front‑end engineering design). A précis must retain the engineering envelope: design capacity, technology selection criteria (e.g., supercritical vs. ultra‑supercritical boiler, PEM vs. alkaline electrolyser), and key performance parameters (efficiency, availability, ramp rate). Summarise the risk register by category — technical, environmental, regulatory, financial, social — recording for each top‑tier risk its probability, impact magnitude, and mitigation (redundancy, contractual allocation, insurance). Decision gates are pivotal: note go/no‑go criteria (NPV > 0, IRR > hurdle rate, ESG threshold, permitting milestone). Preserve CAPEX breakdown (EPC, owner’s costs, contingency) and OPEX assumptions (fuel price scenario, maintenance regime). If a sensitivity tornado diagram is included, report the three most influential variables and the NPV swing. Omit equipment datasheets, vendor lists, and construction schedules unless procurement summary is asked.

  • Collapsing the theory‑of‑change narrative into a single “objective” sentence loses the causal chain examiners expect.
  • Omitting the counterfactual identification strategy, the core of any impact evaluation précis.
  • Reporting only the headline effect size without confidence intervals or heterogeneity turns a rigorous finding into a headline.
  • Neglecting the risk‑register probability‑impact matrix in feasibility studies discards the decision‑gate logic.
  • Failing to distinguish pre‑award and post‑award interest in arbitration quantum, a frequent examiner trap.
  • Treating dissenting opinions in arbitration awards as optional footnotes rather than integral doctrinal signals.
  • Compressing the mitigation package of a legislative impact assessment into a generic “compensation” phrase erases distributional nuance.

These advanced précis formats demand disciplined extraction of methodological architecture, quantitative evidence, and institutional nuance to meet the exacting standards of competitive examinations.

Précis of Scientific Review Articles: Synthesizing Consensus and Gaps

A scientific review article maps the current state of knowledge, highlights methodological convergence, and exposes unresolved questions — unlike a primary research paper. When constructing a précis, first identify the review's organising framework (thematic, chronological, or methodological), which dictates evidence grouping. Preserve the review's explicit taxonomy (e.g., "mechanistic studies", "clinical trials", "meta‑analyses") and inclusion/exclusion criteria (search databases, date ranges, language filters). Next, capture consensus statements: pooled effect sizes, qualitative mechanistic agreements, or endorsed guideline recommendations. Equally important are flagged gaps: missing populations, contradictory findings, methodological limitations (e.g., heterogeneity/I², publication bias/funnel‑plot asymmetry). Represent gaps with the review's precision (e.g., "no RCT has examined dose‑response in children under five"). Do not collapse distinct evidence streams; the examiner expects separation of strong consensus from contested areas. Finally, retain proposed future‑research agendas, especially specific study designs or data‑sharing initiatives, as they often underlie follow‑up questions.

Condensing Diplomatic Negotiation Transcripts: Preserving Positions, Concessions, and Confidence‑Building Measures

Diplomatic negotiation transcripts (e.g., UN climate plenaries, WTO dispute hearings, bilateral summit minutes) feature formalised speech acts: opening statements, positional declarations, procedural motions, and informal corridor exchanges. A précis must first map the negotiation architecture: agenda items, working groups, decision‑making rules (consensus, qualified majority). For each agenda item, record opening positions verbatim where wording carries legal weight (e.g., "shall" vs. "should"). Track concessions explicitly: shifts from binding targets to voluntary pledges, flexibility clauses, or accepted review mechanisms. Confidence‑building measures (CBMs) — data‑exchange protocols, joint verification teams, phased implementation — are often the operative outcomes; retain their conditional language ("subject to", "provided that"). Preserve the procedural timeline: draft dates, bracketed unresolved text, final adoption or deferral. Omit rhetorical flourishes, pleasantries, and off‑record remarks unless they reveal a strategic pivot. The précis should read as a structured decision log, showing you distinguished substantive movement from procedural noise.

Worked Example: Précis of a WTO Dispute Settlement Transcript
A transcript excerpt shows Country A arguing that Country B's tariff on solar panels violates GATT Article III:4. Country B counters that the measure falls under the Article XX(g) exception for conservation of exhaustible natural resources. The Panel Chair notes that both parties agree to a provisional suspension of the tariff pending a technical expert report. The transcript ends with the Chair scheduling a follow‑up meeting in 60 days.
Précis (≈70 words): The WTO dispute transcript records Country A's claim that Country B's solar‑panel tariff breaches GATT Art. III:4. Country B invokes Art. XX(g) conservation exception. Both parties accept a provisional tariff suspension pending a technical expert report. The Panel Chair sets a 60‑day follow‑up. Key elements — legal provisions, competing arguments, provisional measure, and procedural timeline — are retained; diplomatic courtesies omitted.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a précis prompt asks for a "decision‑log" style summary, allocate roughly 25 % to the negotiation framework, 40 % to positional shifts and concessions, 20 % to confidence‑building measures, and 15 % to procedural next steps. Use the transcript's own headings (e.g., "Agenda Item 3", "Procedural Motion") as signposts; copy treaty article numbers and modal verbs (shall, may, should) verbatim to avoid paraphrase drift.

Summarizing Regulatory Impact Analyses: Cost‑Benefit, Distributional Effects, and Alternatives

Regulatory Impact Analyses (RIAs) are structured documents governments and agencies produce to justify new rules. A précis must reproduce the analytical backbone: problem definition, baseline scenario, and evaluated policy options. For each option, capture quantified NPV, benefit‑cost ratio (BCR), and discount rate. Distributional analysis is a separate pillar: record incidence across income quintiles, sectors, regions, or demographic groups, and any equity weighting applied. Summarise rejected alternatives with rejection reasons (e.g., "Option C discarded: BCR < 1 at 7% discount rate"). Sensitivity analysis is essential: list the three most influential parameters and the NPV swing range. If a regulatory flexibility analysis for small entities is included, preserve the compliance‑cost threshold and exemptions. Omit background literature reviews, stakeholder‑consultation summaries, and administrative procedures unless process transparency is explicitly asked. The final précis should read like a decision‑support table in prose, verifying retention of quantitative architecture and equity lens.

Précis of Judicial Sentencing Guidelines: Retaining Principles, Departures, and Appellate Standards

Sentencing guidelines (e.g., US Federal Sentencing Guidelines, England and Wales Sentencing Council guidelines, Indian Supreme Court proportionality jurisprudence) combine grid‑based offense‑level calculation with departure principles. A précis must first reproduce the guideline's structural logic: base offense level, specific offense characteristics, adjustments (role, obstruction, acceptance of responsibility), and criminal‑history category. Next, capture the departure framework (upward/downward), enumerated grounds (e.g., "substantial assistance", "extraordinary family circumstances"), and standard of review (de novo, abuse of discretion, clear error). Preserve constraining policy statements (e.g., "rehabilitation departures require a documented treatment plan"). Appellate standards are critical: note the test applied (reasonableness, procedural error, substantive reasonableness) and cite controlling precedent (e.g., Gall v. United States, 552 U.S. 38 (2007)). If a sentencing table with ranges exists, quote the applicable range. Exclude unrelated procedural history, victim‑impact statements (unless triggering an adjustment), and dissenting opinions (unless doctrinal splits are asked). The précis should enable reconstruction of the guideline computation and appellate checkpoint without the source.

Handling Multi‑Modal Evidence in Comprehension: Integrating Text, Tables, and Figures

Modern comprehension passages often embed tables, charts, diagrams, or infographics alongside prose. Treat each modality as a distinct evidence stream converging on the central argument. Catalogue modalities: (1) narrative text, (2) tabular data (regression coefficients, demographic breakdowns), (3) graphical data (trend lines, confidence intervals), (4) schematic diagrams (process flows, causal maps). For each modality, extract its headline claim (e.g., "Table 2 shows a 12% adoption increase post‑subsidy"; "Figure 3 illustrates a non‑linear dose‑response curve"). Map cross‑references: does the text cite the table's p‑value? Does the figure's caption qualify the trend (e.g., "preliminary data")? Synthesize by stating the convergent conclusion and noting divergences (e.g., "text claims significance, but table's 95% CI includes zero"). For a précis, allocate word‑budget proportionally: ~40% narrative synthesis, 30% quantitative highlights (exact numbers, uncertainty qualifiers), 20% visual‑logic description (axes, scales, annotations), 10% critical appraisal of cross‑modal consistency. Do not merely list visual elements; explain how they reinforce or challenge the textual argument.

  • Collapsing a review's evidence taxonomy into one "findings" paragraph, losing consensus vs. contested distinction.
  • Omitting conditional language of concessions (e.g., "subject to verification"), altering legal effect.
  • Reporting only headline NPV without discount rate, sensitivity range, or distributional breakdown.
  • Failing to cite the controlling appellate standard (reasonableness vs. abuse of discretion) for sentencing‑guideline departures.
  • Treating a figure's visual trend as proof while ignoring caption uncertainty qualifiers or contradictory textual statistics.
  • Neglecting cross‑modal references, producing isolated facts instead of an integrated argument.
  • Copying procedural boilerplate (e.g., stakeholder‑consultation lists) at the expense of the analytical core.

These advanced précis formats demand disciplined extraction of methodological architecture, quantitative evidence, and institutional nuance to meet competitive examination standards.

Précis of Constitutional Court Reasoning on Socio‑Economic Rights: Progressive Realization, Minimum Core Obligations, and Reasonableness Review

Constitutional courts adjudicating socio‑economic rights (South African Constitutional Court, Indian Supreme Court under Article 21, Colombian Constitutional Court, Brazilian Supremo Tribunal Federal) employ a distinct analytical architecture. A précis must capture three doctrinal pillars. First, progressive realization: the state’s obligation to take deliberate, concrete, and targeted steps toward full realization using maximum available resources (ICESCR Art. 2(1); Government of the Republic of South Africa v. Grootboom 2001 (1) SA 46 (CC) para. 45). Quote the court’s phrasing on “reasonable legislative and other measures” and note any temporal benchmarks set. Second, minimum core obligations: the non‑derogable floor (essential food, primary health care, basic shelter, basic education) derived from General Comment No. 3 (CESCR) and applied in Minister of Health v. Treatment Action Campaign (2002) 5 SA 721 (CC). Record whether the court treats breach of the minimum core as per se unreasonable or as a factor in the overall reasonableness calculus. Third, reasonableness review: the structured proportionality inquiry — legitimate aim, rational connection, necessity (less restrictive alternative), and balancing. In Grootboom, the Constitutional Court held that a housing programme excluding people in desperate need was unreasonable; in TAC, the failure to provide nevirapine to prevent mother‑to‑child HIV transmission violated the minimum core. A précis must preserve the court’s own standard of review (e.g., “the state must show that its measures are reasonable”) and any remedial order (structural interdict, declaratory order, supervisory jurisdiction). Exclude factual background unrelated to the doctrinal test; retain the precise constitutional provision, the international law interpretive aid (ICESCR, General Comments), and the disposition.

Summarizing Monetary Policy Frameworks: Inflation Targeting Regimes, Policy Rate Corridors, and Transmission Mechanisms

Central bank communications (RBI Monetary Policy Reports, Fed FOMC Statements, ECB Monetary Policy Decisions, Bank of England Inflation Reports) follow a rigid analytical template. A précis must reproduce the inflation targeting framework: the target (e.g., 4% ± 2% for RBI under the 2016 Monetary Policy Framework Agreement), the horizon (medium‑term), and the escape clauses (supply shocks, fiscal dominance). Next, capture the policy rate corridor: the repo rate (policy rate), reverse repo rate, marginal standing facility (MSF) rate, and the standing deposit facility (SDF) rate where applicable. Note the corridor width (e.g., 50 bps pre‑2022, 25 bps post‑SDF introduction) and any asymmetric adjustments. Third, map the transmission channels explicitly discussed: interest‑rate channel (bank lending rates, bond yields), credit channel (bank balance‑sheet, firm balance‑sheet), exchange‑rate channel, asset‑price channel, and expectations channel. Quote the central bank’s assessment of transmission strength (e.g., “transmission remains incomplete; recent analysis indicates pass‑through to fresh rupee loans exceeds 100 bps for a 100 bps policy rate change”). Preserve forward guidance language verbatim: “accommodative as long as necessary”, “calibrated tightening”, “data‑dependent”. Record dissent votes and the rationale (e.g., “one member voted for a 25 bps hike citing core inflation persistence”). Exclude routine operational details (liquidity management operations unless they signal stance change) and generic growth‑inflation narratives unmoored from the policy decision. The précis should enable reconstruction of the policy stance, the quantitative anchors, and the conditional forward guidance without the source document.

Worked Example: Précis of an MPC Minute Extract
The MPC noted that CPI inflation averaged 6.7% in Q1:2023‑24, above the upper tolerance band. Core inflation remained sticky at 5.1%. The repo rate was kept unchanged at 6.50% with a 5:1 majority. Dr. Patra dissented, preferring a 25 bps hike citing second‑round effects. The MPC retained “withdrawal of accommodation” stance. Growth projection for 2023‑24 retained at 6.5%. The Governor emphasized that monetary policy must remain actively disinflationary.
Précis (68 words): The MPC (5:1) held the repo rate at 6.50%, retaining “withdrawal of accommodation” stance. CPI inflation averaged 6.7% in Q1:2023‑24 (above 6% upper band); core inflation sticky at 5.1%. Dr. Patra dissented for a 25 bps hike citing second‑round effects. Growth projection unchanged at 6.5% for 2023‑24. Governor stressed actively disinflationary policy. Forward guidance: stance remains data‑dependent.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When précis‑ing central bank texts, allocate word‑budget: 30% to the decision (rate, stance, vote split), 30% to the inflation‑growth diagnostics (exact numbers, horizon), 20% to forward guidance (verbatim modal verbs), 15% to transmission assessment, 5% to dissent rationale. Never paraphrase “shall”, “will”, “may”, “should” — they carry legal‑policy weight. If the passage includes a dot‑plot or fan chart, describe the median path and the 70% confidence interval in one sentence; do not list individual projections.

Précis of International Dispute Settlement Mechanisms: WTO Panels, Appellate Body, and DSU Procedures

World Trade Organization dispute settlement reports (Panel Reports, Appellate Body Reports, Arbitration Awards under DSU Article 25) follow the Understanding on Rules and Procedures Governing the Settlement of Disputes (DSU). A précis must reconstruct the jurisdictional basis: the covered agreements cited (GATT 1994 Art. I, III, XI; SCM Agreement Art. 1, 3; TBT Agreement Art. 2.2), the measures at issue (statute, regulation, administrative guidance), and the complainant’s legal claims. Next, capture the standard of review articulated by the panel/Appellate Body: “deference” vs. “de novo” (e.g., US — Wool Shirts and Blouses, AB Report, WT/DS33/AB/R, p. 14); the “margin of appreciation” for regulatory purpose (e.g., Brazil — Retreaded Tyres, AB Report, WT/DS332/AB/R, para. 158). Third, summarize the legal findings claim‑by‑claim: for each provision, state the interpretive approach (ordinary meaning, context, object and purpose per Vienna Convention Art. 31), the factual application, and the conclusion (inconsistent / not inconsistent). Preserve the remedial recommendations: DSU Art. 19.1 recommendation to bring into conformity; Art. 21.3 “reasonable period of time” (RPT) determination (e.g., 15 months in US — Continued Dumping and Subsidy Offset Act, Art. 21.3(c) arbitration); Art. 22.6 retaliation authorization level (e.g., $7.5 bn in EU — Aircraft, WT/DS316/ARB). Note any Appellate Body reversals with the precise legal error identified (e.g., “Panel erred in applying ‘necessity test’ under GATT Art. XX(d) without assessing contribution to objective”). Exclude procedural history (third‑party submissions, interim review) unless it affects the legal reasoning. The précis must enable a reader to state the WTO‑consistency outcome, the controlling interpretive methodology, and the compliance timeline without the full report.

Handling Counterfactual Reasoning in Econometric Causal Inference: Difference‑in‑Differences, Instrumental Variables, and Regression Discontinuity

Policy briefs and academic passages increasingly embed causal inference designs. A précis must identify the identification strategy by name and its core assumptions. For Difference‑in‑Differences (DiD): record the treatment and control groups, the pre‑ and post‑periods, the parallel‑trends assumption (and any event‑study graph evidence), the estimator (two‑way fixed effects, staggered adoption correction per Callaway & Sant’Anna 2021 or Sun & Abraham 2021), and the ATT estimate with standard errors (clustered at what level). For Instrumental Variables (IV): name the instrument, state the exclusion restriction and relevance (first‑stage F‑statistic, e.g., “F = 24.3, well above the Stock‑Yogo weak‑instrument threshold”), the monotonicity assumption (LATE interpretation), and the 2SLS estimate with confidence interval. For Regression Discontinuity (RD): specify the running variable, the cutoff, the bandwidth selection method (IK optimal, CCT robust), the polynomial order, the continuity of covariates at the cutoff (balance tests), and the local average treatment effect (LATE) at the threshold. Preserve all robustness checks mentioned: placebo tests, alternative bandwidths, Donut RD, falsification endpoints, sensitivity to functional form. Quote the authors’ own caveats (e.g., “results may not extrapolate beyond the compliance margin”). Do not collapse distinct designs into a generic “the study finds X”; the examiner tests whether you can distinguish the identifying variation. Allocate word‑budget: 40% to design and assumptions, 30% to main estimates (point estimate, precision, units), 20% to robustness, 10% to external validity caveats.

Précis of Constitutional Amendment Procedures: Comparative Analysis of Rigidity, Referendum Requirements, and Eternity Clauses

Constitutional amendment rules (India Art. 368, US Art. V, Germany Art. 79, South Africa Art. 74, Brazil Art. 60, Japan Art. 96) form a distinct genre requiring precise institutional mapping. A précis must tabulate three dimensions for each constitution compared. First, procedural rigidity: the legislative majorities required (simple, absolute, qualified — two‑thirds, three‑fifths), the number of readings, the role of upper house (e.g., India: Rajya Sabha two‑thirds present and voting; US: two‑thirds both Houses or convention; Germany: two‑thirds Bundestag and Bundesrat). Second, referendum triggers: mandatory (Ireland Art. 46, Denmark Art. 88, Australia s. 128), optional (France Art. 89, Italy Art. 138), or absent (India, UK). Note the turnout/approval thresholds (e.g., Australia: double majority — national majority plus majority in majority of states). Third, eternity clauses / unamendable provisions: Germany Art. 79(3) (human dignity, federalism, democracy, rule of law); India basic structure doctrine (Kesavananda Bharati 1973); Brazil Art. 60 §4 (federalism, direct/secret/universal suffrage, separation of powers, individual rights); Turkey Art. 4 (secularism, republic); South Africa s. 1 (founding values) read with s. 74(2) (75% Assembly + 6 provinces). Record the judicial enforcement mechanism: constitutional court abstract review (Germany BVerfG, India Supreme Court, Brazil STF) or political question doctrine (US). Preserve the exact textual formulation of the eternity clause (e.g., “The division of the Federation into Länder… shall not be amended” — Art. 79(3) GG). Exclude historical amendment counts unless the prompt asks for frequency analysis. The précis must enable reconstruction of the amendment pathway and the substantive limits for each constitution without the source texts.

  • Conflating progressive realization with non‑retrogression; the former permits phased achievement, the latter prohibits deliberate backsliding — both must appear if the court invokes them.
  • Omitting the corridor width or the SDF introduction date in monetary policy précis; these are the quantitative anchors examiners verify.
  • Summarizing a WTO finding as “violation of GATT Art. III” without specifying the paragraph (III:2 first sentence vs. second sentence vs. III:4) and the standard of review applied.
  • Reporting a DiD estimate without stating the parallel‑trends evidence (event‑study coefficients, pre‑trend p‑value) or the clustering level for standard errors.
  • Listing amendment majorities but forgetting the ratification role of states/provinces (India: half the states for federal provisions; US: three‑fourths of states; Germany: Bundesrat two‑thirds).
  • Paraphrasing “shall bring into conformity” as “should comply” — DSU Art. 19.1 creates a legal obligation, not a hortatory recommendation.
  • Collapsing distinct identification strategies (IV vs. RD vs. DiD) into a single “causal estimate” paragraph, losing the assumption‑dependence that defines each design.

Précis of International Human Rights Treaty Body Concluding Observations

Concluding observations from UN treaty bodies — such as the Human Rights Committee (CCPR), the Committee on Economic, Social and Cultural Rights (CESCR), and the Committee on the Elimination of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW) — follow a predictable structure that a précis must preserve. The document opens with the state party’s periodic report, the date of consideration, and the delegation’s composition. The core comprises three layers: positive aspects, principal areas of concern, and concrete recommendations. Each recommendation is anchored to a specific treaty article (e.g., ICCPR Art. 19, ICESCR Art. 12) and often cites the committee’s general comments for interpretive guidance. A précis must retain the hierarchy: first, overarching thematic concerns (e.g., freedom of expression, non‑discrimination, right to health); second, the most serious violations identified (e.g., arbitrary detention, lack of judicial independence); third, time‑bound recommendations (e.g., “within twelve months amend the Press Law to align with Art. 19”). Quantitative data — such as reported cases of torture, children out of school, or budgetary allocations for social protection — must be reproduced verbatim because examiners test the ability to distinguish qualitative assessments from measurable indicators. The concluding paragraph usually contains follow‑up procedures: the deadline for the next periodic report, a request for a mid‑term review, or an invitation to submit information on implementation of selected recommendations. Omitting the follow‑up mechanism loses marks because it signals the monitoring cycle that distinguishes treaty bodies from one‑off special procedures.

Précis of Climate Finance Mechanisms under the UNFCCC

The UNFCCC architecture creates a layered finance landscape that a précis must map without conflating distinct instruments. The Global Environment Facility (GEF) has served as the operating entity of the Financial Mechanism since 1994, channeling funds for mitigation, adaptation, technology transfer, and capacity‑building. The Green Climate Fund (GCF), established by the Cancún Agreements (2010) and operationalized in 2015, is the primary multilateral fund for developing countries, governed by a 24‑member Board with equal representation from developed and developing parties. A précis must note the GCF’s allocation modalities: the Readiness Programme, the Project Preparation Facility, and the main funding windows (mitigation, adaptation, cross‑cutting). The Adaptation Fund (AF), created under the Kyoto Protocol (2001) and now serving the Paris Agreement, finances concrete adaptation projects through direct access modalities — national implementing entities (NIEs) and regional implementing entities (RIEs). The Special Climate Change Fund (SCCF) and the Least Developed Countries Fund (LDCF), both administered by the GEF, target specific vulnerability groups. Quantitative anchors are essential: the $100 billion per year goal by 2020 (reaffirmed in the 2021 Glasgow Climate Pact), the GCF’s first replenishment of $10.3 billion (2019), and the AF’s cumulative approvals exceeding $850 million as of 2023. The précis must also capture reporting obligations — biennial communications, Standing Committee on Finance (SCF) progress reports, and the Enhanced Transparency Framework (ETF) under Art. 13 of the Paris Agreement — because examiners frequently ask for the linkage between finance flows and transparency mechanisms.

Worked Example: Condensing a GCF Board Decision
A 350‑word GCF Board decision approves a $45 million adaptation project in Country X, cites the project’s alignment with the National Adaptation Plan (NAP), notes the co‑financing ratio of 1:3 (GCF:domestic), records the Board’s request for a mid‑term evaluation at month 24, and includes a footnote referencing the GCF’s Environmental and Social Policy (ESP) compliance. Write a 60‑word précis.
The GCF Board approved a $45 million adaptation project for Country X, aligned with its National Adaptation Plan, with a 1:3 GCF‑to‑domestic co‑financing ratio. The Board mandated a mid‑term evaluation at month 24 and confirmed compliance with the GCF Environmental and Social Policy.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a précis prompt asks for “finance mechanisms”, list each fund in a single sentence with its governing body, capitalisation figure, and access modality. Use a semi‑colon separated list to keep the word count low while preserving the distinct legal bases (UNFCCC, Kyoto Protocol, Paris Agreement).

Précis of Digital Identity Frameworks and Interoperability Standards

Digital identity (DID) frameworks — such as the EU’s eIDAS Regulation (Regulation (EU) No 910/2014), India’s Aadhaar Act (2016), and the World Bank’s Identification for Development (ID4D) principles — share a common architecture that a précis must delineate: legal basis, authentication levels, data protection safeguards, and cross‑border interoperability provisions. eIDAS defines three assurance levels (low, substantial, high) and mandates mutual recognition of notified electronic identification schemes across member states. The Aadhaar Act establishes a unique 12‑digit identifier linked to biometric data, with the Unique Identification Authority of India (UIDAI) as the regulator; it prescribes authentication modes (demographic, biometric, OTP) and sets out penalties for misuse under Chapter VI. The ID4D principles emphasise minimal data collection, purpose limitation, and the right to rectification, aligning with the GDPR’s Art. 5 and Art. 12‑22. A précis must capture the interoperability mechanisms: eIDAS’s “peer‑review” and “notification” procedures, the Aadhaar‑enabled “e‑KYC” APIs that allow verified identity sharing with consent, and the ISO/IEC 18013‑5 mobile driving licence standard that enables offline verification. Quantitative anchors include approximately 27 notified eID schemes (2023), Aadhaar enrolments exceeding 1.35 billion, and the ID4D target of providing legal identity to all by 2030 (SDG 16.9). The précis should also note the governance oversight bodies — the eIDAS Cooperation Group, the UIDAI’s adjudicating officers, and the ID4D Multi‑Donor Trust Fund steering committee — because examiners test the ability to distinguish regulatory supervision from operational execution.

Précis of Space Law Treaties: Outer Space Treaty, Moon Agreement, and Registration Convention

The corpus of international space law rests on five core treaties, but the Outer Space Treaty (OST, 1967), the Moon Agreement (1979), and the Registration Convention (1975) form the triad most frequently examined. The OST establishes the principle that outer space, including the Moon and other celestial bodies, is the province of all mankind (Art. I), prohibits national appropriation (Art. II), mandates that states bear international responsibility for national activities (Art. VI), and requires authorisation and continuing supervision of non‑governmental entities (Art. VI). The Moon Agreement elaborates on the “common heritage of mankind” concept (Art. 11), creates an international regime for resource exploitation once it becomes feasible, and obliges parties to establish an international authority to govern extraction — though only 18 states have ratified it, limiting its customary force. The Registration Convention requires launching states to register space objects with the United Nations Office for Outer Space Affairs (UNOOSA), providing the name, launch date, orbital parameters, and general function (Art. II). A précis must preserve the distinction between the OST’s broad normative framework and the Moon Agreement’s specific resource‑governance regime, and must note the Registration Convention’s role in transparency and liability attribution under the Liability Convention (1972). Quantitative data — such as over 110 parties to the OST, 18 parties to the Moon Agreement, and over 70 parties to the Registration Convention — are frequently tested. The précis should also reference the UN Committee on the Peaceful Uses of Outer Space (COPUOS) as the forum for ongoing norm development, including the 2021 “Space2030” agenda.

Précis of Public Procurement Guidelines: WTO GPA and National Regulations

The World Trade Organization Agreement on Government Procurement (GPA, revised 2012) sets the benchmark for transparent, non‑discriminatory procurement among its 48 parties (including the EU as a single party). A précis must map the GPA’s coverage thresholds — SDR 130,000 for central government goods and services, SDR 200,000 for sub‑central entities, and SDR 400,000 for construction services — and the procedural guarantees: open tendering as the default, selective tendering only where justified, limited tendering under strict conditions, and the requirement for electronic procurement (Art. 15). National regimes such as the U.S. Federal Acquisition Regulation (FAR), the EU Public Procurement Directives (2014/24/EU, 2014/25/EU), and India’s General Financial Rules (GFR) 2017 transpose these principles with local variations. The FAR incorporates the Buy American Act (BAA) and the Trade Agreements Act (TAA) as domestic preference exceptions; the EU Directives embed the “most economically advantageous tender” (MEAT) criterion and mandatory exclusion grounds (Art. 57). India’s GFR prescribes a two‑stage bidding process for high‑value contracts, a mandatory e‑procurement portal (GeM), and a preference for Micro and Small Enterprises (MSEs) up to 25 % of procurement value. A précis must retain the interplay between international obligations and domestic derogations — for example, the GPA’s “general exceptions” (Art. 23) allowing measures necessary to protect public morals, health, or essential security interests — and the dispute settlement route: the WTO’s Dispute Settlement Body (DSB) for GPA parties, domestic courts or administrative tribunals for national rules. Quantitative anchors — such as the estimated $1.7 trillion annual value of GPA‑covered procurement (2022) and around 12 % average SME participation rate in EU contracts — are essential for a high‑scoring summary.

  • Conflating the distinct legal bases of the GEF, GCF, AF, SCCF, and LDCF into a single “climate fund” paragraph.
  • Omitting the assurance‑level hierarchy (low, substantial, high) when summarising eIDAS or comparable DID frameworks.
  • Failing to distinguish the Outer Space Treaty’s non‑appropriation principle from the Moon Agreement’s common‑heritage regime.
  • Neglecting the GPA’s SDR thresholds and the specific procedural guarantees (open, selective, limited tendering).
  • Summarising treaty‑body concluding observations without preserving the article‑level linkage of each recommendation.
  • Collapsing the registration requirements of the Registration Convention into a generic “states must register satellites” statement without citing Art. II data fields.
  • Overlooking the follow‑up mechanisms (mid‑term review, next periodic report deadline) that complete the monitoring cycle in human‑rights and climate‑finance texts.

These advanced précis categories demand precise institutional mapping, quantitative fidelity, and clear separation of overlapping legal regimes to meet examiner expectations.

Précis of Environmental Management Plans: Mitigation Measures, Monitoring Protocols, and Adaptive Management Triggers

Environmental Management Plans (EMPs) accompany large‑scale infrastructure, extractive, and industrial projects. A précis must capture three interlocking layers: the mitigation hierarchy (avoid, minimise, restore, offset), the monitoring protocol (indicators, frequency, responsible agency, data‑sharing mechanism), and the adaptive‑management triggers (threshold values, decision rules, escalation pathways). Begin by naming the project, the regulatory framework (e.g., national EIA legislation, World Bank Environmental and Social Framework, IFC Performance Standards), and the spatial‑temporal scope. Then list the key mitigation measures in the order of the hierarchy, preserving any quantitative targets such as “no net loss of 12 ha of wetland habitat” or “PM₂.₅ emissions ≤ 35 µg/m³ annual average”. Monitoring details require the indicator, baseline, target, sampling method, and reporting interval; for example, “quarterly groundwater sampling at 12 piezometers, analysed for arsenic, reported to the Ministry within 30 days”. Adaptive‑management triggers are conditional statements: “if arsenic exceeds 10 µg/L in two consecutive quarters, the contractor shall activate the contingency pumping plan and notify the regulator within 48 hours”. Preserve the linkage between trigger and response; omitting the decision rule collapses the plan into a static checklist. Where the EMP references external standards (ISO 14001, WHO air‑quality guidelines), cite the specific clause. Quantitative anchors — budget allocations for mitigation (e.g., US$4.2 million for offset planting), staffing levels (e.g., 3 full‑time environmental officers), and contingency reserves — are frequently examined. Finally, note any stakeholder‑engagement commitments (public disclosure schedule, grievance‑redress mechanism) because they affect the plan’s legal enforceability.

Condensing Corporate Governance Codes: Board Composition, Committee Charters, and Disclosure Requirements

Corporate governance codes (e.g., UK Corporate Governance Code 2024, OECD G20/OECD Principles, SEBI LODR Regulations 2015) prescribe board structure, committee mandates, and transparency obligations. A précis must distinguish mandatory provisions from “comply‑or‑explain” recommendations. Start with the code’s jurisdiction, version date, and applicability threshold (listed companies, state‑owned enterprises, financial institutions). Board composition: record the required proportion of independent directors (e.g., ≥ 50 % for premium‑listed UK firms), tenure limits (maximum nine years), diversity targets (gender, ethnicity, skills matrix), and separation of chair and CEO roles. Committee charters: for each statutory committee (Audit, Nomination, Remuneration, Risk), note the minimum independent‑member count, meeting frequency, key responsibilities (e.g., audit committee oversight of internal controls, external auditor independence, related‑party transactions), and any special powers (e.g., authority to engage external advisors). Disclosure requirements: enumerate the annual report items — board evaluation methodology, director remuneration policy, shareholder voting results, related‑party transaction register, and ESG‑linked metrics (Scope 1‑3 emissions, board‑level climate oversight). Preserve any “apply‑or‑explain” language verbatim where the code permits deviation, because examiners test the ability to flag non‑compliance rationales. Quantitative anchors such as “minimum 4 audit committee meetings per year”, “maximum 12 % of total remuneration as variable pay”, or “30 % female representation by 2027” are essential. Cross‑reference the code’s enforcement mechanism: listing‑rule sanctions, regulator‑issued guidance, or shareholder‑resolution pathways. A high‑scoring précis retains the hierarchy — mandatory → recommended → voluntary — and the interplay between board‑level oversight and statutory duties under company law.

Worked Précis Exercise
A 420‑word excerpt from a national Corporate Governance Code describes board independence criteria, audit committee mandate, and ESG disclosure rules. Write a précis of no more than 120 words that preserves the mandatory‑versus‑recommended distinction, the quantitative thresholds, and the enforcement route.
The Code (2024 revision) applies to all listed entities. Mandatory: ≥ 50 % independent directors, chair‑CEO separation, audit committee of at least three independents meeting quarterly, and annual disclosure of board evaluation, remuneration policy, and related‑party transactions. Recommended (comply‑or‑explain): 30 % female directors by 2027, ESG metrics including Scope 1‑3 emissions, and a board‑level climate‑risk committee. Enforcement via listing‑rule sanctions and regulator guidance; non‑compliance must be explained in the annual report. (115 words)
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a passage mixes mandatory and recommendatory language, underline every “shall”, “must”, “required” and every “should”, “encouraged”, “comply‑or‑explain”. In the précis, group the mandatory items first, then the recommendatory block, preserving any numeric thresholds. This visual segregation prevents the common error of flattening the hierarchy.

Summarizing International Development Evaluation Reports: Theory of Change, Outcome Indicators, and Lessons Learned

Evaluation reports from bilateral agencies (USAID, DFID/FCDO), multilateral banks (World Bank IEG, ADB Independent Evaluation), and UN bodies (UNDP IEO) follow a structured template: context, theory of change (ToC), methodology, findings against outcome indicators, and lessons learned. A précis must reconstruct the ToC logic chain — inputs → activities → outputs → outcomes → impact — and note any mid‑term revisions. Preserve the primary outcome indicators (e.g., “percentage of target households with improved water access ≥ 80 % by Year 3”) and the evaluation design (quasi‑experimental, contribution analysis, process tracing). Quantitative results: effect sizes, confidence intervals, and statistical significance (p‑values) are examinable. Lessons learned are often presented as “what worked”, “what didn’t”, and “contextual factors”; capture the top three lessons with their evidence base (e.g., “community‑led monitoring increased sustainability — supported by 73 % of case studies”). Flag any methodological limitations (attrition bias, spillover effects) because they affect the credibility of conclusions. Cross‑reference the report’s recommendation matrix: each recommendation linked to a responsible actor, timeline, and monitoring indicator. Retain the funding envelope (e.g., US$45 million over five years) and the evaluation budget share (typically 3‑5 %). A concise summary respects the causal logic, the evidence hierarchy, and the actionable recommendations.

Précis of Cybersecurity Risk Assessment Frameworks: Threat Modeling, Control Mapping, and Residual Risk Ratings

Frameworks such as NIST SP 800‑30 Rev. 1, ISO/IEC 27005, and FAIR (Factor Analysis of Information Risk) guide organisations through asset identification, threat enumeration, vulnerability assessment, likelihood estimation, impact quantification, and risk determination. A précis must differentiate the framework’s scope (enterprise‑wide, system‑specific, supply‑chain), the threat‑modeling technique (STRIDE, ATT&CK, CAPEC), and the control‑mapping approach (NIST CSF functions, ISO 27001 Annex A controls, CIS Controls v8). Preserve the risk‑calculation formula: Risk = Likelihood × Impact, noting whether likelihood is qualitative (Very High–Very Low) or quantitative (annualised loss expectancy). Record the residual‑risk rating scale (e.g., Critical ≥ 15, High 10‑14, Medium 5‑9, Low < 5) and the treatment options (mitigate, transfer, accept, avoid) with any mandatory controls (e.g., encryption of data at rest per PCI‑DSS 3.2.1). Quantitative anchors — number of assets assessed (e.g., 1,240), identified threat scenarios (e.g., 87), and cost‑benefit of recommended controls (e.g., US$2.3 million investment yielding US$12 million risk reduction) — are frequently tested. Note the review cycle (annual reassessment, trigger‑based re‑evaluation after major incidents) and the governance sign‑off (CISO, risk committee, board). A high‑quality précis retains the methodological rigour, the control‑to‑risk traceability matrix, and the decision‑ready residual‑risk profile.

Condensing Public‑Private Partnership Contracts: Risk Allocation, Performance Milestones, and Termination Clauses

PPP contracts (e.g., UK PF2, India’s Viability Gap Funding model, World Bank PPP Reference Guide) allocate construction, demand, revenue, and force‑majeure risks between the public authority and the private concessionaire. A précis must map each risk category to the party bearing it, the mitigation mechanism (e.g., availability payments, demand guarantees, tariff adjustment formulas), and the contractual triggers for re‑balancing. Performance milestones: list the key dates (financial close, construction completion, commercial operation date), the associated liquidated‑damages rates (e.g., 0.5 % of contract value per day of delay, capped at 10 %), and the hand‑back standards (asset condition rating ≥ Grade B). Termination clauses: distinguish termination for convenience (public authority pays termination compensation = senior debt + equity return), termination for default (private party bears senior debt, no equity return), and force‑majeure termination (shared loss per pre‑agreed formula). Preserve the dispute‑resolution ladder — expert determination → mediation → arbitration (ICC Rules, seat, language). Quantitative anchors: total project cost (e.g., ₹12,500 crore), concession period (30 years), equity‑debt ratio (20:80), and the internal rate of return target (12‑14 %). A précis that retains the risk‑allocation matrix, milestone‑linked cash‑flow triggers, and the termination‑compensation waterfall meets examiner expectations for contractual precision.

  • Flattening mandatory and recommendatory provisions in governance codes, losing the comply‑or‑explain nuance.
  • Omitting quantitative thresholds (e.g., independent‑director percentages, liquidated‑damages caps) that are explicitly tested.
  • Collapsing the theory‑of‑change logic chain into a single “project aims” sentence, discarding causal links.
  • Neglecting to cite the specific risk‑calculation formula or residual‑risk rating scale in cybersecurity frameworks.
  • Summarising PPP risk allocation without distinguishing construction, demand, and revenue risk parties.
  • Failing to preserve the termination‑compensation waterfall (senior debt, equity, shared loss) in PPP contracts.
  • Dropping the review‑cycle or governance‑sign‑off details that demonstrate ongoing risk management.

Précis of Scientific Review Articles: Evidence Grading, GRADE Framework, and Narrative Synthesis

A scientific review article condenses primary research into a structured evidence base. A précis must preserve the review’s question formulation (PICO or PECO), the search strategy (databases, date limits, language filters), and the eligibility criteria (study designs, populations, interventions, comparators, outcomes). The GRADE (Grading of Recommendations Assessment, Development and Evaluation) framework is the standard for rating certainty of evidence: high, moderate, low, very low. Capture the domains that lead to downgrading (risk of bias, inconsistency, indirectness, imprecision, publication bias) and upgrading (large magnitude of effect, dose‑response gradient, plausible confounding). When the review presents a meta‑analysis, retain the pooled effect measure (risk ratio, odds ratio, mean difference), the statistical model (fixed‑effect vs random‑effects), heterogeneity statistics (I², τ², χ² p‑value), and any subgroup or sensitivity analyses. For narrative syntheses, note the synthesis method (vote counting, thematic synthesis, framework synthesis) and the qualitative confidence statements. Preserve the review’s conclusions framed as “recommendations” (strong/conditional) linked to the certainty ratings. Omit peripheral background but keep the logic chain: question → search → selection → appraisal → synthesis → judgement.

Précis of Corporate Governance Codes: Principles, Disclosure Requirements, and Compliance Mechanisms

Corporate governance codes (e.g., UK Corporate Governance Code 2018, OECD Principles of Corporate Governance 2015, SEBI LODR Regulations) combine principle‑based guidance with mandatory disclosure rules. A précis must map each principle (board leadership, division of responsibilities, composition, succession, evaluation, audit, risk, remuneration) to its corresponding “comply‑or‑explain” provision. Record key disclosure thresholds: proportion of independent directors (≥ 50 % for UK premium‑listed firms), maximum tenure for independent directors (nine years under UK Code), gender diversity requirements (e.g., at least one woman director under SEBI LODR), and a separate audit committee with at least three independent members. Capture the remuneration policy disclosure: fixed vs variable components, performance metrics, clawback provisions, and shareholder vote frequency (binding vote on policy every three years, advisory vote annually). Note risk‑management and internal‑control reporting obligations (annual statement of effectiveness, internal audit function, whistle‑blower mechanism). Record the enforcement ladder: regulatory monitoring, public naming, possible sanctions (fines, suspension of listing). Retain the code’s review cycle (typically biennial) and the consultation process preceding amendments. A précis that mirrors the principle‑disclosure‑enforcement triad satisfies examiner expectations for governance precision.

Technique: Mapping Principle to Disclosure
A passage states: “The board should establish a nomination committee chaired by an independent director, responsible for board composition and succession planning. The committee’s terms of reference must be disclosed in the annual report, and the committee must meet at least twice a year.” Write a précis sentence that captures the principle, the mandatory disclosure, and the meeting frequency.
The code requires a nomination committee chaired by an independent director to oversee board composition and succession, mandates disclosure of its terms of reference in the annual report, and stipulates a minimum of two meetings per year.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a governance passage lists multiple principles, first tabulate principle → disclosure → frequency/threshold. Then compress each row into a single clause; this prevents omission of quantitative thresholds that are frequently tested.

Précis of International Development Evaluation Reports: Theory of Change, Counterfactual Analysis, and Lessons Learned

Evaluation reports for development programmes (World Bank IEG, OECD‑DAC, UNDP) follow a theory‑of‑change (ToC) logic: inputs → activities → outputs → outcomes → impact. A précis must reconstruct the ToC diagram in prose, preserving the causal pathways and the assumptions at each link. Identify the evaluation design: experimental (RCT), quasi‑experimental (difference‑in‑differences, regression discontinuity, propensity‑score matching), or non‑experimental (process tracing, contribution analysis). Record the counterfactual construction: control group definition, baseline equivalence checks, and any spillover adjustments. Quantitative findings: effect sizes (standardised mean difference, odds ratio), confidence intervals, statistical significance (p‑values), and cost‑effectiveness ratios (cost per DALY averted, cost per beneficiary). Qualitative findings: “what worked”, “what didn’t”, contextual moderators, and the evidence base for each lesson (e.g., “community‑led monitoring increased sustainability — supported by a majority of case studies”). Preserve the recommendation matrix: each recommendation linked to responsible actor, timeline, and monitoring indicator. Flag methodological limitations (attrition bias, measurement error, external validity constraints) because they affect credibility. Retain the funding envelope (e.g., a multi‑million dollar budget over several years) and evaluation budget share (typically 3‑5 %). A concise summary respects the causal logic, the evidence hierarchy, and the actionable recommendations.

Précis of Legislative Drafting Manuals: Definitional Hierarchies, Interpretive Rules, and Amendment Procedures

Legislative drafting manuals (e.g., Commonwealth Legislative Drafting Manual, US Office of Legislative Counsel Guide, Indian Manual of Parliamentary Procedure) codify the architecture of statute law. A précis must capture the definitional hierarchy: “Act” → “Section” → “Subsection” → “Paragraph” → “Sub‑paragraph”, and the binding force of each level. Record the interpretive rules: purposive construction, mischief rule, literal rule, ejusdem generis, noscitur a sociis, and the presumption against retrospectivity. Preserve the drafting conventions for definitions (inclusive vs exhaustive), the use of “shall” or “must” for mandatory obligations, “may” for discretionary powers, and the distinction between “deeming provisions” and “legal fictions”. Note the amendment procedures: simple amendment (majority), special majority (two‑thirds), and entrenched provisions requiring referendum or state ratification. Include the rules for commencement (fixed date, proclamation, “on the day after royal assent”), transitional and savings provisions, and the treatment of repealed enactments (saving of rights, liabilities, and proceedings). Retain the manual’s guidance on gender‑neutral language, plain‑language drafting, and the use of schedules for detailed technical matter. A précis that mirrors the structural, interpretive, and procedural layers meets the examiner’s demand for legislative precision.

Précis of Algorithmic Impact Assessments: Risk Taxonomy, Mitigation Controls, and Audit Trails
