Algorithmic impact assessments (AIAs) — mandated by the EU AI Act, US Algorithmic Accountability Act proposals, and Canada’s Directive on Automated Decision‑Making — follow a risk‑taxonomy: unacceptable risk, high risk, limited risk, minimal risk. A précis must list the criteria that place a system in each tier (e.g., real‑time remote biometric identification in public spaces = unacceptable; credit scoring = high). Preserve the mandatory mitigation controls for high‑risk systems: data‑governance documentation, transparency notices, human‑in‑the‑loop oversight, accuracy and robustness testing (confusion matrix, ROC‑AUC thresholds), and post‑market monitoring plans. Record the audit‑trail requirements: version‑controlled model documentation (e.g., model cards), training‑data provenance logs, change‑management records, and incident‑reporting timelines (72 hours for serious incidents). Capture the conformity‑assessment route: self‑assessment, third‑party conformity assessment body, or regulatory sandbox. Note the governance structure: provider obligations, user obligations, and the role of national supervisory authorities. Retain the penalty regime (administrative fines up to €30 million or 6 % of global turnover for prohibited systems). A précis that aligns risk tier, control set, audit artefacts, and enforcement levers demonstrates mastery of the regulatory architecture.

  • Collapsing the GRADE certainty rating into a single “quality” adjective without citing the specific downgrading/upgrading domains.
  • Omitting the comply‑or‑explain nuance in governance codes and treating all provisions as mandatory.
  • Summarising a development evaluation’s ToC as a linear list, discarding the explicit assumptions that link outputs to outcomes.
  • Neglecting the hierarchy of legislative definitions and the distinct legal effect of “shall” versus “may” in drafting manuals.
  • Failing to differentiate the risk tiers in algorithmic impact assessments and merging mitigation controls across tiers.
  • Dropping quantitative thresholds (confidence intervals, effect sizes, fine ceilings) that are explicitly tested in précis questions.
  • Ignoring the audit‑trail artefacts (model documentation, incident‑reporting timelines) that demonstrate ongoing compliance in AIAs.

Précis of International Human Rights Court Judgments: Preserving Reasoning, Margin of Appreciation, and Remedial Orders

Judgments of the European Court of Human Rights (ECtHR), the Inter‑American Court of Human Rights (IACtHR), and the African Court on Human and Peoples’ Rights follow a predictable architecture: procedural history, admissibility, merits, separate opinions, and reparations. A précis must retain the Court’s articulation of the applicable Convention article, the factual matrix triggering the provision, and the legal test applied — typically a two‑step proportionality analysis (legitimate aim, necessity in a democratic society). The margin of appreciation doctrine, central to ECtHR jurisprudence, requires the précis to note whether the Court afforded a wide, narrow, or no margin, and the reasoning for that calibration (e.g., consensus among Contracting Parties, sensitivity of the policy area). For the IACtHR, the “control of conventionality” test and the Court’s remedial powers (restitution, compensation, guarantees of non‑repetition) must be captured. The African Court’s reliance on the African Charter’s “peoples’ rights” and its provisional measures orders should be reflected. Dissenting or concurring opinions exposing doctrinal splits — such as the scope of positive obligations under Article 2 ECHR or the justiciability of socio‑economic rights under the African Charter — are essential. Quantitative elements (e.g., amounts awarded under Article 41 ECHR, implementation deadlines) and monitoring mechanisms (Committee of Ministers supervision, IACtHR follow‑up hearings) must be preserved because examiners often test the ability to distinguish declaratory relief from enforceable remedial orders.

Summarizing Scientific Meta‑Research Papers: Replication Studies, Registered Reports, and Open Science Practices

Meta‑research — the study of research practices — produces papers evaluating reproducibility, bias, and methodological quality across a field. A précis must distinguish direct replication (identical protocol, same population) from conceptual replication (same hypothesis, different operationalisation). Registered Reports, a format where peer review precedes data collection, involve a two‑stage decision: in‑principle acceptance based on methodology, then final acceptance irrespective of results. The précis should record the sampling frame (e.g., 100 psychology experiments from 2008‑2012), the replication success metric (effect‑size correlation, p‑value concordance, Bayesian factor), and any moderator analyses (sample size, original p‑value, discipline). Open Science badges (Open Data, Open Materials, Preregistration) signal compliance; their presence or absence is a factual detail examiners may probe. Reporting standards — PRISMA‑R for replication systematic reviews, TOP Guidelines for transparency, CONSORT extension for replication trials — provide a checklist (protocol registration, deviation reporting, analysis code availability) that a précis must reflect. Statistical summaries — median replication effect size, proportion of significant replications, heterogeneity (I²) — are quantitative anchors that cannot be omitted. The paper’s implications for research policy (funding mandates, journal editorial changes, institutional reward structures) constitute the “so what” a high‑scoring précis conveys.

Worked Précis Exercise
A meta‑research article reports that of 120 registered replication attempts in experimental economics, 68 % reproduced the original directional effect, with a median effect‑size ratio of 0.62. The authors note that studies with pre‑registered analysis plans showed a 15 % higher replication rate. Summarise the findings in 80 words, preserving the quantitative evidence and the policy implication.
The study examined 120 pre‑registered replication attempts in experimental economics. Sixty‑eight percent reproduced the original directional effect; the median replicated effect size was 62 % of the original. Pre‑registration of analysis plans correlated with a 15‑percentage‑point increase in replication success. The authors recommend that funders and journals mandate pre‑registration to improve cumulative evidence reliability.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a meta‑research passage appears, first locate the replication success metric (percentage, effect‑size ratio, Bayesian factor). Highlight any moderator analysis — these are the “why” examiners may ask you to explain. Write the précis in two sentences: one for the headline numbers, one for the policy recommendation. This structure helps ensure coverage of both evidence and implication within tight word limits.

Précis of Trade Negotiation Texts: Preserving Bracketed Language, Options, and Consensus Language

Multilateral trade negotiations (WTO Doha Round, Regional Comprehensive Economic Partnership, African Continental Free Trade Area) generate texts replete with square‑bracketed alternatives, “options” paragraphs, and “chair’s text” proposals. A précis must map the negotiation architecture: the preamble, core obligations (market access, rules of origin, services schedules), and annexes containing country‑specific commitments. Bracketed language signals unresolved disagreement; the précis should record the number of brackets per article, the substantive alternatives (e.g., “[shall] / [may] provide national treatment”), and any “clean” text achieving consensus. Options paragraphs — often labelled “Option A / Option B” — represent mutually exclusive pathways; the précis must list each option’s key parameters (tariff reduction formula, transition period, safeguard trigger) without merging them. Chair’s text, presented as a compromise, may contain “placeholder” language (“[X] per cent”) that the précis should flag as pending. The document’s version history (revision numbers, dates of informal meetings) and the negotiating group’s mandate (e.g., “Friends of the Chair on Services”) provide context for examiners’ “why” questions. Finally, the précis should note any “early harvest” agreements — provisional implementation of agreed chapters — because they affect the text’s legal status.

Condensing Parliamentary Committee Evidence Sessions: Witness Testimony, Written Submissions, and Committee Conclusions

Parliamentary committees — UK House of Commons Select Committees, Indian Parliamentary Standing Committees, US Congressional Hearings — produce evidence volumes combining oral testimony transcripts, written memoranda, and the committee’s final report with recommendations. A précis must differentiate evidence types: oral evidence captures real‑time questioning, allowing the précis to note the line of inquiry (e.g., “the Chair probed the regulator’s enforcement capacity”) and any concessions extracted. Written submissions, often from interest groups, academics, and government departments, provide data tables, legal analyses, and policy proposals; the précis should summarise the most recurrent themes and any dissenting data points. The committee’s conclusions — findings of fact, policy recommendations, and calls for government response — constitute the actionable output. Structural markers such as “Paragraph 12: The Committee recommends…”, “Minority view: …”, and “Government response due within 60 days” are essential signposts. When multiple sessions address the same inquiry (e.g., pre‑legislative scrutiny followed by post‑implementation review), the précis must show the evolution of the committee’s stance. Quantitative evidence (budget figures, performance metrics, compliance rates) cited by witnesses must be retained verbatim or with precise rounding, because examiners often test the ability to preserve numerical fidelity.

Précis of International Development Evaluation Reports: Theory of Change, Logframes, and Value‑for‑Money Assessments

Evaluation reports commissioned by bilateral agencies (DFID/FCDO, USAID, GIZ) and multilateral banks (World Bank, Asian Development Bank) follow the OECD‑DAC criteria — relevance, coherence, effectiveness, efficiency, impact, sustainability — and are structured around a Theory of Change (ToC) and a Logical Framework (Logframe). A précis must reproduce the ToC’s causal pathway: inputs → activities → outputs → outcomes → impact, noting explicit assumptions at each link (e.g., “assumes stable macro‑economic environment”). The Logframe’s indicator matrix — indicators, baselines, targets, means of verification, risks — provides the quantitative backbone; the précis should capture headline indicators (e.g., “primary school enrolment increased from 78 % to 92 %”) and any indicator missing its target with the reported variance. Value‑for‑Money (VfM) analysis adds four dimensions: economy (cost per unit), efficiency (output per cost), effectiveness (outcome per output), and equity (distribution of benefits). The précis must state the VfM rating (high/medium/low) and the evidence base (cost‑effectiveness ratio, benefit‑cost ratio, qualitative equity assessment). Methodological limitations — attribution gaps, selection bias, short follow‑up periods — are routinely examined; the précis should flag them as the evaluators do. Finally, the management response matrix (accept/partially accept/reject each recommendation, responsible unit, implementation timeline) is required because it translates findings into action.

  • Collapsing distinct Court remedial orders (just satisfaction vs. general measures) into a single “compensation” statement.
  • Omitting the margin‑of‑appreciation calibration (wide/narrow/none) which determines the intensity of review.
  • Merging direct and conceptual replication results without noting the methodological distinction.
  • Dropping pre‑registration status or open‑science badge information that the passage explicitly highlights.
  • Fusing bracketed alternatives into a single “proposed” text, erasing the negotiation dynamics.
  • Summarising committee evidence as a monolithic “stakeholder views” without separating oral testimony, written submissions, and minority opinions.
  • Neglecting the ToC assumptions or VfM equity dimension, which are explicitly scored in evaluation précis rubrics.

Précis of National Security Strategy Documents: Threat Assessment, Capability Gaps, and Resource Allocation

National security strategy (NSS) documents — such as the United States National Security Strategy, the United Kingdom Strategic Defence and Security Review, India's National Security Strategy draft, and the European Union Strategic Compass — share a recurring architecture: strategic context, identified threats, national interests, capability objectives, and implementation roadmap. A précis must preserve the hierarchy of threats (state‑based, non‑state, hybrid, transnational) and the explicit linkage between each threat and the capability gap it creates. For example, if the text states “the proliferation of hypersonic missile technology creates a gap in missile‑defence early‑warning coverage”, the précis must retain the causal verb “creates” and the specific capability “early‑warning coverage”. Resource allocation tables (budget lines, procurement timelines, force‑structure targets) are condensed by citing top‑line figures (e.g., “over $1 trillion over a decade for nuclear modernization”) and prioritisation logic (e.g., “tier‑1: nuclear deterrence; tier‑2: cyber resilience”). The précis should also flag conditional language — “subject to congressional appropriation”, “pending allied burden‑sharing agreements” — because examiners test the ability to distinguish firm commitments from contingent intentions. Finally, the document's review cycle (often quadrennial) and the mechanism for mid‑term updates (e.g., “annual implementation report to the National Security Council”) are essential metadata that explain the text's temporal validity.

Summarizing Public Health Emergency Declarations: Legal Basis, Containment Measures, and Exit Criteria

Public health emergency declarations — issued under the International Health Regulations (2005), the US Public Health Service Act, the UK Public Health (Control of Disease) Act, or India's Epidemic Diseases Act — share a three‑part structure: legal authority, operative measures, and termination conditions. A précis must identify the statutory provision invoked (e.g., “Section 319 of the Public Health Service Act”) and the competent authority (WHO Director‑General, national health minister, state governor). Containment measures are catalogued by category: movement restrictions (curfews, cordon sanitaire), medical countermeasures (vaccination mandates, antiviral stockpiling), surveillance enhancements (contact‑tracing apps, genomic sequencing quotas), and risk‑communication protocols. Each measure should be linked to its legal justification (e.g., “mandatory quarantine under Section 2 of the Epidemic Diseases Act”). Exit criteria are often expressed as epidemiological thresholds (e.g., “14‑day incidence < 5 per 100 000”, “R₀ < 1 for two consecutive weeks”) and operational milestones (e.g., “≥ 80 % of target population fully vaccinated”). The précis must preserve logical conjunctions — “and”, “or”, “provided that” — because examiners frequently ask whether a single threshold suffices or whether all must be met. Any sunset clause (automatic expiry after 90 days unless renewed) and the parliamentary or congressional oversight mechanism (e.g., “report to Parliament every 30 days”) are also required for a complete condensation.

Worked Example: Condensing a Public Health Emergency Declaration
A national health minister issues a declaration under the Epidemic Diseases Act, 1897, invoking Section 2. The declaration imposes a 21‑day nationwide curfew (6 pm–6 am), mandates COVID‑19 vaccination for all citizens aged ≥ 18, requires hospitals to reserve 30 % of ICU beds for COVID patients, and establishes a genomic sequencing target of 5 % of positive samples. Exit criteria: 14‑day case incidence below 10 per 100 000, R₀ below 1 for 14 consecutive days, and ≥ 70 % adult vaccination coverage. The declaration expires automatically after 90 days unless extended by parliamentary resolution.
The Minister, under Section 2 of the Epidemic Diseases Act (1897), declared a public health emergency imposing a 21‑day nationwide curfew (6 pm–6 am), mandatory COVID‑19 vaccination for adults, 30 % ICU‑bed reservation, and a 5 % genomic sequencing target. The declaration lapses after 90 days unless Parliament extends it. Exit requires all three conditions: 14‑day incidence < 10/100 000, R₀ < 1 for 14 days, and ≥ 70 % adult vaccination.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a passage contains a legal instrument, first locate the enabling section and the issuing authority; these two items are almost always tested. Next, list measures in the order they appear, preserving any numerical thresholds verbatim. Finally, note the expiry or review mechanism — examiners frequently ask “what happens if the legislature does not act?”.

Condensing International Maritime Boundary Delimitation Awards: Equidistance, Relevant Circumstances, and Provisional Arrangements

Maritime boundary awards — such as the ICJ judgment in Nicaragua v. Colombia (2012), the Arbitral Tribunal award in the South China Sea (2016), and the ITLOS ruling in Ghana/Côte d'Ivoire (2017) — apply a three‑stage methodology: (1) provisional equidistance line, (2) assessment of relevant circumstances (coastal geography, conduct of parties, economic factors, security concerns), and (3) final adjustment to achieve an equitable solution. A précis must record the baseline coordinates used for the provisional line (e.g., “base points A‑1 to A‑12 as defined in UNCLOS Article 5”) and any agreed‑upon base‑point selections. The relevant circumstances section is condensed by grouping them: geographical (concave/convex coasts, islands, reefs), historical (previous agreements, effective occupation), and socio‑economic (fisheries, hydrocarbon resources). The tribunal's reasoning on each circumstance — whether it warrants a shift, a weighting, or is dismissed — must be captured with the tribunal's own qualifiers (“significant”, “decisive”, “insufficient”). Provisional arrangements (e.g., joint development zones, fisheries agreements pending final delimitation) are noted with their temporal scope and governance structure. The précis should also preserve the award's operative paragraph numbering (e.g., “Paragraph 112: The Tribunal decides…”) because examiners may ask for the exact paragraph containing the final boundary coordinates.

Précis of Corporate Restructuring Plans: Debt Reprofiling, Asset Divestiture, and Stakeholder Consent Thresholds

Corporate restructuring plans — filed under Chapter 11 (US), the Insolvency and Bankruptcy Code (India), the UK Corporate Insolvency and Governance Act, or the EU Preventive Restructuring Frameworks — contain a standard set of modules: financial overview, debt reprofiling terms, asset divestiture schedule, new financing commitments, and stakeholder voting mechanics. A précis must reproduce the debt reprofiling matrix: original principal, new interest rate, maturity extension, haircut percentage, and any conversion features (e.g., “15 % of unsecured claims converted to equity at a 1:5 ratio”). Asset divestiture is summarised by listing the business units, expected proceeds, and the timeline for sale (e.g., “Divestiture of the logistics division by Q3 2026, target proceeds USD 2.3 bn”). New financing (DIP financing, exit financing) is captured by facility size, seniority, covenants, and conditionality. The voting thresholds — typically 66 % in value of each impaired class and 50 % in number — and any cram‑down provisions (e.g., “plan confirmed over dissenting class if fair and equitable test satisfied”) are essential. The précis should also note the court or tribunal confirmation hearing date and any conditions precedent (regulatory approvals, antitrust clearances). Examiners often test whether the student can distinguish between a “pre‑packaged” plan (pre‑negotiated before filing) and a “free‑fall” plan (negotiated after filing), as the procedural timeline differs.

Handling Mixed‑Methods Evaluation Reports: Integrating Qualitative Narrative, Quantitative Indicators, and Triangulation Protocols

Mixed‑methods evaluation reports — common in development agencies (World Bank, UNDP, DFAT) and academic impact assessments — combine a Theory of Change narrative, a quantitative indicator framework (logframe), and a triangulation matrix that maps qualitative findings to quantitative results. A précis must first restate the Theory of Change causal chain (inputs → activities → outputs → outcomes → impact) and flag explicit assumptions at each link (e.g., “assumes stable macro‑economic environment”). The quantitative section is condensed by citing headline indicators, baselines, targets, and actuals with variance (e.g., “Primary enrolment rose from 78 % to 92 %, exceeding the 90 % target by 2 percentage points”). Qualitative findings are grouped by theme (e.g., “community ownership”, “gender equity”) and linked to the specific indicator they illuminate. The triangulation protocol — whether convergence, complementarity, or discrepancy — must be described using the report's own terminology (e.g., “convergent evidence on improved learning outcomes; divergent evidence on teacher attendance”). Methodological limitations (attribution gaps, selection bias, short follow‑up) and the management response matrix (accept/partially accept/reject each recommendation, responsible unit, timeline) are required because they translate findings into operational decisions. The précis should preserve the report's evaluation rating (highly satisfactory, satisfactory, moderately unsatisfactory, unsatisfactory) and the evidence grade (strong, moderate, limited) assigned to each key finding.

  • Omitting the conditional language that qualifies resource commitments in security strategy documents.
  • Collapsing multiple exit thresholds into a single “when cases drop” statement in public health declarations.
  • Failing to distinguish provisional equidistance from the final adjusted line in maritime boundary awards.
  • Merging debt‑reprofiling terms across creditor classes without preserving class‑specific haircuts.
  • Summarising qualitative themes without linking them to the quantitative indicators they triangulate.
  • Dropping the management response matrix that shows which recommendations were accepted or rejected.
  • Neglecting the voting‑threshold mechanics that determine whether a restructuring plan can be crammed down.

These advanced précis techniques ensure that the condensed text retains the legal, procedural, and analytical precision demanded by high‑stakes examinations.

Précis of UN Treaty Body Concluding Observations: Preserving State Party Replies and Follow‑Up Procedures

UN treaty bodies such as the Human Rights Committee, the Committee on Economic, Social and Cultural Rights, and the Committee against Torture issue concluding observations after reviewing a State party’s periodic report. A précis must capture the three‑tier structure: (1) positive aspects noted by the Committee, (2) principal concerns and recommendations, and (3) the State party’s written replies and any follow‑up information submitted under the optional follow‑up procedure. Each recommendation should be recorded with its paragraph number (e.g., “Paragraph 12: The Committee recommends that the State amend the Criminal Code to remove the death penalty for non‑violent offences” — illustrative). The State party’s response is summarised by indicating whether it accepts, partially accepts, or rejects the recommendation, and by noting any concrete legislative or administrative steps announced (e.g., “State accepts; draft amendment submitted to Parliament” — illustrative). Follow‑up reports, when available, are condensed by noting implementation status (implemented, partially implemented, not implemented) and any new data supplied (e.g., “Follow‑up 2025: death‑penalty abolition bill passed; implementation confirmed” — illustrative).

Because concluding observations often contain cross‑references to general comments, jurisprudence, and other treaty bodies, the précis should preserve the citation format used by the Committee (e.g., “General Comment No. 36 (2018) on the right to life”). When the Committee requests a specific timeline (e.g., “within one year”), that deadline must appear verbatim. The précis must also flag procedural remarks such as the Committee’s decision to adopt a “list of issues prior to reporting” or to request a special report. Omitting the State party’s reply or the follow‑up status defeats the purpose of the exercise, which is to show the dynamic dialogue between the treaty body and the State.

In practice, examiners test the ability to distinguish between the Committee’s “concerns” (diagnostic) and “recommendations” (prescriptive). A common error is to merge them into a single list. The correct approach is to keep two parallel columns or bullet groups, each retaining the original paragraph numbers, so that a later question can ask “Which recommendation in paragraph 12 was accepted by the State?” without ambiguity.

Précis of Supreme Court Advisory Opinions on Constitutional Interpretation: Preserving Ratio and Obiter

Advisory opinions issued by a supreme court (e.g., the Supreme Court of India under Article 143, the Canadian Supreme Court in Reference re Secession of Quebec, or the European Court of Justice under Article 267 TFEU) contain a formal answer to a constitutional question posed by the executive or legislature. The binding portion — the ratio decidendi — is the court’s definitive interpretation of the constitutional provision. Obiter dicta include hypothetical reasoning, policy suggestions, or comments on issues not strictly necessary for the answer. A précis must separate these two layers explicitly. The ratio is usually signalled by language such as “We hold that…”, “The Court concludes…”, or “It is declared that…”. Obiter passages often begin with “It may be observed…”, “In the Court’s view…”, or “For future guidance…”.

When condensing, retain the exact constitutional article or clause interpreted (e.g., “Article 21 – protection of life and personal liberty”). Summarise the ratio in a single declarative sentence that mirrors the court’s wording as closely as possible. Obiter observations are included only if they clarify the scope of the ratio or indicate future doctrinal development; they should be marked with parentheses or a prefatory “(obiter)”. The précis must also note the composition of the bench (e.g., “Five‑judge bench, unanimous”) and any dissenting or concurring opinions, because examiners may ask whether the ratio reflects a majority or a plurality view.

Advisory opinions frequently reference prior case law, comparative jurisprudence, and scholarly commentary. The précis should preserve the citation of the most authoritative precedent (e.g., “Kesavananda Bharati v. State of Kerala (1973) 4 SCC 225”) and indicate whether the court affirmed, distinguished, or overruled it. Numerical data such as the number of judges concurring, the date of the opinion, and the reference number (e.g., “Reference No. 1 of 2022” — illustrative) are essential metadata that must survive condensation.

Technique: Mapping Ratio and Obiter in Advisory Opinions
Given a 150‑word excerpt from a Supreme Court advisory opinion on the scope of Article 21, produce a 60‑word précis that separates the binding ratio from the obiter observations.
The Court held that the right to life under Article 21 includes the right to a clean environment (ratio). It further observed that future legislation should incorporate environmental impact assessments (obiter).
Exam‑Day Strategy
When time is limited, first underline the declarative sentences that announce the Court’s holding; those form the ratio. Everything else is obiter and can be omitted or noted in parentheses.

Summarizing Multi‑Jurisdictional Tax Treaty Negotiations: Retaining Residence, Source, and Anti‑Abuse Provisions

Tax treaty negotiations between two or more jurisdictions produce a draft convention that typically follows the OECD Model Tax Convention or the UN Model. A précis must preserve the allocation of taxing rights: residence‑based taxation (Article 4), source‑based taxation (Articles 6‑12 for business profits, dividends, interest, royalties), and the anti‑abuse framework (Article 29‑A on limitation on benefits, principal purpose test, and the Multilateral Instrument (MLI) modifications). Each article should be represented by its headline rule and any negotiated deviations (e.g., “Article 10(2): dividend withholding tax reduced to 5 % for beneficial owners holding ≥25 % voting shares, deviating from the OECD 15 % default” — illustrative).

The précis must also capture the “saving clause” (often Article 1(3) or a domestic law override) that permits a contracting state to tax its own residents as if the treaty did not exist. Any reservation or notification deposited under the MLI (e.g., “State A opts out of the principal purpose test for covered tax agreements” — illustrative) is recorded with the exact MLI article number. The entry‑into‑force mechanism (exchange of instruments of ratification, date of effect) and the termination clause (usually 12‑month notice) are required because examiners may ask for the effective date of a specific reduced rate.

When multiple protocols amend the base treaty, the précis should present a chronological layer: base treaty → Protocol 1 (date, key changes) → Protocol 2 (date, key changes). This layering prevents the common mistake of conflating the original rate with the amended rate. Numerical thresholds (percentage rates, ownership percentages, holding periods) must be reproduced exactly; rounding or approximating them is a fatal error in high‑stakes précis tasks.

Précis of Scientific Peer‑Review Reports: Capturing Reviewer Comments, Revisions, and Editorial Decisions

Peer‑review reports for scholarly journals consist of three components: (1) the reviewer’s evaluation (major strengths, major weaknesses, minor comments), (2) the author’s point‑by‑point response, and (3) the editor’s decision letter (accept, minor revision, major revision, reject). A précis must map each reviewer comment to the corresponding author response and indicate whether the editor deemed the revision satisfactory. The structure is best rendered as a table‑like narrative: “Reviewer 1, Comment 3: ‘Statistical analysis lacks power calculation.’ Author response: ‘Power analysis added (α=0.05, 1‑β=0.80).’ Editor: ‘Accepted.’” (illustrative).

Key methodological details that survive condensation include the study design (randomised controlled trial, cohort, case‑control), sample size, primary outcome, and statistical tests used. If a reviewer requests additional experiments, the précis notes the request and whether the authors complied (e.g., “Reviewer 2 requested dose‑response curve; authors performed additional assay, data included in revised Figure 3” — illustrative). The editor’s final decision must be quoted verbatim (e.g., “Decision: Accept subject to minor copy‑editing” — illustrative). Any conditional acceptance (e.g., “Accept pending deposition of raw data in repository”) is retained because it may affect the paper’s reproducibility.

Confidential comments to the editor (not shown to authors) are excluded from the précis unless the prompt explicitly asks for them. The précis should also preserve the manuscript identifier (e.g., “MS‑2024‑04567” — illustrative) and the journal’s name, as these are often required for citation in subsequent questions. Omitting the editor’s decision or collapsing multiple reviewer comments into a generic “reviewers asked for revisions” loses the granularity examiners reward.

Handling Counterfactual Reasoning in Economic Forecasting Documents: Preserving Scenario Assumptions and Sensitivity Ranges

Economic forecasting documents (e.g., IMF World Economic Outlook, central bank monetary policy reports, government budget outlooks) present a baseline projection together with alternative scenarios built on counterfactual assumptions. A précis must list each scenario by name (baseline, upside, downside, stress) and enumerate the key exogenous assumptions that differentiate them: GDP growth rate, inflation path, oil price trajectory, fiscal stance, and exchange‑rate regime. For each scenario, the précis records the quantitative forecast horizon (e.g., “2025‑2027”) and the headline variables (real GDP growth, CPI inflation, current‑account balance) with their point estimates and confidence intervals (e.g., “Baseline real GDP growth 2025: 3.2 % (95 % CI 2.8‑3.6 %)” — illustrative).

Sensitivity analysis tables are condensed by stating the variable shocked, the magnitude of the shock, and the resulting change in the target variable (e.g., “A 10 % increase in oil price reduces baseline GDP growth by 0.15 percentage points” — illustrative). The précis must preserve the model’s structural assumptions that drive the counterfactual (e.g., “DSGE model with Calvo price setting, Taylor‑rule monetary policy”). If the document includes a fan chart, the précis describes the width of the fan at each horizon (e.g., “Fan width at 2‑year horizon: ±0.8 % for GDP” — illustrative). Any policy‑reaction function embedded in the scenario (e.g., “Interest rate follows a Taylor rule with coefficient 1.5 on inflation gap” — illustrative) is retained because it explains the endogenous response.

When multiple institutions publish forecasts for the same economy, the précis may be asked to synthesize them. In that case, each institution’s baseline and scenario set are kept separate, labelled by source (IMF, OECD, national central bank), and the divergence in assumptions is highlighted (e.g., “IMF assumes oil price $85/bbl; OECD assumes $78/bbl” — illustrative). The précis must not average across institutions unless the prompt explicitly requests a consensus view. Dropping the confidence intervals or the shock magnitudes converts a rigorous counterfactual summary into a vague narrative, which examiners penalise heavily.

  • Merging Committee concerns and recommendations into a single undifferentiated list.
  • Failing to mark obiter dicta separately from the ratio in advisory opinions.
  • Omitting the saving clause or MLI reservations in tax treaty précis.
  • Collapsing multiple reviewer comments into a generic “revisions requested” statement.
  • Dropping confidence intervals or shock magnitudes in economic scenario summaries.
  • Neglecting to preserve paragraph numbers or manuscript identifiers that enable cross‑referencing.
  • Conflating baseline assumptions across different forecasting institutions without attribution.

These traps illustrate the precision required when condensing highly structured, multi‑layered documents for examination purposes.

Précis of International Trade Dispute Settlement Panel Reports: Preserving Findings, Legal Reasoning, and Remedies

WTO panel reports under the DSU follow a fixed structure: factual background, claims and defences, applicable agreements, legal analysis, and conclusions with recommendations. A précis must retain the case citation (e.g., “DS503 — United States — Certain Measures on Steel and Aluminium Products”), panel composition, and circulation date. Each finding is linked to a specific provision (e.g., GATT 1994 Art. XI, SCM Agreement Art. 3) and records violation, non‑violation, or unsubstantiated claim. The reasoning chain — textual interpretation, contextual analysis, reliance on Appellate Body precedent — is summarised by noting the interpretive method (ordinary meaning, object and purpose, subsequent practice) and key precedents (e.g., “Appellate Body Report, EC — Hormones, para. 120”). Remedial recommendations are quoted verbatim when specific (e.g., “bring the measure into conformity with Art. XI:1”) and the implementation period noted. Dissenting opinions are flagged with their legal basis. Omitting provision‑level linkage or collapsing reasoning into a generic “panel found a breach” loses granularity examiners reward.

Précis of National Security Strategy Documents: Threat Assessment, Strategic Objectives, and Resource Allocation

National security strategy (NSS) documents — such as the US National Security Strategy, UK Strategic Defence and Security Review, or India’s draft NSS — share a common scaffold: executive summary, threat landscape, strategic objectives, capability priorities, and implementation mechanisms. A précis must identify the issuing authority, publication year, and classification level. Threat assessments are condensed by category (state‑based, non‑state, transnational, cyber, climate‑linked) preserving priority language (e.g., “primary”, “emerging”, “persistent”). Numbered goals are listed verbatim (e.g., “Goal 1: Protect the homeland”). For each, the précis captures lines of effort, lead agency, and quantitative targets (e.g., “increase defence spending to 3 % of GDP by 2028”). Resource allocations become narrative ranges (e.g., “cyber capabilities: $12‑15 billion over FY2025‑2029”) with conditional language retained (“subject to congressional appropriation”). Implementation mechanisms — coordination bodies, review cycles, reporting requirements — are noted for accountability. Explicit assumptions (e.g., “assumes a stable great‑power balance”) and risk caveats are recorded. Dropping conditionality or merging threat categories erases strategic nuance examiners test.

Worked Example: Condensing a National Security Strategy Excerpt
Given the following excerpt from a 2023 National Security Strategy: “The primary threat to our homeland remains state‑sponsored terrorism, particularly from actors operating in Region X. A secondary but growing challenge is malicious cyber activity targeting critical infrastructure. To address these, Goal 1 directs the Department of Homeland Security to enhance border screening technologies, allocating $4.2 billion over the next five years, contingent on legislative approval. Goal 2 tasks the Cyber Command with achieving a 95 % intrusion‑detection rate by 2027.” Produce a précis of no more than 60 words that preserves threat hierarchy, goals, lead agencies, funding, conditionality, and quantitative targets.
The 2023 NSS identifies state‑sponsored terrorism from Region X as the primary homeland threat and malicious cyber activity as a secondary challenge. Goal 1 assigns DHS to upgrade border screening with $4.2 billion (FY2024‑2028) contingent on legislative approval. Goal 2 tasks Cyber Command to reach a 95 % intrusion‑detection rate by 2027.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a précis prompt specifies a strict word limit, first list every mandatory data point (threat rank, goal number, agency, figure, condition, deadline). Then draft a single sentence per data cluster, using apposition and parenthetical qualifiers to compress. Count words after each sentence; if over budget, replace phrasing with tighter synonyms (e.g., “contingent on” → “pending”) before cutting content.

Précis of Public Policy Evaluation Reports: Counterfactual Design, Cost‑Effectiveness, and Implementation Fidelity

Evaluation reports from audit institutions, think‑tanks, or development agencies (e.g., World Bank IEG, OECD DAC) follow a logic‑model framework: theory of change, questions, methodology, findings, cost‑effectiveness, and lessons. A précis must preserve the design label (e.g., “randomised controlled trial with clustered assignment”, “difference‑in‑differences with propensity‑score matching”) and the counterfactual. Key parameters — sample size, treatment/control groups, baseline equivalence, attrition — are recorded for internal validity. Cost‑effectiveness is condensed by metric (e.g., cost per DALY averted), point estimate, and uncertainty interval (e.g., “$1,200 per DALY averted (95 % CI $950‑$1,500)”). Implementation fidelity — dosage, adherence, moderators — is summarised in one clause (e.g., “average fidelity 78 %; lower in remote districts”). Prescriptive recommendations are quoted (e.g., “scale up conditional cash transfers with enhanced monitoring”). Disaggregated equity analysis (gender, income quintile) is noted by dimension and effect direction. Collapsing methodology to “rigorous evaluation” or omitting confidence intervals yields a vague endorsement.

Précis of Scientific Consensus Statements: Grading Evidence, Declaring Uncertainty, and Recommendation Strength

Consensus statements from bodies like the IPCC, WHO guideline groups, or IARC use formal evidence‑grading systems (GRADE, IPCC likelihood scale, IARC classification). A précis must capture the framework, the range of evidence grades, and the consensus level (e.g., “high confidence”, “likely”, “Group 1 carcinogen”). Each key finding is paired with its grade (e.g., “global mean surface temperature increase of 1.1 °C since 1850‑1900 — high confidence”). Uncertainty language is preserved verbatim for projections (e.g., “sea‑level rise of 0.3‑0.6 m by 2100 under SSP2‑4.5 — medium confidence”). Recommendation strength (strong/conditional) and target audience are recorded. Dissenting or minority views are noted (e.g., “two panel members disagreed on the threshold for ‘likely’”). Systematic review references include identifier and study count. Replacing calibrated grades (e.g., “medium confidence” → “moderate certainty”) alters the probabilistic meaning examiners expect.

Précis of Corporate Restructuring Plans: Debt Reprofiling, Asset Divestiture, and Stakeholder Protections

Restructuring plans under insolvency regimes (e.g., US Chapter 11, UK Scheme of Arrangement, Indian IBC) contain a financial model, creditor classification, debt‑equity swap, asset sale timetable, and stakeholder protections (employee claims, pension deficits, supplier continuity). A précis must identify the debtor, jurisdiction, plan version, and court‑approval date. Debt reprofiling lists each creditor class, pre‑restructuring claim, recovery rate, and new instrument terms (e.g., “Class A senior secured: $1.2 bn claim → 85 % recovery via 5‑year 6 % notes”). Asset divestiture is summarised narratively (e.g., “sale of European logistics unit: $350 m, binding offer from LogiCo, closing Q3 2025”). Stakeholder protections quote specific safeguards (e.g., “employee severance fund $45 m, pension top‑up 100 % of vested benefits”). Conditions precedent (regulatory approvals, creditor vote thresholds) and effective date are recorded. Merging creditor classes or omitting asset‑sale conditionality removes legal precision.

  • Omitting provision‑level citations in trade panel reports, collapsing distinct findings into a generic violation.
  • Dropping conditional funding language in national security strategies, presenting contingent allocations as firm commitments.
  • Reducing counterfactual design in policy evaluations to “rigorous method”, losing internal‑validity markers.
  • Replacing calibrated evidence grades (e.g., “medium confidence”) with informal synonyms, altering probabilistic meaning.
  • Merging creditor classes in restructuring plans, erasing differentiated recovery rates.
  • Omitting dissenting opinions in consensus documents, removing the record of scientific disagreement.
  • Neglecting conditions precedent for asset sales in restructuring précis, making the plan appear unconditional.

Précis of International Human Rights Treaty Body Concluding Observations: Preserving State Party Obligations, Recommendations, and Follow‑Up Procedures

Concluding observations issued by treaty bodies such as the Human Rights Committee (CCPR), the Committee on Economic, Social and Cultural Rights (CESCR), and the Committee on the Elimination of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW) follow a predictable architecture: introductory remarks, positive aspects, principal areas of concern, and concrete recommendations. A précis must retain treaty‑specific article references (e.g., ICCPR Arts. 6, 7, 14; ICESCR Arts. 11, 12) because examiners test whether the candidate can map each concern to its legal basis. The state‑party’s reporting cycle (periodic report number, date of dialogue) is recorded in a single clause (e.g., “third periodic report, dialogue held March 2023”). Recommendations are quoted verbatim when they contain conditional language (“should”, “must”, “is urged to”) and the précis notes the follow‑up procedure (e.g., “follow‑up report due within twelve months under the Committee’s procedural rules”). Positive aspects are summarised only when cited by the Committee as a basis for a recommendation (e.g., “the Committee welcomes the 2022 amendment to the Penal Code but remains concerned about the retention of the death penalty for drug offences”). Omitting the article citation or the follow‑up deadline produces a vague endorsement that loses marks for legal precision.

Summarizing Climate Finance Mechanisms: Green Climate Fund, Adaptation Fund, and Loss‑and‑Damage Modalities

The three principal multilateral climate finance vehicles — Green Climate Fund (GCF), Adaptation Fund (AF), and the Warsaw International Mechanism for Loss and Damage (WIM) — operate under distinct governance structures, funding windows, and eligibility criteria. A précis must identify the governing board composition (e.g., GCF Board: 24 members, equal representation of developed and developing countries), the financial instruments deployed (grants, concessional loans, equity, guarantees), and the accreditation modality (direct access entities vs. international access entities). For the GCF, the précis records the replenishment cycle (e.g., first replenishment 2020‑2023, approximately USD 9.8 bn pledged) and the project‑level result areas (mitigation, adaptation, cross‑cutting). The Adaptation Fund’s unique feature — financing concrete adaptation projects through the Kyoto Protocol’s Clean Development Mechanism proceeds — is captured by noting the 2 % share of CER proceeds and the 2023‑2025 resource mobilization target of approximately USD 300 m. The WIM’s loss‑and‑damage work programme is summarised by its three pillars (enhancing knowledge, strengthening dialogue, and enhancing action and support) and the 2022 decision to establish a dedicated funding arrangement, without conflating it with the GCF’s mitigation window. Quantitative commitments are expressed with the original currency and year (e.g., “USD 10 bn pledged for 2024‑2027 GCF replenishment”). Collapsing the three mechanisms into a single “climate fund” entry erases the examiner’s test of institutional differentiation.

Technique: Isolating Conditional Language in Concluding Observations
A concluding observation reads: “The Committee is concerned that the State party has not yet enacted legislation to criminalise marital rape (Art. 3, 7 ICCPR). It recommends that the State party adopt such legislation without delay and report on progress within twelve months.” Write a one‑sentence précis that preserves the legal basis, the concern, the recommendation, and the follow‑up deadline.
The Committee, citing ICCPR Articles 3 and 7, expresses concern over the absence of legislation criminalising marital rape, urges the State party to enact such legislation without delay, and requests a progress report within twelve months.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a passage contains multiple treaty bodies, first label each paragraph with the body’s acronym (CCPR, CESCR, CEDAW). Then extract article numbers, modal verbs, and deadlines in a table on scratch paper. This visual map prevents omission of conditional language and ensures each recommendation is linked to its legal source.

Précis of National Security Strategy Documents: Threat Assessment, Capability Gaps, and Conditional Commitments

National security strategies (NSS) published by governments — such as the United States 2022 National Security Strategy, the United Kingdom 2023 Integrated Review Refresh, and India’s draft National Security Strategy — share a three‑part structure: strategic environment assessment, national objectives, and implementation roadmap. A précis must capture the threat taxonomy (state‑based, non‑state, transnational, technological) with the document’s own terminology (e.g., “great‑power competition”, “hybrid threats”, “critical‑infrastructure vulnerability”). Capability gaps are recorded by domain (defence, intelligence, cyber, space) and by the specific shortfall metric (e.g., “shortfall of approximately 12 % in submarine‑launched ballistic missile readiness”). Conditional commitments — often phrased as “subject to congressional appropriation”, “pending allied consensus”, or “contingent on technological maturity” — are reproduced verbatim because they define the legal enforceability of the pledge. The implementation roadmap’s timelines (near‑term 0‑2 years, mid‑term 3‑5 years, long‑term 6‑10 years) and responsible agencies (e.g., Department of Defense, Ministry of Defence, National Security Council) are condensed into a single clause per horizon. Merging distinct threat categories or dropping the conditional qualifier converts a nuanced policy statement into an unqualified promise, a frequent examiner trap.

Handling Counterfactual Reasoning in Economic Impact Assessments: Synthetic Controls, Difference‑in‑Differences, and Scenario Weighting

Economic impact assessments (EIAs) for large infrastructure projects, trade agreements, or regulatory reforms routinely employ counterfactual methods to estimate the “without‑project” outcome. A précis must name the identification strategy (synthetic control method, difference‑in‑differences with staggered adoption, instrumental variables, or computable general equilibrium modelling) and the key validity assumptions (parallel trends, no spillovers, exclusion restriction). For synthetic controls, the donor pool composition, pre‑treatment fit statistics (RMSPE), and the number of post‑treatment periods are recorded (e.g., “synthetic control built from 15 donor regions, pre‑treatment RMSPE ≈ 0.018, 8 post‑treatment years”). Difference‑in‑differences specifications require the treatment cohort definition, the event‑study window, and the clustering level for standard errors (e.g., “state‑level clustering, 4‑year pre‑trend window”). Scenario weighting — often presented as a probability distribution over GDP growth paths — is summarised by the central estimate, the 5th‑95th percentile range, and the weighting methodology (Monte‑Carlo simulation, expert elicitation). Omitting the identification label or the uncertainty interval yields a deterministic claim that examiners mark as methodological incompleteness.

Précis of Judicial Review of Administrative Action: Standards of Review, Proportionality, and Remedial Orders

Judicial review judgments across common‑law jurisdictions — India (Articles 226/227), United Kingdom (Administrative Court), Canada (Federal Courts Act), Australia (Administrative Decisions (Judicial Review) Act) — apply a hierarchy of review standards: legality, rationality (Wednesbury unreasonableness), proportionality, and procedural fairness. A précis must identify the statutory or constitutional source of the power under review, the specific ground invoked (e.g., “failure to consider relevant considerations under the applicable administrative procedure provision”), and the standard the court applied (e.g., “proportionality analysis under the four‑stage test: legitimate aim, suitability, necessity, balancing”). The court’s remedial order — quashing order, mandatory order, declaration, or stay — is recorded with its temporal scope (e.g., “quashing order effective from date of judgment, with a six‑month suspension to allow the authority to remake the decision”). Dissenting or concurring opinions that articulate a different standard (e.g., “Justice X would have applied the ‘reasonableness’ test instead of proportionality”) are noted by judge name and the alternative test. Collapsing all grounds into a generic “unreasonableness” label or omitting the remedial detail removes the doctrinal precision examiners expect.

  • Dropping treaty‑article citations in concluding observations, turning a legal concern into a generic comment.
  • Merging distinct climate finance mechanisms (GCF, AF, WIM) into a single “fund” entry, losing institutional differentiation.
  • Omitting conditional qualifiers (“subject to appropriation”, “pending consensus”) in national security strategies, presenting contingent pledges as firm commitments.
  • Failing to name the counterfactual identification strategy (synthetic control, DiD, IV) in economic impact assessments, producing a methodological black box.
  • Collapsing multiple judicial review standards (legality, rationality, proportionality) into a single “unreasonableness” label, erasing doctrinal hierarchy.
  • Neglecting the remedial order’s temporal scope (immediate vs. suspended) in administrative law précis, making the outcome ambiguous.
  • Replacing calibrated uncertainty language (e.g., “5th‑95th percentile range”) with vague terms like “wide range”, altering the probabilistic meaning.

These traps illustrate how precision in legal citation, institutional taxonomy, conditional language, methodological labeling, doctrinal hierarchy, remedial detail, and uncertainty qualification determines the difference between a passing and a distinguished précis.

Précis of United Nations Human Rights Council Universal Periodic Review Reports: Recommendations, State Responses, and Follow‑Up Mechanisms

The Universal Periodic Review (UPR) is a peer‑review mechanism established by General Assembly resolution 60/251 in 2006. Each cycle subjects all 193 UN member states to a review based on three documents: the national report, a compilation of UN treaty‑body and special‑procedure information, and a summary of stakeholder submissions. A précis of a UPR outcome must capture the three‑column structure of the final report: the list of recommendations, the state’s position (accepted, noted, or partially accepted), and the voluntary commitments made for the next cycle. The examiner expects the candidate to preserve the thematic clustering of recommendations — civil and political rights, economic, social and cultural rights, rights of specific groups — because the Council’s “thematic clustering” guides implementation monitoring.

When condensing, retain the exact wording of recommendations that invoke specific treaty articles (e.g., “ratify the Optional Protocol to the Convention against Torture (CAT‑OP)”) and the state’s formal response category. Omitting the response category converts a nuanced diplomatic record into a simple “to‑do” list, a frequent error. The follow‑up mechanism, introduced in 2011 through Human Rights Council resolution 16/21, requires the state to submit a mid‑term report on implementation; the précis should note whether the state has committed to a mid‑term report and whether civil‑society shadow reports are referenced. The “UPR info” database assigns each recommendation a unique identifier (e.g., A/HRC/45/12‑Rec. 124.3); citing this identifier demonstrates methodological rigour.

Comparative précis across two cycles must highlight shifts in acceptance rates, new thematic clusters, and the emergence of “cross‑regional” recommendations. The candidate should also note the role of the “troika” — three rapporteur states that facilitate the review — because their questions often shape the final recommendation language. Finally, the précis must distinguish between “accepted” recommendations (which generate a legal expectation of implementation) and “noted” recommendations (which remain political aspirations), preserving the diplomatic nuance that examiners test.

Summarizing Climate Finance Tracking Reports: Commitment Accounting, Disbursement Ratios, and Additionality Criteria

Climate finance tracking reports — produced by the OECD‑DAC, the UNFCCC Standing Committee on Finance, and the Green Climate Fund (GCF) — follow a standardized reporting framework that distinguishes between “commitments” (pledged amounts), “contributions” (funds transferred to multilateral channels), and “disbursements” (actual outflows to projects). A précis must reproduce the three‑tier accounting: the headline commitment figure (e.g., USD 100 billion per year by 2020), the contribution‑to‑commitment ratio, and the disbursement‑to‑contribution ratio. Dropping any tier collapses the financial pipeline into a single number, a trap that examiners flag as “methodological opacity”.

Additionality is the principle that climate finance must be new and additional to existing official development assistance (ODA). Reports classify finance as “new” (not previously reported), “additional” (above a baseline ODA level), or “re‑labelled” (existing ODA re‑categorised). The précis must record the baseline year used (often 2009‑2010 average ODA) and the percentage of finance meeting the additionality test. The GCF’s “results management framework” further requires reporting on “paradigm‑shift potential”, “sustainable development co‑benefits”, and “gender‑responsive” markers; these qualitative tags must be summarised, not omitted.

When multiple sources are synthesized — OECD‑DAC CRS data, UNFCCC biennial reports, and GCF project‑level disclosures — the précis should note methodological differences: CRS uses “commitment basis” while GCF uses “disbursement basis”. The candidate must flag any double‑counting risk (e.g., a bilateral contribution that also appears in a multilateral channel) and indicate how the source report treats it (usually via a “net‑flow” adjustment). Finally, the précis should capture the “conditionalities” attached to finance — such as “subject to co‑financing” or “contingent on policy reform” — because these qualifiers affect the credibility of the headline numbers.

Worked Example: Condensing a GCF Project Summary
A GCF project document states: “The project aims to enhance climate resilience of coastal communities in Country X through mangrove restoration (USD 12 million GCF grant, USD 3 million co‑financing from the national budget). The grant is conditional on the government enacting a coastal‑zone management law by Q2 2025. Expected outcomes: 5,000 hectares restored, 120,000 beneficiaries, 0.8 MtCO₂e sequestration over 20 years. The project is classified as ‘high paradigm‑shift potential’ and ‘gender‑responsive’.” Write a 60‑word précis preserving all conditionalities, quantitative targets, and qualitative tags.
GCF grant USD 12 m plus USD 3 m national co‑financing for mangrove restoration in Country X; conditional on coastal‑zone law enactment by Q2 2025. Targets: 5,000 ha restored, 120,000 beneficiaries, 0.8 MtCO₂e sequestered over 20 years. Tagged high paradigm‑shift potential, gender‑responsive.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a précis prompt asks for “key financial flows”, first list the three accounting tiers (commitment, contribution, disbursement) in a margin note. Then map each figure in the source text to its tier before writing. This prevents accidental merging of commitment and disbursement numbers, a common source of mark loss.

Précis of National Election Commission Orders: Model Code of Conduct Violations, Adjudication Timelines, and Remedial Directions

Election Commission orders issued under Article 324 of the Constitution of India (and analogous provisions in other jurisdictions) follow a quasi‑judicial format: a statement of facts, the specific Model Code of Conduct (MCC) clause alleged to be violated, the evidence considered, the legal reasoning, and the operative direction. A précis must preserve the MCC clause reference (e.g., “Clause 4.1.2 — prohibition on use of government transport for campaign”) because examiners test the candidate’s ability to map a factual scenario to the exact regulatory provision.

Adjudication timelines are critical: the Commission often issues a “show‑cause notice” with a 48‑hour response window, followed by a hearing and a final order within 7‑10 days. The précis should record the statutory deadline (e.g., “order passed within 10 days of notice under Section 123 of the Representation of the People Act, 1951”) and any extensions granted. Remedial directions vary — censure, bar from campaigning for a specified period, removal of offending material, or referral to the police for criminal prosecution. Each remedy must be listed with its duration or condition (e.g., “bar from campaigning for 48 hours in Constituency Y”).

When multiple orders are issued in a single election cycle, a comparative précis should tabulate the frequency of each MCC clause invoked, the consistency of penalties, and any precedent‑setting reasoning (e.g., “first instance where social‑media live‑streaming was treated as ‘public meeting’ under Clause 4.3”). The candidate must also note dissenting opinions of Commission members, if recorded, because they reveal interpretive splits that may appear in later case‑law questions.

Handling Counterfactual Reasoning in Public Health Intervention Evaluations: Synthetic Controls, Interrupted Time Series, and Mediation Analysis

Public health impact evaluations increasingly rely on quasi‑experimental designs to estimate the counterfactual “what would have happened without the intervention”. The synthetic control method (SCM) constructs a weighted combination of untreated units that reproduces the pre‑intervention trajectory of the treated unit. A précis must name the donor pool, the pre‑intervention fit metric (e.g., root‑mean‑square prediction error), and the number of post‑intervention periods observed. Omitting the donor‑pool composition or the fit statistic renders the causal claim unverifiable.

Interrupted time series (ITS) designs require at least eight pre‑ and eight post‑intervention data points to estimate level and slope changes. The précis should report the estimated immediate level change, the change in trend, the statistical significance (p‑value or confidence interval), and any autocorrelation correction (e.g., Newey‑West standard errors). When multiple interventions occur simultaneously, a “multiple‑ITS” with interaction terms must be summarised, noting the identification assumption that no other concurrent policy changes affect the outcome.

Mediation analysis decomposes the total effect into direct and indirect pathways (e.g., a vaccination campaign reduces disease incidence directly and indirectly via increased health‑seeking behaviour). The précis must specify the mediator, the estimation method (e.g., parametric regression‑based mediation with bootstrap confidence intervals), and the proportion mediated. Reporting only the total effect without the mediation breakdown obscures policy‑relevant mechanisms, a frequent examiner criticism.

Précis of International Maritime Organization Conventions: Safety Standards, Pollution Prevention, and Enforcement Regimes

The International Maritime Organization (IMO) adopts conventions that become binding on ratifying states after entry‑into‑force conditions are met. The three core conventions — SOLAS (1974), MARPOL (1973/78), and STCW (1978) — each contain annexes, codes, and amendments that create a layered regulatory architecture. A précis must identify the specific convention, the relevant annex or code (e.g., “MARPOL Annex VI Regulation 13 — NOx emission limits”), and the amendment status (e.g., “2021 amendment entering into force 1 January 2023”). Dropping the amendment reference converts a current obligation into an outdated one.

Enforcement regimes differ: flag‑state enforcement (primary), port‑state control (PSC) under the Paris MoU or Tokyo MoU, and coastal‑state jurisdiction for pollution incidents. The précis should record the inspection regime (e.g., “PSC inspection targeting vessels with detention history > 2 in 36 months”), the deficiency categories (e.g., “fire‑safety, lifesaving appliances, pollution prevention”), and the follow‑up actions (detention, rectification deadline, flag‑state notification). When a convention establishes a “mandatory audit scheme” (e.g., IMO Member State Audit Scheme), the précis must note the audit cycle (every 7 years) and the consequence of non‑compliance (possible suspension of certificates).

Cross‑referencing is essential: a single vessel may be subject to SOLAS Chapter II‑2 (fire protection), MARPOL Annex I (oil pollution), and the Polar Code (if operating in polar waters). The précis should map overlapping requirements and highlight any “equivalency” provisions that allow alternative compliance (e.g., “equivalent fire‑extinguishing system approved by the Administration”). Failure to capture these interactions produces a fragmented summary that misses the systemic nature of maritime regulation.

  • Merging commitment, contribution, and disbursement figures into a single climate‑finance number, losing pipeline transparency.
  • Omitting the state’s formal UPR response category (accepted/noted) and presenting all recommendations as binding obligations.
  • Dropping the exact Model Code of Conduct clause reference in Election Commission orders, making the violation untraceable.
  • Summarising a synthetic‑control evaluation without reporting donor‑pool composition or pre‑treatment fit statistics.
  • Condensing an IMO convention summary without citing the specific annex, regulation, and amendment entry‑into‑force date.
  • Failing to distinguish flag‑state, port‑state, and coastal‑state enforcement mechanisms in maritime précis, producing a monolithic “enforcement” claim.
  • Presenting total effect estimates from mediation analysis without reporting the indirect pathway or proportion mediated.

These traps illustrate how precise citation of regulatory provisions, methodological identifiers, and enforcement layers separates a competent précis from a distinguished one.

Précis of Scientific Data Repositories and Open Science Practices: Metadata Standards, Versioning, and Reproducibility

Scientific data repositories such as Dryad, Figshare, Zenodo, and institutional archives require a précis that captures the metadata schema, versioning policy, and reproducibility guarantees. The summary must name the metadata standard used (e.g., DataCite Metadata Schema 4.4, Dublin Core, or FAIR‑compliant profiles) and indicate whether persistent identifiers (DOI, ARK, Handle) are minted at deposition or upon publication. Versioning rules are critical: a précis should state whether the repository supports immutable snapshots, incremental version numbers (v1.0, v1.1), or branching, and whether each version receives a distinct identifier. Reproducibility information includes the availability of computational environments (Docker images, Conda environment files, Binder links), the licensing terms for data reuse (CC0, CC‑BY, custom licences), and any embargo periods. When the source text describes a data‑paper pair, the précis must link the article DOI to the dataset DOI and note any peer‑review status of the data descriptor. Omitting the metadata standard or versioning model makes it impossible for a reader to assess the long‑term citability and reusability of the deposited assets.

Précis of International Tax Treaties: Double Taxation Avoidance, Transfer Pricing, and Mutual Agreement Procedures

Bilateral tax treaties follow the OECD Model Tax Convention (or UN Model for developing‑country negotiations). A précis must identify the treaty parties, the date of signature, and the entry‑into‑force provisions. Core articles to capture include Article 4 (Residence), Article 5 (Permanent Establishment), Article 7 (Business Profits), Article 9 (Associated Enterprises) for transfer‑pricing adjustments, and Article 25 (Mutual Agreement Procedure, MAP). The summary should record any specific anti‑abuse rules such as Limitation‑on‑Benefits (LOB) clauses, Principal Purpose Test (PPT) language, or the Multilateral Instrument (MLI) modifications that replace or supplement bilateral provisions. For transfer pricing, note whether the treaty adopts the arm’s‑length principle explicitly, references the OECD Transfer Pricing Guidelines, and provides for corresponding adjustments. MAP details require the competent authority designation, the time‑limit for initiating a request (usually three years from first notification), and the availability of arbitration (e.g., Article 25(5) arbitration clause). If the treaty contains a “savings clause” preserving domestic anti‑avoidance rules, the précis must flag it because it limits treaty benefits for residents of the other state. Excluding the MAP framework or the specific transfer‑pricing article renders the summary incomplete for practitioners who need to assess dispute‑resolution pathways.

Technique: Mapping Treaty Articles to Précis Elements
A source text describes a 2022 treaty between Country A and Country B that incorporates the MLI, includes an LOB clause, adopts the arm’s‑length principle in Article 9, and provides for mandatory binding arbitration under Article 25(5). Write a one‑sentence précis capturing the treaty’s dispute‑resolution and transfer‑pricing framework.
The 2022 Country A–Country B tax treaty, modified by the MLI, embeds an LOB clause, applies the arm’s‑length principle in Article 9 for transfer‑pricing adjustments, and establishes a mandatory binding arbitration mechanism under Article 25(5) for unresolved MAP cases.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a treaty précis appears, first list the model convention (OECD/UN) and any MLI overlay. Then tick off the five pillars: residence, permanent establishment, business profits, transfer pricing, and MAP/arbitration. This checklist prevents omission of the arbitration clause, a frequent examiner trap.

Précis of Space Law Instruments: Outer Space Treaty, Liability Convention, and Registration Practices

The corpus of space law rests on five United Nations treaties, but the Outer Space Treaty (1967), the Rescue Agreement (1968), the Liability Convention (1972), the Registration Convention (1975), and the Moon Agreement (1979) form the core. A précis of a national space legislation or a UN Committee on the Peaceful Uses of Outer Space (COPUOS) report must cite the specific treaty articles invoked. For the Outer Space Treaty, note Article I (freedom of exploration), Article II (non‑appropriation), Article VI (state responsibility for national activities), and Article VII (liability for damage). The Liability Convention requires the précis to distinguish between launching‑state liability (absolute for damage on Earth, fault‑based for damage in space) and the definition of “launching State” (Article I). Registration practice demands the name of the registering State, the date of registration, the orbital parameters (inclination, apogee, perigee), and the function of the space object. When a source discusses debris mitigation guidelines (e.g., IADC or UN COPUOS 2007 guidelines), the précis should indicate whether compliance is voluntary or incorporated into national licensing. Omitting the launching‑state definition or the registration data fields produces a summary that cannot support legal analysis of liability allocation.

Précis of Global Health Governance Documents: WHO International Health Regulations, Pandemic Preparedness Plans, and Equity Mechanisms

The International Health Regulations (IHR) 2005, amended in 2022 (pending entry into force), constitute the binding legal framework for disease surveillance and response. A précis must capture the core capacities required under Annex 1 (surveillance, response, laboratory, risk communication), the decision‑instrument for Public Health Emergency of International Concern (PHEIC) declarations, and the obligations for notification within 24 hours of assessment. Pandemic preparedness plans (e.g., WHO’s “Preparedness and Resilience for Emerging Threats” framework) should be summarised by their strategic pillars: governance, financing, workforce, medical countermeasures, and community engagement. Equity mechanisms such as the COVAX Facility, the Pandemic Influenza Preparedness (PIP) Framework, and the Access to COVID‑19 Tools (ACT) Accelerator require the précis to note the governance structure (e.g., Gavi, CEPI, WHO co‑lead), the allocation formula (population‑weighted, vulnerability‑adjusted), and any advance‑purchase agreements. When a source text references the “One Health” approach, the précis must link human, animal, and environmental health sectors and cite the tripartite collaboration (WHO, FAO, OIE/WOAH). Dropping the 24‑hour notification rule or the COVAX allocation methodology makes the summary insufficient for policy‑level comprehension.

Précis of Artificial Intelligence Ethics Guidelines: Principles, Risk Classification, and Governance Frameworks

Major AI ethics instruments include the OECD AI Principles (2019), the EU Ethics Guidelines for Trustworthy AI (2019), the UNESCO Recommendation on the Ethics of AI (2021), and the US NIST AI Risk Management Framework (2023). A précis must enumerate the high‑level principles (human‑centred values, transparency, accountability, robustness, privacy) and then map the risk‑classification scheme: the EU AI Act’s four tiers (unacceptable, high, limited, minimal), the OECD’s “risk‑based” approach, or NIST’s “Map, Measure, Manage, Govern” functions. Governance structures to capture are the designated supervisory authorities (e.g., national competent authorities under the EU AI Act), conformity‑assessment procedures (self‑assessment, third‑party audit), and post‑market monitoring obligations. When the source discusses “algorithmic impact assessments” or “model cards”, the précis should note the required documentation elements (intended use, performance metrics, limitation, data provenance). Excluding the risk tier or the conformity‑assessment pathway yields a summary that cannot inform compliance planning.

  • Failing to cite the specific metadata schema or persistent‑identifier policy in a data‑repository précis.
  • Omitting the MAP arbitration clause or the LOB/PPT anti‑abuse provisions in a tax‑treaty précis.
  • Neglecting the launching‑state definition or registration orbital parameters in a space‑law précis.
  • Leaving out the 24‑hour IHR notification requirement or the COVAX allocation formula in a global‑health précis.
  • Summarising AI ethics guidelines without stating the risk‑classification tier or the conformity‑assessment mechanism.
  • Conflating voluntary debris‑mitigation guidelines with binding national licensing requirements.
  • Presenting a single “principle list” without linking each principle to its operational governance instrument.

These advanced précis categories demand precise citation of legal articles, technical standards, and governance mechanisms to meet examiner expectations.

Précis of National Security Strategy Documents: Preserving Threat Assessment, Capability Gaps, and Review Cycles

A national security strategy (NSS) is a government’s authoritative statement of its strategic objectives, the threats it perceives, the capabilities it intends to develop, and the timeline for review. A précis must capture three structural pillars: the strategic environment assessment, the ends‑ways‑means framework, and the institutional review mechanism. The strategic environment section typically enumerates state‑based threats (e.g., great‑power competition, regional destabilisation), non‑state threats (terrorism, cyber‑crime, pandemics), and trans‑national challenges (climate change, migration). The précis should note the classification methodology — whether the document uses a “threat‑likelihood‑impact” matrix (as seen in some integrated reviews) or a “priority‑threat” list (as in some national security strategies).

The ends‑ways‑means pillar translates objectives into concrete capability targets. For each strategic end (e.g., “maintain a free and open Indo‑Pacific”), the précis must record the associated ways (diplomatic alliances, forward presence, capacity‑building) and means (budget allocations, force‑structure changes, technology investments). Specificity matters: cite the capability gap identified (e.g., “shortfall of 12 % in maritime patrol aircraft”) and the programmed remedy (e.g., “procurement of 9 P‑8A Poseidon aircraft by FY 2027”). When the source references a “defence industrial base” target, preserve the metric (e.g., “increase domestic defence production share from 45 % to 60 % by 2030”).

The review cycle is the third pillar. Most NSS documents mandate a quadrennial or biennial review, often tied to legislative oversight (e.g., the U.S. Congress receives the NSS annually under the Goldwater‑Nichols Act). The précis should state the statutory basis, the responsible inter‑agency body (National Security Council, Cabinet Committee on Security), and any public‑consultation requirement. Omitting the review trigger (e.g., “major conflict”, “change of government”) produces a summary that cannot support analysis of strategic continuity.

When the source includes annexes — such as a classified annex, a cyber‑strategy annex, or a space‑policy annex — the précis must flag their existence and note whether they are referenced by title only or summarised in the main text. This signals to the examiner that the candidate recognises the layered nature of strategic documents.

Condensing Public Consultation Feedback Reports: Coding Themes, Weighting Submissions, and Preserving Dissent

Public consultation feedback reports aggregate responses to draft legislation, policy papers, or regulatory proposals. A high‑quality précis must demonstrate mastery of three analytical steps: thematic coding, stakeholder weighting, and dissent preservation. Thematic coding begins with a systematic reading of all submissions to generate a codebook. Standard practice (e.g., consistent with “Better Regulation” guidelines in some jurisdictions) uses a two‑level hierarchy: primary themes (e.g., “environmental impact”, “economic burden”, “administrative feasibility”) and sub‑themes (e.g., “carbon‑leakage risk”, “SME compliance cost”). The précis should list the top‑five primary themes by frequency and note any emergent sub‑themes that appear in more than 10 % of submissions.

Stakeholder weighting distinguishes between organisational categories — government agencies, industry associations, civil‑society organisations, academic experts, and individual citizens. Many reports assign a weight reflecting representativeness (e.g., a trade association representing 3,000 firms may be weighted higher than a single‑firm submission). The précis must state the weighting methodology used in the source (explicitly described in the methodology annex) and reproduce the weighted theme distribution. If the source does not disclose weighting, the précis should note the absence and treat all submissions as equal, flagging this limitation.

Dissent preservation is the most examiner‑sensitive element. A consultation report typically highlights consensus areas and records minority views verbatim or in summary. The précis must retain at least one illustrative dissenting argument for each primary theme where dissent exceeds 15 % of weighted submissions.
