For example, if 22 % of weighted industry submissions oppose a proposed emissions‑trading threshold, the précis should capture the core objection (“the threshold imposes disproportionate cost on energy‑intensive sectors without commensurate environmental benefit”) and the stakeholder group. Dropping dissent creates a false impression of unanimity and loses marks for critical appraisal.

Finally, the précis should note the consultation timeline (opening date, closing date, extension periods) and any post‑consultation commitments (e.g., “the ministry will publish a response within 60 days”). These procedural details demonstrate awareness of the regulatory lifecycle.

Thematic Coding Matrix
A consultation on a draft Data Protection Bill receives 1,200 submissions. After coding, the top three primary themes are “Cross‑border data flows” (35 %), “Consent mechanisms” (28 %), and “Regulatory sandbox” (18 %). Industry associations (weight = 2.0) contribute 400 submissions; civil‑society groups (weight = 1.5) contribute 300; individual citizens (weight = 1.0) contribute 500. Dissent on “Consent mechanisms” reaches 20 % of weighted submissions, mainly from civil‑society groups arguing that the bill’s “deemed consent” clause undermines user autonomy. Write a 120‑word précis segment that captures the weighted theme distribution and the dissent.
The consultation on the Data Protection Bill generated 1,200 submissions. Weighted analysis shows “Cross‑border data flows” leading at 35 % of total weighted count, followed by “Consent mechanisms” at 28 % and “Regulatory sandbox” at 18 %. Industry associations (weight 2.0) supplied 400 submissions, civil‑society groups (weight 1.5) 300, and individuals (weight 1.0) 500. Notably, 20 % of weighted submissions dissent on “Consent mechanisms”, chiefly civil‑society groups contending that the “deemed consent” provision erodes user autonomy. The ministry has committed to a formal response within 60 days of the consultation close.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a comprehension passage includes a consultation summary, first sketch a quick two‑column table: Theme | Weighted % (rounded). Then note the highest dissent percentage and the stakeholder group. This visual aid lets you draft the précis segment in under three minutes while preserving the required quantitative nuance.

Précis of International Trade Negotiation Texts: Retaining Tariff Lines, Rules of Origin, and Dispute Settlement Provisions

Trade negotiation texts — whether WTO schedules, bilateral free‑trade agreements (FTAs), or regional accords — are dense with legal‑technical detail. A précis must preserve three layers: market‑access commitments (tariff lines), origin criteria, and the dispute‑settlement architecture. Tariff lines are expressed in the Harmonised System (HS) at the six‑digit level, often with national sub‑headings to eight or ten digits. The précis should list the product categories where the agreement binds tariffs to zero (e.g., “HS 0804.10 – fresh mangoes, bound at 0 %”) and any tariff‑rate quotas (TRQs) with volume and in‑quota/out‑of‑quota rates. If the source provides a staging matrix (e.g., “tariff eliminated over five equal annual stages”), the précis must reproduce the staging schedule.

Rules of origin (ROO) determine whether a good qualifies for preferential treatment. The précis must distinguish between wholly‑obtained criteria, change‑of‑tariff‑classification (CTC) rules, value‑added thresholds, and specific processing requirements (e.g., “textile yarn must undergo spinning and weaving in the party territory”). When the agreement adopts a “regional value content” (RVC) formula, cite the method (build‑down or build‑up) and the minimum percentage (e.g., “RVC ≥ 45 % under the build‑down method”). Also note any cumulation provisions (full, diagonal, or limited) that allow inputs from other parties to count as originating.

The dispute‑settlement chapter typically mirrors the WTO Understanding on Rules and Procedures Governing the Settlement of Disputes (DSU) but may introduce variations: shorter panel timelines (e.g., 90 days vs. 6 months), mandatory mediation, or an appellate review mechanism. The précis should record the key procedural deadlines, the composition of panels (three vs. five members), and any carve‑outs (e.g., “essential security” exception). If the text establishes a standing appellate body, note its membership size and term. Omitting the dispute‑settlement timeline or the ROO method renders the summary useless for trade‑policy analysis.

Finally, capture any “general exceptions” (GATT Art. XX‑type) and “security exceptions” clauses, as these affect the enforceability of commitments. A précis that lists the exception headings without the chapeau conditions (e.g., “necessary to protect public morals”) is incomplete.

Summarizing Judicial Sentencing Guidelines: Preserving Offense Categories, Aggravating/Mitigating Factors, and Departure Mechanisms

Sentencing guidelines translate statutory penalty ranges into structured decision‑making grids. A précis must convey the grid architecture, the factor taxonomy, and the departure framework. The United States Federal Sentencing Guidelines (USSG) use a two‑axis table: offense level (1–43) on the vertical axis and criminal history category (I–VI) on the horizontal axis. The précis should state the base offense level for the principal statute (e.g., “§2B1.1 – theft, base level 6”) and the typical adjustments (e.g., “+2 for loss > $6,500”, “‑2 for minor role”). The United Kingdom Sentencing Council guidelines employ a “starting point” and “category range” for each offence class (e.g., “burglary – starting point 3 years, range 2–6 years”). The précis must note the statutory maximum and any mandatory minimums.

Aggravating and mitigating factors are enumerated in annexes. Common aggravators: use of a weapon, vulnerability of victim, abuse of trust, prior convictions for similar conduct. Common mitigators: early guilty plea (up to one‑third reduction in UK, 2‑level reduction in USSG), remorse, cooperation with authorities, mental disorder. The précis should list the factors that the source highlights as “mandatory considerations” versus “discretionary”. In the USSG, §3E1.1 (acceptance of responsibility) is mandatory; in UK guidelines, “genuine remorse” is discretionary but strongly weighted.

Departure mechanisms allow judges to sentence outside the guideline range. The USSG distinguishes “departures” (policy‑based, e.g., §5K1.1 substantial assistance) from “variances” (post‑Booker 18 U.S.C. §3553(a) factors). The UK permits “departure” only where the court finds the guideline range “contrary to the interests of justice”. The précis must capture the legal standard, the procedural requirement (written reasons, appellate review), and any statutory caps (e.g., USSG §5K2.0 limits upward departures to the statutory maximum). Omitting the distinction between departure and variance, or the UK “interests of justice” test, produces a summary that cannot support legal‑analysis questions.

When the source includes a “sentencing table” excerpt, the précis should reproduce the relevant cell range (e.g., “Offense Level 24, Criminal History III → 63–78 months”) rather than describing it generically. This precision demonstrates command of the guideline’s quantitative core.

Précis of Corporate Restructuring Plans: Capturing Debt Reprofiling, Asset Divestiture, and Governance Changes

Corporate restructuring plans — whether under Chapter 11 (U.S.), the Insolvency and Bankruptcy Code (India), a Scheme of Arrangement (UK), or a voluntary out‑of‑court workout — share a common architecture: a financial restructuring pillar, an operational restructuring pillar, and a governance‑transition pillar. A précis must preserve the quantitative terms of each pillar and the conditionalities that bind them.

Debt reprofiling details include the classes of creditors (secured, unsecured, subordinated), the treatment per class (cash pay‑down, equity conversion, maturity extension, interest‑rate reset), and the voting thresholds required for plan confirmation (e.g., “two‑thirds in value and majority in number of each impaired class” under IBC Section 30). The précis should cite the specific conversion ratio (e.g., “unsecured creditors receive 0.12 equity shares per ₹1 of claim”) and any “cram‑down” provisions that allow confirmation despite dissenting classes.

Asset divestiture is often a condition precedent. The précis must list the assets earmarked for sale (e.g., “non‑core manufacturing plant in Gujarat, book value ₹450 crore”), the expected proceeds, and the timeline (e.g., “sale to be completed within 180 days of effective date”). If the plan includes a “stalking‑horse” bidder, note the bid value and the break‑up fee. Operational restructuring may involve workforce reductions; the précis should capture the headcount reduction target, the severance framework, and any labour‑law approvals required (e.g., “Industrial Disputes Act, 1947 – prior government permission for > 100 workers”).

Governance changes typically involve board reconstitution, appointment of a restructuring officer, and adoption of new financial covenants. The précis should record the new board composition (e.g., “three independent directors, two creditor‑nominees, one management nominee”), the powers of the restructuring officer (e.g., “authority to approve expenditures up to ₹50 crore without board consent”), and the key covenants (e.g., “minimum liquidity ₹200 crore, debt‑to‑EBITDA ≤ 3.0x”). Conditionalities — such as “plan becomes effective only upon receipt of RBI approval for foreign‑currency debt conversion” — must be reproduced verbatim where possible.

Finally, note the exit mechanism: emergence from Chapter 11, issuance of a “certificate of restructuring” under IBC, or court sanction of the Scheme. The précis should state the effective date definition and any post‑emergence reporting obligations (e.g., “quarterly compliance certificates to the monitoring committee for 24 months”).

  • Treating a national security strategy as a simple policy wish‑list and omitting the statutory review trigger.
  • Summarising consultation feedback by raw submission counts instead of weighted stakeholder metrics.
  • Dropping the dissenting minority view when it exceeds the 15 % weighted threshold.
  • Listing tariff eliminations without the staging schedule or the HS code granularity.
  • Confusing rules‑of‑origin methods (CTC vs. RVC) or omitting cumulation provisions.
  • Presenting sentencing guideline ranges without the offense‑level/criminal‑history coordinates.
  • Failing to distinguish between guideline departures and post‑Booker variances, or ignoring the UK “interests of justice” test.
  • Omitting the creditor‑class voting thresholds or the specific equity‑conversion ratio in a restructuring plan.

Précis of International Human Rights Treaty Body Concluding Observations: Preserving State Party Responses, Follow‑Up Procedures, and Thematic Recommendations

Concluding observations issued by United Nations treaty bodies — such as the Human Rights Committee (CCPR), the Committee on Economic, Social and Cultural Rights (CESCR), and the Committee on the Elimination of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW) — follow a structured template: introductory remarks, positive aspects, principal areas of concern, and recommendations. A précis must retain treaty‑specific article references (e.g., ICCPR Art. 6, 7, 14; ICESCR Art. 11, 12) because examiners test whether the candidate can map each concern to the exact legal provision. The state party’s written replies, often submitted under the follow‑up procedure (e.g., the CCPR’s follow‑up mechanism), contain concrete implementation steps, legislative amendments, or institutional reforms. These replies are not optional commentary; they form the evidentiary basis for the next reporting cycle and must be summarised with the same article‑level precision.

The follow‑up procedure itself has a fixed timeline: the treaty body adopts a “list of issues” (LOI), the state replies within twelve months, and the secretariat prepares a “progress report” for the next session. A précis should note the LOI paragraph numbers, the state’s self‑assessed rating (e.g., “partially implemented”, “fully implemented”), and any “request for additional information” flagged by the committee. Thematic recommendations — such as the CEDAW Committee’s General Recommendation No. 39 (2022) on the rights of indigenous women and girls — are cross‑cutting and often referenced across multiple country reviews. When a concluding observation cites a thematic recommendation, the précis must preserve the recommendation number and its operative paragraph, because questions may ask the candidate to link a country‑specific finding to the broader normative framework.

Quantitative data appearing in concluding observations — for instance, a reported maternal mortality ratio declining from 210 to 140 per 100,000 live births over a five‑year period — must be reproduced verbatim. Rounding or paraphrasing loses the evidentiary weight that examiners expect. Similarly, any reference to a “national human rights institution” (NHRI) accreditation status (A‑status under the Paris Principles) should be recorded, as it signals domestic monitoring capacity. Finally, the précis must distinguish between “concerns” (diagnostic) and “recommendations” (prescriptive), because the former describe the factual gap while the latter prescribe remedial action; conflating them leads to loss of marks in analytical questions.

Summarizing Algorithmic Impact Assessments: Bias Metrics, Fairness Constraints, and Mitigation Roadmaps

Algorithmic Impact Assessments (AIAs) have become mandatory in several jurisdictions — the EU’s AI Act (Regulation 2024/1689) requires a “fundamental rights impact assessment” for high‑risk AI systems, while the U.S. Algorithmic Accountability Act (proposed in 2022, not enacted) mandates bias testing for automated decision‑making in credit, employment, and housing. A précis of an AIA must capture three technical pillars: (1) the bias metrics reported (e.g., demographic parity difference, equalized odds difference, disparate impact ratio), (2) the fairness constraints imposed during model training (e.g., constrained optimisation with a Lagrangian multiplier to enforce equal opportunity), and (3) the mitigation roadmap with concrete milestones (e.g., re‑training on a balanced dataset by a target quarter; external audit by an ISO/IEC 42001‑certified body).

The source text often presents a confusion matrix broken down by protected attributes (race, gender, age). The précis should reproduce the key cell values (true‑positive rate, false‑positive rate) for each subgroup rather than describing them qualitatively. If the AIA references a “model card” (per Mitchell et al., 2019), the précis must note the card’s version, intended use cases, and known limitations. Regulatory thresholds are critical: the EU AI Act Annex III lists “credit scoring” as high‑risk, and some frameworks reference the four‑fifths rule (disparate impact ratio ≥ 0.8) as a benchmark. Omitting the applicable numeric threshold makes the summary unusable for compliance‑oriented questions.

Mitigation roadmaps typically include data‑centric steps (re‑sampling, synthetic data generation), algorithm‑centric steps (adversarial debiasing, counterfactual fairness regularisation), and governance steps (establishing an AI ethics board, quarterly bias‑monitoring dashboards). The précis must preserve the sequencing and any conditional dependencies (e.g., adversarial debiasing only after re‑sampling achieves a target subgroup representation). Finally, any reference to external standards — ISO/IEC 23894 (AI risk management), NIST AI RMF 1.0 — should be cited with the specific clause numbers, because examiners may ask the candidate to map the AIA’s mitigation actions to the corresponding normative framework.

Technique: Mapping Bias Metrics to Regulatory Thresholds (Illustrative)
An AIA for a mortgage‑approval model reports a disparate impact ratio of 0.72 for female applicants and 0.85 for applicants over 60. A regulatory benchmark (e.g., the four‑fifths rule) requires a minimum ratio of 0.80 for all protected groups. The assessment proposes re‑sampling the training data to achieve a ratio of 0.82 for females and 0.88 for seniors, followed by adversarial debiasing targeting a final ratio of 0.85 for both groups. Summarise the compliance gap, the proposed interim target, and the final target in a single paragraph of no more than 60 words.
The model fails the 0.80 disparate‑impact threshold for female applicants (0.72) but meets it for seniors (0.85). The AIA sets an interim re‑sampling target of 0.82 (female) and 0.88 (senior), then applies adversarial debiasing to reach a final 0.85 ratio for both groups, achieving full compliance.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When an AIA passage appears, first underline every numeric metric and its associated protected attribute. Then locate the regulatory benchmark (often in a footnote or annex reference). Write the compliance gap as a simple inequality (e.g., 0.72 < 0.80). This three‑step scan — metric, benchmark, gap — lets you construct the required paragraph in under two minutes.

Précis of Multilateral Trade Negotiation Texts: Preserving Bracketed Provisions, Consensus Language, and Reservation Clauses

Negotiating texts of multilateral agreements — such as WTO draft modalities, Regional Comprehensive Economic Partnership (RCEP) working documents, or the UNCITRAL Model Law on Electronic Transferable Records — use a distinctive typography: square brackets indicate unresolved language, curly brackets denote alternative options, and double brackets flag “parked” provisions awaiting political guidance. A précis must reproduce the bracketed status of each clause because the examiner may ask which issues remain contested. For example, a draft article on digital trade might read: “[Each Party shall ensure non‑discriminatory treatment of electronic transmissions] {or} [[Each Party may maintain existing restrictions on cross‑border data flows]]”. The précis should record both alternatives verbatim, noting the bracket type, and indicate the negotiating group (e.g., “G‑90 proposal”, “Quad proposal”) if the source attributes them.

Consensus language — phrases such as “shall endeavour to”, “as appropriate”, “in accordance with domestic law” — softens obligations and is deliberately chosen. The précis must preserve these qualifiers; replacing “shall endeavour to” with “shall” changes the legal effect and will be penalised. Reservation clauses, often found in the final articles of a treaty (e.g., Article 30 of the Vienna Convention on the Law of Treaties), allow parties to exclude or modify specific provisions. When a negotiating text includes a “reservation clause” with a list of permissible reservations (e.g., a provision stating “Reservations may be made only to Articles 5, 7, and 12”), the précis must list the article numbers exactly. If the text specifies a “no‑reservation” rule for core obligations (e.g., “No reservations to Article 2 (most‑favoured‑nation treatment)”), that prohibition must be highlighted.

Quantitative annexes — tariff‑rate quotas (TRQs) with in‑quota and out‑of‑quota rates, staging matrices for tariff elimination (e.g., “Year 1: 5 %; Year 5: 0 %”), and rules‑of‑origin thresholds (e.g., “regional value content ≥ 40 %”) — are integral to the negotiation. The précis should reproduce the numeric schedule for any product category mentioned in the passage, because questions frequently test the ability to calculate the duty at a given year. Finally, any “built‑in agenda” or “review clause” (e.g., “The Parties shall review this Agreement no later than five years after entry into force”) must be captured with its timeline, as it signals future negotiation rounds.

Condensing Scientific Peer‑Review Reports: Capturing Reviewer Comments, Author Rebuttals, and Editorial Decisions

Peer‑review reports for high‑impact journals (Nature, Science, The Lancet) follow a structured format: a summary recommendation (accept, minor revision, major revision, reject), a list of major concerns, minor concerns, and confidential comments to the editor. A précis must preserve the hierarchy: major concerns are methodological flaws that threaten validity (e.g., absence of a power calculation for the primary endpoint; a sample size of n = 42 yielding 0.45 power at α = 0.05), while minor concerns are presentational (e.g., figure 3 axes mislabelled). The author’s rebuttal letter responds point‑by‑point; the précis should record whether each major concern was “fully addressed”, “partially addressed with additional analysis”, or “disputed with justification”. For instance, if the authors add a post‑hoc power analysis showing 0.78 power after recruiting 28 more participants, the précis must note the new n = 70 and the revised power.

Editorial decisions often include conditional acceptance: “Accept pending minor revisions: correct figure labels, add supplementary table S2, and clarify statistical test in Methods.” The précis must list each condition verbatim because examiners may ask which revisions remain outstanding. If the editor requests a “statistical review by an independent biostatistician”, that requirement must be flagged as a separate action item. Confidential comments to the editor — such as a flag on potential image duplication in Figure 4B — are not published but may appear in the source passage; the précis should note the existence of such concerns without reproducing potentially defamatory detail, phrasing it as “editor flagged potential image integrity issue in Figure 4B”.

When the passage includes multiple review rounds (e.g., Round 1: major revision; Round 2: minor revision; Round 3: accept), the précis should present a concise timeline: “Round 1 (illustrative date): major revision — three major concerns; Round 2 (illustrative date): minor revision — two minor concerns; Round 3 (illustrative date): accept.” This chronological mapping demonstrates the candidate’s ability to synthesise iterative feedback. Finally, any “data availability statement” or “code availability” requirement (e.g., raw sequencing data deposited in NCBI SRA under a placeholder accession such as PRJNA123456) must be reproduced with the exact repository and accession number, as these are increasingly tested in research‑integrity questions.

  • Omitting bracket typology in trade texts and treating all language as agreed.
  • Replacing qualified consensus phrasing (“shall endeavour to”) with absolute obligations (“shall”).
  • Summarising bias metrics without the regulatory threshold, making compliance assessment impossible.
  • Failing to distinguish major from minor peer‑review concerns, losing the validity hierarchy.
  • Dropping the exact accession numbers or repository names in data‑availability statements.
  • Neglecting the follow‑up timeline in treaty‑body concluding observations, which is essential for implementation tracking.
  • Review these traps before the exam to avoid common errors.

Précis of WTO Dispute Settlement Panel and Appellate Body Reports: Preserving Findings, Recommendations, and Reasoning on Covered Agreements

A WTO panel report follows the structure mandated by the Dispute Settlement Understanding (DSU): a factual section, a legal analysis under the covered agreements (GATT 1994, GATS, TRIPS, SCM, TBT, etc.), and a concluding recommendation under Article 19.1. The précis must reproduce the exact provision cited (e.g., “Article III:4 of GATT 1994”) and the panel’s interpretative reasoning, including any reference to the Vienna Convention on the Law of Treaties Articles 31‑33. When the Appellate Body reviews a panel report, its review is limited to issues of law and legal interpretation (DSU Article 17.6); the précis therefore distinguishes between factual findings (which are not appealable) and legal conclusions (which are). Landmark reports such as United States — Import Prohibition of Certain Shrimp and Shrimp Products (WT/DS58/AB/R) illustrate the “chapeau” analysis of Article XX; the précis must capture the four‑part test (necessity, arbitrary discrimination, disguised restriction) and the Appellate Body’s emphasis on “good faith” negotiation. In European Communities — Measures Concerning Meat and Meat Products (Hormones) (WT/DS26/AB/R, WT/DS48/AB/R), the précis records the scientific‑evidence standard under the SPS Agreement (Article 5.1) and the distinction between “risk assessment” and “risk management”. Quantitative elements — such as the level of nullification or impairment calculated under Article 22.6 — must be reproduced verbatim because examiners often ask for the exact figure (e.g., approximately US$ 165 million in a notable arbitration). Any “reasonable period of time” for implementation (Article 21.3) and the subsequent arbitration award (Article 21.3(c)) are also essential. Finally, the précis notes whether the report was adopted by the DSB (consensus‑negative rule) and any subsequent compliance panel proceedings.

Précis of International Criminal Court Judgments: Capturing Elements of Crimes, Modes of Liability, and Reparations Orders

An ICC judgment under the Rome Statute is organized into three pillars: (1) the legal characterization of the conduct under Articles 6 (genocide), 7 (crimes against humanity), and 8 (war crimes); (2) the mode of liability under Article 25 (perpetration, co‑perpetration, ordering, aiding and abetting, contribution to a group crime); and (3) the reparations phase under Article 75. The précis must list each charged crime with its specific elements — for genocide, the “intent to destroy, in whole or in part, a national, ethnical, racial or religious group” (Art 6) and the underlying acts (killing, causing serious bodily harm, etc.). In Prosecutor v. Thomas Lubanga Dyilo (ICC‑01/04‑01/06), the Trial Chamber’s finding that the enlistment and conscription of children under fifteen constituted the war crime of “conscripting or enlisting children” (Art 8(2)(b)(xxvi)) must be reproduced with the Chamber’s interpretation of “active participation in hostilities”. The Appeals Chamber’s clarification in Prosecutor v. Germain Katanga (ICC‑01/04‑01/07) on the “common purpose” doctrine for co‑perpetration (Art 25(3)(a)) is a frequent examination point. Reparations orders require the précis to capture the three categories: restitution, compensation, and rehabilitation, together with the Trust Fund for Victims’ implementation plan. The judgment’s “findings of fact” (e.g., the number of child soldiers recruited, the geographic scope of attacks) are not appealable, whereas the “legal findings” (e.g., whether the acts meet the contextual element of a non‑international armed conflict) are. Any dissenting or separate opinions (e.g., Judge Van den Wyngaert’s partial dissent in Bemba on the scope of command responsibility under Art 28) must be summarized with their doctrinal impact.

Technique: Mapping Operative Paragraphs in UNSC Resolutions
A passage reproduces the operative part of UNSC Resolution 1373 (2001). The question asks: “Identify the three binding obligations imposed on all Member States and the reporting deadline established by the resolution.”
Resolution 1373 was adopted under Chapter VII, making its operative paragraphs binding under Article 25 of the UN Charter. The three core obligations are: (1) prevent and suppress the financing of terrorist acts (para 1); (2) criminalize the provision of funds for terrorist purposes (para 2); (3) deny safe haven to those who finance, plan, or commit terrorist acts (para 3). Paragraph 6 establishes the Counter‑Terrorism Committee and requires each State to submit a report on the measures taken within 90 days of adoption. The précis must list the paragraph numbers, the exact wording of each obligation, and the 90‑day deadline, because examiners test the ability to distinguish binding operative language from preambular recitals.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a passage contains a multi‑section legal instrument (WTO report, ICC judgment, UNSC resolution), first annotate the structural headings (facts, law, recommendations; elements, liability, reparations; preambular, operative). Then extract only the operative or dispositive language — recommendations, findings of law, binding obligations — and discard descriptive background. Use a two‑column note: left column for provision numbers, right column for the précis‑ready phrasing. This prevents omission of numeric thresholds (e.g., “US$ 165 million”, “90 days”) that are high‑yield marks.

Précis of Data Protection Impact Assessments under the GDPR: Retaining Processing Purposes, Risk Levels, and Mitigation Measures

A Data Protection Impact Assessment (DPIA) mandated by Article 35 of the General Data Protection Regulation (EU 2016/679) follows the structure set out in the WP29 Guidelines (WP 248). The précis must capture: (1) the systematic description of the processing operations, including the nature, scope, context, and purposes (e.g., “large‑scale biometric identification for access control in a multinational corporation”); (2) the assessment of necessity and proportionality — whether the processing is “necessary for the performance of a task carried out in the public interest” (Art 6(1)(e)) or relies on legitimate interest (Art 6(1)(f)) with a balancing test; (3) the risk evaluation matrix, grading each identified risk to the rights and freedoms of data subjects as “low”, “medium”, or “high” (e.g., “risk of function creep — high”); and (4) the mitigation measures with responsible parties and timelines (e.g., “implement pseudonymisation within 30 days; appoint Data Protection Officer by 1 July 2024”). Where the DPIA triggers prior consultation under Article 36, the précis records the supervisory authority’s opinion and any imposed conditions (e.g., “the authority required a data‑minimisation audit before go‑live”). Quantitative thresholds — such as the number of data subjects affected (e.g., “≈ 2.3 million employees”) or the retention period (e.g., “12 months after termination of employment”) — are reproduced exactly. The DPIA’s version history (initial draft, stakeholder review, final sign‑off) is also relevant when the passage includes iterative updates, because examiners may ask which version was submitted to the authority.

Précis of UN Security Council Resolutions: Distinguishing Binding Decisions, Chapter VII Measures, and Reporting Obligations

UNSC resolutions contain preambular paragraphs (non‑binding context) and operative paragraphs (binding when adopted under Chapter VII). The précis must first identify the Chapter under which the resolution was adopted — Chapter VI (pacific settlement) yields recommendations; Chapter VII (threats to peace, breaches of the peace, acts of aggression) yields decisions binding on all Member States under Article 25. For Chapter VII resolutions, the précis extracts: (1) the specific measures — sanctions (asset freezes, travel bans, arms embargoes), authorization of force, or establishment of subsidiary bodies (e.g., the Counter‑Terrorism Committee under Resolution 1373); (2) the targeted entities or criteria (e.g., “individuals and entities listed in Annex I of Resolution 1267 (1999) as associated with Al‑Qaida”); (3) the reporting obligations — frequency, addressee, and content (e.g., “Member States shall report to the Committee within 90 days on the implementation of the asset freeze”); and (4) any review or sunset clauses (e.g., “the measures shall be reviewed no later than 12 months after adoption”). Landmark resolutions illustrate the pattern: Resolution 1540 (2004) on non‑proliferation obliges States to adopt effective domestic controls and report to the 1540 Committee; Resolution 242 (1967) on the Middle East, adopted under Chapter VI, contains only “principles” and “calls upon” language, which the précis must label as non‑binding. When a resolution establishes a monitoring mechanism (e.g., the Panel of Experts on Sudan under Resolution 1591 (2005)), the précis notes the mandate, reporting cycle, and the Council’s subsequent action on the panel’s recommendations.

Précis of Health Technology Assessment Reports: Preserving Clinical Effectiveness, Cost‑Effectiveness Thresholds, and Recommendation Grades

Health Technology Assessment (HTA) reports produced by agencies such as NICE (UK), IQWiG (Germany), and CADTH (Canada) follow a standardized appraisal framework. The précis must capture: (1) the clinical effectiveness evidence — typically expressed as incremental quality‑adjusted life years (QALYs) gained versus comparator (e.g., “pembrolizumab plus chemotherapy yielded an incremental 0.42 QALYs over chemotherapy alone in metastatic NSCLC”); (2) the cost‑effectiveness analysis — incremental cost‑effectiveness ratio (ICER) and the agency’s willingness‑to‑pay threshold (NICE: £20,000‑£30,000 per QALY; IQWiG: €30,000‑€50,000 per QALY); (3) the uncertainty analysis — probabilistic sensitivity analysis results (e.g., “probability of cost‑effectiveness at £30,000/QALY = 0.68”); (4) the recommendation grade — “recommended”, “not recommended”, “recommended only in research”, or “optimised” (NICE terminology); and (5) any managed‑access arrangements or commercial‑in‑confidence discounts (e.g., “patient access scheme reduces acquisition cost by 15 %”). For instance, in a NICE appraisal of pembrolizumab (TA 683, 2021), the committee recommended use within the Cancer Drugs Fund for a specific population (e.g., PD‑L1 TPS ≥ 50%), noting an ICER after discount near the threshold and a scheduled review. For IQWiG, the “added benefit” classification (major, considerable, minor, non‑quantifiable) and the resulting price‑negotiation outcome under the AMNOG process are essential. The précis also records the methodological hierarchy: systematic review → network meta‑analysis → economic model → committee deliberation, because examiners test the ability to trace a recommendation back to its evidential base.

  • Omitting the exact treaty article or DSU provision when summarising WTO panel findings, making the legal basis unverifiable.
  • Conflating factual findings (non‑appealable) with legal conclusions (appealable) in ICC judgments, leading to mis‑statement of the appellate standard.
  • Failing to reproduce the risk‑level grading (low/medium/high) and the specific mitigation timeline in a GDPR DPIA, which are the only quantitative anchors examiners can test.
  • Treating preambular language of a UNSC resolution as binding, thereby misclassifying a Chapter VI “call upon” as a Chapter VII obligation.
  • Dropping the willingness‑to‑pay threshold or the ICER value in an HTA précis, which removes the decision‑rule that determines the recommendation grade.
  • Neglecting to note the version history or prior‑consultation outcome in a DPIA, losing the procedural compliance dimension.
  • Summarising a WTO reasonable‑period‑of‑time arbitration award without the exact number of months, which is a frequent numeric‑detail question.
The advanced précis techniques above equip candidates to handle the most technically demanding source texts encountered in compulsory language papers.

Précis of United Nations Human Rights Council Universal Periodic Review Outcomes

The Universal Periodic Review (UPR) was established by General Assembly resolution 60/251 in 2006 and operates on a four‑year cycle. Each State submits a national report and receives a UN compilation and stakeholder summary. The Working Group adopts an outcome report listing recommendations classified as “accepted”, “noted”, or “partially accepted”. A précis must capture: (1) the reviewing State and cycle (e.g., “Third cycle, 2022‑2026”); (2) total recommendations and acceptance rate; (3) thematic clusters — civil and political rights; economic, social and cultural rights; rights of specific groups — with the most frequent recommendations; (4) voluntary commitments or pledges; (5) follow‑up mechanism, noting mid‑term reports or UPR‑Info database responses. For example, a 2023 outcome for Country X recorded 212 recommendations (158 accepted, 42 noted, 12 partially accepted); top clusters were freedom of expression (27), women’s rights (22), and disability rights (15). The précis should note the adoption date (e.g., “Adopted 14 March 2023”) and procedural remarks such as “the delegation requested postponement of recommendation 45 pending domestic legislation”.

Summarizing National Cybersecurity Strategy Documents

National cybersecurity strategies typically follow frameworks from the 2015 UN Group of Governmental Experts report and the 2021 Global Cybersecurity Index methodology. A précis must extract: (1) strategic vision and time horizon (e.g., “Secure digital ecosystem by 2030”); (2) threat landscape — state‑sponsored actors, ransomware, supply‑chain vulnerabilities, AI‑driven attacks; (3) governance architecture — national CERT, sectoral regulators, lead ministry; (4) capacity‑building pillars — workforce development, R&D funding, critical‑infrastructure protection standards (often aligned with ISO/IEC 27001); (5) international cooperation — Budapest Convention participation, bilateral cyber dialogues, UN Open‑Ended Working Group contributions; (6) measurable indicators — incident‑response targets, certified government systems percentage, annual exercises. For instance, India’s draft National Cyber Security Strategy 2020 proposes a 2025 “Cyber‑Resilient India” vision, a dedicated Cyber Security Coordinator, a National Critical Information Infrastructure Protection Centre, and ISO 27001 certification targets for central ministries. The précis should preserve conditional language (“subject to budgetary approval”) and note legislative linkages such as the Personal Data Protection Bill.

Technique: Isolating Binding Recommendations in UPR Outcomes
A UPR outcome for State Y contains 180 recommendations: 112 “accepted”, 48 “noted”, 20 “partially accepted”. The examiner asks: “How many recommendations create a legal obligation for State Y under the UPR mechanism?”
Only “accepted” recommendations generate a political commitment to implement; “noted” and “partially accepted” do not create binding obligations. Thus, 112 recommendations constitute State Y’s UPR commitments. The précis must state: “State Y accepted 112 recommendations, constituting its UPR commitments.”
Exam‑Day Strategy
When reading a UPR outcome, underline classification tags (accepted/noted/partially accepted) in the first pass. Count accepted items immediately to avoid re‑reading under time pressure.

Précis of Public‑Private Partnership Concession Agreements

Concession agreements for infrastructure (roads, ports, airports) follow templates such as the Indian Model Concession Agreement (MCA) for National Highways (2020) or the World Bank’s PPP Contractual Provisions (2017). A précis must retain: (1) concession period and extension triggers (e.g., “30 years, extendable by 5 years if traffic exceeds 1.2 × base‑case”); (2) revenue‑sharing — tolls, availability payments, or shadow tolls with indexation (e.g., “annual escalation = CPI + 1 %”); (3) performance standards — service‑level indicators (pavement condition index ≥ 80, incident response ≤ 30 minutes) and penalty regime; (4) risk allocation — construction risk (contractor), demand risk (government or shared), force‑majeure, change‑in‑law; (5) financing structure — debt‑equity ratio, senior lenders’ step‑in rights, debt service reserve requirements; (6) termination provisions — concessionaire default, authority default, compensation formulas (e.g., “termination payment = 90 % of senior debt outstanding + 10 % of equity invested”); (7) dispute resolution — tiered negotiation, mediation, arbitration under UNCITRAL rules with neutral seat. Also note sovereign guarantees, environmental/social safeguards (e.g., IFC Performance Standards), and hand‑back conditions (asset condition report, residual value).

Condensing Scientific Consensus Reports on Emerging Biotechnology

Consensus reports such as the WHO “Human Genome Editing: A Framework for Governance” (2021) or the National Academies “Gene Drives on the Horizon” (2016) follow a structured appraisal: (1) scope and committee charge; (2) evidence‑gathering — systematic review, expert testimony, public consultation; (3) evidence grading via GRADE (high, moderate, low, very low certainty); (4) key findings grouped by scientific, ethical, legal, social implications; (5) recommendations classified as “strong”, “conditional”, or “research‑only” with justification; (6) implementation roadmap — actors, timelines, monitoring; (7) dissenting or minority views. A précis must preserve exact recommendation wording (e.g., “The committee strongly recommends a moratorium on clinical germline editing until safety and efficacy are established”) and certainty ratings. Quantitative thresholds (e.g., “off‑target mutation rate < 0.1 %”) and conditional language (“provided robust oversight mechanisms are in place”) are essential. Capture the proposed governance model — international registry, national licensing authority, periodic review (e.g., “review every 3 years”).

Précis of International Maritime Organization Safety and Pollution Conventions

The core IMO conventions — SOLAS (1974, as amended), MARPOL (1973/78), STCW (1978, as amended), and the Polar Code (2014) — form a regulatory matrix for ship safety, pollution prevention, and seafarer competence. A précis must extract: (1) convention name, adoption and entry‑into‑force dates, major amendment packages (e.g., “SOLAS Chapter II‑2 amended in 2017 for fire‑protection of container ships”); (2) scope — ship types, size thresholds, flag vs. port State responsibilities; (3) key technical standards — subdivision/stability (SOLAS II‑1), fire safety (SOLAS II‑2), oil pollution equipment (MARPOL Annex I), sewage/garbage (Annexes IV‑V), ballast water (BWM Convention 2004); (4) certification and survey regime — initial, annual, intermediate, renewal surveys; issuance of International Ship Safety Certificate, IOPP, ISPP certificates; (5) enforcement — port State control under Paris/Tokyo MOUs, detention criteria, flag State audits under IMO Member State Audit Scheme; (6) recent developments — 2020 sulphur cap (MARPOL Annex VI Reg. 14.1.3), 2023 EEXI and CII requirements, ongoing STCW Code revisions for autonomous surface ships. Preserve the hierarchy: convention → annex → regulation → guideline, and note “tacit acceptance” amendment procedures.

  • Omitting UPR recommendation acceptance classifications, turning political commitments into apparent legal obligations.
  • Failing to distinguish “accepted” from “noted” recommendations, altering the obligation count.
  • Dropping conditional language (e.g., “subject to budgetary approval”) in cybersecurity strategies, presenting aspirations as firm targets.
  • Summarising a concession agreement without the termination compensation formula, leaving financial risk incomplete.
  • Neglecting GRADE certainty ratings in biotechnology reports, obscuring recommendation strength.
  • Leaving out amendment dates or resolution numbers for IMO provisions, making standards unverifiable.
  • Review each précis for omitted conditional language before submission.

Précis of Multilateral Climate Finance Mechanisms

Multilateral climate finance mechanisms — principally the Green Climate Fund (GCF), the Adaptation Fund (AF), and the Loss and Damage Fund established under the UNFCCC — operate through distinct governance structures, funding windows, and eligibility criteria. A précis must capture: (1) the legal basis (e.g., GCF governed by the 2010 Cancun Agreements, AF by the Kyoto Protocol’s Article 12, Loss and Damage Fund by the 2022 Sharm el‑Sheikh decision); (2) capitalisation targets and replenishment cycles (GCF USD 10.3 billion first replenishment 2020‑2023, AF USD 1.2 billion 2023‑2025); (3) allocation modalities — grants, concessional loans, guarantees, and results‑based payments — with the proportion of grant‑equivalent financing explicitly stated; (4) access modalities — direct access through accredited national entities, international access via multilateral implementing entities, and the role of the Private Sector Facility; (5) results‑framework indicators — mitigation tonnes CO₂e avoided, adaptation beneficiaries, and loss‑and‑damage compensation metrics; (6) safeguard policies — environmental and social safeguards (GCF’s Environmental and Social Policy, AF’s Environmental and Social Policy), gender‑responsive budgeting, and indigenous‑peoples’ consent procedures; (7) governance — board composition (equal representation of developed and developing countries), decision‑making rules (consensus or double‑majority), and independent evaluation arrangements. The précis should preserve any conditional language such as “subject to the availability of resources” or “contingent upon the fulfilment of co‑financing requirements”, because these qualifiers determine the binding nature of commitments.

Précis of National Security Strategy Documents

National security strategy (NSS) documents — whether titled “National Security Strategy”, “Defence White Paper”, or “Strategic Defence Review” — follow a recurring analytical architecture. A précis must retain: (1) the strategic context — geopolitical environment, threat taxonomy (state‑based, non‑state, hybrid, cyber, climate‑security), and the assessment of relative power shifts; (2) national interests — survival, sovereignty, economic prosperity, values, and international order; (3) strategic objectives — deterrence, defence, resilience, influence, and partnership, each linked to measurable end‑states; (4) capability priorities — force structure, modernisation programmes, procurement timelines, and budgetary envelopes (e.g., “allocate 2.5 % of GDP to defence by 2027”); (5) institutional reforms — joint‑command structures, intelligence integration, civil‑military coordination mechanisms, and legislative oversight provisions; (6) risk management — scenario planning, red‑team exercises, and contingency triggers; (7) implementation architecture — lead agencies, inter‑ministerial committees, reporting cycles (annual parliamentary review), and performance indicators. Particular attention is required for conditional phrasing such as “subject to parliamentary approval”, “pending fiscal space”, or “in coordination with allies”, which convert aspirational goals into contingent commitments. The précis should also note any dissenting annexes or minority views that signal doctrinal debate.

Technique: Isolating Conditional Commitments in Strategy Texts
A paragraph reads: “The government will modernise its naval fleet, procuring six frigates by 2030, provided that the defence budget reaches 2.5 % of GDP and that the industrial base demonstrates capacity for indigenous construction.” Write a one‑sentence précis entry that preserves the conditionality.
The government plans to procure six frigates by 2030 contingent on the defence budget attaining 2.5 % of GDP and verified indigenous shipbuilding capacity.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a strategy document contains multiple “provided that” or “subject to” clauses, underline each condition in the source text. In the précis, place the condition immediately after the main commitment using “contingent on” or “conditional upon”. This prevents the examiner from marking the summary as an unqualified assertion.

Précis of Corporate Governance Codes

Corporate governance codes — such as the UK Corporate Governance Code (2024), the OECD Principles of Corporate Governance (2023 revision), and the SEBI (LODR) Regulations in India — prescribe board composition, committee mandates, disclosure standards, and shareholder rights. A précis must capture: (1) the scope of application — listed entities, state‑owned enterprises, or “comply‑or‑explain” regimes; (2) board structure — separation of chair and CEO, minimum independent director ratio (e.g., ≥ 50 % independent), tenure limits, and diversity targets (gender, ethnicity, skills matrix); (3) committee charters — audit, nomination, remuneration, risk, and sustainability committees, with explicit terms of reference, meeting frequency, and reporting lines; (4) remuneration policy — linkage of variable pay to long‑term performance metrics, clawback provisions, and say‑on‑pay voting thresholds; (5) risk governance — enterprise risk management framework, board‑level risk appetite statement, and internal audit independence; (6) stakeholder engagement — mechanisms for employee representation, supplier codes, and community impact assessments; (7) disclosure regime — integrated reporting, ESG metrics aligned with TCFD/SASB, and the “comply‑or‑explain” disclosure template. The précis should preserve any transitional provisions (e.g., “effective for financial years beginning on or after 1 April 2025”) and the consequences of non‑compliance (regulatory sanctions, delisting risk).

Précis of Public Procurement Regulations

Public procurement frameworks — exemplified by the UNCITRAL Model Law on Public Procurement (2011), the EU Public Procurement Directives (2014/24/EU, 2014/25/EU), and national statutes such as India’s General Financial Rules (GFR) 2017 — share a common procedural skeleton. A précis must retain: (1) threshold values — monetary limits that trigger open‑tender, restricted‑tender, or direct‑award procedures (e.g., EU works contracts ≥ €5.35 million, services ≥ €139 000); (2) procurement methods — open, restricted, competitive dialogue, innovation partnership, and negotiated procedures, with the legal basis for each; (3) evaluation criteria — most economically advantageous tender (MEAT) weighting of price, quality, lifecycle cost, and sustainability; (4) qualification and exclusion grounds — mandatory exclusion for criminal convictions, optional exclusion for grave professional misconduct, and self‑cleaning provisions; (5) transparency obligations — contract award notices, publication timelines (e.g., 30 days post‑award), and e‑procurement portal requirements; (6) review and remedies — standstill periods (minimum 10 calendar days in EU), administrative review bodies, and judicial appeal routes; (7) special regimes — defence and security procurement (Directive 2009/81/EC), concessions, and public‑private partnership specific rules. Conditional language such as “subject to state‑aid clearance” or “provided the tenderer meets the financial‑capacity test” must be reproduced verbatim.

Précis of International Development Evaluation Reports

Evaluation reports produced by multilateral development banks (World Bank IEG, Asian Development Bank IED, African Development Bank IDEV) and bilateral agencies (USAID, FCDO) follow the OECD‑DAC evaluation criteria. A précis must capture: (1) evaluation purpose — accountability, learning, or both; (2) scope — project, programme, country‑strategy, or thematic evaluation; (3) methodology — theory‑of‑change reconstruction, mixed‑methods design, counterfactual construction (e.g., propensity‑score matching, regression discontinuity), and data sources (administrative data, household surveys, key‑informant interviews); (4) findings structured by the six criteria — relevance, coherence, effectiveness, efficiency, impact, and sustainability — with evidence grades (high, moderate, low confidence); (5) lessons learned — generalisable insights, contextual caveats, and actionable recommendations classified as “strategic”, “operational”, or “knowledge‑generation”; (6) management response — acceptance, partial acceptance, or rejection of each recommendation with implementation timelines; (7) follow‑up mechanisms — tracking matrix, mid‑term review, and independent verification. The précis must preserve any “conditional recommendation” phrasing (e.g., “recommend scaling up conditional on the establishment of a robust monitoring framework”) and the confidence rating attached to each finding.

  • Omitting the grant‑equivalent share in climate‑finance summaries, turning blended finance into pure grant figures.
  • Dropping the “subject to parliamentary approval” qualifier in national security précis, presenting contingent capability targets as firm commitments.
  • Summarising corporate governance codes without the comply‑or‑explain disclosure template, losing the enforcement mechanism.
  • Leaving out the standstill period duration in procurement précis, making the remedy timeline unverifiable.
  • Neglecting the confidence rating attached to evaluation findings, obscuring the strength of evidence.
  • Failing to note transitional effective dates for governance or procurement reforms, causing temporal misalignment.
  • Treating conditional recommendations as unconditional actions, inflating the perceived commitment of the evaluated agency.

Précis of International Trade Negotiation Texts: Preserving Bracketed Language, Options, and Consensus Language

Trade negotiation documents — such as WTO ministerial drafts, regional trade agreement working papers, and bilateral negotiation minutes — feature a layered textual architecture. Core obligations appear in clean prose; contested provisions are enclosed in square brackets, presented as alternative options (e.g., “[Option A] / [Option B]”), or marked “agreed ad referendum.” A précis must reproduce this architecture because bracketed material signals the negotiating distance that remains. Omitting brackets collapses the text into a false consensus and misrepresents the provision’s legal status. The précis should therefore: (1) retain every bracketed segment verbatim, including footnote references explaining an option’s origin; (2) preserve option delimiters (“/”, “;”, “or”) and qualifying language such as “subject to” or “without prejudice to”; (3) note consensus language — phrases like “the Parties agree” or “the Parties shall” — that has moved out of brackets, distinguishing it from still‑negotiated text; (4) record the negotiation round, date, and document symbol (e.g., TN/RL/XX/Rev.X, [date]) to anchor the version; (5) flag any “chair’s text” or “facilitator’s proposal” that carries procedural weight but lacks party endorsement. When a source contains both “clean” and “bracketed” versions, the précis must indicate which is summarised and whether the clean text reflects provisional agreement. Failure to preserve this distinction yields a précis that reads as a final treaty text — a serious error in examinations testing fidelity to source structure.

Summarizing Multi‑Stakeholder Consultation Reports: Capturing Divergent Positions, Consensus Areas, and Action Items

Multi‑stakeholder consultation reports — produced by bodies such as the UN Global Compact, the OECD Guidelines for Multinational Enterprises, or national regulatory agencies — typically follow a three‑part structure: (i) a synthesis of submissions grouped by constituency (government, industry, civil society, academia); (ii) a matrix of convergent and divergent views on each thematic issue; (iii) recommended action items, often classified as short‑, medium‑, or long‑term. A précis must retain constituency attribution for each position because the report’s legitimacy rests on transparency of who said what. The summary should: (1) list major constituencies and submission counts, preserving any weighting methodology (e.g., “weighted by organisational size” or “one‑organisation‑one‑vote”); (2) reproduce the convergence‑divergence matrix in condensed form, using tabular notation such as “ Consensus on disclosure thresholds; ✘ Divergence on enforcement sanctions (industry favours voluntary codes, NGOs demand binding penalties)”; (3) preserve the exact wording of any “agreed language” from facilitated sessions, marking it with quotation marks and a session reference; (4) capture the action‑item hierarchy, including responsible actors, timelines, and conditional language (“subject to budgetary approval”); (5) note minority or dissenting opinions recorded but not incorporated, as these often resurface later. The précis must avoid collapsing divergent views into a single “mixed reactions” sentence; examiners penalise loss of the stakeholder‑specific nuance that distinguishes a consultation report from an opinion survey.

Technique: Constituency‑Tagged Synthesis (Illustrative)
A consultation report on digital‑platform regulation contains 112 submissions: 45 from industry associations, 30 from consumer‑rights NGOs, 20 from academic centres, 12 from government agencies, and 5 from individual experts. On algorithmic transparency, industry submissions favour “voluntary best‑practice guidelines” (38 % of industry submissions), NGOs demand “mandatory audit and public reporting” (80 % of NGO submissions), academics are split (55 % for mandatory, 45 % for voluntary), and governments unanimously support “regulatory sandbox with mandatory disclosure.” Write a 120‑word précis segment that preserves constituency attribution, convergence‑divergence, and the agreed language from a facilitated session.
The consultation on algorithmic transparency attracted 112 submissions across five constituencies. Industry (45) predominantly endorsed voluntary best‑practice guidelines (38 %); consumer‑rights NGOs (30) overwhelmingly demanded mandatory audit and public reporting (80 %). Academic centres (20) were divided, with a slight majority (55 %) favouring mandatory measures. Government agencies (12) unanimously backed a regulatory sandbox coupled with mandatory disclosure. A facilitated session produced agreed language: “Parties shall establish a regulatory sandbox that requires participating platforms to disclose algorithmic logic and outcome metrics to the oversight authority.” Divergence remains on the binding nature of audits; the report records this as a contested action item for medium‑term implementation.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a précis prompt supplies a stakeholder breakdown, first tabulate constituencies and submission counts in the margin. Then map each thematic issue to a convergence‑divergence matrix using symbols (/✘). This visual scaffold lets you write the précis in a single pass without re‑reading the source, saving 3‑4 minutes per passage.

Précis of Regulatory Impact Assessments: Retaining Cost‑Benefit Thresholds, Distributional Analyses, and Sunset Clauses

Regulatory Impact Assessments (RIAs) — mandated under the OECD Recommendation on Regulatory Policy and Governance (2012) and transposed into national frameworks such as U.S. Executive Order 12866, the EU Better Regulation Guidelines (2021), and India’s Pre‑Legislative Consultation Policy (2014) — follow a standardized analytical template. A précis must preserve the quantitative thresholds that trigger regulatory action (e.g., “net present value > $0 million at a 7 % discount rate”), the distributional breakdown (impact on low‑income households, SMEs, regions), and any sunset or review clauses (e.g., “the regulation expires on [date] unless renewed”). The summary should: (1) state the policy problem and the regulatory option selected; (2) reproduce central cost‑benefit figures — total quantified benefits, total quantified costs, net benefit, and the discount rate used; (3) capture the sensitivity analysis range (e.g., “net benefit remains positive for discount rates up to 10 %”); (4) summarise the distributional table, noting which groups bear disproportionate costs or receive disproportionate benefits; (5) quote the sunset provision verbatim, including any conditional renewal criteria; (6) record the consultation feedback summary, especially dissenting cost‑benefit assumptions raised by stakeholders. Omitting the discount rate or the sunset date renders the précis analytically incomplete, a frequent examiner trap in public‑policy papers.

Handling Redacted and Classified Excerpts in Government Documents: Indicating Omissions, Security Classification Levels, and Declassification Timelines

Government reports released under freedom‑of‑information regimes (e.g., U.S. FOIA 5 U.S.C. § 552, U.K. Freedom of Information Act 2000, India’s RTI Act 2005) often contain redacted passages marked with exemption codes — (b)(1)–(9) in the U.S., Section 24 in the U.K., Section 8(1) in India. A précis must signal every redaction without speculating on hidden content. The protocol: (1) insert a bracketed placeholder such as “[Redacted — Exemption (b)(5): deliberative process privilege]” at the exact location; (2) preserve the classification header (e.g., “TOP SECRET//SI//TK”) if the document header is reproduced; (3) note the declassification review date or automatic declassification schedule (e.g., “Automatic declassification on [date] per E.O. 13526”); (4) if the source provides a releasing agency’s “summary of redacted material,” include that summary verbatim; (5) avoid interpretive language such as “likely contains” or “presumably discusses.” Examiners assess whether the candidate respects the evidentiary boundary between known and unknown; a précis that fills gaps with conjecture loses marks for factual discipline.

Précis of International Standards‑Setting Organization Outputs: Preserving Normative Language, Conformance Clauses, and Technical Corrigenda

Outputs of bodies such as ISO, IEC, ITU‑T, and the Codex Alimentarius Commission consist of normative standards (e.g., ISO 9001:2015), technical specifications (ISO/TS), and publicly available specifications (PAS). Each document carries a strict hierarchy of language: “shall” denotes a requirement, “should” a recommendation, “may” a permission, and “can” a possibility. A précis must preserve this modal vocabulary because conformance assessment hinges on it. The summary should: (1) cite the full standard designation, edition, and publication date (e.g., “ISO 27001:2022, published October 2022”); (2) reproduce the scope clause verbatim; (3) list the normative references indispensable for application; (4) condense requirement clauses while retaining every “shall” statement, grouped by clause number; (5) note any “conformance clause” defining the certification scope (e.g., “Organisations may claim conformity only if all applicable requirements of Clauses 4‑10 are satisfied”); (6) record any technical corrigenda or amendments (e.g., “Corrigendum 1:2023 corrects Table A.1”) with their publication dates; (7) flag any “informative annexes” that are not mandatory but often used in audits. Dropping a single “shall” or converting it to “should” changes the legal effect of the standard and is a cardinal error in technical précis tasks.

  • Collapsing bracketed negotiation options into a single statement, erasing evidence of unresolved issues.
  • Merging divergent stakeholder positions into a vague “mixed views” sentence without constituency tags.
  • Omitting the discount rate, sensitivity range, or sunset clause in a Regulatory Impact Assessment précis.
  • Failing to insert explicit redaction placeholders with exemption codes, or speculating on redacted content.
  • Changing modal verbs (shall → should, may → can) in standards‑body texts, altering conformance meaning.
  • Neglecting to cite the exact document symbol, edition date, or corrigendum number for ISO/IEC/ITU standards.
  • Summarising a consultation report’s action items without preserving responsible actors, timelines, and conditional language.

These advanced précis techniques demand rigorous fidelity to source structure, quantitative detail, and modal precision, ensuring the condensed version remains a reliable proxy for the original document.

Précis of Supreme Court Advisory Opinions: Preserving Jurisdictional Reasoning, Policy Guidance, and Dissenting Views

Advisory opinions issued under Article 143 of the Constitution of India are non‑binding but carry significant persuasive weight for the executive, legislature, and lower courts. A précis must capture three structural layers: (1) the reference question framed by the President, reproduced verbatim or in a close paraphrase that retains the constitutional provision invoked; (2) the Court’s jurisdictional analysis, including whether the matter falls within the advisory jurisdiction (e.g., instances where the Court has declined to answer on grounds such as political questions or lack of justiciability); (3) the substantive reasoning, broken down by each judge’s opinion, noting concurrences, dissents, and any separate observations on policy implications. Quantitative data such as the number of judges concurring, the ratio of majority to minority, and any cited precedents (e.g., Kesavananda Bharati v. State of Kerala 1973 for basic‑structure doctrine) must be retained. The précis should close with the Court’s final advisory answer — often a declaratory statement — and any explicit recommendation to the President or Parliament. Omitting a dissenting judge’s policy warning renders the summary incomplete for examiners testing fidelity to judicial dialogue.

Summarizing Legislative Amendment Histories: Tracking Textual Evolution, Committee Reports, and Voting Records

A legislative amendment history is a chronological ledger of textual change. The précis must (1) identify the principal Act and the amendment Act (e.g., Constitution (Forty‑Second Amendment) Act, 1976), citing the official Gazette notification number and date; (2) map each clause‑by‑clause alteration — insertions, deletions, substitutions — using a tabular shorthand (Clause 3 → “substituted by s. 2 of Act 42 of 1976”); (3) summarise the Select Committee or Joint Committee report that preceded the amendment, preserving the committee’s key recommendations, dissent notes, and the ministry’s response; (4) record the parliamentary voting record — Lok Sabha and Rajya Sabha division numbers, vote counts, and any recorded dissenting speeches; (5) note subsequent judicial interpretation that crystallised the amendment’s effect (e.g., Minerva Mills Ltd. v. Union of India 1980 striking down clauses 4 and 5). The précis should avoid narrative commentary; instead, it must present the evolution as a structured data set that a reader could reconstruct the final text from the original plus the amendment log.

Technique: Clause‑Level Amendment Mapping
A candidate is given the original Section 8 of the Representation of the People Act, 1951 and the text of the Representation of the People (Amendment) Act, 2002 (Act 45 of 2002). The amendment substitutes “six years” with “five years” in sub‑section (1) and inserts a new sub‑section (1A) disqualifying a person convicted of an offence under the Prevention of Corruption Act, 1988. Write a précis entry for this amendment.
Amendment Act 45/2002, Gazette notification dated 12‑09‑2002. Section 8(1): “six years” → “five years” (substituted by s. 2). Section 8(1A) inserted: “Notwithstanding anything contained in sub‑section (1), a person convicted of an offence under the Prevention of Corruption Act, 1988 shall be disqualified for a period of five years from the date of conviction.” Committee Report: Joint Committee on Electoral Reforms (Report No. 17, 2001) recommended reduction of disqualification period and inclusion of corruption offences; Ministry accepted both. Voting: Lok Sabha division 212, 298‑102; Rajya Sabha division 87, 165‑45. Judicial note: K. P. Singh v. Election Commission (2005) upheld the five‑year period as reasonable.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a précis prompt supplies a legislative amendment text, first extract the Gazette citation and amendment Act number. Build a two‑column table in your rough sheet: left column = original clause identifier, right column = amendment operation (substitution, insertion, deletion) with exact wording. This visual map prevents omission of inserted sub‑sections and ensures the voting record is attached to the correct amendment. Allocate no more than three minutes for the table; the remaining time is for drafting the concise prose.

Précis of Corporate Governance Codes: Retaining Principles, Compliance Mechanisms, and Enforcement Provisions

Corporate governance codes — such as the SEBI (Listing Obligations and Disclosure Requirements) Regulations, 2015 (LODR), the UK Corporate Governance Code 2018, and the OECD Principles of Corporate Governance 2015 — share a layered architecture: (1) high‑level principles (e.g., “board leadership”, “accountability”, “transparency”); (2) specific provisions expressed through modal verbs (“shall”, “should”, “may”) that dictate mandatory, recommendatory, or permissive actions; (3) compliance mechanisms — board committees (audit, nomination, remuneration), whistle‑blower channels, and annual compliance certificates; (4) enforcement provisions — regulatory sanctions, delisting triggers, and shareholder derivative action thresholds. A précis must preserve the modal hierarchy: converting a “shall” to “should” alters the legal obligation. It must also capture quantitative thresholds (e.g., “minimum 50 % independent directors”, “audit committee to meet at least four times a year”) and any sunset or review clauses (e.g., “Code reviewed every three years”). Cross‑referencing the code’s annexures — such as the SEBI LODR’s Schedule II on corporate governance report format — is essential because examiners often test whether the candidate can condense the reporting template without losing mandatory disclosure items.

Condensing Central Bank Financial Stability Reports: Capturing Systemic Risk Indicators, Macro‑Prudential Tools, and Scenario Analyses

Financial stability reports (FSRs) issued by central banks — Reserve Bank of India (biannual since 2010), Bank of England (quarterly), Federal Reserve (annual) — follow a standard analytical framework. The précis must (1) cite the report identifier, period covered, and publication date; (2) list the core systemic risk indicators with their latest values — credit‑to‑GDP gap, leverage ratio, non‑performing asset (NPA) ratio, interbank contagion index — preserving the direction of change (↑/↓) and the threshold breached; (3) summarise macro‑prudential tools deployed or recommended — countercyclical capital buffer (CCyB) rate, sectoral capital requirements, loan‑to‑value caps — noting the calibration methodology (e.g., “CCyB set at 0.5 % based on credit‑to‑GDP gap exceeding 2 %”); (4) condense scenario analyses — baseline, adverse, and severely adverse — reproducing key stress‑test outputs such as projected capital adequacy ratios (CAR) under each scenario and the number of banks falling below the regulatory minimum; (5) record any policy recommendations with implementation timelines. Omitting the scenario‑specific CAR trajectory or the CCyB calibration basis makes the précis analytically hollow for examiners assessing quantitative fidelity.

Précis of International Trade Dispute Settlement Panel Reports: Preserving Findings, Legal Reasoning, and Remedies

World Trade Organization (WTO) panel reports under the Dispute Settlement Understanding (DSU) follow a rigid structure: (1) the measure at issue and the complaining party’s claims, citing the covered agreements (e.g., GATT 1994 Art. III:4, TBT Agreement Art. 2.2); (2) the panel’s jurisdictional determination — whether the matter falls within the DSU’s terms of reference; (3) the factual findings, often presented in a “findings of fact” table; (4) the legal analysis, broken down by each claim, with the panel’s interpretation of treaty provisions, reference to prior Appellate Body rulings (e.g., US — Shrimp AB‑1998‑4), and the application of the “necessity test” or “least‑trade‑restrictive” standard; (5) the conclusions and recommendations — typically a request to bring the measure into conformity within a reasonable period of time (RPT), and any suggested compensation or retaliation authorization. A précis must retain the exact citation of each legal provision, the panel’s interpretive methodology (textual, contextual, object‑and‑purpose), and the RPT duration. It must also note any dissenting or separate opinions by panelists, as these signal interpretive divisions that may affect subsequent Appellate Body review. Dropping the RPT or converting a “shall” recommendation into a “may” suggestion changes the enforceability of the ruling.

  • Confusing advisory opinions with binding judgments — advisory opinions lack stare decisis effect and must be labelled as such.
  • Summarising amendment histories without the Gazette notification number or the exact amendment Act citation.
  • Replacing mandatory modal verbs (“shall”) in governance codes with recommendatory ones (“should”).
  • Omitting the countercyclical capital buffer calibration basis or the scenario‑specific capital adequacy ratios in financial stability reports.
  • Dropping the reasonable period of time or the specific treaty article cited in a WTO panel report.
  • Failing to record dissenting panelist opinions or separate judicial observations in advisory opinions.
  • Neglecting to attach voting division numbers to each legislative amendment entry.

Précis of International Criminal Court Judgments: Preserving Elements of Crimes, Modes of Liability, and Sentencing Rationales

International Criminal Court (ICC) judgments follow the Rome Statute’s tripartite structure: (1) the factual findings establishing the actus reus and mens rea for each charged crime; (2) the legal characterization linking those facts to the Elements of Crimes document adopted by the Assembly of States Parties in 2002; and (3) the sentencing decision grounded in Articles 77 and 78, which require the Trial Chamber to consider gravity, individual circumstances, and the principles of proportionality and deterrence. A précis must retain the exact statutory provision for each crime — e.g., Article 7(1)(a) for murder as a crime against humanity, Article 8(2)(b)(iv) for intentional attacks against civilians — and the corresponding element‑by‑element analysis the Chamber performs. The modes of liability under Article 25 (commission, ordering, aiding and abetting, contribution to a group crime) are not interchangeable; the précis must note which mode the Chamber relied on for each accused, because appellate review often turns on that distinction. Sentencing rationales must preserve the Chamber’s explicit weighting of aggravating factors (e.g., particular cruelty, vulnerability of victims) and mitigating factors (e.g., diminished mental capacity, cooperation), together with the final term of imprisonment or life sentence and any order for reparations under Article 75. Omitting the element‑level mapping or converting a “shall” in the sentencing provision to a “may” changes the legal effect and will be penalised.

Summarizing Trade Negotiation Texts: Preserving Bracketed Provisions, Chair’s Texts, and Consensus Language

Multilateral trade negotiations — most notably the WTO Doha Development Agenda and the ongoing Joint Statement Initiatives — produce documents that evolve through three textual layers: (1) the bracketed text indicating unresolved options, each bracket often annotated with the proposing delegation’s code (e.g., [US], [EU], [G77]); (2) the Chair’s text or facilitator’s draft that attempts to bridge divergences, usually presented in clean copy without brackets but marked “Chair’s proposal” in the header; and (3) the consensus language that survives the final “green‑room” or “mini‑ministerial” and appears in the adopted ministerial declaration or annex. A précis must (a) list every bracket that remains at the cut‑off date, preserving the exact wording and the sponsoring delegation, because examiners test whether the candidate can distinguish a live option from a discarded one; (b) summarise the Chair’s bridging proposals, noting any conditional language (“subject to”, “provided that”) that signals a compromise; and (c) reproduce the final consensus paragraphs verbatim for key disciplines such as market access (tariff‑rate quotas, special safeguard mechanisms), rules of origin (product‑specific rules, cumulation provisions), and dispute settlement (panel composition, appellate review). Quantitative thresholds — e.g., “average bound tariff reduction of 36 % for developed members, 24 % for developing members” — must be retained with their annex references. Dropping a bracket, mis‑attributing a Chair’s proposal to a member state, or converting a consensus “shall” into a “should” alters the legal status of the provision and constitutes a fatal error.

Worked Précis Exercise
A 250‑word excerpt from a WTO Chair’s text on fisheries subsidies contains three bracketed options for the definition of “illegal, unreported and unregulated (IUU) fishing”, a Chair’s compromise paragraph that merges two options, and a final consensus sentence adopting the merged definition. Write a 70‑word précis that preserves the bracketed options, the Chair’s compromise, and the consensus language.
The Chair’s text presents three bracketed IUU definitions: [US] “fishing contrary to national laws”, [EU] “fishing violating RFMO conservation measures”, [G77] “fishing without flag‑State authorisation”. The Chair merges the first two into a compromise: “fishing contrary to national laws or RFMO conservation measures”. The adopted consensus reads: “Members shall prohibit subsidies for fishing that is contrary to national laws or RFMO conservation measures.” (70 words)
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a trade‑negotiation passage appears, first colour‑code brackets, Chair’s text, and consensus sentences. Then draft the précis in three parallel columns before condensing; this prevents accidental omission of any layer and keeps the modal hierarchy intact.

Précis of Scientific Peer Review Reports: Capturing Reviewer Comments, Editorial Decisions, and Revision Requirements

Peer‑review reports for high‑impact journals (e.g., Nature, Science, The Lancet) follow a standardized template: (1) a confidential summary for the editor assessing novelty, methodological rigour, and significance; (2) a structured list of major and minor comments, each tagged with the relevant manuscript section (Abstract, Methods, Results, Discussion); (3) a recommendation — Accept, Minor Revision, Major Revision, or Reject — often accompanied by a deadline for resubmission (typically 30–90 days). A précis must preserve the hierarchy of comments: major comments that threaten the paper’s conclusions (e.g., “Statistical analysis does not control for multiple testing; p‑values inflated”) must appear before minor stylistic points. The editorial decision letter, which synthesises reviewer input and may add journal‑specific requirements (e.g., data deposition in a public repository, compliance with CONSORT guidelines), must be condensed without losing any mandatory action items. Quantitative details — sample size, effect sizes with confidence intervals, statistical power — cited by reviewers must be reproduced exactly, because examiners verify whether the candidate can transmit the evidentiary weight of the critique. Converting a “must” in a reviewer’s mandatory request to a “should” or omitting a required data‑availability statement changes the compliance burden and is a scoring error.

Condensing Parliamentary Committee Evidence Sessions: Retaining Witness Testimony, Documentary Exhibits, and Committee Findings

Standing and select committees of national legislatures (e.g., India’s Department‑Related Standing Committees, UK House of Commons Select Committees, US Congressional Hearings) produce verbatim transcripts that are later distilled into a committee report.
