The report typically contains: (1) a list of witnesses with their institutional affiliation and the date of appearance; (2) a summary of oral evidence, preserving direct quotations that embody the witness’s core argument or data point; (3) a catalogue of documentary exhibits submitted (written memoranda, datasets, correspondence) with reference numbers; and (4) the committee’s findings and recommendations, each linked to specific evidence paragraphs. A précis must (a) retain the witness‑to‑finding traceability — e.g., “Dr. A. Sharma (ICMR, 12 Mar 2023) testified that the vaccine efficacy against the Delta variant was 78 % (95 % CI 72–83)”, and the committee’s finding “The efficacy threshold of 70 % is met” — because examiners test the ability to map evidence to conclusions; (b) preserve any dissenting or minority views recorded in the report, noting the member’s name and the paragraph reference; and (c) keep quantitative thresholds (budget allocations, timeline milestones) exactly as presented. Omitting a witness’s affiliation, dropping a documentary exhibit number, or paraphrasing a recommendation so that “shall” becomes “may” alters the parliamentary record’s legal force.

Précis of International Environmental Dispute Settlement Awards: Preserving Jurisdictional Findings, Reparation Principles, and Compliance Mechanisms

International environmental disputes are adjudicated before the International Court of Justice (ICJ), the International Tribunal for the Law of the Sea (ITLOS), and ad hoc arbitral tribunals under the Permanent Court of Arbitration (PCA). Awards follow a consistent architecture: (1) jurisdictional determination — whether the tribunal has competence under the relevant treaty (e.g., UNCLOS Part XV, the Convention on Biological Diversity, the Paris Agreement) and whether the dispute is admissible (exhaustion of local remedies, standing); (2) merits analysis — identification of the breached obligation (e.g., duty to prevent transboundary harm, obligation to conduct environmental impact assessment), the standard of due diligence applied, and the factual basis (scientific evidence, expert testimony); (3) reparation — the tribunal’s articulation of the Chorzów Factory principle (restitution, compensation, satisfaction) and the quantification of damages (e.g., “USD 12.5 million for loss of ecosystem services, calculated using the replacement cost method”); and (4) compliance mechanisms — reporting obligations, establishment of a joint monitoring body, and any sunset or review clauses. A précis must retain the exact treaty article numbers, the due‑diligence standard cited (e.g., “the ‘best available techniques’ standard under Article 194(1) UNCLOS”), the reparation formulae, and the compliance timeline (e.g., “State X shall submit a compliance report within 12 months of the award”). Converting a binding “shall” in the compliance paragraph to a recommendatory “should”, or omitting the quantification methodology, renders the précis analytically incomplete.

  • Confusing bracketed negotiation text with Chair’s compromise language and omitting the sponsoring delegation code.
  • Failing to preserve the element‑by‑element mapping of ICC crimes, especially the distinction between Article 25(3)(a) (commission) and Article 25(3)(d) (contribution to a group crime).
  • Dropping mandatory “must” language in peer‑review major comments or editorial decision letters, thereby weakening the revision requirement.
  • Omitting witness affiliation or documentary exhibit numbers in parliamentary committee summaries, breaking the evidence‑to‑finding chain.
  • Replacing the Chorzów Factory restitution‑compensation‑satisfaction triad with a generic “damages” phrase in environmental awards.
  • Neglecting to record dissenting or minority opinions in committee reports or arbitral awards, which signal interpretive divisions.
  • Mis‑attributing a consensus “shall” to a bracketed option, altering the legal status of the provision.

Précis of Scientific Peer‑Review Reports: Preserving Reviewer Comments, Editorial Decisions, and Revision Requirements

A scientific peer‑review report follows a predictable architecture that examiners typically expect to be reproduced in a précis without loss of traceability. The report typically opens with a concise manuscript summary, followed by a structured set of major comments (often numbered) that address methodological soundness, data interpretation, statistical rigor, and adherence to reporting standards such as CONSORT, PRISMA, or STROBE. Minor comments cover language, figure clarity, reference completeness, and formatting. The reviewer then states a recommendation — accept, minor revision, major revision, or reject — which the editor translates into a decision letter. That decision letter lists required revisions, optional suggestions, and a deadline. A high‑scoring précis must (a) keep the comment numbers and their exact wording when they embody a substantive critique; (b) preserve the editor’s mandatory language (“must”, “shall”) versus advisory language (“should”, “consider”); (c) retain any quantitative thresholds the reviewer imposes (e.g., “increase sample size to achieve 80 % power at α = 0.05”); and (d) note the final editorial decision and the response deadline. Omitting a major comment number, converting a “must” into a “should”, or dropping the deadline changes the contractual obligation between author and journal and may be penalised.

When the manuscript undergoes a second round, the reviewer’s follow‑up comments are linked to the author’s point‑by‑point response. The précis should therefore map each original comment to the corresponding revision action (e.g., “Comment 1: reviewer requested mixed‑effects model; author added random intercept for site”). This traceability demonstrates the candidate’s ability to synthesize iterative scholarly dialogue, a skill explicitly tested in advanced comprehension papers.

Condensing Technical Patent Landscapes: Mapping Claim Families, Prior Art Citations, and Freedom‑to‑Operate Analyses

A patent landscape précis must capture three interlocking layers: claim architecture, prior‑art density, and freedom‑to‑operate (FTO) risk. Claim architecture is presented as families — groups of patents sharing a common priority date — with independent claims defining the broadest scope and dependent claims narrowing it. The précis should list the representative independent claim number, its key limitations (e.g., “a method comprising steps A, B, and C wherein step B uses a catalyst of formula X”), and the jurisdiction (US, EP, CN, JP). Prior‑art citations appear on the front page (US 102/103, EP 54/56) and in search reports; the précis must retain the most relevant references, their publication numbers, and the specific claim elements they anticipate or render obvious. FTO analysis adds a risk matrix: each claim family is scored for infringement likelihood (high/medium/low) based on claim‑to‑product mapping, territorial coverage, and licensing status. Quantitative thresholds such as “claim 1 covers 95 % of the target product’s feature set” or “licensing cost estimated at USD 2.3 M per annum” must be reproduced verbatim. Examiners often reward précis that preserve the claim‑to‑citation linkage (e.g., “US 10,123,456 claim 1 anticipated by WO 2018/012345”) and that keep the jurisdictional scope markers (US, EP, CN) because territorial rights differ. Dropping a dependent claim that introduces a critical limitation, or summarising a “high” FTO risk as “moderate”, constitutes a scoring error.

Technique: Comment‑to‑Revision Mapping Table
A peer‑review report contains three major comments: (1) “The statistical analysis lacks a mixed‑effects model for repeated measures.” (2) “Figure 3 does not show confidence intervals.” (3) “The discussion omits recent literature on biomarker X.” The editor’s decision letter states: “Major revision required. Authors must address all major comments within 30 days.” Write a précis that preserves the comment‑to‑revision traceability and the mandatory language.
Précis: The reviewer raised three major comments: (1) absence of a mixed‑effects model for repeated measures; (2) missing confidence intervals in Figure 3; (3) omission of recent biomarker X literature. The editor mandated a major revision, requiring the authors to address all major comments within 30 days. The author’s response added a mixed‑effects model (Comment 1), inserted 95 % confidence intervals in Figure 3 (Comment 2), and cited five 2023‑2024 papers on biomarker X (Comment 3).
Exam‑Day Strategy
During reading time, sketch a two‑column table: left column for each numbered reviewer comment, right column for the author’s exact response. This visual map lets you write the précis in a single pass without hunting for linkages.

Summarizing Multi‑Stakeholder Consultation Reports: Capturing Position Statements, Consensus Areas, and Dissent Records

Multi‑stakeholder consultation reports — such as those produced under the UNFCCC Talanoa Dialogue, the OECD Guidelines for Multinational Enterprises, or national regulatory impact assessments — follow a three‑part structure: (1) a registry of submissions, each tagged with stakeholder category (government, industry, civil society, academia) and a unique reference code; (2) a synthesis matrix that groups submissions by thematic clusters (e.g., mitigation ambition, finance, technology transfer) and flags consensus language (“broad agreement that…”) versus divergent views (“some parties argued…”); (3) an annex of dissenting or minority positions, often reproduced verbatim to preserve procedural fairness. A précis must (a) retain the stakeholder‑to‑position traceability (e.g., “Submission G‑12 (Ministry of Environment, Brazil) advocated a 1.5 °C pathway”); (b) preserve the exact consensus phrasing, especially modal verbs (“shall”, “should”, “encouraged”) because they determine legal force; (c) keep quantitative targets cited in the synthesis (e.g., “collective goal of USD 100 bn per year by 2025”); and (d) list each dissenting submission with its reference code and the specific paragraph where the disagreement appears. Dropping a stakeholder code, converting a “shall” in the consensus paragraph to a “may”, or omitting a dissent annex entry breaks the evidentiary chain that examiners test.

Précis of International Trade Negotiation Texts: Retaining Bracketed Options, Chair’s Compromise Language, and Delegation Attribution

World Trade Organization and regional trade negotiation texts (e.g., the Doha Development Agenda drafts, CPTPP negotiating documents) use a distinctive markup: square brackets enclose unresolved language, curly braces sometimes indicate chair‑proposed compromise text, and delegation codes (e.g., [US], [EU], [BRICS]) attribute proposals. A précis must reproduce the bracketed alternatives verbatim because each represents a live negotiating position; summarising “[US] proposes X; [EU] proposes Y” as “parties disagree on X” loses the attribution that examiners often reward. Chair’s compromise language, often prefaced by “Chair’s text:”, carries procedural weight and must be kept distinct from delegation proposals. Additionally, the précis should note any “clean” text — language agreed by all parties and therefore unbracketed — because it signals settled obligations. Quantitative annexes (tariff‑rate quotas, specific duty rates, transition periods) are reproduced with exact numbers and units. Omitting a delegation code, merging two bracketed options into a single paraphrase, or dropping a chair’s compromise paragraph are all scoring errors.

Handling Qualitative Coding Schemes in Research Summaries: Codebook Structure, Inter‑Coder Reliability, and Theme Saturation

Qualitative research summaries — whether from grounded theory, thematic analysis, or framework analysis — require the précis to convey the coding architecture without flattening analytical depth. The codebook is the primary artifact: it lists each code, its definition, inclusion/exclusion criteria, and illustrative excerpts. A précis must (a) name the coding paradigm (e.g., “inductive thematic analysis following Braun & Clarke 2006”); (b) report the number of initial codes, the number of merged themes, and the final theme hierarchy; (c) cite the inter‑coder reliability statistic (Cohen’s κ or Krippendorff’s α) with its numeric value (e.g., “κ = 0.87, 95 % CI = 0.81‑0.92”); and (d) state the saturation criterion used (e.g., “no new codes emerged after the 12th interview”). When the source text includes a frequency table (code → count), the précis should preserve the top‑three codes with exact counts. Examiners may penalise omission of the reliability metric, vague statements like “good agreement” without a statistic, or collapsing distinct themes into a generic “main findings” paragraph. Maintaining the link between a theme and its defining codes demonstrates the candidate’s ability to handle methodological nuance.

  • Converting mandatory “must” or “shall” language in reviewer comments, editorial decisions, or consensus paragraphs into advisory “should” or “may”.
  • Dropping stakeholder reference codes, delegation attributions, or claim‑family identifiers, breaking traceability chains.
  • Merging separate bracketed negotiation options into a single paraphrase, losing the live positional data.
  • Omitting quantitative thresholds (sample‑size targets, tariff rates, licensing costs, reliability coefficients) or rounding them imprecisely.
  • Summarising inter‑coder reliability as “high agreement” without reporting the exact κ or α value and confidence interval.
  • Failing to map each major reviewer comment to the author’s specific revision action in a multi‑round peer‑review précis.
  • Neglecting to record dissenting or minority submissions with their paragraph locations in consultation reports.

Précis of UN Security Council Resolutions: Retaining Chapter VII Determinations, Operative Paragraphs, and Veto Records

A Security Council resolution follows a fixed template: a preambular section recalling prior resolutions, stating the factual context, and citing the Charter provisions invoked; followed by sequentially numbered operative paragraphs, each beginning with a verb such as “Decides”, “Calls upon”, “Requests”, or “Authorizes”. When the Council acts under Chapter VII (Articles 39‑51), the operative language carries binding force on all member states. A précis must preserve the exact modal verb in each operative paragraph because “Decides” creates a legal obligation, whereas “Calls upon” is hortatory. The preambular paragraphs, while not legally binding, frame the Council’s reasoning and must be summarised without altering the causal links they assert (e.g., “Recalling resolution 242 (1967) and the inadmissibility of the acquisition of territory by war”).

Veto records are part of the procedural history. If a permanent member casts a negative vote, the resolution fails; the précis should note the vetoing state and the date of the vote, because examiners often test whether the candidate recognises that a veto prevents adoption even when a majority supports the text. When a resolution is adopted, the voting breakdown (e.g., “15‑0‑0” or “14‑1‑0 with one abstention”) must be recorded verbatim. Quantitative mandates — such as “imposes an arms embargo on all transfers of weapons to the territory” or “establishes a sanctions committee to monitor compliance” — require exact reproduction of the scope, the targeted entities, and any time‑limits (e.g., “for a period of twelve months”).

Cross‑references to earlier resolutions are frequent. A précis should map each operative paragraph to its predecessor (e.g., “Operative paragraph 3 reaffirms the measures imposed by resolution 1970 (2011)”). Omitting a cross‑reference breaks the chain of cumulative sanctions. Finally, any “sunset clause” or review mechanism (e.g., “the Council shall review the measures no later than [a specific date]”) must be retained with the precise date, because it determines the lifespan of the binding obligations.

Précis of International Criminal Court Judgments: Preserving Elements of Crimes, Modes of Liability, and Reparations Orders

The International Criminal Court (ICC) issues judgments that follow a three‑part structure: findings on the elements of each crime, determination of the accused’s mode of liability, and, where applicable, a reparations order. The Rome Statute defines each crime (genocide, crimes against humanity, war crimes, aggression) by a set of material elements (actus reus) and mental elements (mens rea). A précis must list the specific elements the Trial Chamber found proved for each count, citing the relevant article (e.g., “Article 7(1)(a) – murder as a crime against humanity”). Summarising “the Chamber found the accused guilty of murder” without enumerating the elements loses the analytical granularity examiners reward.

Modes of liability under Article 25 include commission, ordering, soliciting, aiding and abetting, and contribution to a group crime. The judgment often distinguishes between direct perpetration (Article 25(3)(a)) and indirect participation (Article 25(3)(d)). A précis must preserve the exact mode attributed to each accused for each count, because the legal consequences (e.g., sentencing range) differ. If the Appeals Chamber modifies the mode of liability, the précis must reflect the final appellate finding, not the trial‑level characterization.

Reparations orders under Article 75 are separate from the conviction. They specify the beneficiaries, the forms of reparation (restitution, compensation, rehabilitation, satisfaction, guarantees of non‑repetition), and the implementation mechanism (Trust Fund for Victims). A précis must retain the quantitative aspects (e.g., “[a specified amount, e.g., USD 1,000,000] allocated for collective reparations”) and the procedural directives (e.g., “the Registry shall develop a draft implementation plan within [a specified period, e.g., six months]”). Dropping the reparations section or converting a mandatory “shall” into a discretionary “may” misstates the Court’s binding order.

Worked Précis Exercise
A hypothetical 250‑word excerpt from an ICC Trial Chamber judgment might state: “The Chamber finds that the Prosecutor has proved beyond reasonable doubt each element of the crime against humanity of persecution under Article 7(1)(h). The acts consisted of systematic discrimination against the civilian population of Region X on political grounds, including arbitrary arrests, torture, and denial of humanitarian aid. The accused, as the Minister of Interior, ordered the security forces to carry out these acts, thereby incurring liability under Article 25(3)(b) (ordering). The Chamber further finds that the accused contributed to the commission of the crime by providing logistical support, engaging liability under Article 25(3)(d). The Chamber orders reparations for the victims in the amount of USD 2,500,000 to be administered by the Trust Fund for Victims, with a draft implementation plan due within nine months.” Write a précis of no more than 80 words that preserves all legally significant details.
The Trial Chamber convicted the accused of persecution as a crime against humanity (Art. 7(1)(h)), confirming all material and mental elements: systematic political discrimination in Region X involving arbitrary arrests, torture, and denial of aid. Liability was established under Art. 25(3)(b) (ordering) and Art. 25(3)(d) (contribution via logistical support). The Chamber ordered USD 2.5 million in collective reparations via the Trust Fund for Victims, requiring a draft implementation plan within nine months.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a judgment excerpt contains multiple modes of liability, list each mode with its statutory sub‑paragraph in the précis; examiners award marks for precise citation. Use a two‑column mental map: left column for crime elements, right column for liability modes. This prevents accidental merging of distinct legal bases.

Précis of World Trade Organization Dispute Settlement Reports: Preserving Panel Findings, Appellate Body Reasoning, and Implementation Timelines

WTO dispute settlement reports follow the Dispute Settlement Understanding (DSU). A panel report contains: (1) a factual and procedural background; (2) the claims and defences of the parties; (3) the panel’s findings on each claim, referencing the covered agreements (GATT 1994, GATS, TRIPS, SCM, etc.); (4) the panel’s conclusions and recommendations. If appealed, the Appellate Body issues a separate report that upholds, modifies, or reverses the panel’s legal findings. A précis must distinguish between panel findings and Appellate Body modifications, because the final legal standard derives from the latter.

Each finding is tied to a specific provision (e.g., “Article III:4 of GATT 1994 – national treatment”). The précis should retain the exact provision citation and the legal test applied (e.g., “the ‘less favourable treatment’ test”). Quantitative remedies — such as “the respondent shall bring its measure into conformity within a reasonable period of time, not exceeding [a specified period, e.g., 15 months]” — must be reproduced with the precise period. The DSU also provides for retaliation authorisation if compliance fails; the précis should note the level of suspension of concessions authorised (e.g., “[a specified amount, e.g., USD 200 million annually]”).

Implementation timelines are critical. The “reasonable period of time” (RPT) is often determined by arbitration under Article 21.3(c). A précis must record the RPT date and any subsequent compliance panel findings. If a compliance panel finds non‑implementation, the original complainant may request retaliation; the précis must capture this procedural cascade. Omitting the RPT or the retaliation amount converts a binding outcome into a vague recommendation.

Précis of Parliamentary Committee Evidence Sessions: Preserving Witness Testimony, Evidentiary Weight, and Procedural Rulings

Select committees of legislatures conduct evidence sessions that are transcribed verbatim (Hansard). A précis of such a session must capture three layers: (1) the witness’s substantive testimony, including any data, documents, or expert opinions presented; (2) the committee’s assessment of evidentiary weight, often expressed through the chair’s rulings on relevance, privilege, or sub judice; (3) procedural rulings such as the acceptance of written evidence, the ordering of witnesses, or the invocation of parliamentary privilege to refuse answering.

Witnesses may be sworn (under oath) or unsworn; the précis must note the status because sworn testimony carries contempt sanctions for false statements. Quantitative evidence (e.g., “the department spent [a specific amount, e.g., GBP 3.2 billion] on a named programme in a given fiscal year”) must be reproduced exactly. When a witness cites a classified document, the précis should record the classification level and the committee’s decision to hear the evidence in camera.

Committee reports often summarise evidence sessions in a “summary of evidence” annex. A précis of the session itself should map each key assertion to the witness’s name and organisational affiliation (e.g., “Dr. A. Patel, Chief Economist, Ministry of Finance”). If the committee issues a formal recommendation linked to a specific testimony, the précis must preserve that causal link. Dropping the witness attribution or the procedural ruling (e.g., “the chair ruled the question out of order under [the relevant standing order]”) removes the accountability trail examiners test.

Précis of National Human Rights Institution Annual Reports: Preserving Advisory Opinions, Monitoring Findings, and Legislative Recommendations

National Human Rights Institutions (NHRIs) accredited under the Paris Principles publish annual reports that typically contain: (1) a statutory mandate overview; (2) advisory opinions issued during the reporting period; (3) monitoring findings from visits to detention facilities, courts, or marginalized communities; (4) legislative and policy recommendations addressed to parliament, the executive, or the judiciary. A précis must retain the formal reference of each advisory opinion (e.g., “Opinion No. [X]/2023 on the compatibility of a specified act with Article 9 of the ICCPR”).

Monitoring findings are often structured around thematic areas (e.g., “conditions of pre‑trial detention”, “access to legal aid”). The précis should preserve the quantitative indicators reported (e.g., “specific quantitative indicators such as average pre‑trial detention length and percentage of detainees lacking legal representation”). When the NHRI cites international standards (e.g., “the Nelson Mandela Rules”), the précis must keep the citation because it establishes the benchmark used.

Legislative recommendations are usually numbered and directed at a specific authority. A précis must list each recommendation with its addressee and the precise legal change sought (e.g., “Recommendation 5: Amend a specific section of the relevant act to require judicial authorisation for preventive detention”). If the report notes government responses to prior recommendations, the précis should capture the implementation status (e.g., “Recommendation 3 (2022) – partially implemented; draft amendment pending”). Omitting the addressee or the specific statutory provision turns a concrete recommendation into a vague suggestion.

  • Converting a binding “Decides” in a Security Council resolution into a hortatory “Urges”.
  • Omitting the exact mode of liability (Article 25 sub‑paragraph) in an ICC judgment précis.
  • Summarising a WTO panel finding without citing the specific covered agreement provision.
  • Dropping the witness’s sworn status or organisational affiliation in a parliamentary evidence précis.
  • Failing to record the addressee and exact statutory amendment in an NHRI recommendation.
  • Merging preambular reasoning with operative obligations in a UNSC resolution précis.
  • Rounding quantitative reparations or retaliation amounts instead of reproducing the exact figure.

Précis of International Arbitration Procedural Orders: Preserving Jurisdictional Rulings, Evidence Admissibility, and Cost Allocations

Procedural orders under the UNCITRAL Arbitration Rules, ICC Arbitration Rules, or ICSID Convention govern the arbitration before the final award. A précis must capture three pillars: (1) the tribunal’s jurisdictional determination — whether it affirms, declines, or reserves jurisdiction over a claim or counter‑claim, citing the applicable treaty provision (e.g., ICSID Convention Art. 25) or arbitration agreement clause; (2) evidentiary rulings — admissibility of documentary evidence, witness statements, or expert reports, including limitations under UNCITRAL Rules Art. 27 or IBA Rules Art. 9; (3) cost allocation — interim decisions on advances, arbitration fees, and provisional legal costs under ICC Rules Art. 40. The précis must retain exact paragraph numbers, date of issuance, and names of signing arbitrators. Omitting a jurisdictional reservation converts a conditional ruling into a final one; dropping an evidentiary limitation (e.g., exclusion of an expert report for lack of independence) removes a procedural safeguard examiners test. When the order references a prior order, note the cross‑reference (e.g., “see Procedural Order No. 2, para. 7”) to preserve the procedural chain.

Key Drafting Checklist

  • Identify the governing rules and specific article invoked.
  • Record the tribunal’s jurisdictional finding verbatim, including any “subject to” language.
  • Summarise each evidentiary ruling with the rule or principle applied.
  • Reproduce cost allocation figures and the party responsible for each advance.
  • Preserve the procedural timeline: date of order, compliance deadlines, scheduled hearings.

Summarizing National Security Strategy Documents: Retaining Threat Assessments, Capability Targets, and Classification Caveats

National security strategy (NSS) documents — such as the U.S. National Security Strategy 2022, U.K. Integrated Review 2021, or Australian Defence Strategic Update 2020 — combine strategic narrative with classified annexes. A précis must separate the unclassified narrative from classification caveats limiting public disclosure. Three core components: (1) threat assessment — identified state and non‑state actors, geographic theatres, and risk ratings (e.g., “high”, “moderate”); (2) capability targets — quantitative force‑structure goals (e.g., “increase naval fleet to 355 ships by 2030”, “field 1,200 cyber operators by 2025”) and associated funding; (3) classification caveats — markings (TOP SECRET//SI//TK, SECRET//REL TO USA, etc.) and handling instructions (e.g., “NOFORN”, “ORCON”) for specific paragraphs or annexes. Preserve exact threat taxonomy wording (e.g., “great‑power competition”, “transnational terrorism”, “climate‑driven instability”) for semantic fidelity. When a classified annex is referenced (e.g., “Annex A (TS/SCI)”), note its identifier and classification level without reproducing content. Dropping a capability target’s deadline or rounding a fleet number from 355 to “approximately 350” converts a measurable objective into a vague aspiration.

Structure for a One‑Page Précis

  1. Header: Document title, date, issuing authority, classification of the précis (usually UNCLASSIFIED).
  2. Strategic narrative (150‑200 words): core pillars, guiding principles, geographic focus.
  3. Threat matrix (table): actor, domain, risk rating, page reference.
  4. Capability targets (bullet list): metric, target value, fiscal year, funding source.
  5. Classification register: paragraph/annex, classification marking, dissemination control.
Worked Example: Précis of a Procedural Order
A tribunal issues Procedural Order No. 3 under the ICC Rules. It (a) confirms jurisdiction over Claimant’s breach‑of‑contract claim under Article 6(3) of the arbitration agreement; (b) denies jurisdiction over Respondent’s counter‑claim for tortious interference, reserving the issue for the final award; (c) admits Claimant’s expert report dated [Date] but excludes Respondent’s expert report dated [Date] for lack of independence under Article 9(2) of the IBA Rules; (d) orders Claimant to advance [Amount] for arbitration costs within 30 days and Respondent to advance [Amount] within the same period. Write a 120‑word précis.
Procedural Order No. 3 (ICC Rules, [Date]). The tribunal affirms jurisdiction over the Claimant’s breach‑of‑contract claim pursuant to Article 6(3) of the arbitration agreement. Jurisdiction over the Respondent’s tortious‑interference counter‑claim is reserved for the final award. Evidentiary rulings: the Claimant’s expert report ([Date]) is admitted; the Respondent’s expert report ([Date]) is excluded under IBA Rules Art. 9(2) for lack of independence. Cost allocation: Claimant to advance [Amount]; Respondent to advance [Amount], both within 30 days of the order. Signed by Presiding Arbitrator [Name] and Co‑Arbitrators [Name] and [Name].
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for a précis of a procedural order or an NSS excerpt, first annotate the source text with three colour codes: jurisdiction (blue), evidence (green), costs or capability targets (red). This visual map lets you retrieve exact figures and paragraph references in under two minutes, leaving ample time for drafting within the word limit.

Précis of Scientific Peer‑Review Reports: Capturing Reviewer Comments, Editorial Decisions, and Revision Mandates

Peer‑review reports for journals such as Nature, The Lancet, or IEEE Transactions follow a structured template: (1) reviewer’s overall recommendation (Accept, Minor Revision, Major Revision, Reject); (2) specific comments numbered by manuscript section (Abstract, Methods, Results, Discussion); (3) editorial decision letter summarising consensus and stipulating required revisions with deadlines. A précis must preserve the recommendation hierarchy — if two reviewers recommend “Major Revision” and one “Minor Revision”, the editorial decision typically follows the majority, but the précis should note the split. Condense each reviewer comment to its core scientific objection (e.g., “Reviewer 1: statistical power insufficient; sample size n=12 yields 0.45 power for primary endpoint”). Distinguish the editorial decision’s mandatory revisions (e.g., “perform power analysis, increase cohort to n≥30, re‑run mixed‑effects model”) from optional suggestions (e.g., “consider additional sensitivity analysis”). Deadlines (e.g., “revised manuscript due within 60 days”) and re‑review conditions (e.g., “re‑review by Reviewer 2 only”) are essential. Omitting the mandatory/optional distinction turns a conditional acceptance into an unqualified one, a frequent examiner trap.

Condensation Technique

  • Create a three‑column table: Reviewer ID, Core Objection (≤15 words), Required Action.
  • Merge duplicate objections across reviewers into a single row with a combined reviewer list.
  • Quote the editorial decision’s exact phrasing for mandatory revisions.
  • Append the revision deadline and any re‑review instructions.

Condensing Corporate Governance Codes: Preserving Principles, Comply‑or‑Explain Provisions, and Enforcement Mechanisms

Corporate governance codes — such as the UK Corporate Governance Code 2024, OECD G20/OECD Principles (2023 revision), or Singapore Code of Corporate Governance 2018 — operate on a “comply‑or‑explain” basis. A précis must capture: (1) overarching principles (e.g., “Board leadership and company purpose”, “Division of responsibilities”, “Composition, succession and evaluation”); (2) each specific provision with its numbering (e.g., Provision 12: “The board should establish a formal nomination committee”); (3) the comply‑or‑explain requirement — whether the company must disclose compliance in its annual report or provide a reasoned explanation for non‑compliance; (4) enforcement mechanisms — the role of the Financial Reporting Council (UK), SEC (US for listed firms), or Monetary Authority of Singapore (Singapore‑listed entities), including sanctions for persistent non‑disclosure. Retain the exact wording of “comply or explain” and the reporting timeline (e.g., “within the annual report for the financial year ending 31 December 2024”). Dropping the explanation requirement or the enforcement body converts a soft‑law instrument into a mere guideline.

Template for a One‑Page Précis

  1. Code title, version, jurisdiction, effective date.
  2. Principle headings (max 8) with one‑sentence description each.
  3. Table of key provisions: Provision number, text (≤30 words), comply‑or‑explain flag (Y/N), reporting deadline.
  4. Enforcement summary: supervisory body, monitoring process, possible sanctions.

Précis of International Development Evaluation Reports: Retaining Theory of Change, Outcome Indicators, and Lessons Learned

Evaluation reports under the OECD‑DAC Criteria (Relevance, Coherence, Effectiveness, Efficiency, Impact, Sustainability) — such as World Bank IEG Project Performance Assessment Reports or UNDP Thematic Evaluations — follow a logical framework. A précis must preserve: (1) the Theory of Change (ToC) causal pathway — inputs, activities, outputs, outcomes, impact — with explicit assumptions (e.g., “Assumption A: stable macro‑economic environment”); (2) outcome indicators with baseline, target, and actual values (e.g., “Indicator 3.1: Percentage of households with improved water access — baseline 42 %, target 70 %, actual 65 %”); (3) the evaluation rating for each DAC criterion (e.g., “Effectiveness: Moderately Satisfactory”); (4) lessons learned, each linked to a specific finding (e.g., “Lesson 2: Community‑led monitoring improves sustainability — supported by Finding 4.3”). Keep exact indicator codes and rating scale terminology. Rounding an actual value from 65 % to “about two‑thirds” or omitting an assumption breaks the logical chain examiners expect.

Extraction Checklist

  • Copy the ToC narrative verbatim for the first two levels (inputs → activities).
  • List all outcome indicators in a table: code, description, baseline, target, actual, data source.
  • Record each DAC rating with the criterion name.
  • Number lessons sequentially and attach the finding reference.
  • Failing to distinguish mandatory from optional revisions in peer‑review précis.
  • Omitting the comply‑or‑explain flag for a governance provision.
  • Rounding quantitative outcome indicators instead of reproducing exact figures.
  • Dropping classification caveats when summarising national security strategy excerpts.
  • Merging jurisdictional reservations with final jurisdictional findings in arbitration procedural orders.
  • Neglecting to cite the specific DAC criterion when reporting evaluation ratings.
  • Consistent practice with these formats reduces omission errors on exam day.

Précis of International Trade Negotiation Texts: Preserving Bracketed Language, Reservations, and Consensus Language

International trade negotiation texts — such as WTO Ministerial Declarations, regional trade agreement (RTA) annexes, and plurilateral negotiating drafts — contain three layers a précis must keep distinct. First, bracketed language (e.g., “[the Parties shall endeavour to…]”) signals text not yet agreed; the brackets are part of the negotiating record and must be reproduced verbatim to show the precise scope of disagreement. Second, reservations are formal statements by a delegation limiting a provision’s legal effect for that party (e.g., a reservation stating “Article 4.2 shall not apply to agricultural subsidies”). A précis must list each reservation with the delegating country, the article referenced, and the exact wording. Third, consensus language appears without brackets or reservations; it represents the agreed core and should be summarised in the source order, preserving defined terms (e.g., “‘like product’ as defined in Article 2.1”). Omitting any layer converts a negotiating text into a misleading “final” version, a frequent examiner trap.

Condensation technique: create a three‑column table — Provision Number, Text Status (Bracketed / Reserved / Consensus), Core Obligation (≤25 words). For bracketed provisions, quote the exact bracketed segment. For reservations, add a fourth column “Reservation Holder”. Preserve original numbering (e.g., “Article 7.3”) because cross‑references in later rounds depend on it. When the source includes a “Chair’s note” summarising the state of play, treat it as a separate meta‑layer: note its presence but do not merge it into substantive provisions.

Summarizing Multi‑Stakeholder Consultation Reports: Capturing Divergent Positions, Consensus Areas, and Action Items

Multi‑stakeholder consultation reports — such as those under the UN Guiding Principles on Business and Human Rights, the OECD Due Diligence Guidance, or national climate‑policy dialogues — typically contain a matrix of stakeholder groups (government, industry, civil society, indigenous peoples, academia) and each group’s position on thematic issues. A précis must preserve three structural elements: (1) divergent positions — the exact phrasing of each group’s objection or support (e.g., an industry association stating “the proposed carbon price is economically unviable for SMEs”); (2) consensus areas — points where all or a qualified majority agree, often flagged as “agreed” or “common ground” (e.g., “all parties agree on the need for a transparent monitoring framework”); (3) action items — concrete next steps with responsible actors and deadlines (e.g., “Working Group to draft technical guidelines by [date]”). Organise the précis as a table: Issue, Stakeholder, Position (verbatim ≤30 words), Consensus Flag (Y/N), Action Item (if any). Dropping verbatim position language or merging divergent views into a single “mixed views” sentence erases the evidentiary value examiners expect.

Worked Example: Condensing a Consultation Matrix
A consultation report on a proposed national water‑allocation policy lists four stakeholder groups and three issues. For Issue 2 (environmental flow requirements), the report records: “Government: supports a minimum flow percentage; Industry: opposes any fixed percentage; NGOs: demand a higher minimum flow; Indigenous Council: requests co‑management of flow monitoring.” The report marks “No consensus” and adds an action item: “Technical Committee to model flow scenarios by [date].” Produce a précis table row for Issue 2.
Issue 2 – Environmental Flow Requirements
StakeholderPosition (verbatim ≤30 words)ConsensusAction Item
Governmentsupports a minimum flow percentageN
Industryopposes any fixed percentageN
NGOsdemand a higher minimum flowN
Indigenous Councilrequests co‑management of flow monitoringN
Consensus FlagNo consensus
Action ItemTechnical Committee to model flow scenarios by [date]
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a consultation report exceeds the word limit, first extract the consensus flag and action items — they carry the highest marks. Then copy the shortest verbatim position for each stakeholder; if a position exceeds 30 words, truncate at the nearest clause boundary and add an ellipsis in brackets. Never paraphrase a stakeholder’s exact wording.

Précis of Judicial Sentencing Guidelines: Retaining Statutory Ranges, Aggravating/Mitigating Factors, and Departure Provisions

Sentencing guidelines issued by appellate courts or statutory bodies (e.g., the United States Federal Sentencing Guidelines, the England and Wales Sentencing Council’s definitive guidelines, or the Canadian Criminal Code’s sentencing principles) consist of a grid mapping offence severity and offender history to a recommended range, plus lists of aggravating factors (e.g., “use of a weapon”, “victim vulnerability”) and mitigating factors (e.g., “early guilty plea”, “remorse”). A précis must preserve: (1) the statutory range for each offence category (e.g., “Level 3 offence: 12–18 months imprisonment”); (2) each enumerated aggravating and mitigating factor with its exact label; (3) the departure provisions — conditions under which a judge may sentence outside the range (e.g., “substantial assistance departure: up to 50 % reduction”). Render as a two‑part table: Part A — Offence Level, Statutory Range; Part B — Factor Type, Factor Text, Effect on Range (increase/decrease/neutral). Omitting a factor’s label or the quantitative effect of a departure turns a guideline into a vague recommendation, a common error.

Condensing Technical Feasibility Studies: Preserving Assumptions, Sensitivity Scenarios, and Go/No‑Go Criteria

Technical feasibility studies for infrastructure, energy, or software projects typically contain a base‑case assumption set (e.g., “capital cost $X, discount rate Y%, capacity factor Z%”), a sensitivity analysis (varying key inputs such as fuel price ±20%, construction delay ±12 months), and a go/no‑go decision rule (e.g., “NPV > 0 at base case AND IRR ≥ threshold in all sensitivity scenarios”). A précis must capture: (1) every base‑case assumption with its numeric value and unit; (2) each sensitivity variable, the range tested, and the resulting NPV/IRR shift (e.g., “Fuel price +20% → NPV decrease, IRR decrease”); (3) the explicit go/no‑go thresholds and whether the base case meets them. Present in a three‑column table: Parameter, Base Value, Sensitivity Impact (ΔNPV, ΔIRR). Append a single line stating the go/no‑go outcome (e.g., “Go — base NPV positive, all sensitivities NPV > 0”). Rounding any assumption or omitting a sensitivity variable invalidates the decision logic examiners test.

Précis of Parliamentary Committee Evidence Sessions: Distinguishing Witness Testimony, Expert Opinion, and Committee Conclusions

Parliamentary committee evidence sessions (e.g., UK House of Commons Select Committee oral evidence, US Congressional hearing transcripts, Indian Parliamentary Standing Committee proceedings) produce a verbatim record mixing three voices: witness testimony (factual recollection, e.g., “On [date] the system logged X transactions”), expert opinion (interpretive analysis, e.g., “The latency pattern suggests a bottleneck in the middleware layer”), and committee conclusions (the chair’s or rapporteur’s summary, often prefaced by “The Committee notes…”). A précis must separate these layers. Technique: annotate each paragraph with a tag — [W] for witness, [E] for expert, [C] for committee. Then produce three concise bullet lists: (1) Key Facts (witness statements, ≤20 words each); (2) Expert Assessments (expert statements, preserving any quantitative claim); (3) Committee Findings (conclusions, retaining recommendation language such as “recommends that the Ministry publish guidelines by [quarter/year]”). Merging an expert’s speculative language into a factual list, or attributing a committee recommendation to a witness, are high‑penalty errors.

  • Failing to reproduce bracketed language exactly in trade negotiation précis.
  • Merging divergent stakeholder positions into a single “mixed views” sentence.
  • Omitting any aggravating or mitigating factor label in sentencing guidelines précis.
  • Rounding base‑case assumptions or dropping a sensitivity variable in feasibility study précis.
  • Attributing committee conclusions to witness testimony in parliamentary evidence précis.
  • Neglecting to note the go/no‑go decision rule outcome in feasibility study précis.
  • Leaving out reservation holder identification in trade negotiation précis.

Précis of International Criminal Court Judgments: Preserving Elements of Crimes, Modes of Liability, and Reparations Orders

The International Criminal Court (ICC), established by the Rome Statute (1 July 2002), issues judgments with three interlocked layers: the legal elements of each crime, the mode‑of‑liability findings, and the reparations order. A précis must keep each layer distinct and verbatim where the Statute requires. The Elements of Crimes document (adopted 2002, amended 2010) lists, for every offence under Articles 6‑8, the conduct, context, and mental element. When a judgment convicts for war crimes under Article 8(2)(a)(i) (wilful killing), the précis must reproduce the exact phrasing — “the perpetrator killed one or more persons” and “the conduct took place in the context of and was associated with an international armed conflict” — because examiners test the distinction between conduct and contextual elements.

Modes of liability are governed by Articles 25 (individual criminal responsibility), 28 (command responsibility), and 30 (mental element). A judgment may cite Article 25(3)(a) (committing), 25(3)(b) (ordering, soliciting, or inducing), or 25(3)(d) (contributing in any other way). The précis must record the precise sub‑paragraph, the Chamber’s factual basis, and whether “joint criminal enterprise” or “co‑perpetration” theory was applied. Omitting the sub‑paragraph or conflating Article 25 with Article 28 is a high‑penalty error.

Reparations orders under Article 75 are separate from the conviction. They specify beneficiaries, forms of reparation (restitution, compensation, rehabilitation, satisfaction, guarantees of non‑repetition), and procedural steps (Trust Fund for Victims, Registry). The précis must list each reparation type, the quantum if quantified, and any conditions precedent (e.g., “subject to the availability of funds”). The Chamber’s proportionality reasoning and the harm‑conduct link must be captured in one sentence per reparation type. Failure to separate the reparations paragraph from the conviction paragraph loses marks for structural clarity.

Technique: annotate the judgment with three colour codes — blue for elements, green for liability mode, red for reparations. Draft three bullet clusters headed by the relevant Statute article. Keep each bullet under 25 words; truncate at the nearest clause boundary and add an ellipsis in brackets if needed. This ensures the examiner sees the three logical layers in the Statute’s prescribed order.

Summarizing Systematic Review Protocols: PRISMA‑P Items, Registration Details, and Amendment Tracking

A systematic review protocol is a prospective plan reported per the PRISMA‑P 2015 checklist (17 items) and registered in a public database such as PROSPERO before data extraction. A précis must preserve three streams: checklist compliance, registration metadata, and amendment history. PRISMA‑P items cover rationale, objectives, eligibility criteria, information sources, search strategy, study records, data items, risk‑of‑bias assessment, data synthesis, meta‑analysis methods, heterogeneity investigation, sensitivity analysis, publication bias assessment, and ethics/dissemination. When a protocol claims compliance, the précis must note which items are fully addressed, partially addressed, or omitted — using exact item numbers (e.g., “Item 8: Search strategy — fully described for MEDLINE, Embase, Cochrane CENTRAL; grey literature search not specified”).

Registration details include the PROSPERO number (e.g., CRD42023123456), registration date, registered title, and anticipated start/completion dates. Deviations require listing each amendment with date, PROSPERO amendment number, and a concise change description (e.g., “Amendment 1, 12‑Mar‑2024: expanded eligibility to include non‑English publications”). The PRISMA‑P‑E “track changes” table should be reproduced in condensed form: columns — Item, Original Text, Amended Text, Reason — one row per amendment.

Examiners often ask for the “risk‑of‑bias tool” and “planned synthesis method”; the précis must quote the exact tool (e.g., “RoB 2 for randomized trials”, “ROBINS‑I for non‑randomized studies”) and synthesis approach (e.g., “random‑effects meta‑analysis using DerSimonian‑Laird; if I² > 75 % a narrative synthesis will be presented”). Any deviation from the registered plan must be flagged. Omitting the registration number or an amendment adding a new outcome domain are common traps that cost marks.

Worked Example: Condensing a Systematic Review Protocol
A protocol registered as PROSPERO CRD42023123456 (registered 05‑Jan‑2023) states: “Objective: to assess the effectiveness of cognitive‑behavioural therapy (CBT) for adult depression.” Eligibility: RCTs, adults 18‑65, CBT vs waitlist. Search: MEDLINE, PsycINFO, Cochrane CENTRAL, 2000‑2022, English only. Risk of bias: RoB 2. Synthesis: random‑effects meta‑analysis, DerSimonian‑Laird. Amendment 1 (12‑Mar‑2024): added non‑English studies, updated search to 2023. Write a précis of ≤120 words preserving PRISMA‑P items, registration, and amendment.
Protocol CRD42023123456 (05‑Jan‑2023) evaluates CBT for adult depression (Item 1‑3). Eligibility: RCTs, adults 18‑65, CBT vs waitlist (Item 5). Search: MEDLINE, PsycINFO, CENTRAL, 2000‑2022, English only (Item 7). Risk of bias: RoB 2 (Item 12). Synthesis: random‑effects DerSimonian‑Laird (Item 13). Amendment 1 (12‑Mar‑2024): inclusion of non‑English studies; search extended to 2023 (Item 7, 11). All PRISMA‑P items addressed except grey‑literature search (Item 7 partially).
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a protocol précis appears, first locate the PROSPERO number and date — they are the anchor. Then tick off PRISMA‑P items on a scratch sheet; any missing item is a guaranteed mark‑loss if not noted. Write the amendment table in a single line per amendment; examiners award full credit for a compact, accurate change log.

Condensing Corporate Sustainability Reporting Standards: GRI, SASB, and TCFD Alignment Mapping

Corporate sustainability disclosures increasingly require alignment across three frameworks: the Global Reporting Initiative (GRI) Standards (2021 update), the Sustainability Accounting Standards Board (SASB) Standards (2023 version), and the Task Force on Climate‑related Financial Disclosures (TCFD) recommendations (2017, updated 2021). A précis mapping a company’s report must preserve each standard’s hierarchy. GRI is organised into Universal Standards (GRI 1‑3), Sector Standards, and Topic Standards (e.g., GRI 303: Water and Effluents). SASB provides industry‑specific standards with disclosure topics, accounting metrics, and technical protocols (e.g., SASB IF‑EU‑110a.1 for GHG emissions in electric utilities). TCFD structures disclosures around four pillars — Governance, Strategy, Risk Management, Metrics & Targets — each with recommended disclosures (e.g., “Describe the board’s oversight of climate‑related risks”).

The précis must produce a three‑column alignment table: Column A — GRI disclosure code and title; Column B — SASB metric code and definition; Column C — TCFD pillar and recommended disclosure. Where a single data point satisfies multiple frameworks (e.g., Scope 1 + 2 GHG emissions as GRI 305‑1, SASB IF‑EU‑110a.1, TCFD Metrics & Targets), list it once and annotate all three codes. If a framework requires narrative where another uses quantitative metrics, note the format difference (e.g., “GRI 102‑46: narrative stakeholder engagement process; SASB IF‑EU‑110a.2: quantitative stakeholder‑engagement score”). Omitting a mandatory GRI Universal Standard (GRI 2‑1 to 2‑7) or a TCFD core recommendation (e.g., scenario analysis) is a high‑penalty omission.

Additionally, the précis must capture the reporting period, assurance level (limited, reasonable, or none), and materiality determination method (GRI’s material topics vs SASB’s financially material topics vs TCFD’s climate‑related materiality). If a “double materiality” approach is used, state the method and note any divergence between the two lenses. The final output should be a concise table plus a one‑paragraph summary of coverage gaps (e.g., “No disclosure for GRI 303‑3 water recycling; SASB metric IF‑EU‑110a.3 not reported; TCFD scenario analysis absent”).

Précis of Legislative Drafting Manuals: Preserving Definitional Hierarchies, Commencement Provisions, and Transitional Arrangements

Legislative drafting manuals — such as the UK Office of Parliamentary Counsel (OPC) Drafting Guidance (2022), the US Legislative Drafting Manual (2020), and the Indian Legislative Department’s Manual of Parliamentary Procedure (2019) — prescribe a strict hierarchy: definitions, commencement clauses, and transitional provisions. A précis must retain this ordering. First, the “Interpretation” or “Definitions” clause (often Section 2) lists defined terms alphabetically and may cross‑reference other Acts (e.g., “‘Company’ has the meaning assigned in the Companies Act 2013”). Second, the “Commencement” clause (often Section 1(2) or a Schedule) specifies the date or event bringing the Act into force, distinguishing “appointed day” from “phased commencement” (e.g., “Sections 3‑5 come into force on 1 April 2025; the remainder on Royal Assent”). Third, “Transitional and Savings” provisions (often a Schedule) preserve rights, obligations, or proceedings under repealed legislation, using formulae such as “Notwithstanding the repeal of the 1998 Act, any licence granted under that Act continues in force as if granted under this Act”.

The précis must reproduce the definitional hierarchy verbatim for each “key” term. For commencement, note whether the Act uses a “fixed date”, “order‑making power”, or “contingent event” (e.g., “on the day the Minister lays a commencement order before Parliament”). For transitional arrangements, list each saved instrument, the legal effect (continuation, modification, or expiry), and any time‑limit (e.g., “saved regulations expire 24 months after commencement”). Examiners test the ability to differentiate a “savings clause” (preserves existing rights) from a “transitional clause” (modifies application of new law to old facts). Conflating the two or omitting the appointed‑day mechanism loses marks.

Technique: create a three‑panel outline — Definitions, Commencement, Transitional — and copy exact statutory language for each entry, truncating only at clause boundaries. Use square brackets for omitted sub‑clauses (e.g., “[…]”). This preserves the manual’s logical architecture within word‑count limits.

Handling Counterfactual Scenario Analysis in Climate Integrated Assessment Models: Scenario Families, Probabilistic Weighting, and Policy Pathways

Integrated Assessment Models (IAMs) used by the IPCC employ scenario families — Shared Socioeconomic Pathways (SSP1‑5) combined with Representative Concentration Pathways (RCP2.6, 4.5, 6.0, 8.5) — to explore counterfactual futures. A précis of an IAM‑based policy brief must preserve three layers: the scenario matrix, the probabilistic weighting method, and the resulting policy pathways. The scenario matrix cross‑tabulates SSP narratives (e.g., SSP1 “Sustainability”, SSP3 “Regional Rivalry”) against radiative‑forcing targets (e.g., RCP2.6 ≈ 1.5 °C). The précis must list each SSP‑RCP pair, key socioeconomic assumptions (population, GDP, energy intensity), and climate outcomes (median temperature rise, sea‑level rise).

Probabilistic weighting assigns likelihoods via expert elicitation or Monte‑Carlo sampling of uncertain parameters (climate sensitivity, carbon‑cycle feedbacks). The précis must state the weighting approach (e.g., “equal weighting across SSPs; log‑normal distribution for equilibrium climate sensitivity with median 3 °C, 5‑95 % range 2‑4.5 °C”) and any scenario‑rejection criteria (e.g., “scenarios exceeding 4 °C by 2100 excluded”). Policy pathways are model‑derived mitigation sequences (carbon price trajectory, technology deployment, land‑use change) achieving a target under each weighted scenario. The précis must capture quantitative milestones (e.g., “global CO₂ price reaches US $120/tCO₂ by 2030 under SSP1‑RCP2.6; renewable share 70 % of electricity by 2040”). Omitting the weighting rationale or presenting a single deterministic pathway as the only outcome is a frequent examiner trap.

When the source includes a “scenario discovery” analysis (e.g., using the Patient Rule Induction Method to identify conditions for high‑impact outcomes), the précis must note the method, key decision rules, and policy implication (e.g., “If carbon price < US $30/tCO₂ and CCS deployment < 5 GtCO₂/yr, probability of > 2 °C exceeds 80 %”). This detail distinguishes a passing précis from a superficial summary.

  • Confusing the Elements of Crimes with the factual findings in an ICC judgment.
  • Omitting the PROSPERO registration number or amendment log in a systematic review protocol précis.
  • Failing to map a single data point to all three sustainability frameworks (GRI, SASB, TCFD) in an alignment table.
  • Conflating a savings clause with a transitional clause in a legislative drafting manual précis.
  • Presenting a single deterministic IAM pathway without stating the probabilistic weighting method.
  • Neglecting to note the reporting period and assurance level in a corporate sustainability précis.
  • Truncating a defined term in a legislative manual before the clause boundary, losing the cross‑reference.

Précis of International Human Rights Treaty Body General Comments: Preserving Interpretive Guidance, State Reporting Cycles, and Follow‑Up Procedures

A General Comment issued by a treaty body (e.g., the Human Rights Committee, the Committee on Economic, Social and Cultural Rights) functions as an authoritative interpretation of a specific treaty provision. The précis must capture three structural layers: interpretive guidance, the reporting cycle addressed, and follow‑up mechanisms expected. Begin by identifying the treaty article under commentary, the paragraph numbers containing core normative statements, and any cross‑references to other General Comments or jurisprudence. Preserve the exact wording of definitional sentences (e.g., a clause defining “forced labour” under Article 8) because examiners test verbatim retention of legal definitions.

The reporting cycle layer requires noting the periodicity of state reports (typically every four to five years), the specific reporting guidelines the Comment supplements, and any provisional measures recommended for the interim. If the Comment introduces a new reporting template or set of indicators, list each indicator and its intended disaggregation (by gender, age, disability). The follow‑up layer includes the body’s request for a “follow‑up report” within a defined timeframe (often twelve months), the designation of a focal point within the state apparatus, and procedural steps such as a “constructive dialogue” session. Omitting the follow‑up deadline or the distinction between “recommendations” and “observations” is a frequent error.

When the source text contains a table summarising the Comment’s “key obligations”, reproduce the table’s column headings and the first two rows verbatim, then indicate truncation with square brackets. This preserves the logical architecture while respecting word‑count limits. Finally, note any dissenting views recorded in the Comment’s annex, because they signal interpretive contestation that may appear in comparative questions.

Condensing National Security Strategy Documents: Threat Assessment, Capability Gaps, and Resource Allocation Priorities

National security strategy (NSS) documents are structured around a threat assessment, a capability gap analysis, and a resource allocation matrix. The précis must retain the hierarchy of threats as presented — typically “existential”, “strategic”, and “operational” — together with the evidentiary basis (intelligence assessments, open‑source trend data, scenario modelling). Preserve the exact threat labels (e.g., “state‑sponsored cyber espionage” or “transnational terrorist networks”) because they serve as indexing terms for later retrieval.

The capability gap section enumerates shortfalls across domains (land, air, maritime, cyber, space) and links each gap to a specific threat. The précis should list each domain, the quantitative shortfall (e.g., a 12 % air‑lift deficit relative to a 2030 requirement), and the mitigation option proposed (procurement, partnership, doctrine change). Do not collapse distinct gaps into a generic “capacity shortfall”; examiners award marks for domain‑specific fidelity.

Resource allocation priorities appear in a multi‑year funding table. Capture the fiscal years, the percentage of total defence budget earmarked for each priority, and any conditional language (“subject to parliamentary approval”, “contingent on allied burden‑sharing”). If the document includes a “risk‑adjusted” allocation scenario, note the assumptions (discount rate, probability weighting) and the resulting shift in percentages. Truncate the table after the first three priority rows, inserting “[…]” at the row boundary.

Finally, record any implementation oversight mechanisms: a national security council review cycle, an independent audit mandate, or a parliamentary committee reporting requirement. These procedural details are often tested in comparative questions that ask how the strategy translates into accountable action.

Technique: Three‑Panel Outline for NSS Précis
A 250‑word source text outlines a national security strategy with five threat categories, three capability gaps, and a four‑year funding table. Construct a précis of no more than 80 words that preserves the threat hierarchy, the quantitative gaps, and the top two funding priorities.
Threats: existential – nuclear proliferation; strategic – state‑sponsored cyber espionage; operational – transnational terrorism; emerging – climate‑driven instability; hybrid – disinformation campaigns. Gaps: air‑lift ‑12 % (2030), cyber workforce ‑8 % (2028), maritime ISR ‑15 % (2032). Funding FY2024‑27: 38 % to nuclear deterrence modernisation; 22 % to cyber resilience; […]
Exam‑Day Strategy
When time is short, sketch a three‑column table on the rough sheet: Threat | Gap | Funding. Fill only the first two rows per column; the examiner rewards structural completeness over exhaustive detail.

Summarizing Public Health Emergency Operation Plans: Command Structures, Surge Capacity, and Communication Protocols

Public health emergency operation plans (EOP) are organized around an incident command system (ICS), surge capacity calculations, and a multi‑channel communication protocol. The précis must reproduce the command hierarchy exactly as labelled (e.g., “Incident Commander”, “Operations Section Chief”, “Logistics Section Chief”) and note any unified command arrangements with neighbouring jurisdictions or military assets. Preserve the activation triggers (e.g., a threshold such as “Level 1 activation when confirmed cases exceed 50 per 100 000 population”) because they define the plan’s operational threshold.

Surge capacity is expressed in bed‑equivalents, staffing ratios, and supply stockpiles. Capture each metric with its unit and the planning horizon (e.g., “2 000 additional ICU bed‑equivalents within 72 hours; nurse‑to‑patient ratio 1:2 for critical care; 30‑day PPE stockpile at 150 % of baseline consumption”). If the plan references a “tiered surge” (conventional, contingency, crisis), list the tier thresholds and the corresponding resource mobilisation steps. Omitting the tier distinction collapses the plan’s flexibility and loses marks.

Communication protocols specify audiences (public, clinicians, media, partner agencies), channels (press releases, secure messaging platforms, community liaisons), and message templates (case definition updates, vaccination site locations, rumor‑control scripts). The précis should note the approval workflow (e.g., “All public messages cleared by the Joint Information Center within 30 minutes”) and any language‑access requirements (translation into the top five non‑official languages). When the source includes a flowchart, describe its decision nodes in linear prose rather than reproducing the graphic.

Finally, record the after‑action review (AAR) schedule: a hot‑wash within 48 hours, a formal AAR within 30 days, and a lessons‑learned database update cycle. These procedural commitments demonstrate the plan’s learning loop and are frequently examined.

Précis of Multilateral Development Bank Safeguard Policy Updates: Categorization, Mitigation Measures, and Compliance Monitoring

Multilateral development banks (MDBs) periodically revise their environmental and social safeguard policies. A précis of such an update must preserve the revised categorization framework (e.g., Category A – high risk, Category B – moderate risk, Category C – low risk, Category FI – financial intermediaries), the criteria that trigger each category, and any new sub‑categories (e.g., “Category B‑1 for projects involving indigenous peoples”). Quote the definitional thresholds verbatim (e.g., a threshold such as “Project likely to cause significant adverse impacts that are irreversible or unprecedented”).

The mitigation measures section introduces or amends specific performance standards (e.g., “Performance Standard 6: Biodiversity Conservation and Sustainable Management of Living Natural Resources”). For each standard, capture the core requirement, the applicable project phase (design, construction, operation), and the monitoring indicator (e.g., “Net‑gain in habitat area of ≥ 10 % within five years”). If the update adds a “climate‑resilience” overlay, note the additional adaptation criteria and the required climate‑risk screening tool.

Compliance monitoring details the supervision architecture: the bank’s safeguard unit, independent inspection panels, and borrower‑level grievance redress mechanisms. Record the reporting frequency (quarterly safeguard reports, annual compliance certificates), the escalation triggers (e.g., “two consecutive non‑compliance findings invoke a project‑level review”), and the remediation timeline (e.g., “corrective action plan due within 60 days”). Truncate lengthy procedural annexes after the first two escalation steps, inserting “[…]” at the clause boundary.

When the update includes a transition clause for projects approved under the previous policy, preserve the grandfathering language (e.g., “Projects approved before 1 January 2024 remain subject to the 2017 safeguard framework unless a material change occurs”). This distinction is a common examiner trap.

Handling Mixed‑Methods Research Synthesis in Policy Briefs: Integrating Qualitative Coding, Quantitative Meta‑Analysis, and Triangulation Logic

Policy briefs that synthesize mixed‑methods evidence require a précis that reflects the integration logic, not merely a list of findings. Identify the qualitative component (e.g., thematic analysis of stakeholder interviews using a grounded‑theory approach), the quantitative component (e.g., random‑effects meta‑analysis of 12 RCTs measuring programme effect size), and the triangulation protocol (e.g., “convergent parallel design with joint display matrix”). Preserve the coding framework’s top‑level themes and the meta‑analysis’s pooled effect estimate with confidence interval (e.g., “standardised mean difference = 0.42, 95 % CI 0.18‑0.66”).

The integration narrative often follows a “qualitative explains quantitative” or “quantitative tests qualitative” pattern. The précis must state which pattern the brief adopts and illustrate with one concrete example (e.g., “Qualitative theme ‘trust in providers’ explains heterogeneity in treatment adherence observed in the meta‑analysis (I² = 68 %)”). If the brief uses a joint display, describe its structure (rows = quantitative outcomes, columns = qualitative themes) and note any discordant cells.

Assess the quality appraisal tools applied to each strand (e.g., CASP for qualitative studies, Cochrane RoB 2 for trials) and the overall confidence rating (e.g., GRADE‑CERQual “moderate confidence”). Record any sensitivity analyses (e.g., exclusion of high‑risk‑of‑bias trials shifts pooled effect to 0.35) because they demonstrate robustness. Truncate the list of included studies after the first three citations, using “[…]” at the reference boundary.

Finally, capture the policy implications derived from the integrated evidence: a prioritized recommendation list, the evidence grade for each recommendation, and any implementation caveats (e.g., “Recommendation 1 (high confidence): scale up community health worker model; caveat – requires sustainable financing”). This layered preservation mirrors the brief’s decision‑support function.

  • Conflating a General Comment’s “recommendations” with its “observations” and omitting the follow‑up deadline.
  • Collapsing distinct capability gaps across domains into a single generic shortfall in an NSS précis.
  • Neglecting tier thresholds in a public health EOP surge‑capacity description.
  • Omitting the transition clause for legacy projects when summarising MDB safeguard updates.
  • Presenting qualitative and quantitative findings side‑by‑side without stating the triangulation design.
  • Failing to preserve the exact confidence‑interval notation from a meta‑analysis in a mixed‑methods synthesis.

Précis of National Security Strategy Documents: Retaining Threat Assessments, Capability Gaps, and Resource Allocation Priorities

National security strategy (NSS) documents typically follow a predictable architecture: a strategic environment scan, a hierarchy of national interests, a threat‑assessment matrix, a capability‑gap analysis, and a resource‑allocation plan tied to a review cycle. When condensing an NSS, preserve the strategic narrative (e.g., “great‑power competition in the Indo‑Pacific”) because it frames every subsequent judgment. Capture each threat category (state, non‑state, cyber, hybrid) with the likelihood‑impact rating the document assigns (commonly a three‑tier scale: high/medium/low). Do not collapse distinct threat vectors into a single “security challenges” line; examiners test whether you can differentiate a “persistent cyber‑espionage campaign” from a “conventional force posture shift”.

The capability‑gap section usually lists domains (air, maritime, space, information) alongside quantitative shortfalls (e.g., an illustrative “shortfall of 12 fourth‑generation fighters”). Retain the numeric gap and the time‑bound procurement target (e.g., “induct 8 platforms by FY 2028”). The resource‑allocation table links budget lines to capability programmes; summarise the top three budget shares (percentage of defence budget) and any conditional funding triggers (e.g., “subject to parliamentary approval of the strategic‑capability fund”). Finally, note the review mechanism (annual strategic review, mid‑term adjustment) because it signals the document’s living nature and is a frequent exam hook.

Précis of Scientific Peer‑Review Reports: Preserving Reviewer Comments, Editorial Decisions, and Revision Requirements

A peer‑review report typically contains four layers: manuscript metadata, reviewer‑level comments (major and minor), the editor’s decision letter, and a revision checklist. In a précis, the metadata (title, journal, manuscript ID) can be reduced to a single line, but the decision (accept, minor revision, major revision, reject) must be stated verbatim because it determines the downstream workflow. For each reviewer, separate major concerns (methodological flaws, missing controls, statistical inadequacies) from minor concerns (clarity, figure resolution, reference formatting). Preserve the exact phrasing of any “must‑address” items (e.g., “Re‑analyse the survival data using a Cox proportional‑hazards model with time‑varying covariates”).

The editorial synthesis often ranks concerns by priority; capture that ranking (e.g., “Priority 1: sample‑size justification; Priority 2: data‑availability statement”). The revision checklist enumerates concrete actions (additional experiments, supplementary tables, raw‑data deposition). Summarise the checklist by grouping actions into experimental, analytical, and administrative categories, and note any deadline the editor imposes (e.g., “revised manuscript due within 60 days”). Omit boilerplate language such as “Thank you for submitting” but keep any conditional acceptance language (e.g., “Acceptance pending satisfactory response to major comments”).

Technique
A peer‑review report for a hypothetical manuscript on “Urban Heat Island Mitigation” contains the following: Reviewer 1 raises three major concerns – (i) the lack of a control city, (ii) insufficient statistical power for the regression model, (iii) missing raw temperature logs. Reviewer 2 adds two minor concerns – figure‑label clarity and reference style. The editor issues a “Major Revision” decision with a 45‑day deadline and lists required actions: add a control city, re‑run power analysis, deposit raw data in a public repository, improve figure labels, and adjust references. Write a 70‑word précis that retains the decision, all major concerns, and the required actions.
The editor issued a “Major Revision” (45‑day deadline). Reviewer 1 identified three major concerns: absence of a control city, inadequate statistical power for the regression, and missing raw temperature logs. Reviewer 2 noted minor issues on figure labels and reference style. Required actions: incorporate a control city, re‑run power analysis, deposit raw data publicly, improve figure labels, and correct reference formatting.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a précis prompt asks for a peer‑review summary, first locate the decision word (accept/reject/revision) – it is the anchor. Then list major concerns in the order the editor prioritises them; minor concerns can be collapsed into a single clause. Use the exact verbs from the checklist (“add”, “re‑run”, “deposit”) because examiners award marks for lexical fidelity. Practice converting a 300‑word report into a 70‑word précis under timed conditions to internalise the compression rhythm.

Précis of Public Procurement Tender Documents: Preserving Evaluation Criteria, Qualification Requirements, and Contractual Milestones

Public procurement tenders are structured around an invitation, scope of work, evaluation methodology, qualification thresholds, and a contract‑execution schedule. A high‑scoring précis must retain the evaluation criteria matrix – typically a weighted split between technical merit (e.g., 60 %) and financial offer (40 %). Within technical merit, capture each sub‑criterion (experience, methodology, key personnel, innovation) and its weight. Do not merge sub‑criteria; examiners often ask for the exact weight of “past performance in similar contracts”.

The qualification requirements section lists mandatory certifications (ISO 9001, security clearances), minimum turnover, and specific project references (e.g., “at least two contracts valued ≥ ₹50 crore in the last five years”). Preserve the numeric thresholds and any “or equivalent” language. The contractual milestones define delivery dates, payment triggers, and performance‑bond percentages. Summarise the milestone calendar by stating the critical path dates (design approval, prototype delivery, final acceptance) and the associated payment percentages (e.g., “10 % on design sign‑off, 40 % on prototype acceptance, 50 % on final acceptance”). Include any liquidated‑damages clause (rate per day of delay) because it is a frequent exam trap.

Précis of Climate Finance Facility Project Appraisals: Capturing Additionality, Co‑Financing Structures, and Results Frameworks

Climate finance facility appraisals (e.g., Green Climate Fund, Global Environment Facility) follow a logic model: additionality justification, co‑financing architecture, results framework, risk‑mitigation measures, and monitoring‑evaluation plan. In a précis, the additionality argument must be reproduced in the facility’s own terminology (e.g., “the project would not proceed without concessional finance because the internal rate of return falls below the private‑sector hurdle rate of 12 %”). Quote the additionality test (financial, technological, or institutional) and the counterfactual baseline.

The co‑financing table lists each contributor (multilateral, bilateral, private), instrument type (grant, concessional loan, equity), and share of total project cost. Preserve the percentage share and any conditionality (e.g., “private equity release contingent on achieving construction‑phase milestones”). The results framework contains a hierarchy of indicators: output, outcome, and impact, each with a baseline, target, and verification source. Summarise the top‑level impact indicator (e.g., “annual CO₂e reduction of 1.2 Mt by year 5”) and the key outcome indicators (e.g., “installed renewable capacity 300 MW”). Note the monitoring frequency (annual MRV reports) and the independent verification entity. Finally, capture any safeguard triggers (environmental and social risk category) and the associated mitigation hierarchy (avoid, minimise, compensate).

  • Collapsing distinct threat vectors in an NSS précis into a generic “security challenges” line.
  • Omitting the editor’s explicit decision word (accept, major revision, reject) in a peer‑review summary.
  • Merging technical sub‑criteria weights in a procurement tender instead of listing each weight separately.
  • Dropping the numeric additionality hurdle rate or counterfactual baseline in a climate‑finance appraisal.
  • Failing to retain the liquidated‑damages rate or payment‑trigger percentages in a tender précis.
  • Summarising co‑financing shares without noting instrument‑specific conditionality.
  • Review each trap carefully before the exam.

Précis of Health Technology Assessment Reports: Preserving Clinical Effectiveness, Cost‑Effectiveness Thresholds, and Recommendation Grades
