Health technology assessment (HTA) reports follow a structured logic model: clinical effectiveness evidence, economic evaluation, ethical‑social considerations, and a final recommendation grade. A high‑scoring précis must retain the hierarchy of evidence – systematic reviews, randomised controlled trials, and real‑world data – and indicate the grading system used (e.g., GRADE, NICE technology appraisal categories). The clinical effectiveness section should summarise the primary outcome (mortality, morbidity, quality‑adjusted life years) with the pooled effect size and confidence interval, not merely a narrative “the drug works”.

The economic evaluation component requires the incremental cost‑effectiveness ratio (ICER) expressed in the local currency per QALY gained, the willingness‑to‑pay threshold applied (e.g., £20 000–£30 000 per QALY in the UK, ₹1 lakh per QALY in India), and the sensitivity‑analysis results (deterministic and probabilistic). Omitting the threshold or the ICER value is a frequent error. The recommendation grade (e.g., “recommended”, “optimised”, “only in research”) must be reproduced verbatim because examiners award marks for lexical fidelity to the official wording.

Ethical‑social domains (equity, access, budget impact) are often condensed into a single clause; however, if the report flags a specific equity concern (e.g., “higher budget impact for rural populations”), that nuance must survive the compression. Finally, the précis should note the decision‑making body (NICE, CADTH, ICMR) and the date of the appraisal, as these contextual markers are sometimes tested in comparative questions.

Précis of Energy Transition Roadmaps: Capturing Decarbonisation Pathways, Milestone Targets, and Policy Levers

Energy transition roadmaps (e.g., IEA Net Zero by 2050, national long‑term strategies) are built around a scenario architecture: a reference scenario, a policy‑driven scenario, and a net‑zero scenario. The précis must identify the modelling framework (integrated assessment model, bottom‑up optimisation) and the key drivers – carbon price trajectory, renewable capacity additions, electrification rates, hydrogen deployment, and carbon‑capture utilisation and storage (CCUS) volumes.

Milestone targets are the examiners’ anchor points. Preserve the quantitative markers: “coal phase‑out by 2030”, “renewable share of electricity 70 % by 2035”, “electric‑vehicle stock 200 million by 2040”, “industrial CO₂ capture 1.5 Gt yr⁻¹ by 2050”. Each target should be linked to the policy lever that enables it (carbon tax level, subsidy schedule, regulatory standard, public‑investment commitment). Do not merge distinct levers into a generic “policy support” phrase; the weight of each lever is often examined.

The roadmap usually includes a sensitivity or scenario‑analysis table showing how outcomes shift under alternative assumptions (e.g., low‑innovation, high‑fuel‑price). Summarise the direction of change (e.g., “under low‑innovation, renewable capacity falls 15 % and net‑zero is delayed by a decade”) rather than reproducing the full table. Finally, note the governance structure – inter‑ministerial steering committee, independent monitoring body, and reporting cadence (annual progress report, five‑year review) – because these institutional details differentiate a roadmap from a mere aspirational document.

Worked Précis – Energy Transition Roadmap
A 350‑word excerpt from a national net‑zero roadmap states: “The reference scenario projects 2.8 Gt CO₂ emissions in 2030. The policy scenario introduces a carbon price rising from US$25/tCO₂ in 2025 to US$120/tCO₂ in 2040, driving coal retirement by 2030 and raising renewable electricity share to 65 % by 2035. The net‑zero scenario adds a US$200/tCO₂ price by 2045, mandates 100 % zero‑emission vehicle sales by 2035, and targets 2 Gt yr⁻¹ CCUS by 2050. Sensitivity analysis shows a 10 % lower renewable cost advances the 65 % share to 2032. Governance: an inter‑ministerial council reports annually to parliament.” Write a 80‑word précis.
The roadmap contrasts a reference case (2.8 Gt CO₂ in 2030) with a policy scenario (carbon price US$25–120/tCO₂, coal exit 2030, renewables 65 % by 2035) and a net‑zero scenario (price US$200/tCO₂ by 2045, 100 % ZEV sales 2035, 2 Gt yr⁻¹ CCUS 2050). Sensitivity: 10 % cheaper renewables pulls 65 % share to 2032. Governance: inter‑ministerial council, annual parliamentary reporting.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a précis prompt asks for a roadmap summary, first locate the scenario labels (reference, policy, net‑zero) – they are the structural anchors. List each scenario’s headline carbon price, phase‑out date, and renewable target in the order presented. Use the exact verbs (“mandates”, “targets”, “drives”) because examiners reward lexical fidelity. Practice compressing a 300‑word roadmap excerpt to 70‑80 words under a 5‑minute timer to internalise the rhythm.

Précis of International Maritime Law Instruments: Retaining Navigational Rights, Environmental Obligations, and Dispute Settlement Mechanisms

Key instruments – UNCLOS (1982), the International Convention for the Prevention of Pollution from Ships (MARPOL), the International Convention on Standards of Training, Certification and Watchkeeping for Seafarers (STCW), and the Convention on the International Regulations for Preventing Collisions at Sea (COLREGs) – each contain a distinct architecture of rights, duties, and enforcement. A précis must preserve the zonal framework of UNCLOS: internal waters, territorial sea (12 nm), contiguous zone (24 nm), exclusive economic zone (200 nm), continental shelf, and the high seas. For each zone, note the coastal state’s sovereign rights (resource exploitation, marine scientific research) and the freedoms retained by other states (navigation, overflight, laying of submarine cables).

Environmental obligations under Part XII of UNCLOS and MARPOL Annexes I–VI require the précis to capture the discharge standards (e.g., oil content meter 15 ppm, sulphur limit 0.50 % m/m from 2020), the designation of special areas, and the flag‑state/port‑state enforcement matrix. Do not collapse the annexes into a single “pollution prevention” line; examiners often ask for the specific annex governing sewage or air emissions.

Dispute settlement under UNCLOS Part XV offers a choice of procedures: the International Tribunal for the Law of the Sea (ITLOS), the International Court of Justice (ICJ), an arbitral tribunal, or a special arbitral tribunal. The précis must record the compulsory procedures entailing binding decisions (Section 2) and the optional exceptions (marine scientific research, fisheries, military activities). If the source text cites a specific case (e.g., “South China Sea Arbitration, 2016”), retain the case name and the core finding (e.g., “no legal basis for historic rights claim”).

Précis of Public Opinion Survey Methodology Reports: Preserving Sampling Design, Weighting Procedures, and Margin of Error

Methodology reports for large‑scale surveys (e.g., World Values Survey, national election studies) follow a standard reporting template: target population, sampling frame, sampling design (stratified multistage probability, quota, or mixed‑mode), sample size, response rate, weighting methodology (post‑stratification, raking, propensity‑score adjustment), and margin of error (often reported at the 95 % confidence level for the overall sample and for key sub‑groups). A précis must retain the numeric values: “national adult population 18+, stratified by region and urban/rural, 4 000 completed interviews, response rate 62 %, design effect 1.3, weights calibrated to census age‑sex‑education distribution, overall margin of error ±1.5 %”.

If the report details mode effects (face‑to‑face vs. telephone vs. online) and the statistical tests used to assess them (chi‑square, logistic regression), summarise the direction and significance (e.g., “online mode yields 3 % higher support for policy X, p < 0.01”). Non‑response bias analysis (comparison of respondents vs. frame on demographics) should be noted if the report flags a material bias. Finally, capture the data‑access statement (public use file, restricted access, embargo period) because examiners sometimes test awareness of open‑data norms.

Précis of Financial Regulation Consultation Papers: Retaining Regulatory Impact Analysis, Stakeholder Feedback Summaries, and Implementation Timelines

Consultation papers from financial regulators (e.g., Basel Committee, national central banks, securities commissions) typically contain: a problem statement, regulatory objectives, proposed rule text, regulatory impact analysis (RIA) with cost‑benefit estimates, a summary of stakeholder comments categorised by theme (industry, consumer groups, academia), the regulator’s response to each theme, and a proposed implementation timeline (e.g., “effective 1 January 2026, with a 12‑month transitional period for small firms”). A précis must preserve the quantitative RIA outputs – “estimated compliance cost 0.15 % of assets, net benefit US$2.3 bn over 10 years” – and the key assumptions (discount rate, horizon).

Stakeholder feedback should be condensed by theme, not by individual respondent. For each theme, note the regulator’s stance (accepted, partially accepted, rejected) and any resulting rule amendment (e.g., “capital surcharge reduced from 2.5 % to 2.0 % for regional banks”). The implementation timeline must include the effective date, any phased‑in provisions, and the review clause (e.g., “post‑implementation review after 3 years”). Omitting the transitional period or the review trigger is a common trap.

  • Collapsing distinct maritime zones into a generic “coastal waters” description.
  • Omitting the specific MARPOL annex that governs a pollutant type.
  • Failing to quote the exact willingness‑to‑pay threshold in an HTA précis.
  • Merging separate policy levers (carbon price, subsidy, regulation) into a single “policy support” phrase in an energy roadmap.
  • Dropping the design effect or weighting method in a survey methodology précis.
  • Summarising stakeholder feedback by respondent rather than by thematic category in a consultation paper.
  • Neglecting the transitional period or post‑implementation review date in a financial regulation précis.

These advanced précis types demand precise retention of quantitative thresholds, institutional labels, and structural hierarchies to meet examiner expectations.

Précis of International Human Rights Treaty Body Concluding Observations

Concluding observations issued by United Nations treaty bodies — such as the Human Rights Committee (CCPR), the Committee on Economic, Social and Cultural Rights (CESCR), and the Committee on the Elimination of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW) — follow a predictable structure: a brief introduction citing the reporting state and the session, a list of positive aspects, principal areas of concern, and concrete recommendations. A précis must preserve the hierarchy of concerns: the treaty body first flags violations of non‑derogable rights (e.g., torture under Article 7 ICCPR), then systemic issues (e.g., lack of independent judiciary), and finally thematic gaps (e.g., inadequate data on gender‑based violence). Each recommendation is usually numbered; the précis should retain the numbering and the exact wording of the most operative recommendations (e.g., “The State party should amend the Penal Code to criminalise marital rape within twelve months”). Quantitative benchmarks — such as “reduce maternal mortality ratio to below 70 per 100 000 live births by 2030” — must be reproduced verbatim because examiners test the ability to capture measurable targets.

The language of concluding observations is deliberately diplomatic; phrases like “the Committee is concerned” or “the Committee urges” signal different levels of obligation. A précis must differentiate between “concern” (a factual observation) and “urge” (a strong recommendation implying immediate action). When the treaty body references its own General Comments or previous concluding observations, the précis should note the cross‑reference (e.g., “see General Comment No. 31 on the nature of the general legal obligation”). If the state’s reply includes a follow‑up report, the précis should indicate whether the state accepted, partially accepted, or rejected each recommendation, because the follow‑up procedure (the “follow‑up mechanism” under the Optional Protocol to the ICCPR) is a distinct procedural layer that examiners may ask to summarise.

Footnotes in concluding observations often cite specific legislative acts, court judgments, or statistical sources. The précis must retain the citation of the primary source (e.g., “Constitutional Court Decision No. 12/2018”) but may omit the full footnote text unless the question explicitly asks for evidentiary detail. When multiple treaty bodies review the same state in the same cycle, a comparative précis should align concerns thematically (e.g., both CCPR and CESCR flagging inadequate housing) while preserving each body’s distinct legal framework. This alignment demonstrates synthesis skill without collapsing the separate treaty obligations.

Time‑management tip: allocate the first reading to map the document’s four sections (introduction, positives, concerns, recommendations). On the second pass, extract the numbered recommendations and any quantitative targets. Draft the précis in the order of the original document, using the treaty body’s own headings as signposts. This method preserves logical flow and ensures that no recommendation tier is omitted.

Examiner expectation: a high‑scoring précis will reproduce the exact recommendation numbers, retain any deadline language (“within six months”), and distinguish between “concern” and “urge” without paraphrasing them into a generic “recommendation”. Failure to keep the numbering or to flatten the hierarchy into a single list of “issues” is a common cause of mark loss.

Summarizing Climate Finance Mechanisms under the UNFCCC

The United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC) establishes a layered finance architecture: the Financial Mechanism (operated by the Global Environment Facility and the Green Climate Fund), the Standing Committee on Finance, and the biennial assessment and overview of climate finance flows. A précis of a UNFCCC finance decision (e.g., Decision 1/CP.21 on the Green Climate Fund replenishment) must capture the quantitative pledge (e.g., “USD 10 billion for the 2020‑2023 replenishment”), the allocation windows (mitigation, adaptation, cross‑cutting), and the governance rules (board composition, decision‑making by consensus). The précis should also note the “new and additional” language that distinguishes climate finance from Official Development Assistance (ODA), because examiners test whether the candidate can spot the legal qualifier.

When the source text references the “$100 billion goal” (Decision 1/CP.16), the précis must state the goal, the deadline (2020, extended to 2025), and the reporting modalities (Biennial Reports, Biennial Update Reports). If the document includes a table of pledged versus delivered amounts by contributor, the précis should summarise the aggregate gap (e.g., “pledged USD 78.9 billion, delivered USD 62.3 billion, shortfall USD 16.6 billion”) rather than reproducing the full table. The distinction between “public” and “private” finance, and the role of “leveraged” private co‑financing, must be retained because the UNFCCC’s reporting guidelines require separate disclosure.

The Standing Committee on Finance’s “technical paper” on the definition of climate finance often lists criteria: (i) climate‑specific objective, (ii) additionality, (iii) predictability, (iv) transparency. A précis should enumerate these criteria and note any contested interpretations (e.g., whether “additionality” applies to private flows). When the source cites the “Adaptation Fund” and its “direct access” modality, the précis must preserve the term “direct access” and the eligibility criteria (national implementing entities accredited by the Fund). Omitting the accreditation step collapses a key procedural safeguard.

Cross‑referencing the Paris Agreement’s Article 9 (finance) with the UNFCCC’s finance decisions is a frequent examiner twist. The précis should show the linkage: Article 9 obliges developed countries to provide financial resources, while the COP decisions operationalise the pledge through the Green Climate Fund and the Adaptation Fund. If the source includes a “decision on the modalities for the global stocktake” that references finance, the précis must capture the stocktake’s finance‑related indicators (e.g., “total climate finance mobilised, proportion of grants versus loans”).

Practical drafting: first isolate all numeric commitments, then map each to its governing instrument (COP decision, Paris Agreement, Fund policy). Use the instrument’s own headings (e.g., “Replenishment”, “Allocation”, “Governance”) as the précis structure. Preserve the legal qualifiers (“new and additional”, “predictable”, “transparent”) because they are the analytical hooks examiners look for.

Worked Example: Précis of a UNFCCC Finance Decision
Source excerpt (120 words): “The Conference of the Parties, recalling Decision 1/CP.16, decides that the Green Climate Fund shall be replenished with USD 10 billion for the period 2020‑2023. The Fund’s Board shall allocate resources across three windows: mitigation (40 %), adaptation (40 %), and cross‑cutting (20 %). All contributions shall be new and additional to ODA. The Board shall operate by consensus; developing country representatives shall hold at least 50 % of Board seats. The Standing Committee on Finance shall report on progress at COP27.”
Précis (45 words): “COP decides Green Climate Fund replenishment of USD 10 billion (2020‑2023). Allocation: mitigation 40 %, adaptation 40 %, cross‑cutting 20 %. Contributions must be new and additional to ODA. Board decides by consensus with ≥50 % developing‑country seats. Standing Committee on Finance to report progress at COP27.”
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a finance decision appears, underline every figure, percentage, and legal qualifier (“new and additional”, “consensus”, “direct access”). Write the précis in the order of the decision’s operative paragraphs; this mirrors the logical flow examiners expect and prevents omission of governance details such as Board composition.

Précis of Digital Identity Frameworks and Interoperability Standards

Digital identity frameworks — such as the EU’s eIDAS Regulation (Regulation (EU) No 910/2014), India’s Aadhaar Act 2016, and the World Bank’s ID4D Principles — share a common architecture: a trusted identity provider, an authentication protocol, and an interoperability layer. A précis of a policy document on digital identity must retain the legal basis (e.g., “Article 6 eIDAS – qualified electronic signatures”), the assurance levels (low, substantial, high) defined by the framework, and the cross‑border recognition mechanisms (e.g., “mutual recognition of notified eID schemes under eIDAS Article 9”). Quantitative thresholds — such as “minimum 128‑bit encryption for high assurance” or “biometric false‑match rate ≤ 0.001 %” — are examinable details and must appear verbatim.

Interoperability standards (ISO/IEC 24760‑1, OpenID Connect, SAML 2.0) are often referenced in the source text. The précis should list the standards by name and version, and indicate whether the framework mandates or merely recommends each standard. When the source discusses “federated identity” versus “centralised identity”, the précis must preserve the distinction because the governance implications (data sovereignty, single point of failure) differ. If the document cites a pilot project (e.g., “the Nordic eID pilot covering 5 million users across three member states”), the précis should retain the scale and the participating jurisdictions.

Privacy safeguards are a recurring theme: data minimisation, purpose limitation, and the right to be forgotten (GDPR Article 17). A précis must note which safeguards are legally binding (regulation) and which are policy aspirations (guidelines). The presence of a “data protection impact assessment” (DPIA) requirement should be captured with its trigger threshold (e.g., “DPIA mandatory for high‑assurance schemes processing biometric data”). Omitting the DPIA trigger collapses a key compliance checkpoint.

Governance bodies — such as the eIDAS Cooperation Group, the Aadhaar Data Security Council, or the ID4D Multi‑Stakeholder Advisory Group — are often described with their composition and decision‑making rules. The précis should record the composition (e.g., “representatives from each member state’s supervisory authority”) and the voting rule (consensus, qualified majority). When the source mentions a “review clause” (e.g., “the Regulation shall be reviewed by 2027”), the précis must keep the review date and the scope of the review (technical standards, assurance levels, cross‑border recognition).

Drafting approach: segment the source into (1) legal basis, (2) assurance architecture, (3) interoperability standards, (4) privacy safeguards, (5) governance and review. Write the précis in that order, preserving the framework’s own terminology (“qualified electronic signature”, “substantial assurance”, “federated identity”). This structure mirrors the logical hierarchy examiners use to award marks.

Condensing Space Law Treaties: The Outer Space Treaty and Subsequent Agreements

The 1967 Treaty on Principles Governing the Activities of States in the Exploration and Use of Outer Space, including the Moon and Other Celestial Bodies (Outer Space Treaty, OST) establishes the foundational regime: outer space is the province of all mankind (Article I), not subject to national appropriation (Article II), and states bear international responsibility for national activities (Article VI). A précis of the OST must retain the four core principles — freedom of exploration, non‑appropriation, state responsibility, and peaceful purposes — and the exact article numbers because examiners test article‑level recall.

The 1979 Agreement Governing the Activities of States on the Moon and Other Celestial Bodies (Moon Agreement) adds the “common heritage of mankind” principle (Article 11) and establishes an international regime for resource exploitation. Although only 18 states are parties, the précis should note the limited ratification status and the consequent legal uncertainty for commercial mining. The 1975 Convention on Registration of Objects Launched into Outer Space (Registration Convention) requires states to furnish orbital parameters and general function of space objects to the UN Secretary‑General; the précis must capture the registration obligation and the distinction between “launching state” and “state of registry”.

The 1972 Convention on International Liability for Damage Caused by Space Objects (Liability Convention) imposes absolute liability for damage on Earth (Article II) and fault‑based liability for damage in space (Article III). A précis should preserve the liability thresholds (no financial cap) and the claims procedure (diplomatic channel, then Claims Commission). The 1968 Agreement on the Rescue of Astronauts, the Return of Astronauts and the Return of Objects Launched into Outer Space (Rescue Agreement) obliges states to assist astronauts in distress; the précis must note the humanitarian nature and the obligation to return space objects.

When the source text discusses contemporary issues — such as the 2020 Artemis Accords (non‑binding political commitments) or the 2021 UN COPUOS “Space2030” agenda — the précis should differentiate binding treaty obligations from voluntary guidelines. The Artemis Accords’ “deconfliction” and “heritage protection” provisions are not treaty law; the précis must label them as “political commitments” to avoid conflating soft law with hard law.

Effective condensation: create a table in the mind mapping each treaty to its core obligation, liability regime, and ratification count. Then write the précis in chronological order of adoption, using the treaty’s short title and article references. Preserve the legal qualifiers (“absolute liability”, “fault‑based”, “common heritage”) because they are the analytical anchors examiners reward.

Précis of Global Health Emergency Declarations under the International Health Regulations

The International Health Regulations (IHR) 2005, adopted by the World Health Assembly, provide the legal framework for declaring a Public Health Emergency of International Concern (PHEIC). A précis of a WHO Director‑General’s statement declaring a PHEIC must capture the statutory basis (Article 12 IHR), the criteria (serious, sudden, unusual, or unexpected event with international spread risk), and the specific disease or event (e.g., “COVID‑19”, “Ebola virus disease in the Democratic Republic of the Congo”). The précis should retain the Director‑General’s determination language (“I determine that the event constitutes a PHEIC”) and the temporary recommendations issued under Article 15 (e.g., “no travel restrictions, enhanced surveillance at points of entry”).

The IHR Emergency Committee’s composition (geographic balance, expertise) and its advisory role are often described in the source. The précis must note that the Committee’s advice is non‑binding but the Director‑General’s decision is binding on States Parties. When the source lists the temporary recommendations, the précis should enumerate them verbatim, preserving any time‑limited qualifiers (“valid for three months, subject to review”). The distinction between “temporary recommendations” (Article 15) and “standing recommendations” (Article 16) is a frequent examiner test point.

Reporting obligations under Article 6 (notification) and Article 7 (information sharing) require States Parties to provide case counts, laboratory confirmation, and public health measures within 24 hours of assessment. A précis of a country’s IHR core capacity assessment should retain the quantitative benchmarks (e.g., “surveillance capacity score 3/5”, “laboratory capacity score 4/5”) and the identified gaps. If the source cites the “Joint External Evaluation” (JEE) tool, the précis must mention the JEE by name and the relevant technical areas (e.g., “risk communication”, “points of entry”).

The IHR’s dispute settlement mechanism (Article 56) — negotiation, then arbitration, then referral to the International Court of Justice — is rarely invoked but appears in academic commentaries. A précis of a scholarly article on IHR enforcement should preserve the procedural steps and note the absence of any ICJ case to date. When the source discusses the 2022 amendment proposals (e.g., “strengthening compliance through a compliance committee”), the précis must label them as “proposed amendments” and not as current law.

Drafting tip: first isolate the legal trigger (Article 12), then the Director‑General’s determination, then the temporary recommendations, then the State reporting obligations. Keep the WHO’s own headings (“Determination”, “Temporary Recommendations”, “Advice to States Parties”) as the précis structure. This mirrors the regulatory logic and ensures no procedural layer is omitted.

  • Flattening the hierarchy of treaty‑body concerns into a single undifferentiated list of “issues”.
  • Omitting the “new and additional” qualifier when summarising climate finance pledges.
  • Collapsing distinct assurance levels (low, substantial, high) into a generic “security level” in digital identity précis.
  • Confusing the Moon Agreement’s “common heritage” principle with the Outer Space Treaty’s “province of all mankind”.
  • Failing to distinguish temporary recommendations (Article 15 IHR) from standing recommendations (Article 16 IHR).
  • Dropping the quantitative thresholds (e.g., biometric false‑match rate, encryption bit‑length) that the source text specifies.
  • Treating soft‑law instruments such as the Artemis Accords as binding treaty obligations in a space‑law précis.

These advanced précis types demand precise retention of legal qualifiers, quantitative benchmarks, and procedural hierarchies to meet examiner expectations.

Précis of Intelligence Assessments and Threat Analyses: Preserving Classification Levels, Confidence Assessments, and Alternative Hypotheses

Intelligence assessments from national agencies, multinational bodies, or open-source units follow a structured analytic framework a précis must reproduce faithfully. The source typically opens with a key judgment or bottom line up front (BLUF), followed by the evidence base, confidence level (high, moderate, low), alternative hypotheses considered and rejected, and collection gaps. Collapsing these layers into a single narrative loses the analytic rigour examiners test. The confidence assessment must be retained verbatim: "high confidence" indicates consistent, corroborated evidence; "moderate confidence" signals plausible but not fully corroborated; "low confidence" denotes fragmented or questionable sourcing. The précis must also preserve classification markings (TOP SECRET, SECRET, CONFIDENTIAL, UNCLASSIFIED) and control markings (NOFORN, REL TO, ORCON) defining dissemination limits. When the source employs structured analytic techniques — Analysis of Competing Hypotheses (ACH), Devil's Advocacy, Red Teaming, or Key Assumptions Check — the précis should name the technique and summarise its output (e.g., "ACH matrix ranked Hypothesis 3 as most consistent with observed indicators"). Indicators and warnings lists must be condensed into a table-like mental map: indicator, observed status (present/absent/ambiguous), and diagnostic value. Collection requirements (RFIs, PIRs) and intelligence gaps are actionable outputs; omitting them renders the précis operationally incomplete. The précis must distinguish assessments (judgments with confidence) from estimates (projections with probability ranges) and facts (verified observations). When the source cites sourcing rationale (e.g., "based on SIGINT intercepts dated 12–14 March, corroborated by HUMINT source A-7"), the précis retains source type and date range but not the specific identifier unless the prompt demands source protection analysis. Temporal qualifiers — "as of 0600Z 15 March", "valid through 30 April" — are binding; dropping them misrepresents the assessment's currency. Finally, the dissent channel or minority view (if formally recorded) must be preserved with its own confidence label, as intelligence products often carry institutional dissent policymakers weigh.

Précis of Central Bank Digital Currency Design Papers: Preserving Technical Architecture, Privacy Models, and Monetary Policy Implications

CBDC design papers from the BIS, IMF, ECB, Federal Reserve, or national central banks combine technical architecture specifications, privacy and data governance models, monetary policy transmission analysis, financial stability safeguards, and legal framework proposals. A précis must map each domain without conflation. The architecture choice — direct (central bank issues to public), indirect (intermediated via commercial banks), or hybrid (central bank claims with intermediated distribution) — determines balance sheet impact and operational risk; the précis retains the chosen model and rationale (e.g., "hybrid model selected to preserve two-tier banking while ensuring central bank liability"). The ledger technology — centralised database, permissioned DLT, or hybrid — and its consensus mechanism (if DLT) are technical anchors; the précis notes throughput targets (TPS), finality latency, and resilience standards (e.g., "byzantine fault tolerance with 3f+1 validators"). Privacy models follow a spectrum: full anonymity (cash-like), pseudonymity with audit trail (AML/CFT compliant), tiered anonymity (small-value anonymous, large-value identified), or zero anonymity (fully traceable). The précis must preserve chosen tier thresholds (e.g., "anonymity up to €3,000 per transaction, €10,000 monthly") and data access governance (who holds encryption keys, judicial warrant requirements). Monetary policy implications include: interest-bearing vs non-interest-bearing CBDC, remuneration rate linkage (policy rate, deposit facility rate, or separate administered rate), disintermediation risk mitigants (holding limits, tiered remuneration, penalty rates), and transmission channel effects on bank funding and credit supply. The précis retains quantitative safeguards (e.g., "individual holding cap €3,000; corporate cap €50,000; tiered remuneration: 0% up to cap, −0.5% above cap"). Financial stability analysis covers run risk (flight to CBDC in stress), cyber resilience (quantum-resistant cryptography, offline capability), and operational continuity (24/7/365 settlement). Legal framework sections address legal tender status, central bank mandate amendment, data protection law alignment (GDPR, local equivalents), and cross-border interoperability (BIS mBridge, Project Dunbar). The précis preserves the phase-gate timeline (research, proof-of-concept, pilot, launch) and go/no-go criteria at each gate. When the paper models scenario analyses (baseline, stress, adoption curves), the précis cites key parameters (adoption rate, substitution elasticity, interest rate pass-through) and headline results (e.g., "under 30% adoption, bank deposit outflow < 5%; under 60% adoption, outflow 12–15%").

Worked Example: CBDC Design Paper Précis
A central bank publishes a 45-page CBDC design paper proposing a hybrid, intermediated, interest-bearing retail CBDC with tiered anonymity (anonymous up to €3,000/transaction, €10,000/month; identified above), holding caps (€3,000 individual, €50,000 corporate), and tiered remuneration (0% up to cap, −0.75% above cap). The paper uses a permissioned DLT with 2-second finality, targets 10,000 TPS, and includes offline capability via secure element. It models three adoption scenarios (10%, 30%, 50%) and finds bank deposit outflows of 2%, 8%, and 18% respectively. The legal section recommends amending the central bank act to clarify CBDC as legal tender and mandates GDPR-compliant data governance with judicial warrant for identity disclosure. The timeline: pilot H1 2025, go/no-go Q3 2025, launch H1 2026. Write a 120-word précis.
The central bank proposes a hybrid, intermediated retail CBDC on permissioned DLT (2-second finality, 10,000 TPS, offline-capable). Key design: tiered anonymity (anonymous ≤ €3,000/transaction, €10,000/month); holding caps (€3,000 individual, €50,000 corporate); interest-bearing with tiered remuneration (0% up to cap, −0.75% above). Monetary policy transmission preserved via adjustable remuneration linked to deposit facility rate. Financial stability safeguards: caps and negative tiered rate limit disintermediation; scenario modelling shows deposit outflows of 2% (10% adoption), 8% (30%), 18% (50%). Legal framework requires central bank act amendment for legal tender status; GDPR-compliant governance with judicial warrant for de-anonymisation. Timeline: pilot H1 2025, go/no-go Q3 2025, launch H1 2026. (118 words)
Exam-Day Strategy
For CBDC and similar technical policy papers, build a mental checklist: (1) Architecture model (direct/indirect/hybrid), (2) Ledger type and performance specs, (3) Privacy tier thresholds, (4) Holding caps and remuneration tiers, (5) Monetary policy linkage, (6) Financial stability quantifiers, (7) Legal amendments required, (8) Phase-gate timeline. Tick each as you read; the précis writes itself from the checked list.

Précis of International Tax Treaty Negotiations: Preserving BEPS Pillars, Dispute Resolution, and Anti-Abuse Provisions

International tax treaty negotiations, particularly under the OECD/G20 Base Erosion and Profit Shifting (BEPS) framework, layer Pillar One (reallocation of taxing rights to market jurisdictions), Pillar Two (global minimum tax), Multilateral Instrument (MLI) modifications, dispute resolution mechanisms (MAP, arbitration), and anti-abuse rules (LOB, PPT, CFC, hybrid mismatch). A précis must keep these pillars distinct. Pillar One Amount A allocates a share of residual profit (typically 25% of profit above 10% margin) of MNEs with global revenue > €20bn and profitability > 10% to market jurisdictions via a revenue-based allocation key; the précis retains thresholds, profit split percentage, allocation formula, and the marketing and distribution profits safe harbour. Amount B simplifies arm's-length remuneration for baseline marketing/distribution activities; the précis notes the fixed return or simplified approach adopted. Pillar Two introduces the Global Anti-Base Erosion (GloBE) rules: Income Inclusion Rule (IIR), Undertaxed Profits Rule (UTPR), Subject to Tax Rule (STTR), and Qualified Domestic Minimum Top-Up Tax (QDMTT). The précis preserves the 15% minimum effective tax rate, the substance-based income exclusion (5% payroll + tangible assets, phasing down), the GloBE income/loss computation (financial accounting income with adjustments), and the top-up tax calculation mechanics. MLI modifications cover treaty abuse (Principal Purpose Test — PPT, Limitation on Benefits — LOB), hybrid mismatch neutralization, permanent establishment threshold updates (dependent agent, commissionaire), dispute resolution (mandatory binding arbitration under Part VI), and entry-into-force ratchet clauses. The précis must distinguish PPT (general anti-abuse) from LOB (objective criteria) and note which option the treaty adopts. Dispute resolution provisions require preserving the MAP timeline (24 months), the arbitration trigger (if MAP fails), the arbitration panel composition (independent chair, two party-appointed), and the binding nature of the award. Anti-abuse specifics — CFC rules (income threshold, tax rate test), interest limitation (EBITDA-based 30% cap), hybrid mismatch (deduction/non-inclusion, double deduction) — are quantitative; the précis retains the rates and thresholds. When the source covers developing country concerns (e.g., UN Model vs OECD Model, Article 12B technical services withholding, source-based taxation of the digitalised economy), the précis maps the policy divergence without taking sides.

Précis of Nuclear Safeguards Agreements and Verification Reports: Preserving Material Balance Areas, Containment/Surveillance, and Inspection Rights

Nuclear safeguards documents — Comprehensive Safeguards Agreements (CSA) under INFCIRC/153, Additional Protocols (AP) under INFCIRC/540, Small Quantities Protocols (SQP), and IAEA verification reports — operate on a material balance area (MBA) framework a précis must replicate. The State System of Accounting for and Control of Nuclear Material (SSAC) establishes MBAs (geographically defined areas tracking nuclear material quantities), key measurement points (KMPs) (where measurements occur), and strategic points (SPs) (where containment/surveillance is applied). The précis retains the MBA structure (number, type: reactor, enrichment, reprocessing, storage, fuel fabrication) and nuclear material categories (direct-use: plutonium, HEU, U-233; indirect-use: LEU, natural U, depleted U, thorium). Verification activities comprise routine inspections (interim, inventory verification, design information verification), complementary access (under AP, managed access provisions), and special inspections. The précis preserves inspection frequency (e.g., "quarterly interim inspections at MBA-3"), verification methods (NDA, DA, C/S), and C/S systems (seals: metal cup, fiber optic, electronic; surveillance: optical, radiation, environmental sensors). Material balance evaluation uses Shipper-Receiver Difference (SRD), Material Unaccounted For (MUF), and Limit of Error of MUF (LEMUF); the précis cites the MUF/LEMUF ratio and whether it falls within detection probability targets (e.g., "MUF 0.8 kg U, LEMUF 1.2 kg U, ratio 0.67 < 1.0 — no indication of diversion"). Additional Protocol provisions expand declaration scope (all nuclear fuel cycle R&D, manufacturing, location data), grant complementary access (managed access with 24-hour notice, environmental sampling), and enable the state-level concept (acquisition path analysis, information-driven verification). The précis notes the AP status (in force, signed only, not signed) and any subsidiary arrangements (facility attachments) detailing design information and verification procedures. Safeguards conclusions — broader conclusion (all nuclear material in peaceful activities), narrower conclusion (declared material not diverted), or no conclusion — are the ultimate output; the précis must reproduce the exact conclusion language and basis (e.g., "broader conclusion drawn based on CSA + AP implementation, no anomalies detected"). Verification costs (inspection days, PVER days, complementary access days) and resource allocation (inspector-days per facility type) appear in annual implementation reports; the précis retains aggregates.

Précis of Maritime Boundary Delimitation Judgments: Preserving Equidistance/Relevant Circumstances, Special Circumstances, and Proportionality Tests

Maritime boundary delimitation judgments from the ICJ, ITLOS, PCA annex VII tribunals, and annex VIII special chambers apply the three-stage approach crystallised in Black Sea (Romania v. Ukraine, 2009), Nicaragua v. Colombia (2012), and Ghana v. Côte d'Ivoire (2017). A précis must track each stage without collapsing them. Stage 1: provisional equidistance line (median line for opposite coasts, equidistance for adjacent coasts) constructed from base points on the baseline (normal baseline per Article 5 LOSC, or straight baselines per Article 7). The précis records base point selection disputes (e.g., "Tribunal rejected Party A's proposed base point at Cape X as not meeting Article 7(3) 'fringe of islands' criterion"). Stage 2: assessment of relevant circumstances requiring adjustment — geography (coastal length, concavity/convexity, islands, islets, rocks under Article 121(3)), geology/geomorphology (continental shelf natural prolongation — largely superseded by distance criterion post-Libya/Malta), conduct of parties (acquiescence, recognition), security interests (rarely decisive), economic factors (generally excluded per North Sea Continental Shelf). The précis preserves the specific adjustment (e.g., "line shifted 12 nm northward to give half-effect to Island Y, reducing its weight from full to 50% per Jan Mayen precedent"). Stage 3: proportionality test — comparing the ratio of maritime areas allocated to the ratio of relevant coastal lengths. The précis cites coastal length measurements (method: general direction of coast, not fractal), area ratios (e.g., "1.6:1 area ratio vs 1.2:1 coastal length ratio — within acceptable range"), and the conclusion (no disproportionate result, or adjustment required). Delimitation of multiple zones — territorial sea (Article 15 LOSC), EEZ (Article 74), continental shelf (Article 83) — may use a single maritime boundary or separate boundaries; the précis notes the tribunal's choice and rationale. Special circumstances — enclaving, cut-off, encroachment — are assessed at Stage 2; the précis retains the tribunal's finding (e.g., "full effect to Island Z would enclave Party B's EEZ — half-effect applied"). Provisional measures (Article 290 LOSC) and procedural history (jurisdiction phase, merits phase) are summarised only if the prompt asks for procedural posture. Dissenting/separate opinions often contest the relevant circumstances weighting or proportionality methodology; the précis maps the doctrinal split (e.g., "Judge X dissented: argued coastal length ratio should use water-area-weighted method, yielding 1.4:1, making 1.6:1 area ratio disproportionate").

  • Collapsing the three-stage approach into a single "equidistance adjusted for fairness" sentence loses marks for missing the structured methodology.
  • Omitting base point selection rationale in Stage 1 — the choice of baseline and base points is often the decisive factual dispute.
  • Treating "relevant circumstances" as a generic list without linking each to the specific adjustment triggered — each must map to a line shift.
  • Confusing the proportionality test (area-to-coast ratio) with the equidistance line — they are distinct analytical stages.
  • Dropping the Article 121(3) rock/island classification when it drives a half-effect or no-effect decision — the classification is a legal determinant, not a geographical description.
  • Failing to distinguish territorial sea delimitation (Article 15, historic title/equidistance) from EEZ/continental shelf delimitation (Articles 74/83, equitable solution) — the legal standards differ.
  • Summarising dissenting opinions as "Judge X disagreed" without stating the precise doctrinal alternative (e.g., different coastal length measurement method, different island weight) — the dissent's analytical content is what examiners reward.

Précis of Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change Assessment Reports: Preserving Scenario Pathways, Confidence Levels, and Policy‑Relevant Findings

IPCC reports follow a fixed structure: Summary for Policymakers (SPM), Technical Summary, and chapters organized around Representative Concentration Pathways (RCPs) or Shared Socio‑Economic Pathways (SSPs). A précis must retain the scenario label (e.g., SSP1‑2.6, SSP5‑8.5), its radiative forcing trajectory, and the IPCC’s calibrated probability language (virtually certain, very likely, likely, about as likely as not, unlikely, very unlikely, exceptionally unlikely). Confidence qualifiers — high, medium, low — attached to each finding must be reproduced verbatim to preserve epistemic weight. Distinguish observed changes (e.g., global mean surface temperature rise of ~1.1 °C since 1850‑1900) from pathway‑specific projections across time horizons (near‑term 2021‑2040, mid‑term 2041‑2060, long‑term 2081‑2100). Include policy‑relevant metrics: carbon budgets for 1.5 °C and 2 °C limits in GtCO₂, and implied annual mitigation rates. Dropping confidence qualifiers or merging pathways into a single “high‑emission” label loses nuance examiners expect.

Structural Mapping for IPCC Précis

Map each SPM paragraph to its chapter source, then group findings by theme — temperature, sea‑level, extremes, carbon cycle, mitigation. List pathways in ascending forcing order with confidence levels. Use a mental table: columns for pathway, temperature anomaly, sea‑level rise, confidence, policy implication. This mirrors the report’s logical architecture, not a linear summary.

Summarizing Artificial Intelligence Ethics Guidelines: Retaining Principles, Risk Taxonomies, and Governance Recommendations

Major AI ethics frameworks — OECD AI Principles, EU Ethics Guidelines for Trustworthy AI, IEEE Ethically Aligned Design, UNESCO Recommendation on AI Ethics — share a core taxonomy: respect for human autonomy, prevention of harm, fairness, explicability, accountability. A précis must quote principles verbatim; paraphrasing can alter normative force (e.g., “human‑centred” vs. “human‑rights‑based”). Preserve the risk taxonomy — unacceptable, high, limited, minimal — with classification criteria (e.g., biometric identification in public spaces = unacceptable risk under the EU AI Act). Link governance recommendations — conformity assessment, post‑market monitoring, human‑in‑the‑loop, redress — to their risk tier. When synthesizing, map principles to sources, note convergences (fairness in all four) and divergences (UNESCO adds “environmental sustainability”). Capture implementation timelines (e.g., EU AI Act’s 24‑month transition).

Comparative Matrix for Multi‑Guideline Précis

Build a matrix: guidelines as rows, principles as columns. Fill cells with exact phrasing or concise descriptors. This prevents merging distinct concepts and makes comparisons explicit.

Technique: Comparative Matrix Construction
You are given excerpts from the OECD AI Principles and the EU Ethics Guidelines for Trustworthy AI. Write a précis of no more than 120 words that captures the shared principles, the distinctive principle in each document, and the risk‑tier governance link in the EU text.

Both OECD and EU frameworks endorse human‑centred values, transparency, robustness, and accountability. OECD uniquely adds “inclusive growth”; EU uniquely adds “environmental sustainability”. The EU classifies AI systems into four risk tiers — unacceptable, high, limited, minimal — linking conformity assessment, post‑market monitoring, and human‑in‑the‑loop to the high‑risk tier. OECD lacks a tiered taxonomy but recommends risk‑based governance. (115 words)

Exam‑Day Strategy
When a prompt asks for a comparative précis of two or more policy documents, spend the first two minutes building a quick matrix on scratch paper. Align principles side‑by‑side; this prevents omission of unique items and ensures the word‑count budget is allocated to distinctive content rather than repeated commonalities.

Précis of International Migration Law Instruments: Preserving Protection Standards, Non‑Refoulement, and State Obligations

Core instruments: 1951 Refugee Convention and 1967 Protocol, 1990 ICRMW, 2018 Global Compact for Migration (GCM). A précis must retain the refugee definition (well‑founded fear of persecution on five grounds), non‑refoulement (Article 33, Refugee Convention; Article 3, CAT), and the refugee–migrant worker distinction (migrant workers have labor rights, not full refugee protection). ICRMW lists rights — freedom of movement, equality of treatment, social security — and obligations for origin, transit, destination states. The non‑binding GCM sets 23 measurable objectives (e.g., Objective 5: enhance regular migration pathways). Preserve each instrument’s legal character (binding treaty vs. political declaration) and monitoring bodies (CMW, UNHCR, UN Network on Migration). Dropping the non‑refoulement phrase “in any manner whatsoever” or reducing GCM objectives to generic “cooperation” loses legal precision.

Obligation Mapping for Migration Précis

Create a three‑column table: instrument, right/obligation, enforcement mechanism. This clarifies justiciable provisions vs. those relying on political peer review — a frequent exam focus.

Condensing Space Law Treaties: Retaining Liability Regimes, Registration Requirements, and Resource Utilization Provisions

Foundational treaties: 1967 Outer Space Treaty (OST), 1972 Liability Convention, 1975 Registration Convention, 1979 Moon Agreement, 2020 Artemis Accords (political commitment). A précis must preserve OST core principles: freedom of exploration, non‑appropriation (Art. II), state responsibility for national activities (Art. VI), prohibition of WMD in orbit (Art. IV). The Liability Convention sets a two‑tier regime: absolute liability for damage on Earth/aircraft (Art. II), fault‑based for damage in space (Art. III). Registration Convention requires states to submit orbital parameters and object function to the UN Register. The Moon Agreement declares the Moon “common heritage of mankind” and calls for an international exploitation regime (Art. 11). Artemis Accords reaffirm OST, add deconfliction, transparent data sharing, and safety zones. Distinguish binding treaties (OST, Liability, Registration) from non‑binding commitments (Artemis Accords; Moon Agreement — only 18 parties). On resource use: OST’s non‑appropriation does not explicitly ban extraction; Moon Agreement’s common‑heritage implies equitable sharing, not yet negotiated.

Liability Tier Preservation

Separate absolute liability (Earth damage) from fault‑based liability (space damage) and cite article numbers; examiners reward this structural fidelity.

Précis of Global Health Security Frameworks: Preserving Surveillance Obligations, Response Coordination, and Financing Mechanisms

Principal architecture: IHR (2005), GHSA (2014), PIP Framework (2011), WHO One Health Joint Plan (2021). A précis must retain IHR core capacities — detection, assessment, reporting, response (Annex 1) — the 24‑hour PHEIC notification duty, and the Annex 2 decision instrument guiding the DG’s determination. GHSA organizes “Prevent, Detect, Respond” packages with measurable targets (e.g., 80 % of countries achieving real‑time biosurveillance by 2024). PIP Framework creates virus‑sharing/benefit‑sharing: GISRS labs share viruses with WHO centres; manufacturers commit to supply vaccines, antivirals, diagnostics to developing countries via SMTA. One Health integrates human, animal, environmental sectors with joint risk assessment and World Bank Pandemic Fund financing. Preserve legal status (IHR binding, GHSA voluntary, PIP contractual, One Health collaborative) and financing instruments (assessed contributions, voluntary donations, Pandemic Fund grants). Merging frameworks into one “global health security” paragraph erases distinct obligation types examiners test.

Obligation‑Financing Matrix

Build a matrix linking each framework to its binding nature, core capacity, and financing source. This captures the layered governance structure, not a monolithic description.

  • Treating IPCC confidence language as optional, not mandatory.
  • Merging AI risk tiers into a single “high‑risk” category, losing governance links.
  • Omitting the non‑refoulement qualifier “in any manner whatsoever”.
  • Conflating the Moon Agreement’s common‑heritage regime with Artemis Accords’ safety zones.
  • Collapsing IHR’s binding notification duty with GHSA’s voluntary targets into generic “reporting”.
  • Failing to distinguish absolute from fault‑based liability in space law.
  • Neglecting the legal character (binding vs. non‑binding) of each global health instrument.

These traps highlight the precision required when condensing highly structured, multi‑instrument texts for exams.

Worked Passages & Practice

A précis is graded on three things: fidelity to the argument, compression to roughly one-third, and your own words. The passages below show the full method end to end.

Passage 1 (95 words) — model précis
Source: "Democracy is not merely a method of choosing governments; it is a way of living together. It assumes that disagreement is legitimate and that the loser of an election remains a citizen with rights, not an enemy to be excluded. When a society begins to treat its opponents as traitors, it has kept the machinery of elections while losing the substance of democracy. The health of a democracy is therefore measured less by the frequency of elections than by the safety of dissent."
Précis (~32 words): Democracy is a way of living with legitimate disagreement, not just a method of election. Its health is measured by the safety of dissent, not the frequency of elections.
Why it works: it keeps the two claims (democracy = co-existence; dissent = measure of health), drops the example of opponents-as-traitors, and uses original phrasing.
Passage 2 (88 words) — model précis
Source: "Technology has changed the classroom without changing the lesson. Students now carry devices that can reach every library on earth, yet many still memorise and reproduce. The tool has become more powerful while the pedagogy has stayed passive. Real reform is not the distribution of hardware but the redesign of what students are asked to do with it: to question, to build, and to defend an argument."
Précis (~30 words): Technology has modernised classroom tools but not teaching methods. Genuine reform lies not in distributing devices but in redesigning tasks so students question, build and argue.

Common précis errors

ErrorWhy it loses marksFix
Copying sentencesTests transcription, not comprehensionClose the passage and rewrite from memory
Keeping examplesInflates length, dilutes argumentCut illustrations; keep the claim they support
Adding opinionA précis reports, it does not argueNo "I think"; no new facts
Wrong proportionSignals loss of controlTarget one-third ±10%; count words
No title/scaleMisses a stated instructionAdd title and word count if asked
The 30-second test
After writing, ask: if I read only my précis, would I know the author's main claim and its main support? If not, add the missing reason; if yes, cut anything else.
